# Sūrah al-Nūr (Chapter 24) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Chapter Named for Light, Built Around Purity**

Sūrah al-Nūr, "The Light," is the twenty-fourth chapter of the Qur'an, sixty-four verses long, and by the agreement of the overwhelming majority of scholars a Madinan revelation — that is, revealed after the Prophet's ﷺ emigration to Madinah, when the believers were no longer a persecuted minority but a functioning society with homes, courts, marriages, markets, reputations, and neighbors. That social setting explains almost everything about the sūrah's character. A community that has just acquired the power to punish also acquires the power to slander; a community that has just built houses also acquires the question of who may enter them; a community in which men and women now walk the same streets, share the same mosque, and trade in the same market must learn how to look at one another. Al-Nūr legislates for exactly these situations. Some commentators hold that verse 58 alone is Makkan, though the sūrah's texture as a whole belongs unmistakably to Madinah. Its title comes from its most celebrated passage, the Light Verse (v. 35) — *"God is the Light of the heavens and the earth"* — a single sentence that generated more metaphysical, mystical, and artistic reflection than perhaps any other verse of the Qur'an outside Āyat al-Kursī. But the placing of that verse is the sūrah's great lesson. It does not stand at the opening as an abstract theological preface. It arrives at the exact midpoint, after thirty-four verses of the most concrete, unglamorous, socially awkward legislation in the Book: the punishment for adultery, the punishment for slander, the oath-procedure for a husband who accuses his wife, the long correction of a community that gossiped about the Prophet's ﷺ own household, the rules for knocking on a door, the command to lower the gaze, the ordering of a woman's dress, the encouragement to marry the poor and free the enslaved. Only then: *God is the Light of the heavens and the earth.* The architecture is itself an argument. Light is not reached by leaping past the law of the tongue and the eye; it is reached by walking through it. The lamp is lit in the niche of a purified house.

**The Two Halves and the Hinge**

Read as a whole, the sūrah falls into two great movements joined by the Light Verse. The first movement (vv. 1–34) is *the purification of the community's private life*. It opens by announcing itself as a sūrah "sent down and ordained" — legislation with divine warrant (v. 1). It then legislates sexual offence (v. 2), the marital consequences of it (v. 3), and immediately — and this ordering is deliberate — turns to the far commoner crime of *accusing* someone of it without proof (vv. 4–5). Between the two it places the marital oath-procedure of *liʿān* (vv. 6–9), which protects both a wronged husband and a wronged wife. Then comes the long central passage on the slander of ʿĀʾishah (vv. 11–26), in which God rebukes not merely the slanderers but the ordinary believers who repeated a rumor without thinking. Out of that scandal the sūrah derives the everyday etiquette that would have prevented it: ask permission before entering a house (vv. 27–29), lower the gaze and guard chastity (vv. 30–31), marry rather than burn (vv. 32–33), and free those in bondage rather than exploit them (v. 33). The hinge (vv. 35–38) is the Light Verse and its immediate continuation about the houses God permits to be raised — the mosques, and by extension the hearts, in which His name is remembered by men whom trade does not distract. The second movement (vv. 39–64) is *the exposure of the alternative*. Against the lamp in the niche stand two counter-parables: the disbeliever's deeds as a mirage in the desert (v. 39), and as layered darkness in a fathomless sea (v. 40). Then follow the signs of God in creation — the glorification of the birds (v. 41), the driving of the clouds (v. 43), the alternation of night and day (v. 44), the creation of every animal from water (v. 45) — evidence available to anyone who will look. The final section (vv. 47–64) turns to the hypocrites who say "we hear and obey" but withdraw when judgment goes against them, contrasted with the true believers whose whole speech is *"we hear and we obey"* (v. 51), followed by the great promise of *istikhlāf* — succession and security on the earth for those who believe and act rightly (v. 55). The sūrah closes by returning, remarkably, to household manners once more (vv. 58–61) and to the etiquette of the Prophet's ﷺ assembly (vv. 62–63), before its final horizon-opening verse: everything in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, who knows exactly the state you are in (v. 64).

**Why Light and Law Belong Together**

A modern reader may find the combination strange: why should a chapter about lashes, gossip, headscarves, and knocking on doors be named after light? The sūrah's answer is that light in the Qur'anic sense is not a mood but a *medium of perception*. Light is what allows a thing to be seen as it is. The whole first half of the sūrah concerns failures of perception: people who "saw" an act of adultery without four witnesses; people who "heard" a rumor and did not weigh it; eyes that look where they should not; visitors who barge in and see what they were not meant to see. Every one of these is a disorder of vision. Then comes the verse: God is the Light of the heavens and the earth — the source of all seeing, and the one who sees you. The logic is exact. Discipline the eye, the tongue, and the door, and the heart becomes a clean glass; a clean glass takes the flame. The sūrah says as much in v. 21: *"Were it not for God's Bounty toward you, and His Mercy, not one of you would ever be pure."* Purity here — *zakāh*, the same root as the alms — means growth through cleansing. Al-Nūr is the Qur'an's manual for that cleansing at the level of a whole society.

**The Sūrah's Distinctive Rhetorical Devices**

Three features of style are worth noticing before the verses begin, because they recur. First, the *repeated refrain of divine bounty*: "And were it not for God's Bounty upon you, and His Mercy…" appears at verses 10, 14, 20, and 21, each time in a suspended, unfinished sentence whose conclusion the listener must supply. Arabic rhetoric calls this *ḥadhf jawāb al-sharṭ* — the omission of the consequence — and it is far more terrifying and more merciful than any stated threat. God does not finish the sentence about what would have happened to the community that slandered a Prophet's wife. Second, the *closing divine names*. Nearly every legal verse in this sūrah ends by naming God with the two or three attributes most relevant to that ruling: Forgiving and Merciful after repentance; Hearing and Knowing after commands touching what people say and see; Knowing and Wise after evidentiary procedure. The names are not decoration. They are the reason the law can be trusted: the Legislator is not a distant authority but the One who hears the whisper and knows the intention. Third, the *insistence on clarity*: the phrase *āyāt mubayyināt*, "clarifying signs," frames the sūrah at verses 1, 34, 46, 58, 59, and 61. This is a chapter that repeatedly says of itself: this is made plain, you cannot claim you did not know.

**How the Commentary That Follows Proceeds**

Each of the sixty-four verses is treated below as an independent discourse, in strict order. Where the source commentary groups verses — for instance the *liʿān* verses 6–9, or the slander passage 11–22, or the parables 39–40 — they are separated here and each verse receives its own full treatment: its Arabic vocabulary and roots explained in plain English, its cross-references quoted from the Qur'an itself, the relevant authentic Hadith given in its actual wording with its collection named, the views of the classical commentators (al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Rāzī, Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Taymiyyah and others) where they illuminate, the occasion of revelation where it is known, the reasoning behind the ruling, and the bearing of the verse on ordinary life today. Nothing has been shortened on account of the chapter's length. A sūrah that spends four verses teaching a community how to knock on a door deserves a commentary that does not hurry.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:1

> **A sūrah that We have sent down, and ordained, and in which We have sent down clear signs, that haply you may remember.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Chapter That Introduces Itself**

The Qur'an rarely announces a chapter by naming its own genre. Most sūrahs begin with an oath, a letter-cluster, a command, a praise, or a narrative. This one begins by saying, in effect: *this is a sūrah*. The Arabic opens with the single word *sūratun* in the indefinite nominative, which in classical grammar requires an unstated subject to complete it — the commentators supply "This is" — so the sense is: "*This* is a sūrah which We have sent down and ordained." Al-Rāzī notes the alternative reading in which *sūratan* is accusative, governed by an implied verb such as "recite" or "consider," which would render it: "Consider a sūrah We have sent down…" Either way, the effect is that the chapter steps forward and identifies itself before speaking, the way a herald announces the source of the decree he is about to read aloud. The word *sūrah* itself is worth pausing over. Its root is debated among the philologists. Some derive it from *sūr*, the wall that encircles a city — a sūrah being a bounded, walled-off portion of revelation, complete in itself and defended against intrusion. Others derive it from *suʾr*, a remainder or portion, as in a portion of drink left in a vessel; a sūrah is thus one measured serving of the whole. Al-Qurṭubī records both. Both images fit al-Nūr precisely: a chapter whose subject matter is boundaries — the boundary of the household, the boundary of the gaze, the boundary of the tongue — is fittingly named with a word that may itself mean a wall.

**"Sent Down" Twice: The Chapter and Its Contents**

Notice the doubling. God says the sūrah was "sent down" (*anzalnāhā*), and then that within it "We have sent down clear signs" (*anzalnā fīhā āyātin bayyināt*). Why say it twice? The commentators offer a precise answer: the first descent refers to the chapter as a unified legislative whole; the second to the individual verses within it, each of which is itself a self-standing revealed proof. A reader might otherwise treat a long chapter as a single lump of religious rhetoric. The verse forecloses that: every clause you are about to read — the number of lashes, the number of witnesses, the times of day when a servant knocks, the relatives before whom a woman may relax her covering — is *individually* revealed, individually binding, and individually clear. The verb *anzala* (to send down) carries in Qur'anic usage the sense of transmission from a higher source to a lower recipient, and by implication of authority that does not originate with the recipient. This matters enormously for a chapter of this content. Legislation about sexuality, reputation, and dress is precisely the legislation human societies most love to invent and revise according to fashion and appetite. By stamping the chapter at its threshold with "We sent it down," the verse removes it from the marketplace of opinion. Whatever else may be said about these rules, they are not the customs of seventh-century Arabia laundered into scripture; they are presented as descending law.

**"Ordained": Faraḍnāhā and Its Two Meanings**

The pivotal word is *faraḍnāhā*, "We have ordained it," from the root *f-r-ḍ*. This root has two great streams of meaning in Arabic, and the commentators exploit both. The first is *obligation*. A *farḍ* is a duty that must be performed; *farḍ ʿayn* is an individual obligation, *farḍ kifāyah* a communal one. On this reading — favored by al-Rāzī and reported widely — the verse tells us that this chapter is not exhortation but *command*: the rulings within it are compulsory, not recommendations to be weighed against convenience. This is a serious claim about a chapter that will shortly regulate what a Muslim does with his eyes. The second stream is *allocation, apportioning, cutting to measure*. The verb *faraḍa* is used for assigning a fixed share — as in *farīḍah*, the fixed portion of an inheritance, and in the Qur'anic phrase about a dowry *"apportioned"* (2:237). On this reading, reported by al-Qurṭubī, the meaning is that the sūrah's contents have been apportioned and measured out — each ruling given its exact quantity, no more and no less. And indeed this chapter is remarkable for its numbers: one hundred lashes, eighty lashes, four witnesses, four testimonies plus a fifth, three times of privacy. Nothing is left to the inflation of zeal. A society left to itself would either under-punish out of indulgence or over-punish out of fury; the root *f-r-ḍ* means the amount has been *cut to size* by the Lawgiver. Al-Qurṭubī also records a third nuance from the same root: that the Qur'an is revealed in apportioned installments, sūrah by sūrah, as human capacity allows — which resonates with the Book's own statement:

> *"And it is a Qur'an that We have divided in parts, that you may recite it unto mankind at intervals; and We have sent it down in gradual revelation."* (Qur'an 17:106)

Both meanings are true at once, and their combination is the verse's genius: the law is *binding* and it is *measured*. Obligation without measure is tyranny; measure without obligation is advice. Islam's legal ethic lives in the meeting of the two.

**"Clear Signs": Bayyināt and the Ethics of Clarity**

The chapter contains *āyāt mubayyināt* — "clarifying signs," or in an alternative recitation *mubayyanāt*, "clarified signs." The difference is instructive and the commentators note both: on the first reading the verses do the clarifying (they make truth plain to you); on the second they have themselves been made plain (God has removed obscurity from them). The root *b-y-n* gives *bayān*, lucid exposition, and *bayna*, "between" — clarity in Arabic is fundamentally the act of *separating* one thing from another so that the boundary is visible. A *bayyinah* in Islamic law is decisive evidence. There is a moral principle embedded here. Law that punishes must be law that is knowable in advance. A ruler who punishes his subjects for violating rules he never published is a tyrant even if the rules are wise. The Qur'an, before setting out the severest penalties in the entire legal corpus, first establishes that these penalties are announced, explained, bounded, and public. The theological form of this same principle appears elsewhere in the Book:

> *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger."* (Qur'an 17:15)

Clarity precedes consequence. Every serious system of justice has since discovered the same rule and called it the prohibition of retroactive law.

**"That You May Remember": The Purpose Clause**

The verse closes with *laʿallakum tadhakkarūn* — "that haply you may remember," or "take heed." The particle *laʿalla* ordinarily expresses hope, and since hope in the strict sense is impossible for God, who knows all outcomes, the grammarians read it here as expressing the *purpose and expected outcome from the human side*: the sūrah is revealed so that its listeners will in fact take heed, and it is on them that the "perhaps" rests. The verb *tadhakkara* (to remember, to be reminded) is not *taʿallama* (to learn). The Qur'an's characteristic assumption is that the human being already possesses, in his primordial nature (*fiṭrah*), a knowledge that decency is better than indecency, that a household should have a door, that a reputation should not be destroyed by rumor. Revelation therefore functions largely as *reminder* — knocking the dust off a knowledge already present. This is why the same word describes the Qur'an as a whole: *dhikr*, remembrance.

**The Verse as a Contract Between Legislator and Reader**

Put the four elements together and this short opening verse turns out to be a complete statement of what a divine legal chapter is and how it must be received. It is *revealed*, so its authority is not negotiable. It is *ordained*, so it is binding. It is *measured*, so it is proportionate. It is *clear*, so it is knowable. And its aim is *remembrance*, so its ultimate target is not the back of the offender but the conscience of the community. A practical implication follows for how a believer should read what comes next. The verses ahead include some of the most discussed, contested, and — in the modern world — most caricatured passages in the Qur'an. This first verse insists that they be read as *law with a purpose*, published in advance, apportioned by a wise Legislator, and directed at a society's memory of its own better nature. Whoever reads them as raw severity has ignored the chapter's own instructions for its use, which it placed, deliberately, before anything else.

## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:2

> **As for the adulterer and the adulteress, flog them each one hundred lashes, and let not pity for them overcome you concerning God's Judgment, if you believe in God and the Last Day. And let their punishment be witnessed by a group of the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The First Legislated Ruling of the Chapter of Light**

Having announced itself as a revealed, ordained, and clarified body of law, the sūrah opens its legislation with the offence of *zinā*. The choice of starting point is not arbitrary. In the Qur'anic ordering of social harms, the corruption of lineage and the collapse of sexual trust are treated as foundational, because every other institution — marriage, inheritance, child-rearing, the honor of families, the confidence of a husband in his children, the security of a woman in her home — rests upon them. A society may survive bad markets. It struggles to survive the general destruction of trust between men and women.

**Zānī and Zāniyah: What the Words Actually Cover**

*Al-zāniyah wa'l-zānī* — the adulteress and the adulterer — are active participles from *zinā*. In Arabic legal usage as the jurists received it, *zinā* denotes unlawful sexual intercourse generally, whether the persons involved are married or unmarried; English has no single word for it, since "adultery" implies marriage and "fornication" implies its absence. Al-Qurṭubī is explicit that the term embraces both. The Qur'an mentions *zinā* elsewhere:

> *"And approach not adultery. Truly it is an indecency and an evil way."* (Qur'an 17:32)

That verse is itself a model of Qur'anic legal psychology: it does not say "do not commit adultery," but "*do not approach* it," legislating against the road as well as the destination — which is exactly why this sūrah will later regulate the gaze, the door, and the dress. A closely related Qur'anic term is *fāḥishah* / *faḥshāʾ*, usually rendered "indecency," which frequently though not always denotes sexual misconduct; 4:15 is generally read by the commentators as referring to *zinā* under that name.

**Why the Woman Is Named First**

It is a small but much-discussed feature of the verse that the feminine *al-zāniyah* precedes the masculine *al-zānī*, reversing the Qur'an's more usual order (compare 5:38, *"the thief, male and female"*, where the masculine comes first). The commentators offer several explanations: that in the crime of *zinā* the woman's consent is ordinarily the gate through which the act becomes possible, so the deterrent is addressed to her first; that the shame and social consequence fall more heavily upon her, so the warning is placed where the stakes are highest; and that the verse forestalls any assumption that the law is a male instrument for controlling women — the identical punishment is stated for both in a single breath, with no distinction of gender in the number of lashes. Whatever the reason, the effect is a strict symmetry that many pre-Islamic and later legal systems did not achieve: the same act, the same penalty, for both.

**The Prior Legislation and the Question of Abrogation**

Most jurists hold that the first Qur'anic penalty for sexual indecency was the temporary measure of 4:15–16:

> *"As for those of your women who commit an indecency, call four witnesses among you to bear witness against them. And if they bear witness, then confine them to their houses until death takes them, or until God appoints for them another way. And if two of those among you are guilty thereof, punish them both; but if they repent and make amends, then let them be."* (Qur'an 4:15–16)

The words *"until God appoints for them another way"* are an explicit promissory note of future legislation, and the classical consensus is that 24:2 is the "way" that was appointed. This is a valuable illustration of Qur'anic gradualism: the community was first given a containing measure, then the settled ruling.

**The Contested Question of Stoning**

No discussion of this verse is honest without addressing the long juristic debate over stoning (*rajm*) for married offenders — a penalty not found anywhere in the Qur'an, but held by the majority of classical jurists to be established by the Sunnah and by the practice of the first caliphs. The evidence adduced in its favor is well known. There are reports of the Prophet ﷺ adjudicating the case of a Jewish couple by the law of the Torah after having the relevant passage read to him; the case of Māʿiz ibn Mālik, who confessed four times and was turned away three times before the penalty was carried out; the case of the pregnant woman of Ghāmid, whom the Prophet ﷺ sent away until she gave birth and weaned her child; a report of a general rule distinguishing the virgin (*bikr*) from the non-virgin (*thayyib*); and reports attributed to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb affirming the penalty and warning that its absence from the written text should not mislead people. ʿAlī is reported to have said, after both lashing and stoning a woman:

> *"We lashed in accordance with the Book of God and stoned in accordance with the wont of the Messenger of God."*

Some of these reports also mention a so-called "stoning verse" — *"And the shaykh and the shaykhah, stone them outright"* — said to have had its recitation abrogated while its ruling remained, a category (*naskh al-tilāwah dūna al-ḥukm*) that has always been contested among the theorists of jurisprudence, since it asks the believer to accept a binding law whose scriptural text God removed. The difficulty, stated plainly, is this: 24:2 lays down a complete and detailed penalty — the offence, the quantity, the manner, the emotional discipline of those enforcing it, and the requirement of public witness — and it makes no distinction whatever between the married and the unmarried, and no mention whatever of stoning. To hold that a category of offenders is exempted from the stated penalty and subjected instead to death is, in the technical language of *uṣūl al-fiqh*, a substantial restriction or partial abrogation of an explicit Qur'anic text by non-Qur'anic reports. Jurists have always disagreed about whether solitary reports (*akhbār al-āḥād*) can perform that function against a decisive Qur'anic text. Accordingly, a minority stream of juristic opinion — including voices in the contemporary period — has read the stoning reports differently: as *taʿzīr*, discretionary punishment ordered by the head of state in particular circumstances, rather than a *ḥadd*, a fixed divine penalty. This is not a modern novelty of method: it is widely accepted that ʿUmar himself suspended the *ḥadd* for theft during the year of famine, which shows that even fixed penalties were understood as operating within a context of justice rather than as mechanical triggers. Some have further suggested that the stoning incidents, if historical, occurred *before* the revelation of this sūrah, and were therefore superseded by it. There is a striking report on exactly this point: when the jurist al-Shaybānī asked the Companion ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Awfā whether the Prophet ﷺ had stoned, he affirmed that he had — but said he did not know whether it was before or after the revelation of Sūrat al-Nūr. The purpose of laying this out is not to settle a debate that great scholars have not settled, but to model what the sūrah's first verse demanded: that these rulings be approached with clarity, evidence, and reasoning rather than with slogans in either direction.

**"Let Not Pity Overcome You": The Discipline of the Judge**

*Wa lā taʾkhudhkum bihimā raʾfatun fī dīni'llāh* — "let not pity for them seize you in God's religion." The word *raʾfah* denotes a tender, softening compassion, and the verb *taʾkhudh* means to *seize* or take hold of, as an emotion overtakes a person against his will. The verse is not condemning compassion — God Himself is *al-Raʾūf*, the Kind, a name used in this very sūrah at verse 20. It is addressing the moment when a court, having satisfied an almost impossibly demanding standard of proof, is tempted to abandon the sentence out of sentiment for a sympathetic defendant. Every legal system knows this failure. Law is undone not only by cruelty but by selective softness — the well-connected offender who is quietly excused, the sympathetic defendant released while the friendless one is punished in full. The phrase *fī dīni'llāh* — "in God's religion," or "concerning God's judgment" — is the anchor: the penalty is not the judge's property to waive, because it was not the judge's to invent. The condition that follows, *"if you believe in God and the Last Day,"* is one of the Qur'an's sharpest rhetorical instruments. It ties the steadiness of a judge's hand to his eschatology. A judge who genuinely believes he will stand before God is protected from both temptations: he will not exceed the sentence out of anger, and he will not abandon it out of favoritism. It should be said in the same breath that the Prophet's ﷺ own practice pushed hard in the direction of *avoiding* the penalty wherever a doubt existed. The famous juristic maxim, drawn from Prophetic guidance, is:

> *"Avert the fixed penalties by means of doubts."*

And in the report of Māʿiz, the Prophet ﷺ turned the confessor away repeatedly, and — on hearing that the man had tried to flee — asked reproachfully:

> *"Why did you not let him go, so that he might repent and God accept his repentance?"* (transmitted in the collections of Abū Dāwūd and elsewhere)

The severity of the text and the mercy of the practice are not in contradiction. The severity states how gravely God regards the destruction of sexual trust; the practice ensures that the penalty almost never falls, because the standard of proof — four eyewitnesses to the act of penetration itself, seen at one time and one place — is effectively unmeetable outside of open, brazen public indecency. This is the crucial interpretive key that is missed by both hostile critics and overzealous enforcers: the law is designed to make the *offence* unthinkable, not to make the *punishment* frequent. Historically, it was extraordinarily rare.

**The Manner of the Lashing**

Al-Qurṭubī, surveying the rulings of the jurists, describes an implementation that is startlingly restrained for a corporal penalty. The blows are to fall on the back, not the head or face or vital areas. The person is left standing and unbound, not humiliated in posture. Women retain a layer of clothing. The whip must be neither too hard nor too soft. The blows are intended to cause pain but not to break the skin or draw blood. The one administering them must not raise his arm high, so as not to deliver a heavier blow than intended — some jurists enforced this by requiring him to hold an object clamped under his armpit, so that his arm physically could not rise. It is to be carried out only under legitimate legal authority, never by a mob or a family. And where a person's health could not withstand it, the sentence was modified rather than executed to death. In a well-known report, a gravely ill man was brought for punishment; the Prophet ﷺ ordered that a bundle of a hundred twigs be tied together and the man struck once with the bundle (recorded by al-Qurṭubī among others). This single report tells us more about the purpose of the penalty than a library of polemics: what was required was the *fulfilment of the sentence*, not the maximization of suffering.

**"Witnessed by a Group of the Believers": The Function of Publicity**

The final clause requires that a *ṭāʾifah* — a group — of believers witness the punishment. The jurists disagreed on the minimum: some said one or more, some two (the usual number of witnesses), some three (the smallest number that can be called a "group"), and some four (matching the number of witnesses required to establish the offence in the first place), as al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī record. The rationale is threefold, and each part is worth thinking about. First, *deterrence*. A penalty carried out in secret deters nobody. The visible enactment of consequence recalibrates a community's sense of what is serious. Second, *transparency and protection of the accused*. A punishment that must be witnessed cannot be secretly exceeded. The requirement of witnesses is as much a constraint on the state as a shaming of the offender: it puts the enforcer under public scrutiny at the moment of maximum power. Third, *shame as part of the penalty itself*. In a society organized around honor, the public dimension is a large part of the deterrent, which again reduces the physical severity required.

**Why the Law Makes Sense**

Strip away the polemics and consider the underlying logic. Sexual acts outside marriage are treated here not as private preference but as public harm, and the Qur'an's reasoning is traceable. They produce children with uncertain paternity, and therefore uncertain rights of maintenance and inheritance. They introduce a permanent background suspicion into marriage, the institution on which child-rearing depends. They generate the very jealousy, rumor, and vengeance that the following twenty verses of this sūrah will spend so much effort suppressing. And they are — as every honest observer of human conduct knows — profoundly destructive to the individuals involved, most often to the more vulnerable party. Notice too the sequencing of the sūrah. The very next legislative topic (v. 4) is the punishment of *false accusation*, at eighty lashes — only twenty fewer than the offence itself, and accompanied by the permanent disqualification of the slanderer's testimony. A society is thereby placed in a vise: it may not be lax about sexual conduct, and it may not be loose with accusations. Both the offence and the *rumor* of the offence carry ruinous cost. This is what makes the Qur'anic scheme coherent rather than merely severe: the same chapter that authorizes a hundred lashes makes it nearly impossible to prove the case, and punishes the accuser who fails.

**The Verse in Contemporary Life**

For a Muslim living today in a society organized around sexual freedom, the practical force of this verse is not primarily juridical — few readers will ever sit on a court — but formative. It states, with a plainness no amount of cultural pressure can soften, that the sexual act is not morally weightless; that it carries consequences serious enough to be legislated by God; and that the destruction of a household is a matter of public, not merely private, concern. At the same time, the manner of the law teaches the believer how to hold that seriousness. He is not permitted to become an investigator of other people's private lives; the evidentiary bar forbids it. He is not permitted to slander; the next verse forbids it under penalty. He is not permitted to take punishment into his own hands; it belongs to legitimate authority alone. And when he does encounter another's fall, he stands under the Prophetic instruction:

> *"Whoever conceals the fault of a Muslim, God will conceal his faults in this world and the Hereafter."* (Muslim 2699)

The reconciliation is this: hold the *standard* without mercy, and the *person* with mercy — which is precisely the posture of a verse that commands a hundred lashes and, in the same chapter, insists that the God legislating them is Forgiving, Merciful, and Kind.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:3

> **The adulterer shall marry none but an adulteress or idolatress, and the adulteress shall marry none but an adulterer or idolater. And that is forbidden to the believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse About Consequences, Not Merely Penalties**

The previous verse dealt with the courtroom; this verse deals with the drawing room. Punishment addresses the act; this verse addresses the *life that follows* the act — the question every society must answer after it has punished someone: what is this person's place among us now? The Qur'an here says something subtle and much debated: there is an affinity between the sexually corrupt and the sexually corrupt, and between the corrupt and the idolater, and the believer belongs in neither company.

**The Grammatical Puzzle: Statement or Prohibition?**

The verse is phrased as a *statement of fact* — "the adulterer marries none but…" — not as a command ("do not marry"). Only the closing clause turns explicitly prohibitive: *wa ḥurrima dhālika ʿalā'l-muʾminīn*, "and that is forbidden to the believers." Arabic frequently uses the declarative to express the normative, exactly as English does when a parent says "we don't speak to people that way" rather than "do not speak." But the ambiguity generated centuries of juristic discussion, and the commentators produced four broad readings.

**Reading One: A Legal Prohibition on Marriage**

Some jurists took the verse at its most literal legal force: a person guilty of *zinā* is barred from marrying a chaste believer, so long as that status persists. Al-Qurṭubī records this as one of the positions, understanding the verse as underscoring the enormity of the offence by attaching to it a marital disqualification. The logic is one of proportion: if a man has treated the marriage bond as nothing, he has demonstrated that he is not yet fit to be entrusted with one. The great difficulty with this reading, which the commentators themselves raise, is that taken literally it would appear to *permit* a Muslim adulterer to marry an idolatress — and marriage to idolaters is categorically forbidden elsewhere in the Book:

> *"And marry not idolatresses until they believe… nor marry idolaters until they believe."* (Qur'an 2:221)

Since scripture does not contradict itself, this pushes interpreters toward reading the verse as describing an affinity rather than granting a licence.

**Reading Two: A Description of Inclination**

Al-Rāzī and others read the verse as a statement about *desire and compatibility*. A man habituated to unlawful sexual conduct does not, as a rule, want a chaste and God-fearing wife; he wants someone who shares his appetite and will not restrain it. Likewise, a chaste believing woman does not want him. The verse is thus reporting a sociological and psychological law: like seeks like. People pair with those who will not indict them. This reading is confirmed by ordinary observation. A person who intends to keep drinking does not choose a spouse who will object; a person committed to dishonesty in business avoids a scrupulous partner. Human beings do not merely choose companions, they choose *permissions*. That is exactly why the Qur'an legislates about companionship at all.

**Reading Three: Who Consents to Such a Union**

Ibn Kathīr records a third construction: the only people who would *agree to* and be content with such a marriage are those who share the offender's indifference to the sacred — other offenders, or idolaters who never held the standard in the first place. On this reading the verse describes not the offender's taste but the community's response: a household of faith will not open itself to an unrepentant destroyer of households.

**Reading Four: Nikāḥ as Intercourse**

The verb translated "marry" is from *nikāḥ*, which in Arabic usage can denote either the marriage contract or the sexual act itself; al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn Kathīr all note the ambiguity. If the intercourse-sense is intended, the verse becomes a statement about the reciprocity of guilt: *there is no such thing as one-sided zinā*. If one party is an adulterer, so is the other. This reading has real moral force in societies — ancient and modern — where the woman bears the entire stigma of an act that by definition required two. The Qur'an's grammar refuses that asymmetry. Some commentators objected that *nikāḥ* in the Qur'an always means marriage, though al-Qurṭubī notes that interpreters of 4:22 read the same verb as referring to sexual relations, so the objection is not decisive.

**The Occasion of Revelation: The People of the Veranda**

The commentators preserve a specific context that clarifies much. Among the earliest Muslims in Madinah were the *Ahl al-Ṣuffah*, the People of the Veranda — destitute emigrants with no home but the covered platform at the edge of the Prophet's ﷺ mosque. Certain of these men, in extreme poverty, considered marrying women of Madinah who were known professional prostitutes, since such women had houses and income and could provide food and shelter. Reports of this occasion are recorded by al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī, and Ibn Kathīr, and the verse is said to have come down in response. Read against that background, the verse's sharpness makes sense. It is not addressing a person who once fell into sin and repented; it is addressing believers who were about to enter, knowingly and permanently, into a household built on unrepented sexual commerce — and to do so *for money*. The verse says: you may be starving, but you may not purchase relief at the price of the moral foundation of your home. And the very next legislative move of the sūrah (v. 32) will answer the poverty that made such a proposal tempting: *"If they are poor, God will enrich them from His Bounty."*

**Repentance Reopens the Door**

Crucially, no major school treats the disqualification as permanent for one who repents. Al-Qurṭubī rejects the narrow view that the verse refers merely to those upon whom the *ḥadd* had been carried out, precisely because such a reading would produce the absurdity of permitting a believing woman to marry an idolater. The general principle of Islamic law is stated by the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"The one who repents from sin is like one who has no sin at all."* (Ibn Mājah 4250)

And the Qur'an makes repentance the great reversal of even the gravest sexual and violent crimes:

> *"Save those who repent and believe and perform righteous deeds; for them God will replace their evil deeds with good deeds. And God is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 25:70, following the mention of murder and adultery in 25:68)

So the verse is not a caste system. It is a description of a *condition*, and conditions can change. The moment sincere repentance occurs, the description no longer applies, and the person rejoins the community of the chaste.

**The Principle Beneath the Ruling: Marriage Is Formation, Not Only Affection**

The deep logic of this verse is that marriage is a *formative institution*, not merely a romantic or economic arrangement. A spouse is the single greatest environmental influence on a person's character over a lifetime, and the primary moral atmosphere in which children are raised. This is why the Prophet ﷺ instructed:

> *"A woman is married for four things: her wealth, her lineage, her beauty and her religion. So choose the religious one, may your hands be rubbed with dust."* (al-Bukhārī 5090; Muslim 1466)

The advice is not snobbery about piety; it is realism about influence. You will become, over decades, something close to the average of the household you build.

**Modern Application: The Company You Legalize**

Few readers today will confront the exact scenario of the Veranda. But the principle applies with total precision to modern life. Every marriage is a decision about which appetites in oneself will be restrained and which will be licensed. A person struggling with dishonesty who marries someone indifferent to honesty has effectively legislated his own defeat. A person who wishes to keep faith and marries someone who mocks it will spend a lifetime negotiating what should have been settled. The verse also carries a warning against the fantasy of reform-by-marriage — the widespread belief that one can marry a person of loose commitments and change them by love. Sometimes that happens; the Qur'an is describing the ordinary case, and the ordinary case is that the household converges on the lower standard, because the lower standard requires no effort. And the verse contains a quiet compliment to the chaste. It says, in effect, that virtue is not merely a personal ornament but a *criterion of pairing*: that God has arranged the moral universe so that purity is drawn to purity. That theme will return with full force at verse 26 — *"good women are for good men, and good men are for good women"* — where the same principle is applied, in vindication rather than warning, to the household of the Prophet ﷺ himself.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:4

> **And as for those who accuse chaste women, but then do not bring four witnesses, flog them eighty lashes, and never accept any testimony from them. And it is they who are the iniquitous,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Crime of the Tongue Placed Beside the Crime of the Body**

Two verses ago the Qur'an legislated a hundred lashes for the act of *zinā*. Now it legislates eighty for merely *saying* that someone committed it, without proof. The proportion is astonishing and deliberate: the punishment for the accusation is four-fifths of the punishment for the deed. No other legal tradition of the ancient world placed slander so close to the offence it alleged. The message is unmistakable. In the Qur'anic moral order, the destruction of an innocent person's reputation is a crime of nearly the same magnitude as the sexual violation itself. And in one respect the verse treats the slanderer *more* harshly, because the punishment does not end with the lashes: his testimony is permanently rejected, and he is branded with a name — *al-fāsiqūn*, the iniquitous. A convicted adulterer who repents suffers no such lasting civic disability spelled out in the text. The tongue, this chapter insists, is the more dangerous weapon, because it can be used from a distance, in comfort, against anyone, at any time, and by anyone.

**Qadhf: The Legal Term and Its Physical Image**

The technical name of this offence in Islamic law is *qadhf*, from the verb *qadhafa*, meaning to hurl, fling, or cast something at a target. The same root appears in the Qur'an for a physical throwing — as in the casting of the infant Moses into the river (20:39). The image built into the word is exact: slander is *thrown*. It leaves the hand of the thrower, travels a distance, and strikes a person who often never sees it coming and cannot catch it once it is in the air. The verb used in this verse is *yarmūn* — "those who *shoot* at" or "pelt" chaste women. Again the metaphor is ballistic. Arabic did not have to reach for a special vocabulary of defamation; it used the language of projectiles, because that is what an accusation is.

**Muḥṣanāt: The Protected**

The victims are described as *al-muḥṣanāt*. The root *ḥ-ṣ-n* is the root of *ḥiṣn*, a fortress. A *muḥṣanah* is a woman who is "fortified" — a word that carries in Qur'anic usage several overlapping senses: chaste, married, or free (as opposed to enslaved); see the discussion at 4:24. The dominant sense here is chastity: a woman whose known condition is one of moral protection. The imagery is worth dwelling on. A chaste person's reputation is described as a *fortress*, and slander is described as *shooting at it*. The verse presents defamation as a siege. And a reputation, unlike a wall, cannot be rebuilt once breached — which is why the law responds so heavily. Although the feminine plural is used, the commentators are unanimous — Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī state it explicitly — that the ruling applies equally to accusations against men. The feminine is used because women were and are the more frequent targets of sexual slander and suffer the more devastating consequences from it.

**The Four Witnesses: A Bar Designed Not to Be Cleared**

The condition is *four* witnesses. Islamic law ordinarily requires two witnesses in serious matters; here the number is doubled. And al-Qurṭubī records the conditions the jurists derived: the witnesses must have observed the act of penetration itself, directly; they must have seen it *in one place and at one time* — four people each testifying to a different occasion is inadmissible; and both accuser and accused must satisfy conditions of sanity and legal adulthood. Consider what this means in practice. For four upright adults to have simultaneously observed an act of intercourse, the act must have been performed in effectively public view. Any sexual sin committed with the least discretion is, as a matter of law, unprovable — and therefore, as a matter of law, *not to be spoken of*. This is the crucial architecture of the chapter: God did not create a mechanism for the community to police one another's bedrooms. He created a mechanism that makes such policing legally impossible and socially punishable. Ibn Taymiyyah and others in the juristic tradition drew the natural conclusion: the *ḥadd* of *zinā* exists to deter open, flagrant indecency that corrupts public morals, not to hunt private sin, which is left between the servant and his Lord, and for which the door of concealment and repentance is deliberately left wide open.

**"Never Accept Any Testimony From Them"**

The second penalty is civic rather than corporal, and in a functioning society far heavier: the slanderer is permanently disqualified as a witness. In a legal order that ran on personal testimony — contracts, debts, marriages, inheritance, criminal cases — to be declared untrustworthy as a witness was to be removed from public life. It is roughly equivalent to permanent disbarment plus the loss of any credential, and it operates as a standing public notice: *this person's word cannot be relied upon.* There is a deep principle of justice here. The punishment fits the crime in *kind*, not merely in severity. He abused speech; he is deprived of the legal power of speech. He asked the community to believe him without evidence; the community will now never believe him at all. The jurists differed on the scope. Some restricted the permanent disqualification to false testimony in matters carrying *ḥadd* penalties, leaving him competent in ordinary civil matters; al-Qurṭubī records this. And many held that sincere repentance restores competence — a position that turns on how one reads the word *abadan* ("never") in light of the exception in the following verse, discussed there.

**Fāsiqūn: The Name They Are Given**

The verse concludes by naming them: *wa ulāʾika hum al-fāsiqūn* — "and it is they who are the iniquitous." The root *f-s-q* originally describes a date or a nut *breaking out of its skin*. A *fāsiq* is one who has burst out of the shell of obedience — a person who knows the boundary and has stepped deliberately outside it. The construction is emphatic in Arabic — *ulāʾika hum*, "those, they specifically" — the same restrictive construction the Qur'an uses for the successful (*ulāʾika hum al-mufliḥūn*, 2:5). The community may have regarded the slanderer as a concerned citizen, a moral watchman, a man merely reporting what he heard. God calls him the transgressor. That reversal of labels is the verse's rhetorical heart: in a slander case, the person who feels most righteous is the criminal.

**The Occasion of Revelation**

Some commentators hold that this verse came down in connection with the slander against ʿĀʾishah, the wife of the Prophet ﷺ (the incident detailed in vv. 11–26), while others, as al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī note, regard it as a general ruling not tied to any particular event. The two views are easily reconciled: even if the ruling is general, its placement immediately before the ʿĀʾishah passage is clearly purposeful. God states the law, and then narrates the case study.

**Why the Law Is Rational**

Consider what a society looks like without this rule. Sexual accusation is the cheapest and most devastating weapon available to a human being. It requires no resources. It can be deployed by a rejected suitor, a rival wife, a jealous colleague, a hostile neighbor, a child in a custody dispute. It is nearly impossible to disprove — how does one prove a negative about a private act? And it destroys, permanently, in a way that most accusations do not: even after acquittal, the suspicion lingers. "There is no smoke without fire" is a proverb of nearly every language, and it is the mechanism by which innocent lives are ruined. An unregulated market in sexual accusation therefore produces a society in which no one's reputation is secure and everyone is a potential hostage. The Qur'anic solution is to make the weapon extremely expensive to fire: bring four eyewitnesses to the act, or receive eighty lashes and lose your standing forever. The result is an equilibrium in which people simply do not make such accusations unless the matter was already public — which is precisely the aim.

**The Everyday Reach of a Courtroom Ruling**

Very few Muslims will ever be tried for *qadhf*. Nearly all of us commit its moral substance regularly. The modern forms are familiar: the insinuation dropped in conversation, the "I'm not saying anything, but…", the screenshot forwarded to a group chat, the anonymous post, the shared rumor about a colleague's private life, the comment beneath a photograph. The mechanism is identical to the seventh-century original; the range of the projectile is now global and its flight is permanent. The Prophet ﷺ named this among the very worst of destroying sins:

> *"Avoid the seven destructive sins."* They said: "O Messenger of God, what are they?" He said: *"Associating partners with God, sorcery, killing a soul which God has forbidden except by right, consuming interest, consuming the property of an orphan, fleeing from the battlefield, and slandering chaste, unaware, believing women."* (al-Bukhārī 2766; Muslim 89)

Slander of the chaste is listed in the same breath as idolatry and murder. That is the measure of it. And the Qur'an gives the corresponding rule of ordinary speech in Sūrat al-Ḥujurāt:

> *"O you who believe! If an iniquitous person brings you a report, verify it, lest you harm a people out of ignorance and then become remorseful over what you have done."* (Qur'an 49:6)

The practical discipline this verse asks of a believer can be reduced to a single habit: before repeating anything damaging about another person, ask *what is my evidence, and would I state it under oath before God?* If the answer is that someone told you, the correct action is silence. The verse before us is God's assessment of what the alternative is worth: eighty lashes and the permanent loss of your credibility.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:5

> **save those who repent thereafter and make amends, for truly God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Door Left Open at the End of a Severe Sentence**

Immediately after branding the slanderer with eighty lashes, permanent disqualification, and the name *fāsiq*, the Qur'an appends a single exception clause. The syntax matters: this is not a new verse in a different tone, arriving after a pause. It is grammatically joined to the sentence before it, an exception (*istithnāʾ*) attached directly to the condemnation. The severity and the mercy are one utterance. This is a recurring pattern in Qur'anic penal legislation. The Book almost never closes a punishment without an exit. After the punishment of those who wage war against God and His Messenger:

> *"…save those who repent before you overpower them. And know that God is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 5:34)

After the penalty for theft:

> *"But whosoever repents after his wrongdoing and makes amends, God will relent unto him. Truly God is Forgiving, Merciful."* (Qur'an 5:39)

The pattern is theological, not merely legal: God's justice is real and enforceable, but it is never the last word available to a human being who turns back.

**Tawbah: The Grammar of Turning**

The word *tāba* means, at root, to *return*. It describes a physical turning-around — reversing direction on a road. This is why Islamic repentance is not primarily an emotion but a *change of vector*. Remorse is its feeling; reversal is its substance. And the same verb is used of God. God is *al-Tawwāb*, "the Oft-Returning" — He turns toward the servant who turns toward Him. The Qur'an describes both movements in one remarkable verse about the three who stayed behind from the expedition to Tabūk:

> *"Then He relented unto them, that they might repent. Truly God is the Relenting, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 9:118)

The order is stunning: God turns to them *so that* they may turn to Him. Even the capacity to repent is itself a gift already given. The slanderer who feels the first pang of regret should understand that the pang is not his own achievement but the first sign that mercy has already moved toward him.

**Aṣlaḥa: Repentance That Must Be Built, Not Only Felt**

The verse does not say "those who repent" and stop. It says *"those who repent thereafter **and make amends**"* — *wa aṣlaḥū*, from *ṣ-l-ḥ*, the root of *ṣāliḥ* (righteous) and *iṣlāḥ* (reform, repair, restoration to soundness). The word is used of mending what is broken and of reconciling parties who have fallen out. Both senses apply exactly. The slanderer has *broken* something — a person's standing — and he has *ruptured* a relationship between that person and the community. Feeling sorry repairs neither. Al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī explain *aṣlaḥa* here in concrete terms: living for a period of time in a manner visibly honorable, so that the change becomes evident to the people who witnessed the offence; and some jurists spoke of acts such as giving charity to make the sincerity of repentance manifest. There is real wisdom in requiring a *visible* interval. The community's confidence was destroyed publicly; it cannot be restored by a private feeling. And the requirement protects the sincerity of the repentance itself: anyone can produce remorse in the moment of consequence. Time is the only reliable test of a change of direction. The Islamic tradition systematized the conditions of valid repentance in a form worth stating plainly, because this verse is one of its scriptural anchors. Repentance from a sin against God requires: ceasing the sin; regretting it; and firmly resolving not to return to it. When the sin involved a wrong against another human being, a fourth condition is added: restoring the right of the wronged party — returning what was taken, or seeking their pardon. Slander falls into this fourth category. The slanderer's repentance is not complete before God until he has done what he can to repair the damage to the person he named: retracting the accusation publicly, before the same audience that heard it, wherever that is possible.

**The Juristic Dispute: What Exactly Does Repentance Restore?**

Verse 4 imposed three consequences: the lashes, the permanent rejection of testimony, and the label *fāsiq*. The scholars asked which of these the exception in verse 5 lifts. Al-Qurṭubī sets out the debate. The lashes themselves are not lifted. They are a *ḥadd*, a right of the community and of the injured party, and repentance after conviction does not erase an established penalty; if it did, every convict would repent at the courtroom door and the law would evaporate. On the label *fāsiq* there is broad agreement: repentance removes it. The word describes a present condition of transgression, and once the person has turned back, the description no longer fits. The real dispute concerns the *testimony*. One group — associated with the Ḥanafī position — held that the disqualification is permanent, reading the word *abadan* ("never") in verse 4 as absolute and taking the exception in verse 5 to apply only to the last clause, the label of iniquity. The other group — the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Mālik, Aḥmad and others — held that repentance restores the person's competence as a witness, reading the exception as governing the whole preceding sentence, and understanding *abadan* to mean "never so long as he persists in the slander." Al-Qurṭubī records exactly this gloss: never — as long as they remain in the state of being slanderers. The second position seems more consonant with the Qur'an's own logic. If repentance can restore a person to the status of the non-iniquitous, it is strange that he should remain permanently under a legal disability whose entire basis is that he is untrustworthy. And it accords with the general principle:

> *"Say: O My servants who have been prodigal to their own detriment, despair not of God's Mercy. Truly God forgives all sins. Truly He is the Forgiving, the Merciful."* (Qur'an 39:53)

**Two Names, Two Movements of Mercy**

The verse ends with *inna'llāha ghafūrun raḥīm* — "truly God is Forgiving, Merciful." The pairing is not redundant, and the distinction the scholars draw is illuminating. *Ghafūr* is from *gh-f-r*, whose root sense is to *cover* — the *mighfar* is the helmet a warrior wears to shield his head. Divine forgiveness in this name is the *covering over* of a fault: the sin is veiled, screened, hidden from exposure and from consequence. This is exactly the mercy a slanderer needs, and there is a poetic justice in it: the man who *uncovered* another's supposed sin is offered a God who will *cover* his real one. *Raḥīm* is from *r-ḥ-m*, the root of *raḥim*, the womb — mercy in the Qur'anic imagination is womb-like: enveloping, nourishing, unearned, given to a creature that has done nothing to deserve it. So the two names describe two motions: *ghafūr* removes the negative (the sin is covered), *raḥīm* adds the positive (the servant is embraced and given good). Forgiveness alone would leave a person acquitted but empty. Mercy fills him.

**Why the Exception Is Necessary for the Law to Function**

There is a hard practical logic to mercy that is often missed. A legal and moral order that offers no route back produces exactly the wrong incentives. A person who believes he is permanently condemned has nothing left to protect and no reason to reform; his rational course is to become worse, since improvement buys him nothing. This is a well-documented dynamic in every penal system that abandons rehabilitation. The Qur'an's insistence on an open door is therefore not sentimentality attached to justice; it is what makes justice *work*, because it keeps every offender within reach of return. The same dynamic operates in the individual soul. Despair is a more efficient engine of sin than desire. Most people who spiral do not do so because they stopped believing the sin was wrong; they spiral because they concluded they were already finished. Which is why the Prophet ﷺ reported God saying:

> *"O son of Adam, so long as you call upon Me and place your hope in Me, I will forgive you for whatever is in you, and I will not mind. O son of Adam, were your sins to reach the clouds of the sky, and then you sought My forgiveness, I would forgive you. O son of Adam, were you to come to Me with sins nearly as great as the earth, and then meet Me without ascribing any partner to Me, I would bring you forgiveness nearly as great as it."* (al-Tirmidhī 3540)

**Living the Verse**

For the ordinary reader, the practical shape of this verse is twofold. Toward oneself: no sin of the tongue — no matter how public, how humiliating, how many people heard it, how permanent it feels — places a person outside the reach of return. But the return must be *built*, not merely felt. Where you spoke, retract. Where you damaged, repair. Where you were believed, correct the record. Then live long enough in a different manner for the change to be visible. Toward others: the exception clause binds the community as much as the offender. If God restores the repentant, the believers may not maintain a private permanent register of a person's old offences. To hold someone forever to a sin God has covered is to set one's own judgment above His. That is a hard discipline, and it is exactly the discipline this sūrah is training — a chapter whose central narrative concerns a community that could not stop talking about something it should never have discussed at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:6

> **And as for those who accuse their wives and have no witnesses but themselves, then the testimony of one of them shall be four testimonies, swearing by God that he is among the truthful,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Case the Previous Law Could Not Solve**

Verse 4 laid down an iron rule: accuse a chaste person of adultery without four witnesses and receive eighty lashes. That rule protects the innocent magnificently — but it creates one situation of genuine agony, and this verse addresses it. Imagine a husband who has seen his wife's infidelity with his own eyes. He has no other witnesses; by the nature of the act he never will. Under verse 4 he has exactly two options: remain silent and continue in a marriage he believes to be defiled, possibly raising a child he believes is not his; or speak, and be flogged eighty times as a slanderer and stripped of his standing for life. Neither is tolerable. And the reverse case is just as grave: a wife falsely accused by a vindictive husband would, without a remedy, be destroyed by an allegation she cannot disprove. The commentators record that Companions raised this very difficulty. In one report, Saʿd ibn ʿUbādah, chief of the Khazraj, protested on hearing the law of four witnesses: "Is this how it was revealed, Messenger of God? If I found a man with my wife, should I not touch him until I bring four witnesses? By God, I would strike him with the sword before that!" The Prophet ﷺ said to those around him:

> *"Do you wonder at Saʿd's jealousy? By God, I am more jealous than he, and God is more jealous than I."* (Muslim 1498)

And in the best-known occasion of revelation, Hilāl ibn Umayyah came to the Prophet ﷺ and accused his wife. The Prophet ﷺ said: *"Evidence, or the punishment on your back."* Hilāl replied: "O Messenger of God, when one of us sees a man upon his wife, is he to go and look for evidence?" The Prophet ﷺ repeated his demand, and Hilāl said: "By the One who sent you with the truth, I am telling the truth, and God will surely send down something that will spare my back the punishment." Then Gabriel descended with these verses (al-Bukhārī 4747; a parallel account concerns ʿUwaymir al-ʿAjlānī, al-Bukhārī 5259; Muslim 1492). Some commentators reconcile the two accounts by holding that both incidents occurred and the verses covered both.

**Liʿān: The Name and the Procedure**

The procedure established in verses 6–9 is called *liʿān*, "mutual cursing," from *laʿnah*, a curse — named after the invocation in verse 7. It is one of the most remarkable legal instruments in scripture: a solemn, symmetrical, courtroom oath-duel conducted before God, in which no human being adjudicates the facts at all. The structure is exact. The husband swears four times that he is truthful, then a fifth time invoking God's curse upon himself if he lies (vv. 6–7). The wife may then swear four times that he is a liar, then a fifth invoking God's wrath upon herself if he is truthful (vv. 8–9). If she does so, the penalty is averted from her. The marriage is dissolved, and neither party is punished by the court.

**Why Four Testimonies from One Man**

The most striking feature of this verse is the arithmetic: *fa-shahādatu aḥadihim arbaʿu shahādātin bi'llāh* — "the testimony of one of them shall be four testimonies, swearing by God." One man's oath is made to stand in the place of the four witnesses that verse 4 demanded. This is a legal fiction of great elegance, and it discloses the underlying philosophy of Islamic evidence. The requirement of four witnesses was never about a magical number; it was about achieving a threshold of *certainty* sufficient to justify a devastating accusation. In the marital case, that threshold cannot be reached by outside observers — but the husband is himself the only possible witness, and he is placed in a position where lying carries a cost he cannot escape. Four repetitions, each a separate sworn statement in the name of God, each publicly uttered before the community, replace four separate mouths. There is deep psychological wisdom in the repetition. A person can blurt a lie once in a moment of rage. Repeating it four times, formally, slowly, in the name of God, before witnesses, with a fifth oath of self-cursing waiting at the end, gives the liar four chances to stop. Anyone who has watched a furious spouse in the first hours of suspicion knows how much a forced, deliberate, ritualized procedure can accomplish simply by slowing the accusation down.

**The Legal Consequences the Verse Sets in Motion**

The jurists derived from this passage, and from the Prophetic practice attached to it, several settled consequences. The *ḥadd* of slander is lifted from the husband, since he has substituted the oaths for the witnesses. The couple are separated, and the separation was held by the majority to be permanent — the Prophet ﷺ, after the *liʿān* of ʿUwaymir and his wife, is reported to have said that they should never come together again, and the tradition records: *"There is no way for you against her."* The child, if any, is attached to the mother's lineage and the paternity claim is severed; in the case of Hilāl's wife the Prophet ﷺ said to watch what the child would resemble, and the reports state that he added: *"Were it not for the oaths, I would have had a reckoning with her."* And the wife, if she swears her four oaths, suffers no punishment at all.

**The Justice of a Procedure with No Human Verdict**

The most philosophically interesting feature of *liʿān* is that the court does not decide who is telling the truth. Both parties swear; both are believed for legal purposes; the marriage ends; nobody is flogged. The case is, in the exact sense, referred upward. This is not evasion — it is a mature recognition of the limits of human adjudication. A judge who must guess between two sworn accounts of a private act will be wrong roughly as often as he is right, and a wrong guess destroys an innocent life. The Qur'anic solution is to *dissolve the relationship whose foundation has collapsed*, protect both parties from corporal punishment, and leave the truth of the matter to the only Witness who was present. The Prophet ﷺ articulated the general principle in a way that bears directly on this:

> *"I am only a human being, and you bring your disputes to me. Perhaps one of you is more eloquent in his argument than the other, and I judge in his favour according to what I hear. So whoever I award something belonging to his brother, let him not take it, for I am only awarding him a piece of the Fire."* (al-Bukhārī 2680; Muslim 1713)

Even prophetic judgment operates on evidence, not on hidden knowledge. *Liʿān* is the institutional acknowledgment of that limit.

**The Symmetry That Protects the Wife**

It must be emphasized, because it is routinely missed, that the procedure is *symmetrical*. The wife's oath has exactly the same legal weight as the husband's, and it fully averts the penalty from her (v. 8). In a seventh-century context — and in many societies since — an accusation of infidelity by a husband was in practice a death sentence, with the woman having no standing to contradict it. This passage gives her an equal, formal, public, decisive voice, and it makes her word alone sufficient to defeat his. The recognition of a wife's sworn testimony as legally equal to her husband's, in the most emotionally charged case a court can hear, is an extraordinary provision for the era in which it was revealed.

**"And Have No Witnesses But Themselves"**

The phrase *wa lam yakun lahum shuhadāʾu illā anfusuhum* — "and they have no witnesses but themselves" — is the trigger condition. If the husband *does* have four witnesses, the ordinary procedure applies and *liʿān* is unnecessary. The provision exists solely for the case where the only human being with knowledge is the accuser himself. The word *anfusuhum*, "themselves," literally "their own selves," is poignant. A man is being permitted to serve as his own evidence — an arrangement no legal system would ordinarily allow, and which is permitted here only because the alternative is a permanent injustice, and only under the crushing weight of a self-directed curse.

**The Larger Ethic: Jealousy Given a Lawful Channel**

What this verse ultimately does is take one of the most explosive emotions a human being can experience — the certainty that one's spouse has betrayed him — and channel it into a slow, formal, public, God-invoking procedure rather than letting it discharge as violence. Saʿd ibn ʿUbādah's instinct — *I would strike him with the sword* — is the human default, and it has filled the histories and the courts of every nation with so-called honor killings and crimes of passion. Islam's response is not to deny the emotion; the Prophet ﷺ affirmed it and said that God's own *ghayrah* is greater. The response is to give the emotion a lawful, non-violent outlet with a definite legal end: swear, and be released from the marriage; do not kill, do not defame, do not take the law into your hands.

**For Contemporary Marriages**

Most readers will never enter a *liʿān* proceeding. But the verse teaches a directly applicable discipline for the far commoner situation of suspicion within a marriage. First, suspicion is not evidence. The Qur'an says elsewhere: *"O you who believe! Shun much suspicion. Truly some suspicion is a sin"* (49:12). A marriage cannot survive on surveillance. Second, if a person genuinely reaches the point where he cannot live with his spouse in trust, Islam provides an exit — dissolution — rather than requiring him to remain in a poisoned bond or to destroy her reputation. The verse's outcome is separation, not spectacle. Third, the invocation of God's name four times before speaking is a model for how seriously a serious accusation must be treated. Before a person tells his family, his friends, or the internet what he believes his spouse has done, this verse asks him: would you say that under oath, four times, before God, with a curse on your own head if you are wrong? Most accusations do not survive that question — which is precisely the point of asking it.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:7

> **and the fifth shall be that the curse of God be upon him if he is among the liars.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth Oath: Where the Procedure Becomes Terrifying**

The four sworn testimonies of verse 6 replaced the four witnesses of verse 4. The fifth oath does something different in kind. It is not additional evidence; it is a *self-imposed sentence*. The husband declares that if he is lying, the curse of God is upon him. Arabic marks the shift: the first four are *shahādāt*, testimonies, statements about the world. The fifth is an invocation — *anna laʿnata'llāhi ʿalayhi in kāna mina'l-kādhibīn* — a conditional imprecation the man calls down upon his own head. He is asked, in effect, to stake his eternity on the truth of his claim.

**Laʿnah: What Being Cursed Actually Means**

The word *laʿnah* is defined by the philologists as *al-ṭard wa'l-ibʿād min raḥmati'llāh* — expulsion and distancing from the mercy of God. It is not, in Qur'anic usage, a magical spell or a curse of the kind found in folklore. It is exclusion: to be pushed away from the one source of every good a creature can receive. This is worth grasping precisely, because it explains why the fifth oath is such a heavy instrument. The Qur'an applies *laʿnah* to Iblīs (15:35), to those who conceal what God revealed (2:159), to those who slay a believer deliberately (4:93), and here to a man who would lie in this procedure. It places the false accuser of his own wife in the company of the most severely condemned. There is also a fearful appropriateness in the choice of curse for this particular crime. The man is alleging that his wife has destroyed the mercy and intimacy of their household; if he lies, he is asking to be himself expelled from the mercy of God. The punishment mirrors the crime with exactness.

**Why an Oath Can Do the Work of Evidence**

To a secular reader the entire mechanism may appear naive: what stops a liar from simply swearing? The answer lies in what the procedure assumes and what it accomplishes. It assumes a functioning belief in God. In a society where people genuinely believe that God hears and that His curse is real, a self-directed imprecation is an enormous barrier. The oath does not detect the lie; it *prices* it. It converts a costless accusation into one that carries, for a believer, infinite cost. But even setting belief aside, the procedure has real forensic value. It requires the accuser to be specific, public, formal, and irrevocable. It strips away the deniability in which most malicious accusations live — the hint, the implication, the "I heard." A man who is exaggerating, or acting on suspicion rather than sight, or hoping to intimidate his wife into a settlement, is confronted with a ritual that offers him no ambiguity to hide in. Many such men, historically and in reported cases, stopped at this stage. The Prophet ﷺ built exactly this pause into the procedure. In the reports of *liʿān*, before the fifth oath was taken he would warn the swearer:

> *"God knows that one of you two is lying. Will either of you repent?"*

And in the account of ʿUwaymir, the Prophet ﷺ instructed that at the fifth oath the man be stopped and told that this one is the decisive one, the one that brings the punishment down. The tradition records the same warning being given to the wife before her fifth. A hand was literally placed over the mouth of the swearer at that moment. Nothing in the ritual is designed to hurry either party toward damnation; everything in it is designed to offer them an exit before the last step.

**Kādhibīn: Placed Among a Class**

Note the wording: not "if he lied," but *"if he is among the liars"* — *mina'l-kādhibīn*, using the plural. Arabic rhetoric employs this construction to assign a person to a *category* rather than to record a single act. The man is not merely swearing that this one statement is accurate; he is swearing that he does not belong to the class of people who lie in such matters. This is heavier and more precise. A person might tell himself that a single exaggeration under provocation is a small thing. The oath does not let him: it asks him to declare his membership in the company of the truthful (v. 6, *mina'l-ṣādiqīn*) or accept classification with the liars. The Qur'an consistently frames truthfulness as an identity rather than an isolated behaviour:

> *"O you who believe! Reverence God and be with the truthful."* (Qur'an 9:119)

And the Prophet ﷺ described the same trajectory:

> *"Truthfulness leads to righteousness, and righteousness leads to Paradise. A man keeps telling the truth until he is recorded with God as a truthful one. And lying leads to wickedness, and wickedness leads to the Fire. A man keeps lying until he is recorded with God as a liar."* (al-Bukhārī 6094; Muslim 2607)

**The Legal Weight of the Fifth**

The jurists treated the fifth oath as the operative act that completes the husband's side of the *liʿān*. Without it the procedure is incomplete and the *ḥadd* of slander is not averted from him. Some also held that if a man refuses to complete the fifth oath, he is subject to the ordinary penalty of verse 4. The fifth is thus both the most solemn and the most consequential utterance in the process.

**A Warning to the Age of Costless Accusation**

The distance between this verse and modern life is instructive precisely because it is so wide. Our era has made accusation nearly free. A person may allege the most intimate and destructive things about another from behind a keyboard, anonymously, instantaneously, before an audience of millions, at no cost, with no oath, no formality, no requirement of specificity, and no consequence if it proves false. This verse describes the opposite civilization. There, to make a sexual accusation you had to stand up in public, name yourself, say it four times, and then place the curse of God on your own head. The contrast is not merely historical trivia; it is a diagnosis. Cheap accusation produces a society in which no reputation is safe and, paradoxically, in which real victims are less believed, because the channel is saturated with noise. Expensive accusation produces a society in which allegations are rare, serious, and therefore credible. For the individual believer, the practical distillation of this verse is a question to be applied before speaking about anyone: *am I willing to say this under oath, calling God's curse upon myself if I am wrong?* If the honest answer is no, then the accusation was never fit to be spoken at all. This chapter of light asks the tongue to submit to that test before it moves.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:8

> **And the punishment shall be averted from her should she give four testimonies, swearing by God that he is among the liars,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wife's Turn: A Right, Not a Formality**

The husband has sworn five times. In most legal systems of the ancient and medieval world — and, one must add, in the customary practice of many societies down to our own time — that would have been the end of the matter, and the woman's fate would have been settled by her husband's word. This verse refuses that. It gives her a procedure of identical structure, identical solemnity, and decisive legal effect. *Wa yadraʾu ʿanhā al-ʿadhāba an tashhada arbaʿa shahādātin bi'llāhi innahu la-mina'l-kādhibīn* — "and the punishment shall be *warded off* from her should she give four testimonies, swearing by God that he is among the liars."

**Yadraʾu: The Verb of Warding Off**

The verb *daraʾa* means to push away, avert, repel — the same verb used in the celebrated juristic maxim *"idraʾū al-ḥudūd bi'l-shubuhāt"*, "avert the fixed penalties by means of doubts." Its presence here is significant. The Qur'an does not say she "proves her innocence"; it says the punishment is *pushed away* from her. The law is not asking her to establish a positive case; it is recognizing that once she has sworn, the ground for punishing her no longer exists. This distinction matters. Requiring an accused woman to *prove* her chastity would be requiring the impossible — a negative about a private matter. What the verse gives her instead is the power to *neutralize* the accusation. Her oath does not need to convince a judge of anything; it simply removes the legal basis for the penalty.

**The Presumption of Innocence, Institutionalized**
