Behind this construction stands a principle that Islamic jurisprudence articulated early and clearly: a person is presumed free of liability until the contrary is established, and where evidence conflicts irresolvably, the penalty falls away. The Prophet ﷺ stated the evidentiary rule that governs all such disputes:

> *"The burden of proof is upon the claimant, and the oath is upon the one who denies."* (al-Bayhaqī and others; the wording is famous among the jurists, and the principle is reported in al-Tirmidhī 1341)

The wife here occupies the position of the denier, and she is given the oath — but a fourfold, intensified oath matching the accusation's gravity. Symmetry of procedure is the visible form of equality before the law.

**Why Her Word Suffices Against His**

A natural question arises: if both swear, why is she not punished? Why does the tie go to her? Several reasons emerge from the structure of the law. First, the general principle just stated: the claimant carries the burden, and where it is not discharged to the required standard, no penalty follows. The husband is the claimant. Second, the *ḥadd* penalties are averted by doubt, and two opposed sworn oaths are the very definition of doubt. Third, and most importantly, the consequences of the two errors are radically unequal: wrongly punishing an innocent woman is an irreparable catastrophe, while wrongly releasing a guilty one leaves the matter to God, who will not fail to settle it. Islamic penal philosophy consistently prefers the second error to the first, exactly as the Prophetic instruction to avert penalties by doubt requires.

**What She Loses and What She Keeps**

The *liʿān* ends the marriage. That is a real loss for a woman in any society, and especially in the one being addressed. But consider carefully what the procedure preserves for her. She keeps her body: no flogging, no stoning under any reading, no punishment of any kind. She keeps her legal standing: she is not convicted, and her testimony is not disqualified. She keeps her child, who is attached to her lineage. And she keeps her dower and her rights arising from the marriage. She is released from a man who has publicly accused her, which — whatever the truth — is a marriage that cannot be inhabited. Set that against the alternatives available in the surrounding cultures and in most of human history: summary killing by husband or kin, expulsion into destitution, or conviction on a husband's unopposed word. The verse is a shield.

**The Emotional Reality of the Scene**

It is worth pausing to imagine the human scene the verse legislates, because the law is easier to understand when it is pictured. A woman stands before the community. Her husband has just declared, five times, that she committed adultery, and has called God's curse upon himself. Every eye is on her. She is then invited to speak — and the Qur'an gives her words to say that directly contradict the man who was, until minutes ago, her closest intimate: *he is among the liars*. The dignity conferred by that moment should not be underestimated. The verse does not ask her to weep, to plead, to be defended by a male relative, or to have her character vouched for. It gives her a voice of exactly equal legal weight to her accuser's, and it makes that voice sufficient. The Prophet ﷺ, in the reports of the procedure, halted before her fifth oath just as he had halted before his — the same warning, the same mercy, the same chance to withdraw. Neither party is treated as more or less capable of truth.

**The Underlying Vision of Marriage**

One further observation. That the Qur'an provides so elaborate a mechanism for the collapse of trust between spouses tells us how highly it valued the trust itself. The same Book describes marriage as:

> *"And among His signs is that He created for you mates from among yourselves, that you may find rest in them, and He ordained affection and mercy between you. Truly in that are signs for a people who reflect."* (Qur'an 30:21)

*Sakan* — rest; *mawaddah* — affection; *raḥmah* — mercy. When those are destroyed by an accusation of this kind, no legal patch can restore them, and Islam does not pretend otherwise. It does not force the couple to remain together for appearances. It ends the union, protects both bodies, and lets each begin again.

**Applying the Verse Today**

The practical inheritance of this verse for contemporary Muslims is a standard of fairness in domestic conflict: *never conclude against a person who has not been heard.* Families routinely convict a daughter-in-law, a son, a sibling on the strength of one side's account, delivered with emotion and certainty. The Qur'an, in the most serious accusation a marriage can contain, refuses to let the first speaker be the last, and gives the accused's word the same weight as the accuser's. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib is reported to have distilled the same discipline when appointed as a judge: hearing one side, he said, is not judging. This verse is the scriptural root of that instinct — and it stands in a chapter whose entire first half is an extended lesson in what happens to a community that believes an accusation before hearing the accused.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:9

> **and the fifth that God's Wrath shall come upon her if he is among the truthful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Closing of the Symmetry**

The procedure ends where it began, in the mouth of the accused rather than the accuser. As the husband's fifth oath invoked God's curse upon himself if he lied, the wife's fifth invokes God's wrath upon herself if he told the truth. The parallel is deliberate and complete: four testimonies each, one self-imprecation each, one warning from the presiding authority each, and one outcome — no punishment, and no marriage.

**Ghaḍab and Laʿnah: Why the Words Differ**

The one asymmetry in the passage is the choice of word. He calls down *laʿnah*, the curse — expulsion from mercy. She calls down *ghaḍab*, wrath. The commentators noticed this and offered explanations, and the discussion is genuinely illuminating rather than merely technical. *Ghaḍab* denotes the divine displeasure that issues in punishment — the active, executed anger of God, as in the Fātiḥah's *"not of those who incur wrath"* (1:7). *Laʿnah* denotes distance and exclusion. One is a blow; the other is banishment. Several reasons are offered for the assignment. Some commentators observe that *ghaḍab* is, in Qur'anic usage, generally the graver term, since wrath is the consequence that follows the curse: to be cursed is to be pushed away, to incur wrath is to be struck. On this reading the heavier word falls upon the party who, in this final position, would be the last to lie — she speaks after hearing his fivefold oath, so if she is lying she is doing so with full deliberation and against a solemn sworn accusation. Others note simply that variation in the Qur'an's diction avoids mechanical repetition while preserving equivalence of force, and that no diminution of either party's seriousness is implied. Al-Qurṭubī records the discussion without insisting on one resolution. There is also a linguistic observation worth making. The Qur'an elsewhere places *laʿnah* and *ghaḍab* side by side as two faces of one reality:

> *"God's Wrath is upon them, and He has cursed them and prepared for them Hell."* (Qur'an 48:6)

So the two oaths are not measuring different degrees of divine seriousness so much as naming two aspects of the same catastrophe — and each party is asked to invite the whole of it upon themselves if they are lying.

**The Terrible Weight of the Last Word**

The wife's fifth oath is, in a sense, the most frightening utterance in the entire procedure, because she speaks last and therefore with the fullest knowledge of what is at stake. She has heard him swear five times. She knows the community has heard it. She knows the marriage is over regardless of what she says. And she is invited to place the wrath of God upon herself. The Prophet ﷺ understood the weight of it. The reports record that he stopped her before this oath, as he had stopped him, warning that this fifth is the one that brings the consequence — and in some accounts a hand was placed over her mouth so that she could not utter it without a moment's reflection. In the account of Hilāl ibn Umayyah's wife, the narrations say she hesitated at this point, nearly withdrew, then said, "I will not disgrace my people," and completed the oath. That detail is not a legal ruling but it is a profound piece of moral realism, and the tradition preserved it honestly. Human beings under public pressure sometimes swear for reasons that have nothing to do with truth — shame, family honor, the impossibility of retreat before an audience. The Prophet's ﷺ instinct to interrupt and warn at exactly that moment is a lesson in itself about how easily crowds extract oaths that souls cannot afford.

**The Case Is Closed on Earth and Left Open with God**

With this verse the *liʿān* is complete. What is remarkable is what does *not* happen next. No verdict is pronounced. No one is declared guilty. No punishment is executed. The court does not even claim to know what occurred. The Prophet ﷺ is reported, after a *liʿān*, to have said words preserved in the collections to the effect that God knows one of the two is lying, and to have made clear that judgment was passed on the basis of the oaths, not on the basis of the facts. In one account, after separating the couple, he indicated that were it not for the oaths sworn, he would have proceeded differently — an explicit statement that the legal outcome and the underlying truth had been decoupled. This is one of the most important lessons in Islamic legal theory, and it deserves stating baldly: *the courts of this world are not instruments for revealing truth; they are instruments for administering justice under conditions of uncertainty.* The truth of what happened in that house is reserved for the Day when:

> *"On that Day God will pay them their just due in full, and they will know that God is the Manifest Truth."* (Qur'an 24:25, later in this very sūrah)

The sūrah will make that promise explicitly, fifteen verses from now, in the context of the slanderers of ʿĀʾishah. The believer's confidence in justice does not rest on the competence of judges. It rests on the existence of a final court that does not need witnesses.

**Why This Passage Belongs Where It Stands**

Step back and look at the placement of verses 6–9. They interrupt what would otherwise be a smooth sequence: the punishment for adultery (v. 2), the marital consequence (v. 3), the punishment for false accusation (vv. 4–5), and then — from verse 11 — the great case study of false accusation against ʿĀʾishah. Why insert the *liʿān* here? Because it completes the moral system. Verses 2–3 protect society from sexual license. Verses 4–5 protect individuals from false accusation. But between those two protections lies a gap: the private marital case where the accuser may be truthful yet unable to prove it, and the accused may be innocent yet unable to disprove it. Verses 6–9 fill that gap without sacrificing either principle. The husband is not silenced; the wife is not destroyed; the community is not turned into an investigative bureau; and the marriage that can no longer function is dissolved. Only when this structure is complete does the sūrah turn, in verse 10, to the exclamation that surveys the whole of it: *"And were it not for God's Bounty upon you, and His Mercy, and that God is Relenting, Wise!"*

**The Lesson for Ordinary Life**

Three practical inheritances from this verse. First, *the fear of a false oath should be alive in a believer's heart.* Casual swearing by God's name — "wallāhi" scattered through conversation to add weight to trivia — is corrosive precisely because it wears down the instrument this passage relies on. The Prophet ﷺ warned:

> *"Whoever swears an oath by which he takes the property of a Muslim unlawfully will meet God while He is angry with him."* (al-Bukhārī 2356; Muslim 138)

Second, *the last word is not the winning word.* The wife's fifth oath closes the earthly case without settling the heavenly one. Whoever "wins" an argument by outlasting the other party should remember what this passage says about the relationship between procedural victory and truth. Third, *some conflicts should be ended rather than adjudicated.* The Qur'an's answer to an unresolvable marital accusation is not endless investigation but dissolution and release. There is wisdom in that for many disputes: when trust is irreparably gone and the facts cannot be established, the humane course is often to separate cleanly, protect both parties, and entrust the truth to God — which is exactly what these four verses legislate.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:10

> **And were it not for God's Bounty upon you, and His Mercy, and that God is Relenting, Wise!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence That Refuses to Finish**

This verse is grammatically incomplete, and its incompleteness is its entire power. In Arabic it is a conditional clause — *wa lawlā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuh* — "and were it not for God's bounty upon you and His mercy…" — with the consequence deliberately omitted. The rhetoricians call this *ḥadhf jawāb al-sharṭ*, the suppression of the apodosis, and it is one of the most powerful devices in the language. Compare a parent who says to a child, "If I hadn't caught you…" and stops. The unspoken ending is more frightening than any stated one, because the listener's imagination must supply it, and imagination is unbounded. The commentators fill it variously — "He would have hastened your punishment," "He would have exposed the liar among you," "the truth would have been laid bare and the guilty destroyed" — but the Qur'an itself leaves the space open. Each reader completes it according to what he knows of himself. This refrain will recur three more times in this sūrah — at verses 14, 20, and 21 — each time in the aftermath of a discussion of accusation and rumor, and each time unfinished. It is the drumbeat of the chapter's first half.

**Where the Verse Sits**

It stands immediately after the *liʿān* procedure and immediately before the account of the great slander. The commentators read it as facing both directions at once. Looking backward, it comments on the mercy embedded in *liʿān* itself. Without this provision, one of two injustices was inevitable: a truthful husband flogged, or an innocent wife condemned. God provided a route through the impossible, and did so specifically so that a community would not tear itself apart over what happens behind closed doors. That provision was *faḍl* — unearned favor — not something the community had a right to demand. Looking forward, it prepares the reader for verse 11 and what follows. The believers of Madinah are about to be told, at length and in detail, how badly they behaved during the slander of ʿĀʾishah. Before a word of that rebuke is spoken, God places this: *were it not for My bounty and My mercy…* The rebuke arrives already wrapped in the announcement that punishment has been withheld.

**Faḍl and Raḥmah: Two Distinct Gifts**

The pairing of *faḍl* and *raḥmah* is frequent in the Qur'an, and the two are not synonyms. *Faḍl* comes from a root meaning surplus, excess, what remains over and above the requirement. Divine *faḍl* is what God gives *beyond* what is due — the bonus, the overflow, the undeserved addition. Justice would be God giving exactly what is merited; *faḍl* is everything past that line. *Raḥmah* is from the root of the womb, as noted earlier: enveloping, protective, sustaining care given to a being who has earned nothing. Together they describe the two ways God's generosity operates: He adds good that was not owed, and He withholds harm that was owed. Verse 10 invokes both, and in this context the second is uppermost. The community deserved consequences. It received an explanation instead.

**Tawwāb: The Name and Its Direction**

The verse then names God *tawwāb*. This is an intensive form of the same verb used for human repentance (*tāba*, to turn back), and applied to God it means the One who *repeatedly and abundantly turns* toward His servants. The reciprocity built into the vocabulary is one of the most beautiful features of Qur'anic theology. The servant turns; God turns. Indeed, as noted at verse 5, God turns *first*: the very impulse to repent is itself an act of divine turning already accomplished. The Prophet ﷺ described the intensity of that divine motion:

> *"God is more delighted by the repentance of His servant than one of you who, travelling in a desolate land, has his camel — carrying his food and drink — escape him. He despairs of it, comes to a tree and lies down in its shade, having given up hope. Then while he is in that state, there is the camel standing before him. He seizes its halter and, in the intensity of his joy, says: 'O God, You are my servant and I am Your lord!' — erring out of the extremity of his joy."* (Muslim 2747)

The name is placed here, in the middle of a legal passage about adultery and slander, for a reason: these are the sins whose committers most easily conclude that they are beyond return.

**Ḥakīm: The Wisdom That Governs the Mercy**

The final name is *ḥakīm*, Wise. Its root, *ḥ-k-m*, is shared with *ḥukm* (judgment, ruling) and *ḥikmah* (wisdom), and also with *iḥkām* (making something firm and well-constructed) and — significantly — with the bridle placed on an animal to control it. Wisdom in Arabic carries the sense of *putting things in their right place and restraining them from disorder*. The pairing of *tawwāb* and *ḥakīm* is the theological safeguard of the whole passage. Mercy without wisdom would be indulgence, and would collapse the very laws just legislated. Wisdom without mercy would be mechanism. God's forgiveness is not the abandonment of order; it is the highest expression of it — an order that includes, by design, a route of return.

**The Argument Embedded in the Exclamation**

There is a piece of reasoning hidden in this verse that deserves to be made explicit. Every human being lives in a state of continuous, unnoticed reprieve. Consider only the sins of speech that this chapter addresses. If God exposed every false word a person spoke, published every private assumption made about a neighbor, replayed every conversation held behind someone's back — no reputation on earth would survive a single day. The community of Madinah, which had just spent a month repeating a rumor about the Prophet's ﷺ own wife, was being shown this: you are still standing, still respected, still called believers, only because God covers what you do. This is the argument that makes the sūrah's ethics coherent. Why should I conceal my neighbor's fault, forgive a slanderer, refuse to repeat a rumor? Because I am, at this very moment, the beneficiary of exactly that treatment from God — and on a scale I cannot calculate. The Prophet ﷺ made the equation explicit:

> *"Be merciful to those on the earth, and the One in the heavens will be merciful to you."* (al-Tirmidhī 1924)

**How to Feel This Verse**

An exercise is more useful here than an explanation. Take an honest inventory of a single week: the things you said about people, the assumptions you made, the private thoughts you would not want broadcast, the moments of anger, the small dishonesties. Then imagine each of them made public with your name attached. That imaginative exercise is what verse 10 is asking for, and the unfinished sentence is the space in which to perform it. *Were it not for God's Bounty upon you, and His Mercy…* — the reader supplies the ending, and the ending is: *I would have nothing left.* The correct response to that realization is not paralysis but two definite dispositions, both of which the sūrah will go on to demand. First, gratitude expressed as *restraint*: since God has covered me, I will cover others. Second, the *refusal of despair*: the One who withheld the consequence is *tawwāb*, and the door He left open is the whole reason the sentence was never finished.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:11

> **Truly those who brought forth the lie were a group among you. Do not suppose it to be an evil for you. Rather, it is a good for you. Unto each man among them is the sin he committed. And he among them who undertook the greater part of it, his shall be a great punishment.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Incident: What Happened at Banū al-Muṣṭaliq**

With this verse the sūrah turns from legislation to history, and to the most painful episode in the domestic life of the Prophet ﷺ. The account is preserved at length in the most authentic collections, narrated by ʿĀʾishah herself (al-Bukhārī 4141 and 2661; Muslim 2770), and the commentators — al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr — all reproduce it. Returning from an expedition, the army halted near Madinah. ʿĀʾishah, then a young woman travelling in a curtained howdah, stepped away from the camp to relieve herself and discovered on her return that a necklace of hers had been lost. She went back to search for it. Meanwhile the men lifted her howdah onto the camel, assuming from its lightness that she was inside, and the caravan moved off. She returned to find the camp gone, and — reasoning correctly that they would notice her absence and return — sat down where she was and fell asleep. Ṣafwān ibn al-Muʿaṭṭal, a Companion who had been assigned to follow behind the army and collect anything dropped, came upon her at dawn. He recognized her, said aloud the words of return to God — *innā li'llāhi wa innā ilayhi rājiʿūn* — made his camel kneel, and, without a further word passing between them, led the animal on foot until they caught up with the army at its next halt. That is the entire event. From it a rumor was manufactured, spread through Madinah, and repeated for roughly a month. Its principal author was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl, the chief of the hypocrites. Others repeated it, including, painfully, some sincere believers: Ḥassān ibn Thābit the poet, Misṭaḥ ibn Uthāthah, and Ḥamnah bint Jaḥsh. ʿĀʾishah fell ill and, not knowing the cause of the coldness she sensed, went to convalesce at her mother's house. When she finally learned what was being said, she wept for two nights and a day without sleeping. The Prophet ﷺ, receiving no revelation, consulted his Companions. Usāmah ibn Zayd spoke well of her. ʿAlī suggested that women were plentiful and he could replace her — a remark ʿĀʾishah never forgot. Barīrah, her servant, was questioned and said she knew nothing against her but that she was a young girl who would fall asleep over the dough and let the goat eat it. The Prophet ﷺ addressed the people from the pulpit seeking redress against the man who had wounded his household, and a quarrel nearly broke out between the tribes. Finally the Prophet ﷺ came to her, praised God, and said that if she had done something wrong she should seek God's forgiveness. ʿĀʾishah's own account of her reply is one of the most extraordinary passages in the Hadith literature. She looked to her father to answer for her, and he said, "I do not know what to say to the Messenger of God." She looked to her mother, who said the same. And so she said — a girl of her age, before the Prophet ﷺ and her parents:

> *"By God, I know that you have heard this talk until it has settled in your souls and you have believed it. If I say to you that I am innocent — and God knows that I am innocent — you will not believe me. And if I confess to you something, and God knows I am innocent of it, you will believe me. By God, I find no likeness for me and for you except the words of the father of Joseph: 'Beautiful patience; and God is the One whose help is sought against that which you describe' (12:18)."*

Then she turned over on her bed. She said afterwards that she thought herself too insignificant for God to reveal Qur'an concerning her, but hoped the Prophet ﷺ might see something in a dream. Before he left the house, revelation came upon him, and when it lifted he was smiling, and the first thing he said was: *"ʿĀʾishah — as for God, He has declared your innocence."* Her mother told her to rise and go to him. She said: *"By God, I will not rise to him, and I will praise none but God."*

**"A Group Among You" — Naming the Source Without Naming Names**

The verse opens: *inna alladhīna jāʾū bi'l-ifki ʿuṣbatun minkum* — "truly those who brought forth the lie were a group among you." Two words carry the weight. *ʿUṣbah* denotes a band of ten to forty men, bound together — from a root meaning to bind or wrap. It suggests coordination, not accident: this was not a rumor that arose spontaneously but a story propagated by an identifiable faction. And *minkum*, "from among you," is the wound. They were not enemies outside the walls. They were inside the community, praying in the same mosque, counted in the same census. The verse deliberately refrains from listing them. This is a discipline the entire sūrah is teaching: God, who could have named every guilty tongue, does not. Even in a Qur'anic rebuke about slander, no one is slandered. The word chosen for the rumor is *al-ifk*, from a root (*a-f-k*) meaning to overturn, to invert, to turn a thing upside down. The Qur'an uses the same root of the overturned cities of Lot's people (69:9). An *ifk* is therefore not merely a falsehood but an *inversion*: it takes the truth and stands it on its head. The chaste is called unchaste; the rescuer is called the seducer; the victim is called the criminal. That is precisely what slander does, and the Arabic names it exactly.

**"Do Not Suppose It to Be an Evil for You"**

Then comes one of the most startling sentences in the Qur'an: *lā taḥsabūhu sharran lakum, bal huwa khayrun lakum* — "do not suppose it to be an evil for you; rather, it is a good for you." The address is to ʿĀʾishah, to Abū Bakr, to the Prophet ﷺ, and to every believer wounded by the affair. And it is a claim about the nature of calamity so bold that it requires unpacking. In what sense was a month of public humiliation *good*? First, because it produced Qur'an. Sixteen verses of the eternal Book were revealed on this incident — verses that would legislate the ethics of rumor for every generation of Muslims until the end of time. Had the slander not occurred, this passage would not exist. ʿĀʾishah's suffering purchased a permanent chapter of divine instruction for the human race. Second, because it produced *vindication from heaven*. Any human defence would have been contestable; whispers would have continued for a lifetime. Instead her innocence was written into the Qur'an, recited in prayer in every land and every century. ʿĀʾishah herself understood this and said, in a report preserved in the collections, that she had not thought God would reveal Qur'an about her, but that she considered herself too lowly for that — and yet He did. Her mother's instruction to rise and thank the Prophet ﷺ, and her refusal to praise anyone but God, capture exactly whose vindication it was. Third, because it exposed the hypocrites. A community cannot defend itself against enemies it cannot identify. The affair forced ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy into the open. Fourth, because it trained the believers. Those sincere Muslims who had repeated the rumor learned a lesson about their own tongues that no sermon could have taught them. And fifth, because it established the standing of ʿĀʾishah herself. She would go on to be among the greatest scholars of the community, the transmitter of over two thousand traditions, the teacher of the Companions in matters of law and prophetic practice. Her authority rested in part on a certification of character issued by God Himself.

**The Principle: Calamity Is Not the Same as Evil**

The verse states a general theological law through a particular case. The Qur'an repeats it elsewhere:

> *"…But it may be that you dislike a thing though it is good for you, and it may be that you love a thing though it is evil for you. God knows and you know not."* (Qur'an 2:216)

And the Prophet ﷺ described the believer's structural advantage:

> *"Wondrous is the affair of the believer, for there is good for him in every matter — and this is not the case with anyone except the believer. If he is happy, he thanks God, and there is good for him in it; and if he is harmed, he shows patience, and there is good for him in it."* (Muslim 2999)

Note what the verse does *not* say. It does not say the slander was not a sin — the same verse ends by promising punishment to the slanderers. It does not say ʿĀʾishah should not have wept; she wept for two nights, and her tears are recorded in scripture-adjacent narration without reproach. The claim is narrower and more precise: *the outcome for you is good*, even though the act was evil and its authors are guilty. Human beings do not have to pretend that pain is pleasant in order to trust that God extracts good from it. This is the difference between Islamic patience and stoicism. Stoicism says the harm does not matter. Islam says the harm is real, the wrongdoer is guilty, the tears are legitimate — and God is producing something from it that you cannot yet see.

**"Unto Each Man Is the Sin He Committed": Individual Accountability**

*Li-kulli imriʾin minhum mā'ktasaba mina'l-ithm* — each of them bears what he earned of the sin. The verb *iktasaba* is from the root *k-s-b*, "to earn," in a reflexive form that implies deliberate acquisition. The Qur'an consistently uses the language of *earning* for sin, which is a striking metaphor: the sinner is depicted as a worker who labors and takes home wages. Nothing accidental, nothing inherited, nothing assigned by association. This clause dismantles the psychology that makes mass rumor possible. In a crowd, guilt feels diluted. Everyone is saying it; I only repeated it; the real fault lies with whoever started it; I added nothing. The verse cuts the crowd into individuals and hands each a bill. Divine accounting has no concept of collective responsibility for speech: fifty tongues repeating a lie produce fifty separate files. The Qur'an states the principle universally:

> *"And no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another."* (Qur'an 6:164)

**"He Who Undertook the Greater Part": Proportionate Guilt**

But individual accountability is not equal accountability. *Wa'lladhī tawallā kibrahu minhum lahu ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — "and he among them who undertook the greater part of it, his shall be a great punishment." *Kibr* here means the major share, the bulk, the heaviest portion. And *tawallā* means to take charge of something, to assume responsibility for it, to become its patron — the same root as *walī*, guardian. The phrase depicts a person who did not merely participate but *took the thing on*, made himself its manager and promoter. The commentators are essentially unanimous that this refers to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl. He is not named. But the description is unmistakable, and the punishment assigned is *ʿaẓīm*, immense. The moral logic here is one that every modern reader should study carefully, because our information systems have made it urgently relevant. Guilt in the spread of a lie is *graduated by function*. The originator bears more than the amplifier; the amplifier with a large audience bears more than the one with a small; the person who packages and promotes bears more than the one who merely mentioned it once. God's accounting distinguishes the author from the forwarder, and the man with the platform from the man in the corner. Yet — and this is the balance the verse holds with great precision — the lesser participants are not innocent. Each still bears *what he earned*. The two clauses stand side by side: nobody escapes because someone else was worse, and nobody is charged with more than his own share.

**The Sincere Believers Who Fell**

It must be said plainly, because the tradition says it plainly, that among those punished were genuine and beloved Muslims. Ḥassān ibn Thābit was the Prophet's ﷺ own poet, the man who defended Islam in verse. Misṭaḥ ibn Uthāthah was a cousin of Abū Bakr, a veteran of Badr, a poor man whom Abū Bakr personally supported. Ḥamnah bint Jaḥsh was the sister of the Prophet's ﷺ wife Zaynab. These were not hypocrites. They were believers with excellent records who repeated something they should not have repeated, and the *ḥadd* of slander was carried out upon them. That fact is the sharpest edge of this passage. A record of service does not immunize a tongue. Badr did not save Misṭaḥ from the lashes. The lesson for anyone who has ever justified a piece of gossip on the grounds of their own general piety is delivered here with unusual severity.

**The Verse in the Age of Instant Transmission**

Every structural feature of the Madinan slander now exists in intensified form. There is still a *ʿuṣbah* — a coordinated group that manufactures a narrative. There are still amplifiers who spread it without malice. There is still an *ifk* — an inversion in which the innocent party is recast as the guilty. There is still the interval of silence in which the accused has no defence. What has changed is only speed, reach, and permanence: the rumor of Madinah took a month to circulate a city; its modern equivalent takes minutes to circulate the planet and never disappears. Three disciplines follow directly from this verse. *Do not be the ʿuṣbah.* Refuse to participate in coordinated destruction of a person, however satisfying the cause feels. *Do not assume the crowd absorbs your share.* Each forward is separately recorded. "Everyone was sharing it" is not a defence that appears anywhere in the Qur'an. *Do not conclude that the harm done to you is simply evil.* When you are the one being talked about, this verse addresses you directly: *do not suppose it to be an evil for you.* God has a use for it that you will only see later — and sometimes, as with ʿĀʾishah, the vindication that follows is greater than the reputation that was attacked.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:12

> **Why, when you heard it, did not the believing men and women think well of their own, and say, "This is a manifest lie"?**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Cross-Examines the Bystanders**

Verse 11 dealt with those who manufactured and propagated the lie. This verse turns to a much larger group: everyone else. The ordinary Muslims of Madinah who did not invent the rumor, did not necessarily repeat it, but *heard* it and did not immediately reject it. The verse is phrased as a question — *lawlā idh samiʿtumūhu…* — and in Arabic *lawlā* followed by a past-tense verb functions as a reproach: "why did you not…?" It is the grammar of a superior demanding an account. Divine questions in the Qur'an are never requests for information; they are indictments in interrogative form. And notice what is being indicted. Not slander. Not lying. Merely *hearing without objecting*. The Qur'an here criminalizes passivity in the face of a lie about a fellow believer. Silence is treated as a failure with a name and a consequence.

**"Think Well of Their Own": Ẓann and the Duty of Good Opinion**

The demand is *ẓanna al-muʾminūna wa'l-muʾminātu bi-anfusihim khayran* — "the believing men and women should have thought well of their own selves." *Ẓann* is one of the Qur'an's key psychological terms. It covers the whole spectrum of belief short of certainty: supposition, conjecture, assumption, working hypothesis. It is what the mind does automatically in the absence of proof — and the point of this verse is that the automatic operation of the mind is itself subject to moral command. You do not merely control what you say; you are held responsible for what you *assume*. The technical term the scholars derived from this is *ḥusn al-ẓann*, "thinking well," and its opposite *sūʾ al-ẓann*, "thinking ill." The Qur'an forbids the latter in Sūrat al-Ḥujurāt with equal directness:

> *"O you who believe! Shun much suspicion. Truly some suspicion is a sin. And do not spy, nor backbite one another. Would any of you like to eat the flesh of his dead brother? You would abhor it. So reverence God; truly God is Relenting, Merciful."* (Qur'an 49:12)

The image in that verse — that speaking ill of an absent person is cannibalism of a corpse — is one of the most violent metaphors in scripture, and it is applied not to murder or theft but to conversation.

**"Of Their Own Selves": The Astonishing Phrase**

The single most remarkable word in this verse is *bi-anfusihim* — literally, "about *themselves*." God does not say "think well of ʿĀʾishah," or "think well of one another." He says the believers should have thought well *of their own selves*. The commentators dwell on this. Al-Qurṭubī and others explain that the believers are declared to be a single self: an accusation against one member of the body is an accusation against the whole. To slander a believer is to slander yourself; to entertain suspicion about a fellow Muslim is to entertain suspicion about your own flesh. The Prophet ﷺ built his social teaching on precisely this identification:

> *"The believers, in their mutual love, mercy and compassion, are like a single body: when one limb of it suffers, the whole body responds to it with wakefulness and fever."* (al-Bukhārī 6011; Muslim 2586)

> *"The believer to the believer is like a building, one part supporting another"* — and he interlaced his fingers. (al-Bukhārī 481; Muslim 2585)

There is also a practical, almost logical, force to *"your own selves."* If you know your own inner life — your struggles, your good intentions misread by others, the times you were innocent and appeared guilty — then you already possess the evidence you need about the believer being discussed. You know how easily a decent person can be placed in a compromising-looking situation. Extend to him the interpretation you would demand for yourself. This is the Golden Rule stated in Qur'anic form, and the Prophet ﷺ made it a condition of faith:

> *"None of you truly believes until he loves for his brother what he loves for himself."* (al-Bukhārī 13; Muslim 45)

**"And Say: This Is a Manifest Lie"**

The verse does not stop at internal charity. It requires *speech*: *wa qālū hādhā ifkun mubīn* — "and say: this is a manifest lie." Three things follow from this. First, good opinion must be *audible*. A rumor is defeated only when someone in the room refuses it out loud. Silent disapproval leaves the speaker with the impression of consent and the room with the impression of consensus. The dynamic is well documented: most people in a group repeating something ugly are privately uncomfortable, but each assumes he is alone, and so each stays quiet. One clear voice saying "that is a lie" collapses the illusion. Second, note the required *strength* of the statement. Not "I'm not sure that's true," not "we shouldn't be discussing this," but *"this is a manifest lie."* The Qur'an demands a flat, confident, unhedged rejection. And it calls the lie *mubīn*, "manifest" — from the same root *b-y-n* used in verse 1 for the "clarifying signs." The falsehood should have been as evident as the revelation. Third, this is the verse's answer to a question a modern reader will inevitably raise: how could they have known she was innocent before revelation came? The Qur'an's answer is that they had all the evidence they needed. They knew ʿĀʾishah. They knew Ṣafwān. They knew the Prophet's ﷺ household. Character, established over years, is evidence — and the failure of Madinah was a failure to treat known character as the evidence it is. Al-Qurṭubī reports the model response, that of Abū Ayyūb al-Anṣārī, when his wife asked whether he had heard what people were saying: he replied that it was a lie, and asked her, *"Umm Ayyūb, would you have done it?"* She said no, by God. He said: *"And ʿĀʾishah is better than you."* Then, in the tradition, this verse came down in confirmation of that reasoning. That exchange is the whole verse in miniature: reason from what you know of decent people to what you should assume about a better one.

**The Line Between Good Opinion and Naivety**

An objection must be addressed, because it is a serious one. Does this verse require Muslims to be credulous — to disbelieve every accusation, to dismiss every victim, to treat all reports as slander? It does not, and reading it that way would contradict the rest of the sūrah and the wider Book. Several boundaries are clear. *Ḥusn al-ẓann* governs *unsubstantiated* rumor, not investigated claims. The Qur'an itself commands verification: *"If an iniquitous person brings you a report, verify it"* (49:6). Verification is the opposite of both credulity and dismissal. It governs *private* moral judgment, not the operation of justice. Courts exist precisely to examine accusations, and this same sūrah has just spent eight verses constructing evidentiary procedures. And it does not require ignoring demonstrated patterns. Islamic law recognizes that a person's known conduct is relevant; the whole institution of *jarḥ wa taʿdīl* — the critical assessment of narrators' reliability — was built by scholars who took truthful evaluation of character to be a religious duty when necessary for protecting the community. The distinction the tradition draws is between *ḥukm* and *ẓann*: you may decline to trust someone with your money on the basis of experience, while still refusing to assume the worst about their soul or to broadcast unproven allegations. What this verse forbids is the specific, and extremely common, act of hearing an unverified damaging story about a fellow believer and letting it lodge in the mind as probably true.

**The Psychology the Verse Understands**

Why is good opinion so hard, and ill opinion so easy? Because suspicion is cheap and flattering. It costs nothing to believe the worst, and it quietly elevates the one who believes it: if she is corrupt, I am comparatively decent. Rumor is, in this respect, a form of self-congratulation performed at another's expense. Modern research on the spread of information has confirmed at scale what this verse assumed: falsehood, especially scandalous falsehood, travels faster and further than truth, because it is more novel and more emotionally charged. Good opinion, by contrast, requires effort. It requires imagining an alternative explanation, resisting the pleasure of the story, and often accepting the social cost of being the one person who declines to join in. The Qur'an, characteristically, does not merely condemn the failing; it prescribes the exact behaviour that prevents it, in words a person can actually use in a room: *this is a manifest lie.*

**A Practical Rule for the Reader**

Reduced to a usable discipline, this verse asks four things whenever damaging information about another person arrives: Assume the best available interpretation consistent with the facts you actually possess. Recognize that your knowledge of the person's established character *is* evidence, and weigh it. Say something. Refusal must be spoken, or the room hears consent. Remember that you are defending *yourself*: the standard of interpretation you apply to your brother today is the one that will be applied to you tomorrow, in this world and, as the Prophet ﷺ warned, in the next.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:13

> **Why did they not bring forth four witnesses thereto? For when they brought not the witnesses, it is they who were then liars in the Eyes of God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Moral Duty to Legal Standard**

Verse 12 addressed the *heart*: you should have thought well. This verse addresses the *procedure*: you should have demanded evidence. The two together constitute the Qur'an's complete answer to rumor — an inward disposition of charity and an outward requirement of proof. The question is again framed with *lawlā*, the particle of reproach: *lawlā jāʾū ʿalayhi bi-arbaʿati shuhadāʾ* — why did they not produce four witnesses? The community had been given, in the fourth verse of this very sūrah, an unambiguous evidentiary rule. Weeks later, they abandoned it the moment a sufficiently interesting story arrived. The verse holds them to their own law.

**The Standard Was Already Known**

This is a point worth pressing, because it shows how the sūrah is constructed. Verse 4 was not new information by the time of the slander; the evidentiary requirement of four witnesses for sexual accusation was established law in Madinah. Every believer who heard the rumor was in possession of the rule that should have stopped it in his mouth. This is why the reproach is so severe. The failure was not one of ignorance but of *application under emotional pressure*. Knowing a principle and applying it when a juicy story concerns the household of the Prophet ﷺ are two different capacities, and the second is the one that religion is actually trying to build.

**"Then It Is They Who Are the Liars in the Eyes of God"**

The verse's conclusion is legally and morally striking: *fa-idh lam yaʾtū bi'l-shuhadāʾi fa-ulāʾika ʿinda'llāhi humu'l-kādhibūn* — since they brought no witnesses, *they* are the liars in God's sight. Consider the logic carefully, because it establishes a principle of enormous practical importance. The verse does not say "they *may* be lying," or "their claim is unproven." It declares them liars — flatly, using the same emphatic restrictive construction (*ulāʾika hum*) used earlier for the iniquitous. Why? Because in the Qur'anic scheme, *an unsubstantiated accusation of this kind is itself a lie, regardless of the underlying facts*. A person who asserts as true something he cannot establish has spoken falsely about his own epistemic position even if, by accident, the content happens to correspond to reality. He claimed knowledge he did not have. That claim was false. He is a liar. This is a sophisticated and profoundly useful distinction. Most rumor-mongers defend themselves by saying "but it might be true." The verse forecloses that defence entirely. The question is not whether the statement might correspond to reality; the question is whether the speaker had the standing to assert it. He did not. Therefore he lied. The Prophet ﷺ stated the same principle as a general rule of speech:

> *"It is enough falsehood for a man that he narrates everything he hears."* (Muslim, in the introduction to his Ṣaḥīḥ)

Note the word: *falsehood* — not carelessness, not indiscretion. Transmitting the unverified is classified as lying.

**"In the Eyes of God": Two Courts, Two Verdicts**

The qualifier *ʿinda'llāh*, "in God's sight," is essential. It acknowledges implicitly that in the court of Madinah's gossip, these people were not regarded as liars at all. They were regarded as informed, as concerned, as people who knew something. Socially, the rumor-carrier occupies a position of prestige: he has information others lack. God relocates the verdict. Whatever the drawing rooms of Madinah concluded, the register that matters had already recorded a different entry. This phrase recurs in the sūrah — verse 15 will say the community treated the matter as slight *"though it is great in the Eyes of God"* — and it is one of the chapter's structuring devices: a constant contrast between the human assessment of an act and the divine one.

**The Deeper Wisdom of the Four-Witness Rule**

This verse gives occasion to state plainly why the four-witness requirement is not, as sometimes alleged, a device for protecting predators, but a structural protection for the whole society. Consider the two possible errors a system can make with sexual accusations. It can punish the innocent, or it can fail to punish the guilty. No system avoids both. The question is which error to prefer, and the Qur'an's answer — visible in this verse and throughout the sūrah — is that punishing the innocent is the graver catastrophe, because it is irreversible, because it destroys the very trust the law exists to protect, and because the unpunished guilty remain subject to a judgment that no human court can evade. But notice something further. The rule does not merely regulate the *court*; the verse applies it to *conversation*. This is the crucial move. The standard is not "you may not convict without four witnesses"; it is "you may not *say it* without four witnesses." A rule of evidence has been converted into a rule of speech, and applied to everyone, everywhere, at all times. The practical consequence is that in a functioning Muslim community, sexual accusation should be almost entirely absent from ordinary conversation. Not because such things never happen — the sūrah's second verse assumes they do — but because virtually no one will ever be in a position to speak about them lawfully.

**Applying the Standard Today**

The transfer to modern conditions is direct and uncomfortable. Ask, of the damaging claims that circulate in any community — in family gatherings, in mosque committees, in professional circles, and above all online — how many would survive the question: *where are your four witnesses?* The honest answer is almost none. Most such claims rest on a chain of the form: someone told someone who saw something that looked like something. Under this verse, every link in that chain is a liar in the sight of God. The verse also supplies the practical intervention. When a damaging story arrives, one does not need to argue about the character of the accused or the motives of the accuser. One asks a single question: *what is your evidence?* It is a question that ends most rumors on the spot, because most rumors have no answer to it. And asking it costs nothing and accuses no one — it merely enforces, in a living room in the twenty-first century, the standard that God imposed on Madinah.

**A Closing Reflection on the Word "Liars"**

There is a hard mercy in the bluntness of the label. God could have described these people gently — as hasty, as careless, as insufficiently rigorous. He calls them liars, because that is the only word severe enough to interrupt the behaviour. Most people will accept being called careless. Almost no one will accept being called a liar. By assigning the harshest available term to the mildest-seeming act — repeating what you heard — the verse makes it psychologically impossible for a believer to keep doing it comfortably. That is what the word is for.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:14

> **And were it not for God's Bounty upon you, and His Mercy, in this world and the Hereafter, a great punishment would have befallen you for having engaged [in vain talk] concerning it,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain Returns — This Time With Its Ending**

The suspended sentence of verse 10 returns, but with a crucial difference: here the consequence is *stated*. *La-massakum fī mā afaḍtum fīhi ʿadhābun ʿaẓīm* — "a great punishment would have touched you for that in which you plunged." The rhetorical strategy across the sūrah is now visible. At verse 10 the ending was withheld, leaving the reader to imagine it. Here, having narrated the offence in detail (vv. 11–13), the Qur'an supplies the missing clause. The reader who wondered at verse 10 what would have happened now knows: a great punishment, and it would have *touched* them — the verb *massa* implying contact, the punishment reaching them where they stood. And the scope is widened: *fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhirah*, "in this world and the Hereafter." God's bounty spared them in both registers — the worldly consequence of a community destroying itself over a rumor about its Prophet's ﷺ household, and the otherworldly consequence of the sin itself.

**Afaḍtum: The Verb of Plunging In**

The word chosen for what they did is remarkable: *afāḍa*, from the root *f-y-ḍ*, meaning to overflow, to flood, to pour out abundantly. The same root gives *ifāḍah*, the surging departure of the pilgrims from ʿArafah, described in the Qur'an: *"then surge along (afīḍū) from where the people surge along"* (2:199) — the image of a great mass of people moving as a flood. Applied to speech, the metaphor is devastatingly accurate. Gossip is not a considered act; it is a *pouring*. It overflows the banks of intention. A person does not decide to spend an hour destroying someone's reputation; he mentions one thing, and the conversation floods. The word also carries the sense of *immersion* — plunging into a subject and being carried by it — which is exactly the experience of a community in the grip of a scandal. There is a further nuance. A flood is a *collective* phenomenon: no single raindrop is the flood, yet the flood is nothing but raindrops. That is precisely the moral situation of a rumor, and it is why verse 11 had to insist that each individual bears what he earned.

**"Concerning It" — The Deliberate Vagueness**

Notice that the verse says *"that in which you plunged"* rather than restating the accusation. Throughout this entire passage of sixteen verses, the Qur'an narrates a sexual slander scandal *without ever repeating the content of the slander*. It is called "the lie," "it," "that in which you plunged," "what you spread with your tongues." The substance is never given. This is a masterclass in the ethics of discussing wrongdoing, and it should govern how Muslims speak about scandals in their own communities. It is possible — and this passage proves it — to address a moral failure with total seriousness, at length, with legal consequences, while refusing to circulate the details. Modern religious and secular discourse alike has largely lost this capacity: the condemnation of a scandal has become the primary vehicle for its transmission.

**The Structure of the Reprieve**

What exactly was the bounty? The commentators identify several layers, and each is instructive. The bounty of *delay* — God did not strike immediately, giving time for repentance. Earlier nations, the Qur'an repeatedly notes, were destroyed for less; this community was given a month of error and then a chapter of instruction instead of a punishment. The bounty of *revelation* — that God intervened at all, sending down verses that settled the matter definitively, rather than leaving Madinah to tear itself apart in factional suspicion. Sending an explanation to people who deserved a penalty is the essence of *faḍl*. The bounty of *the open door* — the guilty were given the possibility of repentance, as the exception in verse 5 provided, and history records that Ḥassān ibn Thābit continued to serve the Prophet ﷺ and that ʿĀʾishah herself, in later years, treated him with respect, saying he used to defend the Messenger of God ﷺ with his poetry.

**A Doctrine Worth Naming: Deferral Is Not Approval**

There is a theological point here that has enormous practical importance for how believers read their own lives. Human beings systematically misread the absence of immediate consequence as evidence of divine indifference or approval. This is a documented feature of human moral psychology: unpunished behaviour is rapidly normalized. The Qur'an addresses the error directly:

> *"And let not those who disbelieve suppose that Our granting them respite is good for their souls. We only grant them respite that they may increase in sin, and theirs shall be a humiliating punishment."* (Qur'an 3:178)

And:

> *"And thy Lord is Forgiving, Possessed of Mercy. Were He to take them to task for that which they have earned, He would surely hasten the punishment for them. Nay, but they have a tryst, beyond which they shall find no refuge."* (Qur'an 18:58)

The Madinan believers were being taught to read their own survival correctly: *you are not innocent, you are covered.* That reading of one's circumstances is the foundation of gratitude — and its absence is the foundation of arrogance.

**Why "In This World and the Hereafter" Matters**

The dual scope has a practical dimension often overlooked. Slander has *worldly* consequences that are punishment in themselves, quite apart from the Hereafter: communities fracture, families feud, marriages break, the innocent withdraw from public life, and the general level of trust falls so that everyone lives more suspiciously and more poorly. The Madinah of that month was, by ʿĀʾishah's own account, a city in which the Prophet ﷺ could not receive revelation on the matter, in which the tribes nearly came to blows in the mosque, in which a family was divided, and in which the leader of the community was visibly distressed for weeks. That is *already* a punishment, and the verse implies that God prevented it from becoming far worse. The believer should therefore notice that the Qur'an's threats are not exclusively eschatological. Sin has physics in this world. Households that gossip are households in which no one is safe; organizations that run on rumor are organizations that cannot function; nations that lose the distinction between assertion and evidence lose the capacity to govern themselves.

**What to Do With This Verse**

The instruction embedded here is not complicated, though it is difficult. First, when you find yourself in the middle of a conversation that has begun to *flood* — recognize the verb. The physical sensation of a conversation gathering momentum around someone's disgrace is the sensation this verse names. That recognition is the moment of choice. Second, when you have escaped a consequence you deserved — a mistake nobody discovered, a harsh word nobody reported, a private failing nobody saw — read it as this verse instructs: not as luck, and not as permission, but as *faḍl*. And let the appropriate response be the one the next verses will demand: change the behaviour before the cover is lifted. Third, when speaking of others' failures becomes necessary — and sometimes it is — imitate the Qur'an's own method in this passage: name the wrong, establish the seriousness, insist on the standard, and *do not repeat the details*.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:15

> **when you accepted it with your tongues, and spoke with your mouths that whereof you had no knowledge, supposing it to be slight, though it is great in the Eyes of God.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**An Anatomy of How a Lie Travels**

This verse is a forensic description of the mechanism of rumor, delivered in three clauses that trace its path from ear to mouth to conscience. It is among the most psychologically precise verses in the Qur'an, and every clause repays close attention.

**"You Received It With Your Tongues"**

The first clause is *idh talaqqawnahu bi-alsinatikum*. The verb *talaqqā* means to *receive*, to take something handed to you — the same verb used of Adam receiving words from his Lord: *"Then Adam received words from his Lord"* (2:37). But look at the organ named. One receives with the *ears*, or with the *mind*. Here the community received the rumor *with their tongues*. The commentators seize on this, and al-Qurṭubī notes the strangeness deliberately. The meaning is that the information never stopped anywhere in between. It did not pass through the ear into the mind, get examined, weighed against known character, tested against the evidentiary standard, and only then be spoken. It entered and exited at the same point. The tongue was both the receiving organ and the transmitting organ, with no intervening processing at all. Anyone who has watched — or been — a person hearing a piece of gossip and reaching for their phone before the sentence is finished knows exactly what is being described. There is an alternative reading of the same word, *tulquunahu*, "you fling it to one another," which yields a complementary image: the rumor as an object tossed from hand to hand in a circle, no one holding it long enough to look at it.

**"And You Spoke With Your Mouths That Of Which You Had No Knowledge"**

The second clause, *wa taqūlūna bi-afwāhikum mā laysa lakum bihi ʿilm*, contains an apparent redundancy that is in fact the point: with what else does one speak, if not the mouth? The Qur'an uses this construction — "saying with their mouths" — precisely to mark speech that is *only* mouth: words with no backing from the heart or the intellect. The same idiom appears of the hypocrites:

> *"They say with their mouths what is not in their hearts."* (Qur'an 3:167)

The phrase thus classifies the rumor-repeaters' speech as mere noise-production: air shaped into syllables, disconnected from knowledge, conviction, or verification. And the fatal words are *mā laysa lakum bihi ʿilm* — "that of which you have no knowledge." This is the exact charge, and it links to one of the Qur'an's great general commands:

> *"And pursue not that whereof thou hast no knowledge. Truly hearing, sight, and heart — all of these shall be called to account."* (Qur'an 17:36)

That verse is the Qur'an's epistemological constitution, and it is remarkably rigorous: every faculty of perception is legally accountable. You will be asked what you did with your ears — what you allowed into them, what you concluded from what you heard. You will be asked what you did with your eyes, and what your heart accepted as true. Islamic scholarship built an entire civilization of verification on this principle. The science of *isnād* — the chain of transmission by which every Prophetic report was traced back through named, individually assessed narrators — was, at bottom, an institutional response to verses like this one. Ibn al-Mubārak's famous statement captures it: *"The isnād is part of the religion; were it not for the isnād, whoever wished would say whatever he wished."* A religion that treats unverified speech as a sin will inevitably produce a scholarly culture obsessed with sources.

**"Supposing It To Be Slight, Though It Is Great in the Eyes of God"**

The third clause diagnoses the underlying error: *wa taḥsabūnahu hayyinan wa huwa ʿinda'llāhi ʿaẓīm*. *Hayyin* means light, easy, trivial, of no weight. *ʿAẓīm* means immense, weighty, momentous. The verse states that the community's *measurement scale was broken*. They were not, for the most part, malicious. They simply did not think it was a big deal. This is the most universally applicable sentence in the entire passage, because it names the specific failure that permits almost all sins of speech. Nobody thinks of a passed-on remark as a major crime. It is conversational filler, social lubricant, a way of showing interest in others' lives. The Qur'an's response is not to argue about the facts but to *correct the weights*: your scale says light, God's scale says immense. The Prophet ﷺ addressed exactly this discrepancy:

> *"A man may speak a word that he thinks nothing of, and by it he sinks in the Fire further than the distance between the east and the west."* (al-Bukhārī 6477; Muslim 2988)

And in another report:

> *"A servant may speak a word pleasing to God, to which he attaches no importance, and by it God raises him in degrees; and a servant may speak a word displeasing to God, to which he attaches no importance, and by it he falls into Hell."* (al-Bukhārī 6478)

The phrase "to which he attaches no importance" is the Prophetic paraphrase of *taḥsabūnahu hayyinan*. Both texts are describing the same gap between human and divine weighing. There is a further tradition on the sins the believer regards as trivial, and it produces a memorable image: Anas ibn Mālik is reported to have told the generation after the Companions that they were doing things they considered finer than a hair which the Companions had counted among the destroyers (al-Bukhārī 6492). And the Prophet ﷺ compared the small sins a person accumulates to the twigs each traveller gathers in a valley until, together, they cook the food — a fire built from nothing but things thought too small to matter.

**Why Human Beings Systematically Underweight Speech**

It is worth asking *why* the scale is broken, because understanding the mechanism helps repair it. Speech feels weightless because it produces no visible immediate effect. If you strike someone, you see the bruise. If you steal, the object is gone. If you say something, the room simply continues. The damage is delayed, dispersed, and invisible to the speaker: it happens in the mind of the listener, then in the listener's next conversation, then in a decision made months later by someone the speaker never meets. Cause and effect are separated so widely that the moral connection is never felt. Speech is also cheap and constant. A person may steal once in a lifetime and speak fifty thousand words in a day. Anything done that often ceases to register as an action at all. And speech is *social*: it is performed in company, with approval, as part of belonging. Nothing dulls conscience faster than the agreement of a group. The Qur'an's remedy is to attach *external weight* — a stated divine assessment — to an act whose internal weight the human being cannot feel. That is precisely what revelation is for: calibrating a scale we cannot calibrate ourselves.

**The Meaning of "Great in the Eyes of God"**

Why is it *great*? Several reasons emerge from the passage as a whole. Because it destroys what cannot be restored. Wealth can be repaid, property replaced, injuries healed. A reputation, once broken, is never fully rebuilt; the suspicion outlives the retraction. Because it violates a right of a human being (*ḥaqq al-ʿabd*), and the tradition teaches that such rights are not forgiven by repentance alone but require the pardon of the wronged party — or settlement on the Day when the currency is good deeds. The Prophet ﷺ described the bankrupt of his community as the one who arrives with prayers, fasting and charity but also with having insulted this one, slandered that one, and struck another, until his good deeds are distributed to his creditors and, when they run out, their sins are loaded onto him and he is thrown into the Fire (Muslim 2581). And because, in this case, it struck at the household of a Prophet and at the cohesion of a community that God was building for the world.

**The Discipline This Verse Requires**

Three habits follow directly. *Insert a gap between ear and tongue.* The failure named here is the absence of any interval. Anything that creates one — a day's delay, the deliberate practice of not repeating anything on the day you hear it — dismantles the mechanism the verse describes. *Ask the knowledge question in its strict form.* Not "is this probably true?" but "do I have *ʿilm* of this?" Almost nothing that circulates about other people meets that standard. *Distrust your own sense of weight.* This is the deepest instruction. The verse's whole point is that the offender's assessment was sincere and wrong. Whenever you find yourself thinking *this is a small thing*, treat that thought itself as a warning sign, since it is the exact phrase the Qur'an quotes from people who were about to be punished.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:16

> **And why, when you heard it, did you not say, "It is not for us to speak of this! Glory be to Thee! This is a tremendous calumny!"**

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Supplies the Words**

Verse 12 said the believers should have declared the rumor a manifest lie. This verse goes further: it *scripts the sentence* they should have spoken, word for word. God does not merely rebuke a failure; He hands the community the exact reply for the next time. This is a striking pedagogical method, and it is more common in the Qur'an than readers notice. Much of the Book is composed of words to be *said*: the Fātiḥah, the invocations at the end of al-Baqarah, the responses of the prophets, the formulas of refuge in the final sūrahs. Islam trains conduct partly by supplying language, on the sound principle that people are far more likely to do the right thing when they already know how to phrase it. Most failures to object in a room are not failures of conviction but failures of vocabulary — the person disapproves but cannot think what to say. So the verse gives the line.

**"It Is Not For Us To Speak Of This"**

The first clause is *mā yakūnu lanā an natakallama bi-hādhā* — literally, "it is not *for* us," "it does not belong to us," "it is not fitting or permitted to us to speak of this." This is not a claim about the truth of the allegation. It is a claim about *standing*. There is a category of matter that is simply not one's business, and the appropriate response to it is not investigation, not judgment, not even careful evaluation — but withdrawal. That is an unfamiliar thought in a culture that treats access to information as a right and commentary as a duty. The verse proposes something else: that a mature moral agent recognizes subjects on which he has no license to speak at all, and says so. The Prophet ﷺ made this a defining trait of a sound Muslim:

> *"Part of the excellence of a man's Islam is his leaving alone what does not concern him."* (al-Tirmidhī 2317; Ibn Mājah 3976)

And he tied salvation itself to the tongue in the answer he gave ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir, who asked what salvation is:

> *"Restrain your tongue, let your house suffice you, and weep over your sin."* (al-Tirmidhī 2406)

The three items in that reply — the tongue, the house, and one's own sin — are almost a summary of Sūrat al-Nūr's first half.

**"Glory Be to Thee": Why an Exclamation of Divine Transcendence?**

The second clause is the surprising one. *Subḥānaka* — "Glory be to Thee," "You are far exalted above all imperfection." Why does the correct response to a piece of gossip include an address to *God*? The commentators offer several explanations, and they are complementary. Some read it as an expression of *astonishment* — in Arabic usage, *subḥān Allāh* is uttered at something amazing or shocking, as the Prophet ﷺ himself would say it upon encountering something remarkable. The believer, hearing such an accusation about the wife of God's Messenger, should have been struck with disbelief: *God be glorified — such a thing said about her?* Others read it as *declaring God far above* permitting such a thing to occur in His Prophet's household. It would be a defect in the divine care of prophecy for a Messenger's wife to be corrupt, and the Qur'an elsewhere establishes the principle governing this very sūrah at verse 26: *good women are for good men.* So *subḥānaka* is a theological argument compressed into one word: God is exalted above allowing that. Others still read it as the standard Islamic reflex of turning to God at the arrival of evil — the way *aʿūdhu bi'llāh* or *lā ḥawla wa lā quwwata illā bi'llāh* function. All three readings converge on one insight: the believer's response to slander should be *vertical before it is horizontal*. Before analyzing the claim, before defending the accused, before assessing motives, the first movement is upward — a recollection of God, which instantly reframes the entire situation. A conversation in which someone says *subḥān Allāh* in this tone is a conversation that has been interrupted at the level of atmosphere, not merely of content.

**"This Is a Tremendous Calumny"**

The third clause is *hādhā buhtānun ʿaẓīm*. *Buhtān* is a specific and terrible word. Its root, *b-h-t*, means to be struck dumb, stupefied, rendered speechless with shock — as in the Qur'anic description of the disputant confounded by Abraham's argument: *"and so he who disbelieved was confounded (buhita)"* (2:258). A *buhtān* is therefore a lie so outrageous that the person against whom it is told is left *stunned*, unable even to respond. The word is chosen with terrible accuracy. That is precisely what happened to ʿĀʾishah: she wept for two days and could not speak in her own defence, and when finally called upon she said she could find no words except those of Jacob. Slander produces speechlessness in the victim; the Arabic names the crime after that effect. And observe the adjective: *ʿaẓīm*, "tremendous" — the same word used in verse 15 for how God weighs the act ("*great* in the Eyes of God") and in verse 11 for the punishment of the chief slanderer ("a *great* punishment"). The Qur'an is deliberately hammering one word: *the thing you thought was light is ʿaẓīm.* The scripted sentence therefore contains the corrected weight. In saying it, a believer trains his own scale.

**Comparing the Two Prescribed Responses**

It is worth noting the difference between the sentence prescribed in verse 12 and the one prescribed here. Verse 12: *"This is a manifest lie"* — a statement about *the content*: it is false, and evidently so. Verse 16: *"It is not for us to speak of this. Glory be to Thee. This is a tremendous calumny."* — a statement about *the speaker's standing*, about *God*, and about *the moral magnitude of the act*. Together they give a complete response covering the four dimensions of the situation: the falsity of the claim, the impropriety of the discussion, the presence of God, and the seriousness of the sin. A believer who says both has left nothing out.

**Why Scripted Speech Works**

There is a practical insight here about moral formation that the verse assumes and modern experience confirms. In the moment of social pressure, deliberation is unavailable. The gossip is underway; six people are enjoying it; the newcomer has perhaps two seconds to respond before the moment passes. Nobody composes an ethical position in two seconds. What operates in that window is *prepared reflex*. This is why religion works largely through formulas — memorized phrases, ritual responses, habitual invocations. They are pre-loaded conduct, available before thought. A person who has internalized *"it is not for us to speak of this"* will produce it automatically; a person who merely holds the general opinion that gossip is bad will produce nothing, and will join in.

**The Verse in Practice**

The application is unusually concrete: memorize the line and use it. When a conversation turns to someone's private failings, the believer has a divinely authored intervention available: *this isn't ours to discuss.* It is short, non-accusatory, and — crucially — it does not require the speaker to know whether the allegation is true. That is its genius. One need not defend the accused, argue the facts, or accuse the gossiper of lying. One simply declines jurisdiction. And when the matter is grave, the second half is available too: *God be glorified — that is a terrible thing to say about someone.* Fourteen centuries later, in a language the first hearers never knew, the sentence still performs exactly the function it was revealed to perform: it stops the flood at the moment it begins.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:17

> **God exhorts you, lest you ever return to the like of it, if you are believers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Rebuke to Prohibition of Recurrence**

The passage now shifts from analyzing what happened to legislating against its repetition. *Yaʿiẓukumu'llāhu an taʿūdū li-mithlihi abadan in kuntum muʾminīn* — "God admonishes you never to return to the like of it, if you are believers." Three features of this short verse carry great weight: the nature of the admonition (*waʿẓ*), the object prohibited (*the like of it*, not merely *it*), and the condition attached (*if you are believers*).

**Waʿẓ: Admonition That Aims at the Heart**

The verb *yaʿiẓu* is from *w-ʿ-ẓ*, and *waʿẓ* is a specific mode of moral address. The lexicographers define it as reminding a person of consequences in a way that softens the heart — instruction accompanied by warning and delivered with the intent to move, not merely to inform. It is distinct from *taʿlīm* (teaching facts) and from *amr* (issuing a command). Its use here characterizes the whole passage. God is not simply legislating against gossip; He is *counselling* a community He loves. The Qur'an uses the same word of itself:

> *"O mankind! There has come to you an admonition (mawʿiẓah) from your Lord, and a healing for what lies within the breasts, and a guidance and mercy for the believers."* (Qur'an 10:57)

Notice the pairing there: admonition and *healing for what lies within the breasts*. The Qur'anic diagnosis of the slander was not primarily behavioural but internal — broken scales, absent good opinion, undisciplined tongues. Admonition is the treatment appropriate to an internal disease. And there is tenderness in the choice. After the severity of verses 11 through 16 — liars, tremendous calumny, great punishment — the tone here is that of a teacher who has finished the rebuke and now says: *I am telling you this so that it never happens again.* The word *yaʿiẓukum* is in the present tense, indicating ongoing action: God *keeps* admonishing you. The Book is a permanent counsellor, not a one-time judgment.

**"To the Like of It": The Prohibition Is Generalized**

The most legally significant word in the verse is *mithlihi*, "the like of it." God does not forbid a return to *that* incident — which could not recur in any case — but to anything *resembling* it. This is how a specific historical episode becomes a permanent law. The prohibition is not "never again slander ʿĀʾishah"; it is "never again do anything of this type." And since the passage has just anatomized the type in exhaustive detail — receiving with the tongue, speaking without knowledge, thinking it slight, failing to demand witnesses, failing to think well, failing to object — the reader knows precisely what "the like of it" covers. The commentators note that this generalization catches every scale of the offence. The victim need not be a Prophet's wife; the rumor need not concern adultery; the community need not be Madinah. Any structurally similar event — an unverified damaging claim, received and passed on, treated as trivial — falls under the prohibition. The word *abadan*, "ever," seals it. No expiry, no exceptions, no circumstances in which the community may revisit this behaviour.

**"If You Are Believers": The Condition That Cuts**

The verse ends with the sūrah's characteristic device: *in kuntum muʾminīn*. The same conditional appeared at verse 2, tying the judge's firmness to belief in God and the Last Day. Here it ties the discipline of the tongue to the possession of faith itself. Arabic conditionals of this form are not literal tests of membership; they are rhetorical instruments that make a behaviour and an identity inseparable. The implication is: *this is what belief looks like when it is real.* A person who continues to trade in rumors is not being accused of unbelief, but he is being told that his conduct and his claim are pulling in opposite directions. This is precisely how the Prophet ﷺ framed the ethics of speech:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent. Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him honour his neighbour. Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him honour his guest."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

The identical conditional structure — *whoever believes… let him* — attaches the highest theological claim to the most ordinary conversational behaviour. And in a definition that has become proverbial:

> *"The Muslim is the one from whose tongue and hand the Muslims are safe."* (al-Bukhārī 10; Muslim 40)

Note the order: tongue before hand.

**Why Recurrence Is the Real Danger**

The verse's focus on *return* recognizes something important about moral failure: the danger is rarely the first offence but the pattern. A community that gossips once and is corrected may become permanently careful. A community that gossips, is corrected, and gossips again has established the corrected behaviour as its norm and the correction as a ritual it can absorb.
