The second offence is worse than the first, because the first was ignorance and the second is contempt. The Qur'an is explicit elsewhere that returning to a sin after admonition is a distinct and heavier matter:

> *"…God has pardoned what is past, but whosoever returns, God will take vengeance upon him."* (Qur'an 5:95, on a different subject, but the principle of return is identical)

The Islamic tradition therefore treats *iṣrār* — persistence in a small sin — as capable of transforming it into a major one, while a major sin abandoned in repentance is erased. Which of a person's sins are truly dangerous is determined less by their size than by whether he has decided to keep doing them.

**The Structure of the Admonition: Why It Comes Here**

Notice where this verse falls. The passage has moved through: the event (v. 11), the failure of good opinion (v. 12), the failure of evidence (v. 13), the reprieve (v. 14), the mechanism (v. 15), and the scripted correct response (v. 16). Only now, after full understanding has been established, comes the forward-looking prohibition. That order is itself instruction. Effective moral correction does not begin with "never do that again." It begins with making the person *see* what he did, why it was serious, how it happened inside him, and what he should have done instead. Only then is the injunction against recurrence meaningful, because only then does the person know what he is committing to avoid. Anyone who has tried to correct a child, a student, or a colleague will recognize the wisdom of the sequence. A prohibition issued before comprehension produces resentment; the same prohibition issued after comprehension produces change.

**A Personal Application**

The practical instruction here is to convert the incident from an episode into a *policy*. Everyone has been part of a conversation they later regretted. The ordinary response is momentary embarrassment, which fades. This verse asks for something structural: a decision, made once and held permanently, that one does not participate in this category of speech. Such a policy is far easier to keep than a series of individual judgments. A person who has decided in advance that he does not repeat unverified claims about others does not have to relitigate the question every time an interesting story arrives — which is fortunate, because in the moment, with the story in hand and the audience waiting, the relitigation almost always goes the wrong way. That is what *an taʿūdū li-mithlihi abadan* — "never return to the like of it" — asks for: not vigilance in each instance, but a settled and permanent refusal.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:18

> **And God makes clear unto you the signs, and God is Knowing, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Short Verse Closing a Long Argument**

After eight verses of narrative, rebuke, analysis, and prohibition, the passage on the slander pauses with a formula: *wa yubayyinu'llāhu lakumu'l-āyāt, wa'llāhu ʿalīmun ḥakīm.* Such formulaic closings recur throughout this sūrah — at verses 34, 46, 58, 59, and 61 — and it is tempting to read past them as pious punctuation. That would be a mistake. Each is precisely fitted to the passage it closes, and the two divine names chosen at the end are always the two names that make the preceding ruling intelligible. Read carefully, this short verse contains the theological justification for everything that came before it.

**Yubayyinu: The Verb of Making Clear**

The verb *bayyana* is the same root that gave *āyāt mubayyināt* in verse 1 and *ifkun mubīn* in verse 12. Its core meaning is *separation*: to make a thing distinct from what surrounds it, so that its edges are visible. The choice is exact for this passage. What had gone wrong in Madinah was precisely a loss of distinctness — truth and rumor had become indistinguishable, the innocent could not be told from the guilty, the trivial had been confused with the grave. Revelation's function, named by this verb, is to restore the boundaries: this is a lie, this is a great sin, this is what you should have said, this is what you must never do again. The present-tense form *yubayyinu* indicates continuous action. God does not clarify once and fall silent; the clarifying is an ongoing property of the Book. Every generation that reads this passage receives the same separation of categories performed for it afresh.

**Āyāt: Signs, Verses, Evidences**

The word *āyāt* has a range that English cannot capture with one term. An *āyah* is a sign, a token, a marker, a miracle, and also a verse of the Qur'an. The Book uses the same word for the natural phenomena that point to God — as this very sūrah will do at verses 41 through 45, with the birds, the clouds, the night and day, and the creatures created from water — and for its own sentences. The identification is deliberate and theologically rich. The verses of scripture and the phenomena of creation are two systems of signs from one Author, and both require reading. This sūrah will make the parallel explicit: after the legislation of the first half comes the Light Verse and then the cosmic signs, and after them, at verse 46, the same formula: *"We have indeed sent down clarifying signs."* So when God says here that He "makes clear the signs," He is describing both an act of revelation and an act of illumination — which is entirely fitting in a chapter named for light.

**ʿAlīm: The Name That Guarantees the Narrative**

The first name is *ʿalīm*, the Knowing — an intensive form indicating knowledge that is total, immediate, and without gap. Its relevance to this passage is direct and consoling. The entire crisis of the slander was a crisis of *knowledge*: nobody knew what had happened. ʿĀʾishah knew her own innocence but could not prove it. The Prophet ﷺ awaited revelation. The community speculated. Verse 15 condemned them for speaking "of that which you had no knowledge." Against that darkness the name stands: *God is Knowing*. There was never a moment in that month when the truth was unknown; it was simply unknown *to them*. Every whisper in every house of Madinah was heard; every private assumption was registered. This has two effects at once, and both are intended. To the wronged, it is comfort: your vindication does not depend on anyone believing you. To the wrongdoer, it is warning: your contribution was recorded even if no one traced it to you. The anonymity that makes rumor possible — nobody can say who started it — does not exist in the register that matters. The Qur'an states this with particular force in relation to speech:

> *"He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the breasts conceal."* (Qur'an 40:19)

And:

> *"Not a word does he utter, but there is a watcher by him, ready."* (Qur'an 50:18)

**Ḥakīm: The Name That Justifies the Law**

The second name is *ḥakīm*, the Wise — the root, as noted at verse 10, connected with *ḥukm* (judgment), *iḥkām* (making firm and sound), and the bridle that restrains an animal from wandering. Its placement here answers the question a reader might raise after this long passage: *why so severe about words?* Why should repeating a rumor be classed with the destroyers, punished with eighty lashes, and treated as a matter for divine intervention? The name is the answer: because the Legislator is *wise*, and the severity is calibrated by a knowledge of consequences that the legislated do not possess. The community thought the matter *hayyin*, light (v. 15). They were wrong about the weight because they could not see the causal chain — how a rumor fractures a city, how a slandered woman's silence spreads to every other woman's insecurity, how a community that cannot distinguish evidence from assertion eventually cannot govern itself or transmit its religion. Wisdom, in the Qur'anic sense, is not abstract cleverness; it is *putting each thing in its proper place*. The pairing of the two names describes exactly what a lawgiver must possess: complete knowledge of the facts and perfect judgment about proportion. A legislator with knowledge but no wisdom produces harsh or arbitrary law; a legislator with wisdom but incomplete knowledge produces well-meaning error. Only the combination yields law that can be trusted where one cannot see the reasons.

**Why This Closing Formula Matters for How Muslims Obey**

There is a general lesson here about the relationship between a believer and the divine law, and it applies far beyond this passage. Human beings obey rules for two different reasons: because they see the point, or because they trust the source. The Qur'an consistently supplies both. It explains extensively — this entire passage is explanation — and then it closes by naming the attributes of the Explainer, because explanation always runs out before obedience does. There will always be a residue of the law whose wisdom is not apparent to a given person at a given moment. For that residue, the closing names are the answer. *He is Knowing* — He is aware of factors I am not. *He is Wise* — His rulings are proportionate even where the proportion is invisible to me. This is not the abandonment of reason but its completion: it is rational to defer to a source demonstrably wiser than oneself on precisely those points where one's own vision fails.

**The Verse as a Rest Point**

Structurally, this verse functions as a breath. The passage has been intense: an accusation against the Prophet's ﷺ household, a month of communal failure, a divine cross-examination, threats of great punishment. Now, before the argument resumes at verse 19 with a warning to those who *want* indecency spread, the Qur'an pauses to say: this has all been *clarification*, from One who knows and is wise. Even the rhythm is instruction. Correction should end in reassurance about the corrector, not in the corrected person's despair. The community is left knowing that it was addressed by One who saw everything, judged proportionately, and took the trouble to explain.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:19

> **Truly those who desire that indecency be spread among those who believe, theirs shall be a painful punishment in this world and the Hereafter. God knows, and you know not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Law Reaches Into the Chest**

Up to this point the passage has punished acts: manufacturing a lie, repeating it, failing to demand evidence. This verse goes deeper than any of them. It attaches a punishment — in both worlds — to a *desire*. *Inna'lladhīna yuḥibbūna an tashīʿa'l-fāḥishatu fi'lladhīna āmanū* — "truly those who *love* that indecency should spread among those who believe." The verb is *yuḥibbūna*, from *ḥubb*, love. Not those who spread it; those who *love* that it spread. This is one of the very few places in the Qur'an where a punishment is announced for an inclination of the heart, and it therefore requires careful handling.

**What Is and Is Not Punished**

Islamic theology is clear, on the authority of the Prophet ﷺ, that fleeting thoughts and involuntary impulses are not held against a person:

> *"God has pardoned my community for what their souls whisper to them, so long as they do not act upon it or speak of it."* (al-Bukhārī 5269; Muslim 127)

So the verse cannot mean that a passing curiosity about a scandal incurs a painful punishment. What it addresses is something settled and cultivated: a *disposition* — the state of a person who has come to find pleasure in the exposure of others' sins, who wants such stories to exist and to circulate, who is gratified when a reputation falls. The distinction is between a thought that crosses the mind and an appetite one has taken up residence in. And the reason the Qur'an targets the appetite rather than only the act is that the appetite is the engine. Punish acts alone, and a person merely learns discretion; the desire remains and will find safer outlets. The Qur'anic method, here as elsewhere, is to reach the source.

**Tashīʿ: The Verb of Diffusion**

The verb *shāʿa* means to spread, become widespread, become common — the root of *shāʾiʿ*, "widespread," and of the modern Arabic word for a rumor, *ishāʿah*. The image is diffusion: something entering a medium and permeating it entirely, like a drop of ink in water or a smell through a house. The choice of verb identifies precisely what the crime is. It is not the existence of sin in a community — the Qur'an is realistic about that, and the second verse of this sūrah legislates for it. It is the *diffusion* of the knowledge and normalization of sin through the whole social body. There is a world of difference between a community in which a wrong occurs and is concealed, and a community in which the same wrong occurs and becomes the subject of universal conversation.

**The Reasoning: Why Publicity Multiplies Sin**

The logic behind this verse is not obvious at first, and it is worth reconstructing, because once seen it is very hard to unsee. When a sin is committed privately and remains private, its damage is bounded: it harms the doer and any direct victim. When the same sin becomes public knowledge, several new harms are created that did not exist before. *Normalization.* Human beings calibrate their behaviour to what they believe others are doing. A society in which indecency is constantly discussed becomes a society in which indecency appears ordinary, and the psychological barrier against it falls for thousands of people who never witnessed the original act. Widely publicized wrongdoing measurably increases imitation, a pattern well documented in the study of suicide and of violent crime alike. *Loss of shame.* Shame is a real and functional moral resource. When a community treats a category of act as unmentionable, that unmentionability is itself a deterrent. Constant discussion wears it away. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Modesty is part of faith."* (al-Bukhārī 9; Muslim 35)

> *"If you feel no shame, do as you wish."* (al-Bukhārī 3483)

That second statement is often misread as permission; it is a diagnosis. Shame is the internal restraint, and when it is gone, nothing external will substitute. *Destruction of the sinner's route back.* A person whose fault is concealed can repent quietly and rebuild. A person whose fault is broadcast has, in the eyes of the community, become his fault permanently. Publicity closes the door that verse 5 of this very sūrah insisted on leaving open. *Corrosion of general trust.* If everyone's private failings are public property, then no one can be safe, and a community becomes a place of mutual surveillance rather than mutual support.

**The Prophetic Counter-Instruction: Concealment**

Against this, Islam builds an entire ethic of *satr* — covering. The relevant traditions are numerous and unusually emphatic:

> *"No servant conceals the fault of another servant in this world but that God will conceal his faults on the Day of Resurrection."* (Muslim 2590)

> *"Whoever relieves a believer of a distress of this world, God will relieve him of a distress on the Day of Resurrection… and God is in the aid of His servant so long as the servant is in the aid of his brother."* (Muslim 2699)

And the Prophet ﷺ addressed those whose faith had not entered their hearts:

> *"O company of those who have believed with their tongues but in whose hearts faith has not entered! Do not backbite the Muslims, and do not pursue their private faults; for whoever pursues their private faults, God will pursue his, and whomever God pursues, He exposes, even within his own house."* (Abū Dāwūd 4880)

Even in cases of established sin, the Prophetic instinct was concealment. When Māʿiz confessed, he was turned away three times. When a man was brought drunk and some present cursed him, the Prophet ﷺ said: *"Do not help Satan against your brother."* (al-Bukhārī 6781)

**The Necessary Boundaries**

This principle can be abused, and honesty requires stating its limits, because concealment has sometimes been invoked to protect predators rather than sinners. The tradition distinguishes clearly. Concealment applies to a *past, private, individual* sin, particularly one the person regrets. It does not apply where concealment enables *ongoing harm to others*. Reporting an abuser to the authorities is not *ishāʿat al-fāḥishah*; it is the prevention of harm, which the Sharīʿah treats as an obligation. Warning a family about a prospective spouse's known dangerous conduct is not slander; the Prophet ﷺ himself, when consulted about a proposal by Fāṭimah bint Qays, told her plainly that one suitor was harsh with women and the other was penniless (Muslim 1480) — advice-giving (*naṣīḥah*) in a matter of consequence is a recognized exception. The test that emerges from the sources is one of *purpose and audience*. Speaking to a person who can prevent or remedy harm, for the purpose of prevention, is legitimate. Speaking to an audience that can do nothing but enjoy the information is the offence this verse names. The same words, spoken to a judge and spoken to a group chat, are two different acts.

**"In This World and the Hereafter"**

The punishment is doubled in scope. The commentators identify the worldly portion variously: the *ḥadd* of slander, which was in fact carried out on several participants in this very incident; the disgrace that eventually attaches to known rumor-mongers; and — a subtle point — the outcome named in the Prophetic tradition above, that whoever pursues others' faults will find his own pursued and exposed. There is a natural justice in that mechanism. The person who trades in others' shame lives in a community where shame is currency, and eventually the currency is spent on him.

**"God Knows, and You Know Not"**

The verse closes with a five-word sentence of tremendous force: *wa'llāhu yaʿlamu wa antum lā taʿlamūn.* Read in context, it carries at least three distinct meanings, and the commentators note several. *He knows what really happened, and you do not.* This is the direct application to the slander: the community was speculating about an event only God had witnessed. *He knows the hearts, and you do not.* The verse has just made love of scandal punishable. Only God can identify who harbours it — which forecloses any attempt by believers to police one another's inner states. The verse gives no human being licence to accuse another of *loving* indecency; that judgment belongs to the One who reads chests. *He knows the consequences, and you do not.* This connects to verse 15's "you supposed it slight." The reason the community underestimated the harm is that they could not trace where the words would travel or what they would do. God can. Therefore the assessment of gravity must be taken from Him rather than from one's own intuition. The clause also closes the passage with the appropriate posture: intellectual humility. A community that had spent a month confidently discussing what it did not know is told, in the plainest possible words, what its actual epistemic position was. Whoever has felt the certainty that comes with hearing a piece of inside information should hear this sentence addressed to himself: *God knows, and you do not.*

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:20

> **And were it not for God's Bounty toward you, and His Mercy, and that God is Kind, Merciful!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Sounding of the Refrain**

For the third time in this passage — after verses 10 and 14 — the sentence begins *wa lawlā faḍlu'llāhi ʿalaykum wa raḥmatuh*, and for the second time it is left unfinished. The consequence was stated once, at verse 14 ("a great punishment would have touched you"), and then withheld again here, so that the reader must carry the earlier statement forward and supply it himself. The repetition is not redundancy. In Arabic rhetoric, *takrār* — deliberate repetition — is a technique of impression: it fixes a thought so that it cannot be read past. And the placement of each instance is precise. Verse 10 came after the marital oath procedure; verse 14 after the description of the community's failure; and this one after the severest statement in the passage, the threat of punishment in both worlds to those who love indecency to spread. That placement is the key to this verse. Having just described a category of people facing painful punishment in this world and the next, the Qur'an immediately reminds the same audience that they are still standing only because of unearned favor. The threat and the reprieve are placed side by side so that neither can be read alone: fear without hope produces despair, and hope without fear produces negligence. The Qur'an consistently binds them.

**Two New Names**

What distinguishes this instance of the refrain is its closing pair of divine names: *wa anna'llāha raʾūfun raḥīm* — "and that God is Kind, Merciful." Verse 10 closed with *tawwāb ḥakīm*, Relenting and Wise. Here the names are *raʾūf* and *raḥīm*, and the change is significant. Each refrain ends with the attributes suited to the fear it is answering. *Raʾūf* is from *raʾfah*, and this is the word we met at verse 2 — *"let not pity (raʾfah) for them overcome you"*. That earlier occurrence gives us its exact register: *raʾfah* is the tender, softening compassion that recoils at the sight of another's suffering. It is the most delicate word for mercy in the Qur'anic vocabulary. The lexicographers and commentators distinguish it from *raḥmah* in a way that is worth stating. *Raḥmah* is the broader mercy that includes doing good to one who deserves punishment; *raʾfah* is specifically the reluctance to inflict harm, the wincing sympathy that precedes and prevents the blow. Some scholars express it thus: *raʾfah* is mercy in the face of hardship — the shielding of a person from difficulty — while *raḥmah* is comprehensive, extending to blessing and provision as well. Their combination here is exquisitely appropriate. God is *raʾūf* — He was pained, so to speak, at what would have had to befall this community, and withheld it. And He is *raḥīm* — He did not merely withhold the harm but replaced it with good: sixteen verses of guidance, a vindicated wife, an instructed community, a permanent chapter of teaching for the human race. Notice also the deliberate echo of verse 2. There the believers were told *not* to let *raʾfah* stop them from carrying out God's judgment. Here they are told that God's own *raʾfah* has stopped Him from carrying out theirs. The verbal repetition invites the comparison, and the lesson is precise: human beings are not permitted to suspend God's law out of sentiment, because they lack the knowledge and authority to judge when suspension is right. God, who has both, exercises exactly that prerogative. Mercy is His to give, not ours to grant on His behalf.

**Why the Sentence Is Left Unfinished**

It is worth returning to the rhetorical device, because its function deepens with repetition. A stated punishment is *bounded*. If God had said "you would have been destroyed," the reader's imagination would rest at that image and go no further. An unstated one is unbounded: every reader completes it with the consequence that his own conscience finds most plausible, which is precisely the consequence most likely to move him. There is also something merciful in the silence. To describe in detail what a community deserved would itself be a kind of punishment — a permanent public record of their worst possible fate. God stops the sentence, and in stopping it, performs the very *raʾfah* the verse goes on to name. The grammar enacts the attribute.

**What the Reprieve Was For**

A reprieve raises a question: *for what purpose?* A stay of execution is not an acquittal; it is time granted, and time is granted for something. The answer arrives immediately in the next verse, which begins with the vocative that always signals a demand: *"O you who believe! Follow not the footsteps of Satan."* The mercy of verse 20 is the ground of the command of verse 21. Because you were spared, you are now required to change direction. This is the consistent structure of Qur'anic ethics: indicative before imperative, gift before demand. The believer is not asked to earn God's favor by obedience; he is informed of a favor already received and then asked to respond to it. Gratitude, not fear of loss, is the engine.

**Living Under Reprieve**

The practical spirituality of this verse is a particular way of reading one's own life. Every person carries a set of things that could have gone very badly and did not: the illness caught in time, the accident narrowly avoided, the words said in anger that the other person chose to forget, the sin nobody discovered, the decision that would have ruined a life had it been taken. The ordinary mind files these under luck, or does not file them at all. This verse asks for a different accounting. Each of them is *faḍl* — surplus favor — and *raʾfah*, a divine reluctance to let harm reach you. The believer who takes that seriously develops a quality the Qur'an prizes and modern life makes rare: a sense of being continuously, undeservedly protected. That awareness has an immediate ethical output, and it is exactly the output this sūrah wants. A person who knows himself to be under permanent reprieve finds it very difficult to be merciless toward someone else's exposed failure. The link is not sentimental; it is logical. You cannot simultaneously believe that your own record is covered by an unearned kindness and that another person's record deserves publication. Which is why the next command in the sūrah, after this refrain, concerns following footsteps — and why the verse after that will ask the offended party in the very slander itself to forgive the man who slandered him, with the unanswerable question: *"Do you not desire that God forgive you?"*

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:21

> **O you who believe! Follow not the footsteps of Satan! And whosoever follows the footsteps of Satan, truly he enjoins indecency and wrong. And were it not for God's Bounty toward you, and His Mercy, not one of you would ever be pure. But God purifies whomsoever He will, and God is Hearing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Diagnosis Behind the Whole Episode**

This verse steps back from the particular scandal to name the mechanism that produced it. Everything the community did — receiving with the tongue, thinking it slight, failing to object — is now given a single explanation: they were walking in someone else's footsteps. *Yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū lā tattabiʿū khuṭuwāti'l-shayṭān.* The vocative "O you who believe" marks a shift from rebuke to legislation; in the Qur'an it almost always introduces a direct command to the community.

**Khuṭuwāt: The Footsteps and the Method They Describe**

The key word is *khuṭuwāt*, plural of *khuṭwah*, a single step or pace — the distance covered by one stride. The phrase "the footsteps of Satan" occurs four times in the Qur'an (2:168, 2:208, 6:142, and here), and the commentators universally read the image as a description of *method*. Satan does not, in the Qur'anic account, invite people to catastrophe in one leap. He invites them to a *step*. Then to another step. Each individual step is small, defensible, barely worth noticing — and the cumulative trajectory arrives somewhere the person would have refused outright at the beginning. Apply this to the slander and the anatomy becomes exact: *Step one*: hearing something interesting. No sin at all.
*Step two*: wondering whether it might be true. Barely a thought.
*Step three*: mentioning it to one trusted person, "just to ask what they think."
*Step four*: discussing it further, adding an observation.
*Step five*: the story is established in the city, a woman is destroyed, a Prophet's household is in crisis, and a community stands under threat of divine punishment. No one in Madinah intended step five. Every one of them chose only the next step. That is the entire strategy the word *khuṭuwāt* names. This is why the Qur'an so often forbids the *approach* to a sin rather than only the sin: *"And approach not adultery"* (17:32) — not "do not commit," but do not *approach*. It is also why this sūrah, having legislated against *zinā* in verse 2, will spend verses 27 through 33 legislating about doorways, glances, dress, and marriage: it is closing the steps. The general command was already given in al-Baqarah:

> *"O you who believe! Enter into peace all together, and follow not the footsteps of Satan. Truly he is a manifest enemy unto you."* (Qur'an 2:208)

**"He Enjoins Indecency and Wrong"**

The verse then states where the footsteps lead: *fa-innahu yaʾmuru bi'l-faḥshāʾi wa'l-munkar.* *Faḥshāʾ* denotes gross indecency, especially of a sexual nature — the excessive, the shameless, that which exceeds all bounds. *Munkar* is from the root *n-k-r*, "to reject or find unfamiliar," and denotes what is universally rejected by sound nature and sound reason — the recognizably wrong. Its opposite, *maʿrūf*, means both "the good" and "the known/familiar," which reveals the Qur'anic assumption: virtue is what any decent person *recognizes*, and vice is what a healthy conscience finds *alien*. The verb *yaʾmuru*, "he commands," is chosen with care. Satan is not described as suggesting, tempting, or persuading here, but as *commanding* — which is what following footsteps eventually amounts to. A person who has taken enough steps in a direction is no longer choosing; he is being led. The word describes the terminal state of the process the earlier word describes.

**"Not One of You Would Ever Be Pure"**

Then comes the fourth and most theologically weighty appearance of the refrain, and this time the consequence is stated in full: *mā zakā minkum min aḥadin abadan* — "not one of you would ever be pure." The claim is absolute and universal, and it should be read slowly. It does not say most people; it says *not one, ever*. Left to unaided human effort, no human being achieves purity of soul. The verb *zakā* is from the root *z-k-w*, which carries two joined meanings: *purity* and *growth*. It is the root of *zakāh*, the obligatory alms — so named because giving away part of one's wealth simultaneously purifies the remainder and causes it to grow. The Arabic assumes what agriculture demonstrates: a thing becomes clean and a thing becomes fruitful by the same process. So the verse is saying: no human being, unaided, becomes clean; and no human being, unaided, grows.

**A Careful Statement of the Theology**

This raises the great question of divine grace and human effort, and the verse must be read alongside the rest of the Qur'an to be understood correctly. Elsewhere the Book places purification squarely in human hands:

> *"Successful indeed is he who purifies it (man zakkāhā), and failed indeed is he who buries it."* (Qur'an 91:9–10)

> *"Successful indeed is he who purifies himself, and remembers the Name of his Lord and prays."* (Qur'an 87:14–15)

And here it says no one is purified except by God's bounty, and that *"God purifies whomsoever He will."* There is no contradiction; there are two true statements about a single act, viewed from two ends. From the human side, purification is a task, commanded and achievable, for which one is responsible. From the divine side, the capacity to perform that task — the guidance that shows the way, the strength to act, the very desire to be better — is itself given. The Qur'an refuses both errors: the fatalism that says effort is pointless, and the arrogance that says effort is sufficient. The Prophet ﷺ held the same two truths together in a single statement:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds."* They said: "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Not even I, unless God covers me with His mercy. So aim for what is right, draw near, and be moderate."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)

Note the structure: works do not earn Paradise — *and therefore* aim for what is right. The conclusion drawn is exertion, not passivity. There is also a psychological effect intended here, and it is the reason the statement appears in *this* passage rather than anywhere else. A community has just been rebuked at length for a grave failure. Two dangers now threaten it: despair ("we are corrupt beyond repair") and self-righteousness ("we have now been corrected and are better than those who fell"). This verse eliminates both in one sentence. Nobody is pure by his own achievement, so nobody may look down on the fallen; and God purifies whom He wills, so nobody need despair of being purified.

**"God Purifies Whomsoever He Will"**

The clause *wa lākinna'llāha yuzakkī man yashāʾ* is not arbitrary favoritism. Divine will, in Qur'anic usage, is never capricious; it operates in accordance with divine wisdom, and the Book repeatedly explains *whom* God wills to guide and purify — those who turn to Him, who strive, who seek:

> *"And those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them in Our ways."* (Qur'an 29:69)

> *"God chooses for Himself whomsoever He will, and guides unto Himself whosoever turns in repentance."* (Qur'an 42:13)

The believer's practical stance follows from this: purification is *asked for* as much as it is worked for. Which is why the Prophet ﷺ taught supplications of exactly this form:

> *"O God, grant my soul its piety and purify it; You are the best of those who purify it; You are its Guardian and Master."* (Muslim 2722)

**Hearing and Knowing**

The verse closes with *samīʿun ʿalīm*, Hearing and Knowing — and after a passage entirely concerned with speech, the choice could not be more pointed. *Samīʿ*: every word of the rumor was heard, in every house, by every whisperer, including the ones spoken quietly to a single confidant. There is no private conversation. *ʿAlīm*: and every intention behind those words was known — who spoke from malice, who from thoughtlessness, who from a secret pleasure in another's fall. Together the names close every escape route. One cannot hide the words from the *Samīʿ*, and one cannot dress up the motive before the *ʿAlīm*. But the same two names are also a comfort: the supplication for purification just described is *heard*, and the sincerity behind it is *known*.

**Practical Consequences**

Three disciplines emerge from this verse. *Fight the first step, not the fifth.* By the time a person is doing something clearly wrong, the battle was lost several steps earlier, at a point that did not look like a battle. This is the entire practical value of the footsteps image: it relocates vigilance to the beginning of the sequence, where resistance is still cheap. *Never assume you are secure.* "Not one of you would ever be pure" is addressed to the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ — the best generation of this community. If they required continuous divine bounty to remain clean, no later reader may consider himself past the danger. *Ask for purification as you work for it.* The Qur'an's balance is to strive as though everything depended on the effort, and to supplicate knowing that it does not.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:22

> **And let not the men of bounty and means among you forswear giving to kinsfolk and the indigent and those who emigrated in the way of God. And let them pardon and forbear. Do you not desire that God forgive you? And God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hardest Verse in the Passage**

Everything so far has been directed at the slanderers and at the community that enabled them. This verse turns to the *victims' side* — and asks something almost unbearable of them. The occasion is recorded in the same authentic sources that preserve the whole incident (al-Bukhārī 4750; Muslim 2770). Among those who repeated the slander against ʿĀʾishah was Misṭaḥ ibn Uthāthah. Misṭaḥ was a poor relative of Abū Bakr — a maternal cousin — and Abū Bakr had supported him financially for years. He was also a veteran of Badr. When the vindication came down and Abū Bakr learned that Misṭaḥ had been among those who spread a lie about his own daughter, he swore an oath before God that he would never spend another coin on him. It is difficult to imagine a more understandable oath. The man he had fed had helped destroy his daughter's name. Then this verse was revealed. And ʿĀʾishah's narration records what her father said when he heard it:

> *"By God, I certainly love that God should forgive me."* And he resumed his spending on Misṭaḥ, and said: *"By God, I shall never withdraw it from him."*

That exchange is the verse in action, and no commentary can improve on it.

**"Let Them Not Swear Off": The Grammar of an Oath Undone**

The verb is *lā yaʾtali*, which the philologists derive either from *alw* (to fall short, to withhold) or from *ilyah/alīyah* (an oath). Both senses are present and both fit: "let them not *swear*" and "let them not *fall short*." Al-Qurṭubī notes both derivations. The verse is thus doing something legally interesting: it is instructing a man to break a solemn oath, because the oath was made to withhold a good. Islamic law systematizes this, and the Prophet ﷺ stated the rule:

> *"Whoever swears an oath and then sees something better than it, let him do that which is better and make expiation for his oath."* (Muslim 1650)

An oath is binding, but it is not a licence to entrench a wrong. A vow to be permanently unforgiving is a vow that should be broken and expiated.

**"Men of Bounty and Means"**

Those addressed are *ulu'l-faḍli minkum wa'l-saʿah* — "the possessors of bounty and of capacity among you." The description is not incidental. The word *faḍl* is the same word used four times in this passage for *God's* bounty — the surplus favor He gives beyond what is owed. Now it is used of Abū Bakr and those like him. The parallel is deliberate and it is the whole argument of the verse: *you have been given surplus; you were asked to pass surplus on.* A person who has received unearned generosity and then withholds earned generosity from another has misunderstood the nature of what he holds. *Saʿah* means breadth, spaciousness, capacity — financial room. Together the two words describe a person with more than he needs. The Qur'an regards such a condition as creating obligation rather than entitlement.

**Three Categories of Recipient**

The verse names those whom the wealthy must not cut off: *ulu'l-qurbā* (kinsfolk), *al-masākīn* (the indigent), and *al-muhājirīn fī sabīli'llāh* (those who emigrated in the way of God). Misṭaḥ was, remarkably, all three at once: a relative of Abū Bakr, a poor man, and an emigrant. The verse names each of his claims in turn, as if to say: *look how many bonds you are proposing to sever.* The listing also establishes a general priority in Islamic charity. Kinship first — the Qur'an consistently ranks relatives ahead of strangers in the order of giving, and the Prophet ﷺ said: *"Charity to a poor person is charity, and to a relative it is two: charity and maintaining kinship"* (al-Tirmidhī 658). Then need. Then sacrifice for the faith.

**"Let Them Pardon and Forbear"**

The two verbs are *wa'l-yaʿfū wa'l-yaṣfaḥū*, and they are not synonyms. The distinction the commentators draw between them is one of the most useful things in this passage. *ʿAfw* means to erase, efface, obliterate — the same root gives the Arabic for wind erasing tracks in the sand. To pardon is to *cancel the debt*: I no longer claim what you owe me for what you did. *Ṣafḥ* is from *ṣafḥah*, the flat surface, the face, the *page*. To *yaṣfaḥ* is to turn the page, or to present a smooth face — to behave as though it had not happened. The progression is exact and psychologically astute. *ʿAfw* is a decision; *ṣafḥ* is a manner. It is entirely possible to forgive someone in principle while continuing to treat them coldly, to remind them, to let the injury show in every interaction. The Qur'an closes that gap: cancel the debt *and* turn the page. The Prophet ﷺ combined both in his practice, most famously at the conquest of Makkah, where he addressed the people who had persecuted him for twenty-one years, driven him from his home, and killed his companions, and said:

> *"Go, for you are free."*

And he explicitly quoted Joseph's words to his brothers: *"No reproach shall be upon you this day"* (12:92) — a verse whose entire content is *ṣafḥ*: not merely forgiveness, but the refusal to even mention it.

**The Question That Ends the Argument**

Then comes the sentence that made Abū Bakr reverse his oath on the spot: *a lā tuḥibbūna an yaghfira'llāhu lakum* — "Do you not love that God should forgive you?" This is not a threat and it is not, strictly, a command. It is a *question*, and it works by placing two facts beside each other and letting the listener draw the conclusion himself. Fact one: you want God's forgiveness. Every human being does, and Abū Bakr more consciously than most.
Fact two: you are proposing to permanently withhold forgiveness from another human being. The verse does not even state the incompatibility. It simply asks the question and stops. This is the most effective form of moral argument, because the listener performs the reasoning himself and therefore cannot resist it. The principle it invokes runs throughout the tradition:

> *"The merciful are shown mercy by the All-Merciful. Be merciful to those on the earth, and the One in the heavens will be merciful to you."* (al-Tirmidhī 1924)

> *"God will not be merciful to those who are not merciful to people."* (al-Bukhārī 7376; Muslim 2319)

And in the same spirit the Prophet ﷺ taught his community to pray using the same reciprocity: the supplication *"forgive us as we forgive"* is embedded in the moral logic of Islam even where it is not a fixed liturgical formula.

**The Reasoning Beneath the Reciprocity**

Why should God's forgiveness be linked to ours? There is a real logic here, not merely a rule. First, a *proportion argument*. What another person owes me is finite and small; what I owe God is unbounded. To demand the small debt while begging the cancellation of the large one is incoherent. The Prophet ﷺ made this the point of the parable of the servant forgiven an immense debt who then seized his fellow servant by the throat over a trivial one — an image found across the prophetic traditions of the monotheistic religions and perfectly consonant with this verse. Second, a *capacity argument*. Forgiveness received must be *recognized* to be received, and a person incapable of forgiving is a person who does not understand what forgiveness is. He cannot value the thing he is asking for. Third, a *practical argument*. Unforgiveness is a state, not an act. A heart that maintains a permanent claim against someone is a heart occupied by the injury, replaying it, keeping it alive. Forgiveness releases the forgiver at least as much as the forgiven. Abū Bakr, by resuming his support of Misṭaḥ, freed himself from carrying a grievance for the rest of his life.

**What Forgiveness Does Not Mean**

The verse should not be flattened into sentimentality, and the passage itself supplies the limits. Misṭaḥ was *punished*. The *ḥadd* of slander was carried out on the participants; justice was done through the proper channel. What Abū Bakr surrendered was not the legal consequence but his *personal* vengeance — the withdrawal of charity as a private penalty. So the verse distinguishes between the claim of justice, which belongs to the law and the community, and the claim of the heart, which belongs to the individual and may be waived. Islam does not ask victims to pretend crimes did not occur or to obstruct their prosecution. It asks them not to run a private, permanent, extra-judicial punishment of their own. Nor does forgiveness require restoring the *same* relationship. The Qur'an says pardon and turn the page; it does not say entrust the same person with the same access. Prudence and forgiveness are compatible.

**The Verse in Ordinary Life**

Family estrangements — the brother not spoken to for a decade, the parent cut off, the cousin removed from the will, the friend whose name is not mentioned — are among the most common and most corrosive features of human life, and they are almost always founded on a genuine grievance. This verse addresses precisely that situation, and it does so with the hardest possible test case: a father, a slandered daughter, and a man who repaid years of charity by helping to destroy her name. If forgiveness was required there, the ordinary quarrels of ordinary families are not exempt. The verse's method is worth imitating when one is stuck in such a state. Do not begin by asking whether the other person deserves forgiveness; they usually do not, which is why it is called forgiveness. Begin with the question the verse asks: *do I want God to forgive me?* Everything else follows from the honest answer.

**The Closing Names**

*Wa'llāhu ghafūrun raḥīm* — Forgiving, Merciful. The same pair that closed verse 5, and for the same reason. The verse asks human beings to forgive, then names God by the attribute it is asking them to imitate. The ethics of Islam are, at bottom, an imitation of divine character within the limits of creaturehood: God covers, so cover; God pardons, so pardon; God gives beyond what is owed, so give.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:23

> **Truly those who accuse chaste and heedless believing women are cursed in this world and the Hereafter, and theirs shall be a great punishment**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Return to Severity**

Verse 22 asked the wronged to forgive. This verse, immediately after, restates the gravity of the crime in the harshest terms the Qur'an uses. The juxtaposition is not a contradiction; it is the sūrah's balance. The *victim* is asked to pardon; the *offence* is not thereby diminished. Forgiveness is a gift the injured may give, not a reassessment of the injury. The severity here exceeds even verse 4. There the penalty was eighty lashes, disqualification, and the label of iniquity. Here it is *laʿnah* — the curse, expulsion from divine mercy — in this world and the next, plus a *great punishment*. The same word used of Iblīs, of those who conceal revelation, and of those who kill believers deliberately.

**Three Descriptions of the Victim**

The verse describes those slandered with three adjectives, and each adds something. *Al-muḥṣanāt* — the chaste, the fortified, as explained at verse 4: women whose known condition is one of moral protection. *Al-ghāfilāt* — the heedless, the unaware. This is the striking one. The root *gh-f-l* means to be inattentive, oblivious, not on guard. The commentators explain it as describing women whose minds are so far from such conduct that the accusation would never have occurred to them; women who are innocent in the deepest sense, not merely abstaining from a temptation they feel but unacquainted with the thought at all. There is a second and complementary sense the commentators note: unaware *of the slander itself*. ʿĀʾishah spent the first weeks of the crisis with no idea what was being said. She noticed only that the Prophet's ﷺ manner toward her had changed during her illness, and could not account for it. The whole city was discussing her, and she alone did not know. That detail makes the crime particularly cruel and defines its essential cowardice. The victim of slander cannot defend herself, because she is not in the room. Backbiting is by definition an attack on the absent. The Qur'an's word for it elsewhere — *ghībah*, from the root meaning *absence* — names the same feature. *Al-muʾmināt* — the believing. The final descriptor adds the bond of faith: the woman attacked is not a stranger but a sister in religion, which recalls the argument of verse 12, that the believers should have thought well "of their own selves."

**Does This Verse Refer Only to ʿĀʾishah?**

The commentators discuss whether the verse is specific or general, and the discussion matters. The immediate reference is unmistakable: the description fits ʿĀʾishah in every particular, and a significant body of scholarly opinion holds that this verse in particular concerns the wives of the Prophet ﷺ, or her specifically, since the curse in both worlds — with no exception clause for repentance attached, unlike verses 4 and 5 — indicates something graver than the general offence. But the wording is a general plural, and the majority position is that the ruling extends to the slander of any chaste believing woman while retaining its greatest intensity in the case that occasioned it. This is standard Qur'anic method: a particular event is legislated in general terms so that the ruling outlives its occasion.

**Why No Exception Clause Here?**

An observant reader will notice that verse 4 was immediately followed by *"save those who repent"* (v. 5), while verse 23 offers no such exception. The commentators discuss this and offer two main resolutions. Some hold that the exception in verse 5 governs the offence generally, so that repentance remains available here too — since the Qur'an's principle that sincere repentance is accepted for all sins short of dying in unbelief is stated with maximum breadth: *"Truly God forgives all sins"* (39:53). Others hold that the absence of the exception here is deliberate, marking the slander of the Prophet's ﷺ wives as a category apart, because it strikes at prophethood itself and not merely at an individual's honor. On this view the specificity of the curse in both worlds and the withholding of the exception clause point in the same direction. Both readings agree on the practical point: this is among the gravest sins a tongue can commit, and its author should regard himself as in extreme danger.

**"Cursed in This World and the Hereafter"**

*Luʿinū fi'l-dunyā wa'l-ākhirah.* The verb is in the past tense — "they *have been* cursed" — which in Qur'anic usage often indicates a settled, accomplished fact rather than a future event. The sentence is already recorded. The worldly portion of the curse is not merely metaphorical. A person known to have destroyed an innocent reputation acquires a reputation of his own, and it follows him. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy, the chief author of the slander, lived out his remaining time in Madinah as a man whose character was publicly known, and the Qur'an itself became the permanent record of his conduct. There is an exact justice in that: he sought to make a woman's name a byword, and instead his own became one. The commentators also connect this to the Prophetic warning already cited:

> *"Whoever pursues the private faults of the Muslims, God will pursue his faults; and whomever God pursues, He exposes, even within his own house."* (Abū Dāwūd 4880)

**Placing This Sin Among the Destroyers**

The classification is made explicit in the hadith already quoted at verse 4, and it bears repeating here because this verse is its scriptural anchor:

> *"Avoid the seven destructive sins."* They said: "O Messenger of God, what are they?" He said: *"Associating partners with God, sorcery, killing a soul which God has forbidden except by right, consuming interest, consuming the property of an orphan, fleeing from the battlefield, and slandering chaste, heedless, believing women."* (al-Bukhārī 2766; Muslim 89)

Note that the Prophet's ﷺ wording uses the exact three adjectives of this verse — *al-muḥṣanāt al-ghāfilāt al-muʾmināt*. The hadith is a direct application of the Qur'anic phrase. And observe the company the sin keeps in that list: idolatry, murder, magic, devouring orphans' wealth. A modern moral intuition would not place "saying something false about someone" in that company. The Qur'an and the Sunnah do, and the reason is the one this passage has been developing throughout: because it destroys what cannot be restored, because it can be done by anyone at any time at no cost, and because it corrodes the trust on which every other good depends.

**Why Women Specifically?**

The verse names women, though — as noted at verse 4 — the ruling covers men as well. The reason for the emphasis is empirical rather than theoretical: then and now, sexual slander is disproportionately directed at women and disproportionately destructive to them. In the society being addressed, a woman's marriageability, her family's standing, her physical safety, and her entire social existence could be destroyed by an unproven allegation from which no defence was possible. That asymmetry has not disappeared. In many communities today a rumor of this kind still ends a woman's prospects while leaving the man involved largely untouched, and the modern technologies of publication have made the reach and permanence of such attacks vastly greater. The Qur'an's response to a real asymmetry of vulnerability is a deliberate asymmetry of protection: the harshest curse in its penal vocabulary, attached to the crime most easily committed against the most easily destroyed.

**The Application**

There is little to add by way of application beyond stating the plain sense of the verse, which is deliberately unqualified. To speak of a chaste woman's supposed sexual misconduct without four eyewitnesses is to place oneself under a curse in both worlds. That includes the insinuation, the joke, the speculation about why she was seen where she was seen, the forwarded message, the anonymous comment, and the "everyone knows about her." The one safe course is the one the sūrah has already scripted at verse 16: *"It is not for us to speak of this."*

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:24

> **on the day their tongues, their hands, and their feet bear witness against them as to that which they used to do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Court in Which There Are No Missing Witnesses**

The whole crisis of the slander turned on the absence of witnesses. Verse 13 asked: *why did they not bring four witnesses?* Nobody could produce them, because the alleged event never happened; and equally, nobody could be prosecuted for starting the rumor, because rumors have no traceable author. In a human court, the case is unwinnable in both directions. This verse describes the court where that problem does not exist. The witnesses will be the defendant's own body parts. *Yawma tashhadu ʿalayhim alsinatuhum wa aydīhim wa arjuluhum bimā kānū yaʿmalūn* — "on the day their tongues, their hands, and their feet bear witness against them concerning what they used to do."

**Why These Three Organs**

The selection is precise and thematically perfect for this passage. *The tongues* — the instrument of the crime being discussed. The very organ that spread the lie becomes the organ that reports it. *The hands* — the classical Qur'anic symbol of action and acquisition; and in the context of slander, the hands are what write, what pass a note, what — in our own era — type and share and forward. *The feet* — the organ of *going*: walking to the gathering where the rumor was told, carrying the story from house to house. The image recalls verse 21's "footsteps of Satan"; the feet that followed those footsteps will testify to where they went. Between them, the three cover the complete anatomy of a rumor's transmission: what was said, what was done, and where it was carried.

**The Testimony of the Limbs Elsewhere in the Qur'an**

The theme appears in several places, each adding a detail.

> *"Until, when they come to it, their hearing, their sight, and their skins bear witness against them concerning that which they used to do. And they will say to their skins: 'Why did you bear witness against us?' They will say: 'God, who gives speech to all things, has given us speech. He created you the first time, and unto Him you are returned.'"* (Qur'an 41:20–21)

That passage is extraordinary. The condemned actually *argue with their own bodies*, and the body answers that it was given speech by the One who gives speech to everything. There is no possibility of appeal, because the witness is not a stranger who might be mistaken or malicious; it is the defendant's own flesh.

> *"This day We shall seal their mouths, and their hands will speak to Us, and their feet will bear witness to that which they used to earn."* (Qur'an 36:65)

Here the sequence is even more striking: the mouth is *sealed* first. The faculty of self-defence, excuse-making, and spin is disabled, and then the body speaks. The commentators note the poetic justice: the tongue, having been used all one's life to construct a favorable version of events, is silenced precisely when a favorable version is most needed. And Muslim (2969) records the Prophet ﷺ describing the scene in a report that has an almost conversational intimacy:

> A man will say: "O Lord, have You not protected me from injustice?" He will say: "Yes indeed." The man will say: "Then I do not permit any witness against myself except from myself." He will say: "Enough is your own self this day as a reckoner against you, and the noble scribes as witnesses." Then his mouth will be sealed, and it will be said to his limbs, "Speak" — and they will speak of his deeds. Then he will be permitted to speak, and he will say to them: "Away with you and ruin! It was for you that I was contending!"

That last line — *"it was for you that I was contending"* — is one of the most poignant sentences in the Hadith corpus. The person spent his life defending, indulging, and serving his body, and it is his body that convicts him.

**Why Evidence Rather Than Simple Judgment?**

A theological question naturally arises. God knows everything; why does He need witnesses at all? Why not simply pronounce the verdict? The commentators give an answer rooted in the nature of divine justice. The purpose of the testimony is not to *inform God* but to *establish the case against the person, before himself and before all creation*, so that the judgment is manifestly just and no one can claim to have been wronged. The Qur'an insists on this repeatedly: the Day of Judgment is a day of *demonstration*, not merely of decision. Books are distributed, deeds are weighed, records are read, witnesses testify. All of it exists so that the outcome is *seen* to be right.

> *"Read thy book! Thine own soul suffices as a reckoner against thee this day."* (Qur'an 17:14)

There is even a mercy in the procedure. A person condemned without explanation might carry an eternal grievance. A person shown the complete, undeniable evidence of his own conduct — in his own voice, from his own limbs — is left with nothing to say. That is why the Qur'an describes the condemned in the Hereafter as *silent* rather than protesting.

**The Body as Recording Device**

There is a way of thinking about this that makes the doctrine less alien to a modern reader. We already accept, without difficulty, that our actions leave traces we do not control: the fingerprint, the DNA sample, the location history, the browser record, the recovered deleted message. Contemporary life has made us intimately familiar with the experience of being convicted by our own devices. A generation ago, the notion that one's own pocket would testify against one in court would have seemed fanciful; today it is routine. The Qur'an describes something structurally similar but far more complete: not devices, but the body itself, retaining a perfect record of what it did, and given voice on the appointed day.

**The Motive Behind the Image: Making the Unseen Vivid**

Why does the sūrah insert this scene here, between the curse of verse 23 and the settlement of verse 25? Because the entire failure diagnosed in this passage was a failure of *weighting* — "you supposed it slight" (v. 15). And weighting is driven by vividness far more than by argument. Human beings are moved by what they can picture. The abstract statement "you will be held accountable for your speech" changes very little behaviour. The image of one's own tongue standing up in court and giving evidence against one — that is a picture that stays. The Qur'an is using narrative concreteness to accomplish what moral instruction alone cannot.

**The Practical Consequence**

The application is a discipline of imagination rather than of rule. Before speaking about another person, picture the tongue that is about to move being questioned about what it is about to say. Before typing, picture the fingers being asked to give an account of what they typed. Before walking into a gathering where you know the conversation will turn to someone's disgrace, picture the feet being asked where they went. This is not morbidity. It is simply an accurate perception of the situation, made vivid — and it is exactly what the sūrah is trying to install in place of the broken scale of verse 15. The Prophet ﷺ was asked what he feared most for his community, and he took hold of his tongue and said:

> *"Restrain this."* (al-Tirmidhī 2410)

The organ he pointed to is the first one named in this verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:25

> **On that Day God will pay them their just due in full, and they will know that God is the Manifest Truth.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Settlement of Accounts**

This verse completes the courtroom scene begun in the previous one. The witnesses have testified; now the judgment is executed. *Yawmaʾidhin yuwaffīhimu'llāhu dīnahumu'l-ḥaqq, wa yaʿlamūna anna'llāha huwa'l-ḥaqqu'l-mubīn.* Every word in it is chosen from the vocabulary of accounting and of truth, and the two vocabularies turn out to be the same.

**Yuwaffī: Payment in Full**

The verb *waffā* means to pay completely, to discharge an obligation without shortfall — the root of *wafāʾ*, fidelity to a promise, and of *awfū*, the command to "give full measure" in commercial dealings, as the Qur'an repeatedly requires:

> *"And give full measure when you measure, and weigh with a straight balance. That is better and fairer in the end."* (Qur'an 17:35)

Its use here means that on that Day nobody receives an approximation. Not a rounded figure, not a rough justice, not a settlement negotiated downward — the exact amount, discharged in full. This cuts in both directions, and the Qur'an intends both. For the wrongdoer it means no discount: the punishment corresponds precisely to what was earned. For the wronged it means no shortfall: every unrecompensed injury, every unavenged humiliation, every whispered lie that destroyed a life and was never traced to its author, is paid. That second implication is the consolation of this verse, and it is aimed directly at ʿĀʾishah and at everyone in her position. In this world, slander is almost never fully redressed. Even after a public vindication — and hers was as complete as any in history, written into scripture — the memory of the accusation lingers somewhere. The verse promises a settlement that is *complete*, and it locates it on a specific day.

**Dīn: The Word That Means Both Religion and Reckoning**

The object of the payment is *dīnahum*. The word *dīn* is usually translated "religion," but its root *d-y-n* carries a cluster of related meanings: debt (*dayn*), judgment, requital, obedience, and the way of life one follows. Here the sense is *requital* — "their due," what is owed to them. The same usage appears in the Fātiḥah: *māliki yawmi'l-dīn*, "Master of the Day of Reckoning." The overlap of "religion" and "debt" in one root is worth pausing over, because it is not accidental. In the Semitic understanding embedded in the language, religion *is* the recognition of an obligation owed — a debt to the Creator that structures one's life. And the Day of *Dīn* is the day the debt is called in. Whoever lived as if he owed nothing discovers on that Day that the accounting was running the whole time.

**Al-Ḥaqq: Truth That Is Also Reality and Also Right**

The verse says they will know that God is *al-ḥaqq al-mubīn* — the Manifest Truth. *Ḥaqq* is one of the richest words in the Qur'an. It means: truth as opposed to falsehood; reality as opposed to illusion; and a right or just claim, as in the *ḥuqūq* owed between people. All three senses operate here, and the commentators note the convergence. *Truth*: on that Day the facts will be known. The month of speculation in Madinah, in which nobody knew what happened, will be answered definitively. *Reality*: God is *the* Real — the one existence that is not derivative, contingent, or borrowed. Everything the slanderers pursued — status, entertainment, the pleasure of an audience, factional advantage — will be seen for what it was: insubstantial, a mirage. And it is no coincidence that fourteen verses later this sūrah will describe the deeds of the disbelievers with exactly that image: *"like a mirage upon a desert plain which a thirsty man supposes is water, till when he comes upon it, he does not find it to be anything, but finds God there"* (v. 39). The parallel is deliberate: the one who chased illusion arrives and finds *al-Ḥaqq*. *Right*: God's judgment is not merely accurate but *just*. Accuracy alone would be a machine; justice requires that the right response be given to the accurate finding.

**Al-Mubīn: The Manifest**

The second name is from the same root *b-y-n* that has run through this whole sūrah — the *āyāt mubayyināt* of verse 1, the *ifk mubīn* of verse 12, the *yubayyinu* of verse 18. The chapter has been insisting throughout on *clarity*, and here the attribute is applied to God Himself. The point is a contrast between the two states. In this world, truth is contested: it must be argued for, evidenced, defended, and is often defeated by a better-told lie. On that Day, truth is *manifest* — self-evident, requiring no advocate, impossible to deny.

**"They Will Know"**

The verb *yaʿlamūn* — "they will know" — is the verse's quiet sting. Throughout this passage, the failure has been described as a failure of knowledge. Verse 15: "you spoke of that whereof you had no knowledge." Verse 19: "God knows, and you know not." The community acted in ignorance and mistook its ignorance for information. Now: *they will know.* The knowledge they lacked arrives — too late to be useful, and no longer optional. It is one of the Qur'an's recurring ironies that the disbelievers and the wrongdoers are always described as *finally knowing*: knowledge is not withheld from them forever, merely until the point at which it can no longer be acted upon.

> *"…and they will know, when they see the punishment, who is further astray from the way."* (Qur'an 25:42)

The lesson is that certainty is coming for everyone. The only question is whether one acquires it now, when it can shape a life, or then, when it can only complete a verdict.

**Why This Verse Is the Foundation of Patience**

There is a practical and psychological point here that deserves emphasis, because it is the reason the Qur'an mentions the Hereafter so relentlessly. Injustice is *survivable* if it is temporary. It is unbearable if it is final. The person who believes that a lie told about him will simply stand forever, unanswered and unbalanced, is a person for whom the only rational responses are despair or revenge. That is the psychology behind most cycles of retaliation in human history: the conviction that if I do not settle this, no one will. The Qur'anic belief in a complete final settlement dissolves that necessity. It is precisely what makes the extraordinary demand of verse 22 — Abū Bakr resuming his charity to the man who slandered his daughter — psychologically possible. He was not being asked to accept that the wrong would go unanswered. He was being asked to leave the answering to God, who pays in full. Forgiveness, in the Islamic framework, is therefore not the denial of justice but the *transfer* of it to a more competent court. This is why the Qur'an can, without contradiction, command pardon in one verse and promise a great punishment three verses later.

**The Verse in Ordinary Life**

Most human beings carry at least one unredressed injustice: a theft never proven, a reputation damaged by someone who was never held to account, credit taken by another, a betrayal that the world never noticed. The natural instinct is either to keep pressing the case forever or to conclude bitterly that nothing matters. This verse offers a third option, and it is a discipline rather than a feeling: *file the case and stop carrying it*. The evidence is preserved better than you could preserve it — by tongues, hands, and feet. The settlement is guaranteed to be complete. And the Judge is *al-Ḥaqq al-Mubīn*, who requires no persuading and cannot be misled. The believer who genuinely holds this can put down a grievance he has carried for years, not because it was small, but because it has been transferred to someone who will handle it properly.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:26

> **Vile women are for vile men, and vile men are for vile women, and good women are for good men, and good men are for good women. They are innocent of what they say. Theirs is forgiveness and a generous provision.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Seal on the Whole Passage**

This verse closes the sixteen-verse account of the slander, and it does so not with a further rebuke but with a *principle* — a statement about how God has ordered the moral universe. The argument it makes is elegant, and it is the argument that should have prevented the entire crisis. *Al-khabīthātu li'l-khabīthīn, wa'l-khabīthūna li'l-khabīthāt, wa'l-ṭayyibātu li'l-ṭayyibīn, wa'l-ṭayyibūna li'l-ṭayyibāt.* The Arabic is built as a chiasmus — a mirrored structure, ABBA — in which each pairing is stated from both directions. The symmetry is itself part of the meaning: whatever the principle is, it applies identically to men and to women.

**Khabīth and Ṭayyib: The Vocabulary of the Wholesome and the Foul**

The two key words come from the Qur'an's basic vocabulary of purity, and both are drawn from the world of food, water, and soil rather than from abstract ethics. *Ṭayyib* means good, wholesome, pure, pleasant — the word used for lawful food (*ḥalālan ṭayyiban*, 2:168), for clean earth used in dry ablution (*ṣaʿīdan ṭayyiban*, 4:43), for a pleasant scent, for good land that yields its crop. Its core sense is *that which is sound, agreeable, and life-giving*. *Khabīth* is its exact opposite: foul, corrupt, rotten, noxious — used for bad wealth (2:267), for the foul tree with no roots (14:26), for impure food. Its core sense is *that which is spoiled and harmful*. The Qur'an sets them against each other as a fundamental division:

> *"Say: The foul and the wholesome are not equal, though the abundance of the foul may impress thee. So reverence God, O possessors of intellect, that haply you may prosper."* (Qur'an 5:100)

And it uses the same pair for the two kinds of soil, in an image that illuminates this verse:

> *"And the good land brings forth its vegetation by the leave of its Lord, while that which is bad brings forth naught but scantly. Thus do We vary the signs for a people who give thanks."* (Qur'an 7:58)

**What the Verse Actually Claims: Three Readings**

The commentators offer three main interpretations, and they are complementary rather than competing. *Reading one: words.* The feminine plurals *al-khabīthāt* and *al-ṭayyibāt* can refer to *statements* rather than persons — foul words belong to foul people, wholesome words to wholesome people. On this reading the verse is a direct comment on the slander: the filthy accusation revealed the character of its authors, not of its target. Al-Ṭabarī records this interpretation, and it has real force: what comes out of a person's mouth is evidence about the person speaking, not about the person spoken of. This is a psychological insight of permanent value. The corrupt imagination assumes corruption everywhere, because it can only project what it contains. A person who instantly believes the worst about a woman travelling with a man is telling you what he himself would have done. *Reading two: affinity and pairing.* The more common interpretation takes the words as referring to persons: corrupt people are suited to and drawn to corrupt people, and wholesome people to wholesome. This is verse 3 of this sūrah stated as a general law, and it is the reading that makes the verse an argument for ʿĀʾishah's innocence. The reasoning runs thus: the Prophet ﷺ is the best of creation, unquestionably *ṭayyib*. God does not pair the pure with the corrupt. Therefore the accusation is impossible on principle, before any evidence is examined. This was precisely the argument the believers should have made — and it is what verse 16 gestured at with *subḥānaka*, "Glory be to Thee," the declaration that God is far above allowing such a thing in the household of His Messenger. Ibn Kathīr and others develop this into a general point about the prophets: God protected the households of His messengers from this particular corruption. The wives of Noah and Lot were disbelievers — the Qur'an says so plainly at 66:10 — but the tradition is emphatic that they were not unchaste. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have said, regarding the phrase *"they betrayed them"* in that verse, that the betrayal was never in matters of sexual immorality. *Reading three: deserving.* Some read the preposition *li-* as indicating desert rather than attraction: the foul *deserve* the foul, and the wholesome *deserve* the wholesome — whether as spouses in this world or as recompense in the next.

**Is This a Law of Nature or a Statement of Norms?**

An honest reading must acknowledge a complication. Good people are sometimes married to bad ones. The Qur'an itself supplies the examples: Pharaoh's wife Āsiyah, whom the Book presents as a model for all believers (66:11), was married to the worst tyrant in its pages; Noah and Lot had disbelieving wives. So the verse cannot be an exceptionless empirical law about marriage. How then should it be read? Several considerations resolve this. First, if the verse concerns *sexual* purity specifically — which is the subject of the entire passage — the exceptions disappear. The wives of Noah and Lot were unbelievers, not adulteresses. Āsiyah's marriage to a tyrant was not a case of moral contamination in this domain. Second, the verse describes a *tendency and an affinity* rather than a mechanical rule. As a general matter, people gravitate toward those who share their values and tolerate their behaviour, for the reasons discussed at verse 3. Third, and most importantly, the verse states a *norm and an aspiration*: this is how things ought to be, and how they ultimately will be in the final settlement. In the Hereafter, the pairing is exact and without exception — the wholesome with the wholesome, in a garden from which everything *khabīth* has been excluded.

**"They Are Innocent of What They Say"**

*Ulāʾika mubarraʾūna mimmā yaqūlūn.* The word *mubarraʾūn* is from the root *b-r-ʾ*, meaning to be free, clear, quit of something — the root of *barāʾah*, exoneration, and of the medical sense of recovering from an illness. The grammar is worth noting: it is a passive participle. They are *declared innocent* — not merely innocent in fact, but formally exonerated, by God, in a text that will be recited until the end of the world. The commentators differ over whether *ulāʾika* refers to ʿĀʾishah and Ṣafwān specifically, to the Prophet's ﷺ household, or to all the wholesome. The plural is general, and the generality is a mercy: every wrongly accused believer has a share in this declaration. There is also, for the wronged, an important lesson in the *source* of the exoneration. ʿĀʾishah did not clear her own name; she was unable to. Her father could not clear it. The Prophet ﷺ himself did not clear it. Her defence, when finally called upon, was to quote Jacob: *"Beautiful patience; and God is the One whose help is sought against that which you describe."* And then God cleared her, permanently and publicly, in a way no human vindication could match. The instruction for anyone falsely accused is contained in that sequence. Do what is proper — state the truth once, calmly. Then leave the vindication to the One who can actually accomplish it.

**"Forgiveness and a Generous Provision"**

The verse ends with the reward: *lahum maghfiratun wa rizqun karīm.* The pairing is instructive and slightly surprising. Why *forgiveness* for the innocent? If they are exonerated, what is there to forgive? Several answers are given. That no human being, however wronged in one matter, is free of sin in others — so the exoneration in this case is accompanied by a general pardon. That the trial itself, patiently endured, is a cause of expiation, as the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"No fatigue, illness, anxiety, sorrow, harm or distress befalls a Muslim, not even a thorn that pricks him, but that God expiates by it some of his sins."* (al-Bukhārī 5641; Muslim 2573)

And that the mention of forgiveness here quietly acknowledges the very human reactions of the wronged during the ordeal — the weeping, the anger, ʿĀʾishah's sharp refusal to rise and thank the Prophet ﷺ. God does not require the wronged to be flawless in their suffering. *Rizq karīm* — "a generous provision" — is a Qur'anic phrase for the sustenance of Paradise: not merely adequate but *noble*, given lavishly and honorably. The word *karīm* implies giving that honors the recipient.

**The Whole Passage in Retrospect**

Looking back over verses 11 to 26, the structure is now visible as a complete moral education. It began with the event and the assurance that it was good for them (v. 11). It moved to the community's failures of charity (v. 12) and of evidence (v. 13). It described the mercy that spared them (v. 14) and dissected the psychology of transmission (v. 15). It scripted the correct response (v. 16), prohibited recurrence (v. 17), and named the Legislator (v. 18). It reached into the heart to condemn the love of scandal (v. 19), invoked mercy again (v. 20), exposed the incremental method of Satan and the total dependence of every soul on divine purification (v. 21), and then — astonishingly — turned to the victims and asked them to forgive (v. 22). It restated the gravity of the crime (v. 23), pictured the court in which no crime is untraceable (v. 24), promised full settlement (v. 25), and now closes with the principle that should have made the whole affair impossible (v. 26). Nothing in that sequence is decorative. It is a complete treatment of a single social disease: its symptoms, its mechanism, its treatment, its prevention, and its final adjudication.

**Application: The Company One Becomes**

Two practical inheritances from this verse. For *choosing*: the verse is a criterion. Whom you marry, whom you befriend, whose company you seek — these are not neutral preferences but statements about what you are and predictions about what you will become. And the reverse inference holds: if you want to know what you are, look honestly at whose company you find comfortable. For *judging*: when someone speaks filth about another, the verse redirects the evidential inference. The words are evidence about the speaker. That single reframing, if genuinely internalized, would end most gossip — because the gossiper's unconscious assumption is always that he is reporting on someone else, when in fact he is reporting on himself.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:27

> **O you who believe! Enter not houses other than your own, until you inquire and greet the dwellers thereof. That is better for you, that haply you may remember.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Diagnosis to Prevention**

The sūrah now performs one of its most instructive moves. Having spent sixteen verses on the anatomy of a scandal, it turns — without transition — to *doorways*. This looks like an abrupt change of subject. It is not. It is the shift from treatment to prevention. The slander began, at bottom, because a private moment became public property.
