The Qur'an's response is to build a wall around private life before anything can escape it: rules for entering houses, rules for looking, rules for dress, rules for marriage. The scandal was the disease; these verses are the sanitation system. The connection was not lost on the classical commentators, who read verses 27 to 33 as the practical implementation of the lessons of verses 11 to 26.

**"Enter Not Houses Other Than Your Own"**

*Lā tadkhulū buyūtan ghayra buyūtikum ḥattā tastaʾnisū wa tusallimū ʿalā ahlihā.* The prohibition is absolute in form: do not enter *at all* until two conditions are met. And the phrase *ghayra buyūtikum* — "other than your own houses" — establishes the fundamental category: there is a domain that belongs to a person, and everyone else stands outside it until admitted. The commentators note that the ruling covers all dwellings, including those of close relatives and friends. It is not a rule about strangers. Al-Qurṭubī records the report of a man who asked the Prophet ﷺ whether he should seek permission to enter upon his own mother, and was told yes. The man said: "But there is no one in the house but her." He said: *"Would you like to see her naked?"* The man said no. He said: *"Then seek permission."* Another well-known report has the Prophet ﷺ explaining the underlying rationale:

> *"Permission is sought only because of the gaze."* (al-Bukhārī 6241; Muslim 2156)

That single sentence is the key to the entire section, and it links the door directly to the eye — which is exactly what verse 30 will legislate next.

**Tastaʾnisū: The Beautiful Verb of Asking Permission**

The most remarkable feature of this verse is its choice of verb. One would expect *tastaʾdhinū* — "until you ask permission," from *idhn*, permission. That is the ordinary legal term, and it is used in the very next verse. Instead the Qur'an says *ḥattā tastaʾnisū*, from the root *ʾ-n-s*, which gives *uns* (intimacy, familiarity, warmth of company), *insān* (the human being, so named by many philologists because he is a creature made for companionship), and *anīs* (a friendly companion). *Istiʾnās* therefore means something like: *to seek familiarity, to make oneself known in a friendly way, to establish a warm acquaintance before entering.* The implications are lovely and precise. Asking permission is not to be a bureaucratic transaction — a knock and a demand — but a *gesture of friendliness*. You announce yourself in a way that puts the household at ease, that lets them know who is at the door and that the visitor is a friend. Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to have understood the word in exactly this sense, and some early recitations and readings glossed it as *tastaʾdhinū* to make the legal content plain, while preserving the warmth of the original. The verse thus regulates not merely *whether* one enters but *how one approaches* — and the required manner is gentleness. A visitor who bangs on the door and shouts has complied with the letter and violated the word the Qur'an actually used.

**"And Greet the Dwellers Thereof"**

The second condition is the greeting: *wa tusallimū ʿalā ahlihā*. The Islamic greeting is *al-salāmu ʿalaykum* — "peace be upon you." It is not a neutral hello; it is a declaration of non-hostility and a prayer for the other's wellbeing. Delivered at a threshold, it is exactly the right utterance: the person outside announces that he comes in peace before he is admitted. The Prophet ﷺ elevated this greeting into a foundation of communal life:

> *"You shall not enter Paradise until you believe, and you shall not believe until you love one another. Shall I not tell you of something which, if you do it, you will love one another? Spread the greeting of peace among yourselves."* (Muslim 54)

The sequence in that hadith is worth noticing: Paradise depends on faith, faith on mutual love, and mutual love on the practice of greeting. A small habitual courtesy is placed at the base of the structure.

**The Prophetic Procedure**

The Sunnah supplies the operational detail, and it is unusually specific. Permission is sought *three times*. Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī reported the Prophet ﷺ saying:

> *"Permission is to be sought three times. If you are given permission, enter; otherwise, go back."* (al-Bukhārī 6245; Muslim 2153)

The visitor does not stand directly in front of the door but to one side, so that the door opening does not expose the interior. The reports describe the Prophet ﷺ standing to the right or left of a doorway and saying *al-salāmu ʿalaykum* rather than facing it directly. And the visitor identifies himself clearly. Jābir reported that he came to the Prophet's ﷺ door and knocked. The Prophet ﷺ said: "Who is it?" Jābir said: "It is I." The Prophet ﷺ said, repeating it with evident disapproval: *"I, I!"* (al-Bukhārī 6250; Muslim 2155). Anonymity at the door is a small discourtesy that the Prophet ﷺ corrected explicitly.

**Why the Home Is Protected**

The reasoning behind this legislation runs deep, and it is worth setting out because the modern world has largely dismantled what it protects. *The home is where a person is unguarded.* Everywhere else, human beings perform: they dress, compose their faces, watch their words. The home is the one place where that effort is set down — where a person may be dishevelled, unwell, irritable, praying, weeping, or simply resting. That release is not a luxury; it is a psychological necessity. A creature that can never stop performing eventually breaks. *Unannounced entry destroys that release retroactively.* If a household can be entered at any moment, then it can never fully relax, even when no one is there. The value of the protection lies in its reliability. *The home is where the family is formed.* Marriage, child-rearing, the transmission of religion and character — all of it happens in a space whose privacy is the precondition of its intimacy. *And privacy protects the vulnerable.* The one caught unprepared is not usually the head of the household; it is the woman changing, the elderly relative being washed, the child being disciplined, the man who cannot afford furniture.

**"That Is Better For You"**

*Dhālikum khayrun lakum.* The Qur'an does not merely command; it argues. And note the direction of the benefit: better *for you* — the visitor — not only for the household. The commentators explain this in several ways, all of them true. The visitor is spared seeing what he should not see, and therefore spared the sin of the gaze and the burden of knowledge he cannot unlearn. He is spared the embarrassment of an unwelcome entry. He preserves the relationship, since nothing damages a friendship faster than an intrusion. And he receives the reward of obedience. There is a general principle here about Islamic etiquette that is easy to miss. The rules of courtesy in the Sharīʿah are not one-sided burdens imposed for the benefit of others; they are usually *mutually* protective. The one who knocks is protected as much as the one who opens.

**"That Haply You May Remember"**

The verse closes with *laʿallakum tadhakkarūn* — the same phrase that closed verse 1 of this sūrah. The frame is deliberate: the chapter opened by saying its signs were revealed for remembrance, and now, beginning its practical etiquette, it says the same. And again the verb is *remember*, not *learn*. The dignity of the threshold is something human beings already know. Every culture on earth has some version of it. Revelation is restoring an instinct, not imposing an alien rule.

**The Modern Threshold**

The most useful thing a contemporary reader can do with this verse is to ask what counts as a door now. The physical door still exists, and the rule still applies: relatives who walk in unannounced, adult children entering parents' rooms, parents entering adolescents' rooms, spouses reading each other's private correspondence, in-laws arriving without warning. All of it falls squarely under this verse. But the *functional* thresholds of modern life have multiplied. A phone is a house — it contains correspondence, photographs, finances, private thoughts. A message inbox is a house. A closed browser is a house. An unpublished document is a house. A person's medical information is a house. The principle transfers exactly: *do not enter until you have made yourself known and been admitted.* Picking up another adult's phone to look through it is, in the Qur'anic scheme, entering a house without permission — and the Prophet's ﷺ explanation applies precisely: *"Permission is sought only because of the gaze."* There is one further modern application worth stating. The verse's requirement to announce oneself *warmly* rather than merely legally has an obvious extension: a message that begins abruptly with a demand, a call placed at an unreasonable hour, an unannounced video call that catches someone unprepared — these violate the spirit of *istiʾnās* even where no door is involved. The Qur'an asked for warmth at the threshold, and thresholds are now everywhere.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:28

> **But if you find not anyone therein, then enter them not until permission is granted you. And if it is said to you, "Turn back," then turn back. That is purer for you, and God knows what you do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Closing the Loopholes**

Having established the rule, the Qur'an now addresses the two ways a determined person might get around it: entering when nobody is there to object, and entering after being refused. Both are situations in which the *letter* of verse 27 might be thought satisfied — no one refused permission, because no one was asked — while its purpose is entirely defeated. The precision of the legislation here is a model of how law must anticipate evasion.

**Case One: The Empty House**

*Fa-in lam tajidū fīhā aḥadan fa-lā tadkhulūhā ḥattā yuʾdhana lakum* — "if you find no one in them, then do not enter until permission is given you." This closes the most obvious gap. A visitor knocks; there is no answer; he reasons that since no one is inside, there is no privacy to violate, and lets himself in to wait. The Qur'an forbids it, and the reasoning is important: *the right of privacy belongs to the house and its owner, not merely to whoever happens to be standing in it.* A home is not a public space that becomes private only when occupied. It is a protected domain at all times, because it contains the owner's possessions, his affairs, and — crucially — the possibility that someone is inside who cannot or does not wish to answer. That last point deserves emphasis. "No answer" does not mean "no one there." Someone may be asleep, unwell, praying, unable to reach the door, or simply unwilling to receive visitors at that moment. The absence of a response is not consent; it is the absence of consent. Islamic law, in this ruling, adopts precisely the standard that modern ethics has taken a long time to articulate: silence is not permission.

**Case Two: Being Told to Leave**

*Wa in qīla lakumu'rjiʿū fa'rjiʿū* — "and if it is said to you, 'Go back,' then go back." The Arabic is brutally economical: the command to the householder's visitor is the same word the householder used. *Irjiʿū* — go back. *Fa'rjiʿū* — so go back. There is no negotiation in the grammar. And there is no exception for the offended. The verse does not add "unless your business is urgent," or "unless you have come a long way," or "unless you are a close relative," or "unless you find the refusal rude." Turn back. This is harder than it sounds, and the Qur'an knows it, which is why it adds the reassurance that follows. Being turned away at a door is a small humiliation, and the natural human response is to argue, to ask why, to press, or to leave in visible offence — all of which are ways of refusing to accept the refusal. The Prophetic procedure discussed at verse 27 anticipates this: three attempts, and then departure. Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī was famously questioned by ʿUmar when he came to ʿUmar's door, greeted three times, received no answer, and left. ʿUmar later asked him why he had gone; he cited the Prophet's ﷺ instruction, and the matter was resolved when other Companions confirmed the hadith (al-Bukhārī 6245; Muslim 2153). The episode is instructive on two counts: the rule was followed exactly even by a senior Companion at the door of the Caliph, and the Caliph accepted it once the evidence was produced.

**"That Is Purer For You"**

*Huwa azkā lakum.* The comparative *azkā* is from *z-k-w* — the root of purity-and-growth met at verse 21, and the root of *zakāh*. Verse 27 said the etiquette was *khayr*, "better." This verse says it is *azkā*, "purer." The escalation is deliberate, and the shift in vocabulary marks a shift in what is at stake. Complying with the rule when someone opens the door is a matter of *goodness* — courtesy, benefit, social oil. Complying when nobody is watching, or when you have been rebuffed and are stung by it, is a matter of *purity*. It touches the heart directly, because there is no external pressure enforcing it. And note the growth-sense of the root. Accepting a refusal gracefully does not merely avoid a sin; it *develops* something in the person — the capacity to have one's will thwarted without resentment, which is among the most valuable and least common of adult virtues.

**"And God Knows What You Do"**

The closing clause, *wa'llāhu bimā taʿmalūna ʿalīm*, is placed exactly where it is needed. Both scenarios in this verse are unobserved. The empty house has no witness. The refusal at the door is heard by two people, and the visitor's inner reaction — the resentment, the calculation of how to get in anyway, the grudge stored for later — is known to one. So the verse names the Witness. And note that it does not say "God sees" but "God *knows what you do*." Knowledge of the deed includes knowledge of the intention behind it. A man may turn back from a door in outward compliance while cataloguing the insult; God knows which of the two he did. This is the recurring architecture of Qur'anic ethics: a rule, a reason, and then the name of the One before whom the rule is kept. Islamic law is enforceable in a court for perhaps a tenth of its content; the rest is enforced by the awareness this clause installs. A society that maintains that awareness needs remarkably little policing, and a society that loses it cannot be policed at all.

**Why Refusal Must Be Respected**

There is a deeper social logic worth drawing out. The right to refuse entry is what makes the home a home. A door that can be opened by anyone with sufficient persistence, status, or claim of relationship is not a door. So the enforceability of "no" is not a detail of the etiquette; it *is* the etiquette. Everything in verse 27 depends on it. And the same principle scales upward and downward. It applies to a person declining an invitation, declining to discuss a subject, declining to lend money, declining a marriage proposal. In each case a refusal that is met with pressure, guilt, or repeated approach has been effectively nullified, and the person's autonomy along with it. Islamic manners take this seriously. The Prophet ﷺ warned against the general disposition of imposing on people, and the etiquette of the guest is regulated as carefully as that of the host — a guest is not to overstay, not to burden, and, as this verse teaches, not to insist.

**The Dignity of the One Who Refuses**

One further observation, which is easy to overlook and matters greatly in practice. The verse imposes no obligation on the householder to *explain*. He is not required to justify his refusal, produce an excuse, or apologize. He may simply say *"go back."* This is a considerable protection. In real life, the reasons a person cannot receive a visitor are often things he would rather not disclose: he is ill, he is in financial difficulty, the house is in disorder, there is a family dispute, he is exhausted. Requiring an explanation would strip away the very privacy the rule exists to protect. So the burden is placed entirely on the visitor: accept the refusal, ask nothing, assume nothing, and leave without offence. And the sūrah has already supplied the interpretive posture that makes this possible — *ḥusn al-ẓann*, thinking well of one's brother (v. 12). The believer who is turned away at a door and does not construct a grievance out of it is applying, in a small and daily way, exactly the discipline that this chapter spent sixteen verses teaching.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:29

> **There is no blame upon you for entering uninhabited houses wherein there is some good for you. And God knows what you disclose and what you conceal.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Exemption That Proves the Rule's Purpose**

Two verses have established a strict regime around entry into homes. This verse carves out an exception, and the exception is as instructive as the rule, because it reveals exactly what the rule was protecting. *Laysa ʿalaykum junāḥun an tadkhulū buyūtan ghayra maskūnatin fīhā matāʿun lakum.*

**"Uninhabited Houses"**

The phrase *buyūtan ghayra maskūnah* means buildings that are not *dwellings* — not inhabited as private residences. The word *maskūnah* is from *s-k-n*, the root of *sakan* (a place of rest and repose), *sakīnah* (tranquillity), and *sākin* (a resident). A *maskan* is not merely a structure but a place where a person *settles and is at rest*. That etymology settles the interpretation immediately. What the previous verses protected was not architecture but *repose*. The right belongs to the place where a human being lets down his guard. A building in which nobody is at rest has no such right, because there is nothing to protect. The commentators identified various categories: inns and caravanserais where travellers stop; shops and market stalls open for trade; public baths; ruins and empty structures; storehouses; and any building erected for general use rather than private habitation. Some early authorities extended it to any place licensed for public entry.

**"Wherein There Is Some Good For You"**

The second condition is *fīhā matāʿun lakum* — "in which there is some benefit, enjoyment, or goods belonging to you." *Matāʿ* means goods, provisions, enjoyment, or benefit. The condition is therefore that there is a *legitimate purpose* for the entry. The exemption is not a licence to wander into any empty structure out of curiosity; it applies where one has business there — goods stored, a service to obtain, shelter to take, trade to conduct. The two conditions together produce a workable public/private distinction. A place is open to entry when it is not someone's repose *and* when one has a reason to be there. Islamic law thereby recognizes a category of quasi-public space and regulates it lightly, while defending the private domain absolutely.

**Why This Verse Was Necessary**

Without it, verse 27 read literally would have paralyzed ordinary commercial and social life. Every shopkeeper would need to be asked permission by every customer at the threshold; every traveller would be unable to enter an inn; every market would grind to a halt. This illustrates a general characteristic of Islamic legislation that is worth naming: the Sharīʿah legislates rules and then immediately legislates their *scope*. The Qur'an does not issue absolute-sounding commands and leave the community to work out the exceptions by guesswork. Here the exception arrives two verses after the rule, in the same passage, from the same source. The underlying principle is stated by the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"Religion is ease, and no one makes the religion difficult except that it overwhelms him. So aim for what is right, draw near, rejoice, and seek help in the morning, the evening, and something of the night."* (al-Bukhārī 39)

And by the Qur'an:

> *"God desires ease for you, and He does not desire hardship for you."* (Qur'an 2:185)

**Junāḥ: The Language of Non-Liability**

The word for "blame" is *junāḥ*, from a root meaning to *incline* or *lean* — the same root as *janāḥ*, a wing. A *junāḥ* is a leaning away from the straight, hence a fault or sin. The Qur'an uses the formula *laysa ʿalaykum junāḥ* ("there is no blame upon you") in a number of places to lift a presumed restriction — regarding shortening prayer while travelling (4:101), regarding certain matters of marriage and dower (2:236), regarding eating in the houses of relatives (24:61, later in this sūrah). The formula's function is psychological as much as legal. It addresses people who might otherwise be *scrupulous to the point of paralysis* — the sort of conscientious believer who, having heard verse 27, would hesitate at the door of a shop. The Qur'an speaks to that anxiety directly and dissolves it. Excessive scrupulosity is a real spiritual disease, known in the tradition as *waswasah*, and the Prophet ﷺ treated it as such. Religion that generates paralysis has malfunctioned.

**"God Knows What You Disclose and What You Conceal"**

The closing clause — *wa'llāhu yaʿlamu mā tubdūna wa mā taktumūn* — is the verse's real weight, and its placement immediately after a *relaxation* of the rules is deliberate and psychologically shrewd. Exemptions are the natural home of self-deception. The moment a rule acquires an exception, the human mind begins working to place its own conduct inside the exception. A man who wishes to enter somewhere will discover, on inspection, that the building is arguably not a dwelling, and that he arguably has business there. The exception becomes a doorway wider than the rule. The verse pre-empts this. *God knows what you disclose* — the stated reason, the plausible justification, the business you claim to have. *And what you conceal* — the actual motive. The pairing is exact: for every stated purpose there is a real purpose, and God has access to both. Nothing more needs to be said, and the verse says nothing more.

**The General Lesson: Rules Without Sincerity Are Loopholes**

This verse thus completes a small but important teaching about the nature of law in Islam. Law is necessary: it establishes clear categories, protects rights, and coordinates a society. But law alone can always be gamed by anyone willing to comply with words while defeating purposes. Every legal system in history has generated a profession devoted to precisely that skill. Islamic jurisprudence names the abuse: *ḥiyal*, legal stratagems — formally valid manoeuvres that achieve a forbidden end. Ibn Taymiyyah and Ibn al-Qayyim wrote at length against them, and Ibn al-Qayyim's treatment of the subject in *Iʿlām al-Muwaqqiʿīn* is among the most sustained critiques of legalism in religious literature. Their argument is exactly the argument of this verse: God legislated for purposes, and He knows whether the purpose or the form is being served. The Qur'an's method, therefore, is to pair every legal rule with an awareness of divine observation. The rule handles the outward act; the awareness handles the intention. Neither works alone.

**Practical Reach**

The direct application of this verse is small — it tells a Muslim he may walk into a shop. Its extended application is not small at all. Ask, of any conduct that technically falls within an allowance: *what would I say if my actual motive were displayed alongside my stated one?* The verse guarantees that both are already displayed to the only audience that finally matters. That question, honestly applied, is more effective at governing behaviour than any amount of rule-refinement — which is precisely why the Qur'an ends a technical exemption about buildings with a statement about the contents of the human chest.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:30

> **Tell the believing men to lower their eyes and guard their private parts. That is purer for them. Surely God is Aware of whatsoever they do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Door Within**

Verses 27 to 29 built a wall around the house. This verse builds one around the eye — and the order is not accidental. The Prophet's ﷺ explanation of the previous section was that *"permission is sought only because of the gaze"* (al-Bukhārī 6241; Muslim 2156). Having regulated the physical threshold, the Qur'an now regulates the threshold that travels with a person everywhere.

**Men Are Addressed First**

It is worth noting immediately, because the point is so often reversed in popular discussion, that the Qur'an addresses *men* first. The command to lower the gaze comes to the believing men in verse 30; only in verse 31 is the parallel instruction given to women, along with the additional rules of dress. The ordering carries an argument. The primary responsibility for the discipline of male desire is placed on men. Before a single word is said about what women should wear, men are told what to do with their eyes — and the responsibility is not made conditional on how anyone else behaves. The verse does not say "lower your eyes if women are immodestly dressed." It is unconditional. This forecloses the oldest excuse in the world. A man who says his gaze is the fault of what he saw has been answered before he speaks.

**"Tell the Believing Men": The Command Through the Messenger**

*Qul li'l-muʾminīn* — "Say to the believing men." The instruction is routed through the Prophet ﷺ, which the commentators note gives it the weight of a direct prophetic delivery. And the address is to *believers* specifically: this is presented as an implication of faith, in the same manner as the "if you are believers" of verses 2 and 17.

**Yaghuḍḍū: The Verb of Lowering, Not Closing**

The key phrase is *yaghuḍḍū min abṣārihim*, and both of its elements repay attention. The verb *ghaḍḍa* does not mean to shut, blind, or avert entirely. It means to *lower*, to *reduce*, to *diminish the intensity of* something. It is used in Arabic for lowering the voice — as the Qur'an says of Luqmān's advice:

> *"And be modest in thy bearing, and lower thy voice (wa'ghḍuḍ min ṣawtik). Truly the ugliest of voices is the voice of asses."* (Qur'an 31:19)

Just as lowering the voice does not mean silence, lowering the gaze does not mean blindness. It means moderating, restraining, taking the intensity out of a look. The partitive *min* — "*of* their eyes" — reinforces this. The command is to lower *some of* the gaze, not all of it. The commentators derive from this precisely that the prohibition targets a particular kind of looking, not vision as such. A Muslim man is not required to walk with his eyes on the pavement. He is required not to *stare*, not to look with desire, not to pursue with the eye what he may not pursue with anything else. The distinction was drawn explicitly by the Prophet ﷺ, who said to ʿAlī:

> *"O ʿAlī, do not follow a glance with another glance, for the first is yours but the second is not."* (Abū Dāwūd 2149; al-Tirmidhī 2777)

That is the operative rule, and it is a humane one. The involuntary first sight — the person who walks into your field of vision — is not a sin, because it was not chosen. What is legislated is the *second look*: the deliberate return, the lingering, the decision to keep looking. Jarīr ibn ʿAbd Allāh reported: *"I asked the Messenger of God ﷺ about the sudden glance, and he ordered me to turn my gaze away."* (Muslim 2159)

**"And Guard Their Private Parts"**

*Wa yaḥfaẓū furūjahum.* The verb *ḥafiẓa* means to guard, protect, preserve — the same verb used for memorizing the Qur'an (*ḥifẓ*) and for God's preservation of the Book. The pairing of the eye and the private parts in a single command establishes the causal chain the entire sūrah is built on. The eye is not an isolated organ; it is the *front gate*. What the eye consumes, the heart desires, and what the heart desires, the body pursues. The classical scholars described this sequence with precision. Ibn al-Qayyim, in a much-quoted analysis, traced it: a glance produces a thought; a thought produces an idea; an idea produces a desire; a desire produces a will; a will strengthens into a resolve; and the resolve produces the act. His conclusion follows: it is easier to repel the glance than to undo the act, and the whole chain can be broken at its first and weakest link. This is the practical wisdom of the Qur'anic method encountered at verse 21 — the *footsteps* of Satan. Nobody arrives at the destination in one step. Legislate the first step, and the rest never occurs. The Prophet ﷺ named the same chain:

> *"The share of adultery has been written for every son of Adam, which he will inevitably attain: the adultery of the eyes is looking, the adultery of the tongue is speech, the adultery of the ears is listening, the adultery of the hands is grasping, the adultery of the feet is walking. The soul desires and wishes, and the private parts confirm that or deny it."* (al-Bukhārī 6243; Muslim 2657)

The hadith describes an escalating sequence through the senses, ending at the point where the whole thing is either consummated or refused.

**"That Is Purer For Them"**

*Dhālika azkā lahum.* Again the comparative *azkā* — purer and more growth-producing — as at verse 28. The Qur'an is careful, here as elsewhere, to supply the reason and to place the benefit with the person being commanded. The gaze is not restrained to protect women from being looked at, though that is a real benefit; it is restrained because it makes *the man himself* purer and enables *his own* growth. That framing matters, because it reverses the way this command is usually experienced. Presented as a restriction imposed for the sake of others, it feels like a tax. Presented as a discipline that purifies and develops the one practising it, it is recognizable as what it actually is: training.

**The Reasoning: Why the Gaze Must Be Governed**

Several arguments converge, and they are worth stating plainly because this is the point at which modern readers most often part company with the text. *The economics of desire.* Appetite is not diminished by feeding it; it is enlarged. This is one of the most reliable observations about human psychology, confirmed by everything known about habituation: stimulus that once satisfied ceases to satisfy, and escalation follows. A man who governs his eye is not depriving himself of pleasure; he is preventing the inflation that destroys pleasure. A man who does not is on an escalator whose direction he did not choose and cannot easily reverse. *The protection of the marriage.* Comparison is the acid that dissolves contentment. A man whose eyes are unrestrained spends his life measuring his wife against an endless procession of others, most of them presented in idealized form. No real person survives that comparison, and no marriage does either. The discipline of the gaze is, in practical terms, one of the most effective marital protections ever legislated. *The dignity of persons.* A look of desire converts a person into an object of consumption. Whatever else may be said about it, the person on the receiving end is not being seen as a person. The command to lower the gaze is, from the other side, a right: the right to move through the world without being appraised. *The integrity of attention.* Attention is finite, and it is the medium of every serious human activity — prayer, study, work, conversation, love. A man whose attention is repeatedly captured by whatever passes in front of him has, in a real sense, lost ownership of his own mind.

**The Twenty-First Century Application**

It must be said directly that this verse now governs a situation immeasurably more demanding than the one it was revealed into. In seventh-century Madinah, the gaze was limited to what was physically present: a street, a market, a gathering. Today the eye has access, at any moment, to a functionally infinite supply of engineered imagery, curated for maximum stimulation, available in complete privacy and at no cost. Nothing in the history of the species has resembled this. The command has not changed, and it is more necessary than it has ever been. But the mechanics of obeying it have changed entirely. In Madinah the discipline was exercised in the moment of the second glance. Today the decisive discipline is exercised *before* the moment — in the arrangement of one's devices, one's habits, one's hours, and one's environment. Anyone who relies on in-the-moment willpower against a system designed by specialists to defeat in-the-moment willpower will lose, and will keep losing. The Qur'anic principle of *khuṭuwāt* — footsteps — supplies the strategy. Move the point of resistance earlier: to the installed filter, the phone left outside the bedroom, the account unfollowed, the hour of the night not spent alone with a screen. That is the modern form of lowering the gaze, and it is entirely continuous with what the verse asked of the first community.

**"Surely God Is Aware of Whatsoever They Do"**

The closing name is *khabīr* — the Aware, from *kh-b-r*, denoting knowledge of the *inner reality* of a thing, its hidden nature, as opposed to mere observation of its surface. The choice is exquisitely precise. The gaze is the most deniable of all sins. No one can prove where a man's eyes went, or what was behind the look. A glance can always be explained as accidental, as absent-mindedness, as noticing something else. There is no act more perfectly suited to self-deception and to concealment from other people. Against that, the Qur'an names not *the Seer* but *the Aware* — the One who knows not only that the eye moved, but why. The commentators note the Qur'an's use of the same register elsewhere:

> *"He knows the treachery of the eyes and what the breasts conceal."* (Qur'an 40:19)

*Khāʾinat al-aʿyun*, "the treachery of the eyes," is a phrase of remarkable exactness: the stolen look, the glance disguised as something else, the sidelong observation the observer would deny. It is the only sin the Qur'an describes as *treachery of an organ* — because it is committed by an organ that can pretend it was doing something else. The verse therefore ends where it must: with the reminder that this particular command has no external enforcement whatsoever, and is kept, if it is kept at all, entirely between a man and the One who is *khabīr*.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:31

> **And tell the believing women to lower their eyes and to guard their private parts, and to not display their adornment except that which is visible thereof. And let them draw their kerchiefs over their breasts, and not display their adornment except to their husbands, or their fathers, or their husbands' fathers, or their sons, or their husbands' sons, or their brothers, or their brothers' sons, or their sisters' sons, or their women, or those whom their right hands possess, or male attendants free of desire, or children who are innocent of the private areas of women. Nor let them stamp their feet such that the ornaments they conceal become known. And repent unto God all together, O believers, that haply you may prosper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Parallel Command and What It Shares**

This verse begins by repeating verse 30 word for word in the feminine: *wa qul li'l-muʾmināti yaghḍuḍna min abṣārihinna wa yaḥfaẓna furūjahunna* — "and tell the believing women to lower their eyes and guard their private parts." The repetition is significant and often overlooked. The discipline of the gaze is not a male obligation from which women are exempt, nor a female obligation imposed by men. It is identical for both, stated in identical language, and stated for men first. Everything said at verse 30 about the nature of *ghaḍḍ al-baṣar* — that it means lowering rather than closing, that it targets the second look rather than the first, that it protects the one who practises it — applies here without alteration. Only after establishing this shared foundation does the verse add the instructions specific to women's dress. The structure itself is an argument against reading what follows as a burden placed on one sex alone: it is one command with an extension, not two separate regimes.

**"Except That Which Is Visible Thereof"**

The first additional instruction is *wa lā yubdīna zīnatahunna illā mā ẓahara minhā* — "and let them not display their adornment except what is apparent of it." *Zīnah* means adornment, ornament, beauty, decoration. The commentators divided it into two categories: *natural* adornment (the features of the body itself) and *acquired* adornment (jewellery, cosmetics, fine clothing). The exception — *"except what is apparent of it"* — became one of the most discussed clauses in the sūrah, and the classical difference of opinion is genuine and well documented. Ibn ʿAbbās and a group of the early authorities understood it to refer to the face and the hands, and this is reported as the view of a substantial body of the Companions and Successors, and it is the position adopted by the majority of the classical schools regarding what a woman may leave uncovered before unrelated men. Ibn Masʿūd and others understood it more restrictively, taking "what is apparent" to mean the outer garment itself and whatever cannot be helped. And another early view took it to mean whatever is unintentionally exposed — that a woman is not accountable for what is revealed by wind, by movement, or by necessity. Al-Ṭabarī, having surveyed the positions, inclined to the view that the exception covers the face and hands, reasoning from the fact that these are what a woman must uncover in prayer and in the state of pilgrim sanctity (*iḥrām*), where a woman is instructed not to veil her face. The existence of this range of opinion among the earliest and most learned Muslims is itself worth recording honestly, and it should temper the confidence with which the question is sometimes discussed in either direction.

**"Let Them Draw Their Kerchiefs Over Their Breasts"**

The second instruction is the specific and unambiguous one: *wa'l-yaḍribna bi-khumurihinna ʿalā juyūbihinn.* *Khumur* is the plural of *khimār*, from the root *kh-m-r*, meaning to cover — the same root as *khamr*, wine, so called because it covers the intellect. A *khimār* is a head-covering; the word denotes the garment by its function of covering. The crucial point of historical context, recorded by the commentators, is that Arab women already wore the *khimār*. It was existing dress. What they customarily did, however, was to throw its ends back over their shoulders, leaving the neck, upper chest, and the opening of the garment exposed. *Juyūb* is the plural of *jayb*, the opening or slit at the front of a garment — the neckline and the bosom beneath it. So the instruction is not "begin wearing a head-covering"; it is *"take the covering you already wear and bring it forward over the chest."* This is a small, specific, practical adjustment to an existing custom. ʿĀʾishah's testimony about the moment this verse was revealed is preserved:

> *"May God have mercy on the women of the earliest emigrants. When God revealed 'and let them draw their kerchiefs over their breasts,' they tore their waist-wrappers and covered themselves with them."* (al-Bukhārī 4758)

The image is of women who, upon hearing the verse, immediately took whatever cloth was at hand. And a related report describes the women of the Anṣār coming out to the dawn prayer thereafter as though there were crows upon their heads, from the dark cloths they had wrapped. That immediacy of response — unlegislated, unenforced, unprompted by any authority — is part of the verse's own history and worth remembering when the subject is discussed as though it were only ever a matter of imposition.

**The List of Exceptions: Who May See the Adornment**

The verse then enumerates those before whom the fuller adornment need not be concealed. The list is long and deserves attention, because its structure reveals its logic: *Husbands* — before whom nothing is concealed. *Fathers, husbands' fathers, sons, husbands' sons, brothers, brothers' sons, sisters' sons* — the categories of permanently unmarriageable male relatives (*maḥārim*), whose relationship makes marriage permanently impossible and therefore makes the ordinary dynamic of attraction inapplicable, or at least removes it from the field of possibility that the rule exists to regulate. *Their women* — variously interpreted as Muslim women, or as women generally, or as women with whom they are familiar. *Those whom their right hands possess* — those in bonded service within the household, a category discussed further at verse 33. *Male attendants free of desire* — *al-tābiʿīna ghayri ūlī'l-irbah mina'l-rijāl*. The word *irbah* means need, want, or desire. The commentators explained this as covering men who are dependants of the household and have no sexual interest in women — the very old, the mentally incapable, and those physically incapable. The classifying principle is explicit in the wording itself: the exemption tracks the *absence of the relevant desire*. *Children who are innocent of the private areas of women* — those too young to have any awareness of such matters. Again, the criterion is stated rather than assumed: the exemption ends when the awareness begins, which is exactly the ruling verse 59 will make for the related matter of seeking permission. What emerges from the list is that Islamic modesty is *relational* rather than absolute. There is no notion that a woman's body is inherently shameful; if that were the premise, no one would be exempt. The rule concerns a specific social space — the domain of potential attraction between marriageable adults — and outside that space it simply does not apply.

**"Nor Let Them Stamp Their Feet"**

The final instruction is unexpected and, once understood, illuminating: *wa lā yaḍribna bi-arjulihinna li-yuʿlama mā yukhfīna min zīnatihinn* — "nor let them strike their feet so that what they conceal of their adornment becomes known." The reference is to the anklets worn under the garment, which would ring when a woman stamped. The practice was a deliberate attention-drawing device: the sound announced what the eye could not see. The commentators note the sophistication of what is being addressed. A woman who did this was fully covered. She had violated no rule of dress. And she had defeated the entire purpose of the dress by other means. This is the same principle that governed verse 29's warning about disclosed and concealed motives: *the Sharīʿah legislates purposes, not merely forms*. Modesty is not achieved by satisfying a checklist while working to attract by every other available channel. A person can be technically compliant and substantively in violation. The modern applications are direct and require no elaboration: the covering that is itself an ornament, the manner that draws every eye, the presentation of oneself online in a way that satisfies the rule of cloth while defeating the rule of restraint. The anklet has many descendants.

**Why Modesty Is Legislated At All**

It is worth setting out the reasoning, since it is frequently assumed rather than argued. *It regulates the terms of social encounter.* In any society, men and women must work, trade, learn, and cooperate together. The Islamic solution is to keep the register of these interactions non-sexual by convention and dress, so that a woman's presence in a public space is not automatically an event of attraction. Modest dress is, functionally, a signal that says: *engage with me on some basis other than appearance.* *It resists the reduction of a person to a surface.* The Qur'an is not embarrassed by beauty; the very word it uses is *zīnah*, adornment, which it treats as a real good, given and enjoyed within its proper setting. What it refuses is the conversion of that good into public currency. There is a real question about whether a society in which appearance is the primary medium of female value serves women well, and the evidence of the last several decades — the rise of appearance-related anxiety, the industry of alteration, the age at which girls begin to experience their bodies as objects of evaluation — is not obviously on the side of the critics of this verse. *It preserves intimacy by restricting access.* What is available to everyone is special to no one. The Islamic principle across many domains is that value is conserved by boundary.

**The Ending: "Repent Unto God All Together"**

The verse concludes with something remarkable: *wa tūbū ila'llāhi jamīʿan ayyuha'l-muʾminūna laʿallakum tufliḥūn* — "and repent unto God all together, O believers, that haply you may prosper." Three features stand out. First, the address switches to the *masculine plural*, *ayyuha'l-muʾminūn* — "O believers," addressing the whole community. After a verse largely directed at women, the closing call is universal. Second, the command is *repent* — and it is addressed not to sinners but to *believers*, collectively. The implication is that in this domain nobody is faultless: not the men who looked, not the women who displayed, not the community that permitted the atmosphere. And more broadly, that repentance is the permanent condition of the believer rather than an emergency measure. The Prophet ﷺ, whose sins were forgiven, said:

> *"O people, repent unto God, for I repent unto Him a hundred times a day."* (Muslim 2702)

Third, the promise is *falāḥ* — prosperity, flourishing, from the agricultural root discussed in the commentary on al-Baqarah: the root of the ploughman and the thriving field. The chapter that legislates the discipline of the eye and the covering of the body ends its most demanding verse with the word for a harvest. That is the Qur'an's final argument here, and it is characteristic. What looks like restriction is presented as cultivation. The field is bounded so that it can bear.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:32

> **And marry those who are single among you, and the righteous among your male slaves and your female slaves. If they are poor, God will enrich them from His Bounty. And God is All-Encompassing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Positive Half of the Programme**

Two verses have told believers what to restrain: the eye, the display, the desire. This verse tells them what to *provide*. That sequence is essential to understanding Islamic sexual ethics, and it is the point at which many discussions of the subject go wrong. A system that prohibits without providing is not a moral order; it is a pressure vessel. The Qur'an legislates restraint in verses 30 and 31 and then, immediately, commands the community to open the lawful channel in verse 32. The commentators consistently read these verses as one argument. Desire is not condemned as such. It is directed. What is forbidden outside marriage is not merely permitted inside it but described elsewhere in the Book as a sign of God:

> *"And among His signs is that He created for you mates from among yourselves, that you may find rest in them, and He ordained affection and mercy between you."* (Qur'an 30:21)

**"And Marry": A Command Addressed to the Community**

*Wa ankiḥū'l-ayāmā minkum.* The verb is in the imperative, and — crucially — it is not addressed to the single person. *Ankiḥū* is the causative form: it means "*marry them off*," "arrange their marriage," "give them in marriage." So the command falls on the community: on families, guardians, neighbours, and communal leadership. Society is instructed to take active responsibility for getting its unmarried members married. This is a striking reversal of the modern assumption that marriage is a purely private matter to be solved by each individual alone. The Qur'anic view is that a society which demands chastity of its young people while doing nothing to facilitate their marriage has created an unjust and unsustainable situation, and bears responsibility for the result.

**Ayāmā: The Unmarried of Both Sexes**

The object of the command is *al-ayāmā*, plural of *ayyim*. The word denotes a person without a spouse — and the commentators note that it covers men and women, the never-married, the widowed, and the divorced alike. Some lexicographers give its primary sense as a woman who has no husband, whether or not she was previously married, with the masculine application following. The breadth matters. The verse does not command the community to arrange marriages for young virgins only. Widows and divorcees — precisely the people most likely to be left unmarried in any society, and most vulnerable when they are — are explicitly included. The Prophet's ﷺ own marriages, most of which were to widows and previously married women, embodied this.

**"And the Righteous Among Your Slaves"**

The verse extends the command to those in bondage: *wa'l-ṣāliḥīna min ʿibādikum wa imāʾikum.* This requires comment, because a modern reader is rightly troubled by the institution's presence in the text. Several observations are in order. The Qur'an addresses a society in which slavery was universal, ancient, and economically embedded across every civilization on earth. Its method with such institutions was not immediate abolition by decree but systematic constriction: it closed off sources of enslavement, made emancipation the standard expiation for a wide range of offences, made it an act of the highest merit, commanded the emancipation contract (as the very next verse does), and — here — commanded that the enslaved be *married*. That last point is more radical than it appears. Legal marriage confers dignity, family, recognized lineage, and rights. Slave systems throughout history characteristically denied the enslaved exactly these things, precisely because recognizing a slave's family made the slave a person. The Qur'an commands the opposite, and commands it in the same breath as the marriage of the free. The Prophet ﷺ reinforced the direction consistently:

> *"Your brothers are your servants whom God has placed under your authority. So whoever has his brother under his authority, let him feed him from what he eats, clothe him from what he wears, and not burden him with what overwhelms him; and if he does burden him, let him help him."* (al-Bukhārī 30; Muslim 1661)

The trajectory of the legislation is unmistakable, and the institution it aimed at has, by the working out of that trajectory and the consensus of the Muslim world, ceased to exist.

**"If They Are Poor, God Will Enrich Them From His Bounty"**

Here is the heart of the verse, and its most quoted line: *in yakūnū fuqarāʾa yughnihimu'llāhu min faḍlih.* The Qur'an anticipates the objection before it is raised. The single most common reason marriages do not happen — then and now — is money. He cannot afford a household. She expects a certain standard. The families cannot meet the costs. The young man is told to wait until he is established, and the waiting extends into a decade. To this the verse replies with a promise: *God will enrich them from His bounty.* Several things must be said about how to read this promise correctly. It is not a guarantee of wealth to every married couple, and the Qur'an is not a prosperity manual. It is a statement about *provision*: that the sustenance of a household is in God's hands and is not calculable in advance from the resources of the individuals entering it. The commentators note that *ghinā*, enrichment, includes the sufficiency of contentment as well as the increase of means — the Prophet ﷺ having said:

> *"Richness is not in the abundance of possessions; richness is the richness of the soul."* (al-Bukhārī 6446; Muslim 1051)

It is also an observation about the real economics of marriage, and one that ordinary experience confirms. Two people sharing a household spend less than two people maintaining separate ones. Responsibility for a family is one of the strongest known motivators of productive work. And the Prophet ﷺ named marriage among the situations in which divine help is promised:

> *"There are three whom it is a right upon God to help: the one striving in the way of God, the slave with a contract of emancipation who intends to pay, and the one who marries desiring chastity."* (al-Tirmidhī 1655; al-Nasāʾī 3120)

That last clause — *"the one who marries desiring chastity"* — is precisely the case this verse addresses.

**The Argument Against Excessive Marriage Costs**

There is a direct and uncomfortable implication here for many Muslim communities today, and it should be stated. If God has commanded the community to marry off its unmarried, and has promised provision to those who marry in poverty, then the erection of enormous financial barriers to marriage — inflated dowries, competitive wedding expenditure, the expectation of a fully furnished home before a contract can be signed — is a direct obstruction of a Qur'anic command. It produces exactly the harm the sūrah is legislating against: a generation of young people told to remain chaste while the lawful route is priced beyond reach. The Prophetic standard was the opposite. He ﷺ said:

> *"The most blessed marriage is the one with the least burden."* (Aḥmad; al-Bayhaqī)

And he married a man to a woman for a dowry of an iron ring, and when the man had not even that, for what he had memorized of the Qur'an (al-Bukhārī 5087; Muslim 1425). ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb publicly discouraged the inflation of dowries.

**"God Is All-Encompassing, Knowing"**

The closing names are *wāsiʿ* and *ʿalīm*. *Wāsiʿ* is from *w-s-ʿ*, meaning breadth, spaciousness, capacity — the same root as the *saʿah* of verse 22, the "means" of the wealthy. Applied to God it means: His capacity is unlimited, His provision is not a fixed quantity that runs short, and His giving to one does not diminish what is available to another. This directly answers the anxiety behind the objection. The reluctance to marry in poverty rests on an implicit picture of a *closed system* — a fixed pot of resources, in which adding a person means everyone gets less. The name *al-Wāsiʿ* denies the premise. The pot is not fixed and it is not ours. *ʿAlīm*, the Knowing, adds the necessary qualification: God knows each case. The promise is not a mechanical formula but the assurance of a Provider who is aware of the particular circumstances of the particular household. And it forecloses the misuse of the verse as a licence for recklessness — the One who promises is also the One who knows whether a person married in trust or in negligence.

**Practical Reflections**

Three applications suggest themselves. *For communities.* The imperative is plural, and it is a command. Facilitating marriages — introductions, reduced costs, communal support for young couples, refusal to participate in dowry inflation — is not social work adjacent to religion; it is religion. *For families.* A father who imposes conditions that keep his daughter unmarried into her thirties, or who rejects a religious and capable suitor on grounds of income alone, should read this verse and consider whether he is obstructing what God commanded and doubting what God promised. *For individuals.* The verse is a corrective to the paralysis of waiting for perfect readiness. There is a real difference between prudence and the endless postponement that is, at bottom, a failure of trust. The verse does not command imprudence; it names the One from whom provision actually comes.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:33

> **And let those who are unable to marry be chaste till God enriches them from His Bounty. And as for those among the ones whom your right hands possess who seek a contract [of emancipation] with you, contract with them if you know of any good in them, and give unto them from the Wealth of God, which He has given you. And compel not your female slaves into prostitution if they desire to be chaste, for the sake of seeking after the ephemeralities of the life of this world. And whosoever compels them, then truly God, after their having been compelled, will be Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Rulings for Those Without Power**

This long verse contains three distinct instructions, and they are united by a single concern: the situation of people who cannot simply act on the previous verse's command. Those who cannot yet marry. Those who are owned and wish to be free. And those who are owned and are being exploited. In each case the Qur'an addresses both the vulnerable party and those with power over them.

**Part One: The Command of Chastity to Those Who Cannot Marry**

*Wa'l-yastaʿfifi'lladhīna lā yajidūna nikāḥan ḥattā yughniyahumu'llāhu min faḍlih.* The verb is *yastaʿfif*, from the root *ʿ-f-f*, which gives *ʿiffah*: chastity, self-restraint, abstinence, the refusal to take what is not one's own. The verb form used here (form X) carries the sense of *seeking* or *striving for* the quality — "let them work at being chaste." It is not passive endurance but active effort. The realism of this instruction should be appreciated. Verse 32 commanded the community to marry off its single members and promised divine enrichment. This verse acknowledges immediately that for some people, at some times, marriage will not be available. The Qur'an does not pretend otherwise, and it does not leave that person without guidance. The instruction has three components: strive for chastity, understand it as *temporary* (*ḥattā* — "until"), and know that the relief will come from God's bounty rather than from one's own contrivance. The Prophetic guidance supplies the method. Addressing exactly this group, the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"O young men, whoever among you is able to marry, let him marry, for it restrains the gaze and guards the private parts. And whoever is not able, let him fast, for it is a shield for him."* (al-Bukhārī 5065; Muslim 1400)

Fasting is prescribed as the interim discipline — a physical practice that trains the will by requiring the denial of a more basic appetite than the one in question. The wisdom is that self-control is a general capacity: a person who can govern hunger is better equipped to govern desire. The tradition also records the Prophet's ﷺ promise regarding those who succeed in this specific struggle. Among the seven whom God will shade on the Day when there is no shade but His:

> *"…a man who is called by a woman of beauty and position and says, 'I fear God'…"* (al-Bukhārī 660; Muslim 1031)

Restraint exercised in private, with the opportunity available and no possibility of discovery, is placed among the highest acts in the religion.

**The Honesty of "Until"**

The word *ḥattā* deserves a moment. It frames the difficulty as an interval, not a permanent state. This matters enormously for anyone living it. The hardest aspect of enforced abstinence is not the physical difficulty but the sense of *permanence* — the conviction that this is simply one's life now, indefinitely. The verse refuses that framing. It is *until*, and the ending is in the hands of the One described in the previous verse as *al-Wāsiʿ*, whose provision is not limited.

**Part Two: The Contract of Emancipation**

*Wa'lladhīna yabtaghūna'l-kitāba mimmā malakat aymānukum fa-kātibūhum in ʿalimtum fīhim khayran, wa ātūhum min māli'llāhi'lladhī ātākum.* This is the institution the jurists call *mukātabah* — a written contract by which an enslaved person purchases his own freedom in instalments, working and earning until the agreed sum is paid, at which point he becomes free. Several features of the ruling should be noted. *The initiative belongs to the enslaved person.* The verse describes those who *seek* the contract. It is a right they may invoke. *The response is commanded.* *Fa-kātibūhum* is an imperative: "then contract with them." A significant body of jurists, including those following the ẓāhir of the text, held this to be obligatory upon the owner when the conditions are met — a legally enforceable right of the enslaved against the owner. Others read it as strong recommendation. The direction of the text is unmistakable either way. *The condition is capacity and integrity.* *"If you know of any good in them"* — *khayr* here being interpreted by the commentators as the ability to earn and the trustworthiness to fulfil the contract, so that the arrangement is not set up to fail. *And then the owner must contribute.* *"And give unto them from the wealth of God which He has given you."* This is the most remarkable clause. Not only must the owner accept the contract; he must *fund part of it* — either by reducing the agreed sum, or by giving from his own wealth toward its payment. The Qur'an also assigns a portion of the obligatory alms to exactly this purpose:

> *"Charity is only for the poor, the indigent, those who work for it, those whose hearts are to be reconciled, for slaves and debtors, in the way of God, and for the traveller."* (Qur'an 9:60)

*Fi'l-riqāb* — "for slaves," literally "for necks" — is a standing category of the community's compulsory charity, dedicated to buying people out of bondage.

**"From the Wealth of God Which He Has Given You"**

This phrase is one of the most theologically loaded in the verse, and it reframes the entire transaction. The owner is not told to give from *his* wealth. He is told to give from *God's* wealth, which God gave him. Ownership is relocated in a single clause. What a person holds is a trust, temporarily assigned, whose real proprietor is the One who assigned it — a doctrine the Qur'an states repeatedly:

> *"And give them from the wealth of God which He has given you."*

> *"Believe in God and His Messenger, and spend of that whereof He has made you trustees."* (Qur'an 57:7)

The implication for the specific case is pointed: a man who claims absolute rights over his property, and who claims absolute rights over another human being as part of that property, is told in one phrase that neither claim is what he thinks it is.

**Part Three: The Prohibition of Forced Prostitution**

*Wa lā tukrihū fatayātikum ʿala'l-bighāʾi in aradna taḥaṣṣunan li-tabtaghū ʿaraḍa'l-ḥayāti'l-dunyā.* The commentators record the occasion of revelation. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ubayy — the same man who was the principal author of the slander against ʿĀʾishah — had enslaved women whom he forced into prostitution for his own profit. One or more of them came to complain, and this verse was revealed. That the leader of the hypocrites appears twice in this sūrah, as the chief slanderer and as the trafficker of women, is a coherence the reader is meant to notice: the man who publicly attacked a woman's chastity was privately destroying the chastity of women in his own house. The prohibition is absolute, and its wording repays attention. *Fatayāt*, "young women," is used rather than a term of servitude — a deliberate softening of language that grants dignity to the victims. The Prophet ﷺ instructed the same usage generally, telling people not to say "my slave" but *"my young man, my young woman"* (Muslim 2249). *Bighāʾ* is prostitution. And *in aradna taḥaṣṣunan* — "if they desire chastity" — uses the same root *ḥ-ṣ-n* met at verse 4: the fortress. The verse recognizes in an enslaved woman the *will to be fortified*, and makes that will legally decisive against the will of her owner. The stated motive of the exploiter is named with contempt: *li-tabtaghū ʿaraḍa'l-ḥayāti'l-dunyā* — "seeking the ephemeralities of the life of this world." *ʿAraḍ* means something incidental, transient, a passing accident — the word is used for goods that come and go. The Qur'an weighs the trade explicitly: a human being's chastity, dignity, and eternal welfare, exchanged for a passing profit.

**"God Will Be Forgiving, Merciful — After Their Having Been Compelled"**

The final clause is one of the most compassionate sentences in the Qur'an, and its grammar must be read carefully: *fa-inna'llāha min baʿdi ikrāhihinna ghafūrun raḥīm* — "then truly God, after their having been compelled, is Forgiving, Merciful." The forgiveness is directed at *the compelled women*, not at those who compelled them. The commentators are clear on this. The phrase *min baʿdi ikrāhihinna*, "after their compulsion," specifies the condition: having been coerced. The significance is enormous, and it addresses something that societies have historically got badly wrong. A woman forced into prostitution was, in most legal and social systems of the ancient and medieval world — and in many since — regarded as defiled, as guilty, as ruined. The Qur'an declares the opposite: she bears no sin, and God is Forgiving and Merciful toward her. The general principle is established elsewhere in the Book and in the Sunnah. Coerced acts are not attributed to the coerced:

> *"…except one who is compelled while his heart is at peace in faith."* (Qur'an 16:106)

> *"God has lifted from my community error, forgetfulness, and what they are compelled to do."* (Ibn Mājah 2045)

The victim of a sexual crime, in Islamic law, is a victim and not an offender. That this needed to be stated then, and still needs to be stated now in many places, is a measure of how easily human societies transfer shame from the perpetrator to the person harmed.

**The Unity of the Verse**

At first reading these three rulings appear unrelated. They are not. Each addresses a person in a position of *constrained choice* regarding chastity and freedom: the person who cannot marry because he lacks means; the person who cannot marry freely because he is owned; the person whose chastity is being destroyed by someone with power over her. And in each case the Qur'an does two things simultaneously: it gives the vulnerable person a directive of dignity (*strive for chastity*, *seek the contract*, *your desire for chastity is decisive*), and it imposes a corresponding obligation on the powerful (*fund their freedom*, *do not compel them*). That double movement is characteristic of the Sharīʿah's treatment of inequality. It does not merely counsel patience to the weak; it binds the strong.

**Contemporary Relevance**

The verse's third ruling is, tragically, not historical. Trafficking and forced prostitution exist today on a scale that dwarfs anything the seventh century knew, and the pattern is identical: a person with power, a person without it, and a passing profit. The verse supplies the Islamic position on every element of it. The exploiter's motive is contemptible transience. The victim's desire for chastity is legally and morally decisive. And the victim is *forgiven* — which, in practical terms, means the community that receives her must treat her as innocent, because God has declared her so. Its second ruling has an application too. The obligation to fund the freedom of those in bondage — *"give them from the wealth of God which He has given you"* — establishes a principle: those with means bear a positive duty to buy people out of captivity, not merely to refrain from enslaving them. Wherever human beings are held in debt bondage, forced labour, or trafficking today, that duty has an obvious address.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:34

> **We have indeed sent down unto you clarifying signs and a description of those who have passed before you, and an exhortation for the reverent.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Closing of the First Movement**

This verse marks the end of the sūrah's first great section. Everything from verse 1 to verse 33 has been legislation and its accompanying narrative: the penalties, the evidentiary standards, the slander and its lessons, the etiquette of entry, the gaze, dress, marriage, and the treatment of the powerless. Now the sūrah pauses, summarizes what it has delivered, and prepares for the transition that will produce, in the very next verse, the Light Verse. The placement is deliberate: the law has been laid down, and now comes the light. The verse echoes verse 1 almost exactly — *"a sūrah… in which We have sent down clear signs"* — closing the section with the same formula that opened it. This is a recognized structure in Qur'anic composition, sometimes called ring composition: the passage is framed by matching statements, so that the reader knows where a movement begins and ends.

**Three Things Sent Down**

The verse names three distinct gifts, and each corresponds to a different faculty of the human being.

**One: Āyāt Mubayyināt — Clarifying Signs**

The first is what the sūrah has been giving throughout: *clear rulings*. This addresses the *intellect*. It tells a person what is required, permitted, and forbidden, in language whose boundaries can be known. The importance of clarity as a category has already been discussed at verse 1. Here it is worth adding one point: the Qur'an's repeated insistence on its own clarity is also an implicit critique of religious obscurantism — the notion that divine guidance is a mystery available only to initiates. The Book presents itself as *mubīn*, plain, and expects to be understood by ordinary people.

**Two: Mathalan mina'lladhīna khalaw min qablikum — A Description of Those Who Passed Before**

The second is *history*, or more precisely *example*. The word *mathal* means a likeness, a parable, an illustrative case, a proverb. And *alladhīna khalaw min qablikum* means those who have gone before — previous nations and communities. This addresses the *imagination* and the *memory*. Rules alone do not persuade; people need to see what happens. The Qur'an is, by volume, largely narrative. It tells the stories of Noah's people, ʿĀd and Thamūd, Pharaoh, Lot's city, the Children of Israel, the People of the Cave, the companions of the ditch. And it states explicitly that these are not told as antiquarian interest:

> *"Truly in their stories is a lesson for those possessed of intellect. It is not a fabricated account, but a confirmation of that which came before it, and an elaboration of all things, and a guidance and a mercy for a people who believe."* (Qur'an 12:111)

> *"Say: Travel upon the earth and behold how those before you fared in the end."* (Qur'an 30:42)

The relevance to this sūrah is direct. The community of Madinah had just enacted, in miniature, a pattern older than itself: a society whose loose tongues nearly destroyed it. The peoples before them offer the same lesson written larger — and Lot's people, whose destruction the Qur'an attributes to precisely the public normalization of indecency that verse 19 condemned, are the most directly relevant example. There is a general principle of human learning here. Abstract rules are stored shallowly; stories are stored deeply. A person may forget the ruling on backbiting and remember, for life, the image of eating a dead brother's flesh (49:12). The Qur'an's use of *mathal* is a deliberate exploitation of how human memory actually works.

**Three: Mawʿiẓatan li'l-muttaqīn — An Exhortation for the Reverent**

The third is *waʿẓ*, admonition — the same word encountered at verse 17. As discussed there, it denotes moral address that aims at the heart, reminding of consequences in a manner that softens and moves. This addresses the *will*. Knowing the rule and knowing the precedent still leaves the gap that every human being knows too well: the gap between understanding and doing. Admonition is the pressure applied to that gap.

**"For the Reverent" — Why the Restriction?**

The final phrase raises a question. The signs are sent to everyone, and the historical examples are for everyone. But the admonition is specified as *li'l-muttaqīn*, "for the reverent" — those who possess *taqwā*. Why is the benefit restricted to a group? The answer lies in the nature of admonition. Its effectiveness depends entirely on the receptivity of the listener. The same sermon that transforms one person leaves another unmoved, not because the words differ but because the hearts do. The Qur'an says this of itself repeatedly:

> *"And We send down of the Qur'an that which is a healing and a mercy for the believers, but it increases the wrongdoers in naught but loss."* (Qur'an 17:82)

*The same rain*, one might say, *falls on the good soil and the bad*, and the sūrah's own imagery elsewhere makes this comparison — the good land bringing forth its vegetation, the bad bringing forth little (7:58). So the restriction is not God withholding benefit; it is a description of who is capable of receiving it. And it contains an implicit instruction to the reader: *become the kind of person for whom admonition works.*

**Taqwā: The Quality That Makes Guidance Effective**

Since the verse names the reverent as the beneficiaries, the quality itself deserves explanation. *Taqwā* is from the root *w-q-y*, meaning to protect, shield, or guard against harm. A *wiqāyah* is a shield or protective covering. So *taqwā* is not "fear of God" in the sense of terror, though awe is part of it. It is the *protective wariness* of someone who knows there is real danger and moves accordingly. The classical illustration, attributed in various forms to Ubayy ibn Kaʿb and others, remains the best: *taqwā* is like walking through a path thick with thorns. You do not run, you do not stroll obliviously, and you do not stand paralyzed. You gather your garment close and place each step with attention. A modern equivalent is driving on a road known to be dangerous — a mountain pass in the rain, or a street where children play. The careful driver is not panicking. He is simply *awake to the actual conditions*, and his awareness translates continuously into small adjustments of speed and attention that prevent harm before it materializes. That is precisely the posture this sūrah has been trying to install: the awareness that a glance has consequences, that a repeated rumor is recorded, that an unasked entry harms someone, that a small step leads somewhere. The person with *taqwā* is the one for whom admonition works, because he is already looking for the hazards it names.

**Why Three Modes Are Necessary**

There is a lesson here for anyone engaged in teaching, preaching, parenting, or leading. *Rules alone* produce legalism: people who know the boundaries and spend their effort finding the edges, exactly as verse 29 warned. *Stories alone* produce entertainment: people who enjoy the narratives and derive nothing binding from them. *Exhortation alone* produces emotionalism: intense feeling that fades within hours, leaving no structure behind. The Qur'an supplies all three, and this verse names them together. Effective moral formation requires the clear rule, the vivid example, and the warmed heart — the intellect, the imagination, and the will, addressed at once. Anyone who has heard a sermon composed entirely of rules, or entirely of stories, or entirely of emotional appeal, has felt the deficiency. The Book's own practice is the model.

**Transition to What Follows**

This verse also functions as a hinge, and its placement immediately before the Light Verse is the sūrah's most significant structural decision. The reader has now been given the law in full. What comes next is not more law but a statement about the nature of God and of guidance itself — followed by the description of the houses in which He is remembered, and then by the two great parables of the deeds of those who reject Him. The message of the arrangement is one already noted in the introduction to this sūrah: light is not reached by bypassing the law of the tongue, the eye, and the door. It is reached by passing through it. Verses 1 through 34 are the discipline; verse 35 is what the discipline is for.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:35

> **God is the Light of the heavens and the earth. The parable of His Light is a niche, wherein is a lamp. The lamp is in a glass. The glass is as a shining star kindled from a blessed olive tree, neither of the East nor of the West. Its oil would well-nigh shine forth, even if no fire had touched it. Light upon light. God guides unto His Light whomsoever He will, and God sets forth parables for mankind, and God is Knower of all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Named the Chapter**

This is *Āyat al-Nūr*, the Light Verse — one of the most meditated-upon passages in the Qur'an, the source of the sūrah's name, and the inspiration for more mystical commentary, philosophical exposition, calligraphy, and architecture than perhaps any verse outside Āyat al-Kursī. Al-Ghazālī devoted an entire treatise to it, *Mishkāt al-Anwār* ("The Niche of Lights"). Its imagery is inscribed in the mihrabs of mosques from Andalusia to India, and the lamp hanging in a niche became one of the standard motifs of Islamic sacred art precisely because of these words. And its placement, as noted repeatedly in this commentary, is the sūrah's central argument. It does not open the chapter. It arrives after thirty-four verses of law about adultery, slander, doorways, glances, and dress. The structure asserts: *this* is what all of that was for.

**"God Is the Light of the Heavens and the Earth"**

*Allāhu nūru'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ.* The sentence is grammatically the simplest in the verse and theologically the most demanding. It does not say God *has* light, or *created* light, or *is a source of* light. It uses the direct predication: God *is* the Light of the heavens and the earth. The commentators handled this with characteristic care, because the Qur'an also insists absolutely that *"there is nothing like unto Him"* (42:11), and light as we know it is a created physical phenomenon. Several readings emerged, and they are complementary rather than exclusive. *The Illuminator.* Ibn ʿAbbās and a substantial body of the early commentators read it as *munawwir al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — "the Illuminator of the heavens and the earth," the One by whom they are lit. Al-Ṭabarī inclines to this, with the sense that God is the guide of their inhabitants: the light by which they see. On this reading the predication is one of *agency*: He is their light in the sense that all their light is from Him. *The Guide.* A closely related early reading, also from Ibn ʿAbbās and others, is *hādī ahl al-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ* — the Guide of their inhabitants. Light in the Qur'an is overwhelmingly a metaphor for guidance, and this reading connects directly to the verse's own conclusion: *"God guides unto His Light whomsoever He will."* *The Ground of all manifestation.* The philosophical and mystical commentators, al-Ghazālī foremost among them, developed a further reading. Light, they observed, is that by which things are made visible while itself requiring nothing to make it visible. It is the condition of seeing rather than one of the objects seen. In this sense, they argued, God alone is Light in the fullest meaning of the word: the ground by which all existence is manifest, while He is manifest by Himself. Everything else that we call light is light *derivatively* — it makes some things visible under some conditions and is itself dependent on a source. Al-Ghazālī's formulation was that created lights are lights only by borrowing; the name belongs in reality to God alone. *Al-Nūr* is one of the divine names, and the Prophet ﷺ invoked it directly in his supplication upon rising for the night prayer:

> *"O God, praise be to Yours: You are the Light of the heavens and the earth and all within them."* (al-Bukhārī 1120; Muslim 769)

And when Abū Dharr asked whether he had seen his Lord, he ﷺ replied:

> *"Light — how could I see Him?"* (Muslim 178)

**Why Light Is the Chosen Image**

Before examining the parable, it is worth asking why light in particular carries so much of the Qur'an's theological weight. Light has a set of properties that make it uniquely suited to the task. It is *invisible in itself but the cause of all visibility* — one never sees light, one sees things *by* light. It is *immaterial yet utterly real*.
