It *does not diminish by being shared*: a thousand candles lit from one flame take nothing from it. It *reveals without altering* what it reveals. It *penetrates* without force. And its opposite, darkness, is not a substance at all but simply its absence — which is why the Qur'an consistently uses light in the singular and darkness in the plural:

> *"…that He may bring you out of the darknesses into the light."* (Qur'an 33:43; and similarly 2:257, 5:16, 14:1, 65:11)

The grammatical asymmetry is doctrine. There is one light and many darknesses, because truth is one and error is innumerable; and because darkness has no independent existence, being merely the place light has not reached.

**The Parable Begins**

*Mathalu nūrihi ka-mishkātin fīhā miṣbāḥ.* Note the precise wording: *the parable of **His Light***, not "the parable of God." The Qur'an does not offer an image of the Divine Essence, which would violate its own principle of incomparability. It offers an image of the *light He gives* — which the commentators variously identify as guidance, as faith in the heart of the believer, as the Qur'an, as the Prophet ﷺ, and as the light of the *fiṭrah*, the primordial nature. Ubayy ibn Kaʿb is reported to have said that this is the parable of the light of faith in the heart of the believer, and this became one of the dominant readings among the early authorities.

**The Elements of the Image**

The parable builds a single object out of five components, and each has been the subject of extensive interpretation.

**The Niche (*mishkāt*)**

A *mishkāt* is a recess or alcove in a wall, not passing through it, in which a lamp is placed. Its function is precise and practical: a niche gathers and concentrates light that would otherwise disperse, and shelters the flame from wind. Two features therefore define it: *concentration* and *protection*. The same light in an open room diffuses and weakens; in a niche it is focused and steady. The commentators read the niche as the human breast — the chest that contains the heart — or, on other readings, as the body of the believer, or as the lamp-niche of the mosque. In every case the point is the same: the light of guidance is not scattered abroad but *housed* in a specific place, protected and concentrated there.

**The Lamp (*miṣbāḥ*)**

The lamp is the flame itself — the actual source of illumination within the image. It is read as faith itself, or as the Qur'an, or as the heart's knowledge of God.

**The Glass (*zujājah*)**

The lamp sits inside a glass. This is the most beautiful element of the image and the most instructive. Glass has a paradoxical set of properties. It is *transparent* — it does not block the light. It is *protective* — it shields the flame from being extinguished. It *amplifies* — a flame in clear glass is brighter than a bare flame, because the glass gathers and reflects. And it is *fragile*. The commentators read the glass as the heart of the believer. The reading is exact in every particular. A heart must be transparent — free of the opacity of hypocrisy, resentment, and hidden motive — or the light within it will not show. A heart protects the faith it contains. A pure heart magnifies faith outward into visible conduct. And a heart is breakable. The Qur'an says the glass is *ka-annahā kawkabun durriyy* — "as though it were a shining star." *Durriyy* is variously derived from *durr*, pearl (pearl-like in its luminous whiteness), or from *darʾ*, to push away (a star that repels darkness), or read as *dirrīʾ* connected with brilliance. The image is of the brightest stars in the sky — Venus, Sirius — the ones that seem to have a liquid, pearly intensity. Note that the *glass* is described as the star, not the lamp. The vessel has become luminous. A heart that houses faith is not merely a container; it is transformed by what it contains and begins to shine on its own account. This is an observation anyone can verify: genuinely devout people are recognizable, and the recognition has nothing to do with what they say.

**The Blessed Olive Tree**

*Yūqadu min shajaratin mubārakatin zaytūnatin lā sharqiyyatin wa lā gharbiyyah.* The lamp is kindled from the oil of a blessed olive tree, "neither of the east nor of the west." The olive was the standard lamp fuel of the Mediterranean world, so at the literal level the detail is domestic and familiar. But the Qur'an calls the tree *mubārakah*, blessed, and elsewhere swears by it:

> *"By the fig and the olive."* (Qur'an 95:1)

The commentators explain the blessing in terms the tree genuinely possesses: it is extraordinarily long-lived, with individual trees surviving for centuries; it is hardy, growing in poor and rocky soil; every part of it is useful; and its oil serves as food, medicine, fuel, and unguent. It is a tree associated with the lands of the prophets.

**"Neither of the East Nor of the West"**

This phrase has generated more interpretation than any other in the verse, and the readings are worth setting out because each captures something true. *The horticultural reading*, favored by many commentators: a tree standing in open ground, not shaded on its eastern or western side, so that it receives sunlight from dawn to dusk. Such a tree produces the finest and purest oil. On this reading the phrase describes an ideal growing condition — full, unobstructed exposure to light. *The geographical-symbolic reading*: the tree belongs to no particular region or nation. The light of God is not the property of a people, an ethnicity, or a civilization. Given that this sūrah was revealed in a Madinah surrounded by competing religious claims — Byzantine Christianity to the west, Persian Zoroastrianism to the east — the phrase asserts a universality that transcends both empires. *The metaphysical reading*, developed by the philosophical commentators: the light is not confined by any of the categories by which created things are located. It is not "here" as opposed to "there." All three readings converge on a single meaning: this light is unlimited by place, unshadowed, and unaffiliated.

**"Its Oil Would Well-Nigh Shine Forth, Even If No Fire Had Touched It"**

*Yakādu zaytuhā yuḍīʾu wa law lam tamsashu nār.* The oil is so pure, so refined, that it seems on the verge of luminescence *before any flame reaches it*. This is the most theologically suggestive line in the parable, and the commentators drew several conclusions from it. At the level of the image, it describes oil of the highest possible grade — clear, unclouded, without sediment. Anyone who has seen the difference between crude and refined oil will recognize the observation: the pure oil seems to hold light within itself. At the level of meaning, it describes the *fiṭrah* — the primordial disposition toward truth that the Qur'an holds every human being to possess:

> *"So set thy face toward religion as a ḥanīf, in the primordial nature from God upon which He originated mankind. There is no altering the creation of God. That is the upright religion, but most of mankind know not."* (Qur'an 30:30)

The Prophet ﷺ stated it in the well-known report:

> *"Every child is born upon the fiṭrah; it is his parents who make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Zoroastrian."* (al-Bukhārī 1358; Muslim 2658)

On this reading, the human soul in its original condition is already *almost* luminous. It is disposed toward truth before it receives revelation. Revelation is the flame that ignites what was already prepared to burn. This has an important implication for how Muslims should understand the reception of guidance. Faith is not the installation of something alien into a resistant object. It is the *kindling* of something already present and already inclined. Which is why the Qur'an's characteristic word for what it does is *dhikr*, reminder, and why the sūrah's first verse said it was revealed *"that you may remember."*

**"Light Upon Light"**

*Nūrun ʿalā nūr.* The phrase is bare, unqualified, and deliberately open. The commentators offered many completions, and their multiplicity is itself appropriate to a verse about light. The light of the *lamp* upon the light of the *glass*, upon the light of the *oil* — the physical layering within the image itself, each element contributing luminosity. The light of *revelation* upon the light of the *fiṭrah* — the flame meeting the oil that was already almost shining. The light of the *Qur'an* upon the light of previous *prophecy*, since the Book describes itself as confirming what came before. The light of *faith* upon the light of *good works*, each increasing the other. The light of one *prophet* after another, culminating in the final Messenger. And, in the reading of the spiritual commentators, the successive increases of illumination as a believer progresses: knowledge upon knowledge, certainty upon certainty, each stage revealing what the previous stage could not have shown. The Qur'an itself describes guidance as cumulative:

> *"And God increases in guidance those who are rightly guided."* (Qur'an 19:76)

> *"As for those who are rightly guided, He increases them in guidance and gives them their reverence."* (Qur'an 47:17)

**"God Guides Unto His Light Whomsoever He Will"**

After the parable comes the theological statement: *yahdi'llāhu li-nūrihi man yashāʾ.* The point is the one already made at verse 21 concerning purification: the light is a gift, not an achievement. No amount of intelligence guarantees it; the Qur'an is full of clever people who did not see. No lineage guarantees it; the son of Noah drowned. It comes from God. And, as noted at verse 21, divine will is not arbitrary. The Book consistently identifies the dispositions toward which guidance flows:

> *"And those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them in Our ways."* (Qur'an 29:69)

The believer's stance is therefore twofold: to strive as though the outcome depended on effort, and to ask as one who knows it does not. Which is why the Prophet ﷺ prayed, in a supplication of striking relevance to this verse:

> *"O God, place light in my heart, light in my hearing, light in my sight, light on my right, light on my left, light above me, light below me, light before me, light behind me, and make for me a light."* (al-Bukhārī 6316; Muslim 763)

**"And God Sets Forth Parables for Mankind"**

The verse then comments on its own method: *wa yaḍribu'llāhu'l-amthāla li'l-nās.* Why does God speak in images at all? Because the subject exceeds direct description. Human language is built from sensory experience, and the reality being described is not sensory. A parable does not define; it *orients*. It gives the mind a structure it can hold, from which it can reach toward what cannot be held. And because images reach where propositions do not. A person who is told "faith illuminates the heart" has received an abstraction. A person given a niche, a lamp, a glass like a star, and an oil that nearly shines untouched has been given something he will carry for life and understand in layers as he ages. The Qur'an notes that parables sort their audiences:

> *"And these parables We set forth for mankind, but none understand them save those who know."* (Qur'an 29:43)

**"And God Is Knower of All Things"**

The closing name, *ʿalīmun bi-kulli shayʾ*, seals the verse. Its function here is to guarantee the appropriateness of the parable. The One who chose these images knows exactly what a human being needs in order to grasp what can be grasped and to sense what cannot. And He knows which hearts are glass and which are opaque, which oil is pure and which is clouded.

**Why the Light Verse Sits Where It Sits**

Return, finally, to the placement, because it is the interpretive key to the whole sūrah. The parable's central image is a *pure oil in a clear glass in a sheltered niche*. Every element of it is about the conditions under which light can be received and held. And the thirty-four preceding verses have been about exactly those conditions. The niche is the protected household — hence the laws of entry. The clear glass is the heart uncorrupted by suspicion, slander, and the love of scandal — hence the sixteen verses on the tongue. The pure oil is the soul undegraded by unlawful appetite — hence the lowering of the gaze, the guarding of chastity, the marriage of the single, and the protection of the exploited. You cannot light a lamp in a room with no walls. You cannot see a flame through clouded glass. You cannot burn contaminated oil. The law of Sūrat al-Nūr is not an obstacle placed before the light. It is the lamp being assembled.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:36

> **[It is] in houses that God has permitted to be raised and wherein His Name is remembered. He is therein glorified, morning and evening,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Where the Lamp Is Found**

The Light Verse described a lamp in a niche. This verse tells us where that niche is located. The grammatical connection is direct: the phrase *fī buyūtin*, "in houses," attaches to what precedes it, so that the whole is read as one continuous image — the lamp of divine light burning *in houses* that God has permitted to be raised. The commentators note several possible grammatical attachments, but the effect of all of them is the same: the parable of light is now anchored in a place.

**Buyūt: Houses — And Which Houses?**

The word is the same one used throughout verses 27 to 29 for the private dwellings whose thresholds must be respected. That repetition is not accidental, and it produces a deliberate ambiguity that the commentators explored in two directions. *The mosques.* The dominant interpretation, reported from Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, al-Ḥasan and others, is that these are the mosques — the houses of God. The description fits: places raised for the remembrance of His name, in which He is glorified morning and evening. *Houses of the believers generally.* A second reading, also reported among the early authorities, extends the reference to any home in which God is remembered — since the Prophet ﷺ made the ordinary house a place of worship in principle, saying:

> *"The whole earth has been made a place of prayer and a means of purification for me."* (al-Bukhārī 335; Muslim 521)

Both readings are sound, and together they make an important point. The mosque is the paradigm case, but the quality that qualifies a building is not its architecture — it is *what happens inside it*. A house in which God is remembered morning and evening is, functionally, one of these houses. A mosque in which He is not is not. And there is a further connection to the sūrah's structure. The homes protected by verses 27 to 29 — whose doors may not be crossed without permission, in which the gaze is guarded and modesty maintained — are precisely the houses that can become the niche of the lamp. The privacy legislated earlier is what makes the sanctity described here possible.

**"That God Has Permitted to Be Raised"**

*Adhina'llāhu an turfaʿa.* The verb *rafaʿa* means to raise, elevate, lift up. It has both a physical and a moral sense, and the commentators took it in both. *Physically*: to build, to construct, to raise the walls. The same verb is used of Abraham and Ishmael:

> *"And when Abraham raised the foundations of the House, with Ishmael: 'Our Lord, accept it from us. Truly Thou art the Hearing, the Knowing.'"* (Qur'an 2:127)

*Morally*: to honor, to hold in high esteem, to keep elevated above what is unworthy of it. On this reading the command is to keep such places dignified — clean, respected, protected from noise, commerce, filth, and frivolity. The reported practice of the early community reflects both. Mosques were built plainly but kept scrupulously. There are reports of the Prophet ﷺ seeing sputum on the wall of the mosque and scraping it away, of his instructing that mosques be built in the tribal quarters and kept clean and perfumed, and of his displeasure at raised voices in them. And the word *adhina* — "God has permitted" — is significant in itself. The building of houses for God's remembrance is not a human initiative that God tolerates; it is something He has authorized. Which is why the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"Whoever builds a mosque for God, God will build for him the like of it in Paradise."* (al-Bukhārī 450; Muslim 533)

**"Wherein His Name Is Remembered"**

*Yudhkaru fīhā ismuhu.* This is the defining activity: *dhikr*, the remembrance of God. *Dhikr* in the Qur'an is a broad term. It covers the recitation of the Qur'an, the formulas of glorification, supplication, the prayer itself, and the study of religious knowledge — anything that brings God to mind. It is the opposite of *ghaflah*, heedlessness, the condition of a person who lives as though God were not there. The centrality of remembrance to the whole religious enterprise is stated in one of the Qur'an's most quoted verses:

> *"Those who believe and whose hearts find rest in the remembrance of God. Behold, in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* (Qur'an 13:28)

And the Prophet ﷺ described the gatherings in which it occurs:

> *"No people gather in one of the houses of God, reciting the Book of God and studying it among themselves, but that tranquillity descends upon them, mercy envelops them, the angels surround them, and God mentions them to those who are with Him."* (Muslim 2699)

Note the correspondence between that hadith and this verse: *houses of God*, *remembrance*, and a descent of light-like presence. The hadith is essentially a commentary on the verse.

**"He Is Therein Glorified, Morning and Evening"**

*Yusabbiḥu lahu fīhā bi'l-ghuduwwi wa'l-āṣāl.* *Tasbīḥ* — glorification — is from the root *s-b-ḥ*, whose primary sense is *swimming* or *moving swiftly through a medium*. To glorify God is, in this image, to move *away from* and *above* all imperfection attributed to Him. *Subḥān Allāh* means: God is far removed from every deficiency — a phrase encountered already at verse 16, where the believers were told they should have said *subḥānaka* on hearing the slander. *Al-ghuduww* is the early morning; *al-āṣāl* the late afternoon and evening. The pairing is a Qur'anic idiom for *continuously*, marking the two ends of the day — but it also corresponds to the actual rhythm of the prescribed prayers, which bracket the day at both edges. The specification of morning and evening carries a practical wisdom worth noticing. These are the two hinges of human time: the point at which a person enters the world of work and the point at which he leaves it. A life anchored by remembrance at both ends is a life in which the intervening hours are framed rather than free-floating. The Qur'an legislates this elsewhere:

> *"And glorify the praise of thy Lord before the rising of the sun and before its setting."* (Qur'an 50:39)

**The Mosque as the Niche**

Read together with the Light Verse, the image now completes itself. The lamp is in a niche; the niche is in a house; the house is one that God permitted to be raised for His remembrance. Anyone who has stood in a traditional mosque at night, looking at a lamp hanging in the recess of the mihrab, has seen the image literally realized — which is exactly why Muslim architects built it that way for a thousand years. But the interior meaning is the one the sūrah is after. The mosque is where the light is *tended*. A flame requires a place where it is sheltered from wind, refilled with oil, and kept burning by people who come regularly to attend to it. That is precisely the function of communal worship: individual faith, left entirely to private effort in the middle of ordinary life, tends to gutter. The congregation is the niche. The Prophet ﷺ made the point with an image of his own. Seeing a man who had stopped attending the congregation, and taking a burning ember from the fire and setting it apart from the rest, he pointed out that it dimmed and died alone — a lesson preserved in the tradition and echoed in his statement:

> *"Adhere to the congregation, for the wolf takes the sheep that strays."* (Abū Dāwūd 547)

**Practical Reflections**

*On mosques.* The verse describes what a mosque is *for*, and the description is startlingly narrow: the remembrance of God, morning and evening. Every community should occasionally measure its mosque against that description. A place that has become primarily a venue for committee politics, ethnic association, or social display has drifted from the terms of its own licence — and a place that is locked outside prayer times, or unwelcoming to the young, the poor, or women, has drifted in a different direction. *On homes.* If the second reading of *buyūt* is sound — and the weight of the tradition supports it — then any household can enter this verse's description by a simple change: that God's name be remembered in it, morning and evening. The Prophet ﷺ contrasted the two kinds of dwelling directly:

> *"The likeness of a house in which God is remembered and a house in which God is not remembered is that of the living and the dead."* (Muslim 779)

That is a severe image, and it identifies exactly what the difference is. A house full of activity, conversation, and entertainment, in which God is never mentioned, is described as a grave. The remedy is not complicated, and the sūrah has been building toward it. The house has been given a door that others may not cross uninvited (v. 27). Within it the eye and the tongue have been disciplined (vv. 30–31). Marriage has been urged to fill it lawfully (v. 32). And now the last requirement is added: that God's name be spoken there at the beginning and the end of every day. Then the lamp has its niche.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:37

> **by men whom neither trade nor buying and selling distract from the remembrance of God, the performance of prayer, and the giving of alms, fearing a day when eyes and hearts will be turned about,**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The People Who Keep the Lamp**

The parable has given us the light, and the previous verse gave us the house. This verse gives us the *people* — the ones who fill those houses and keep the remembrance going morning and evening. And the description is remarkable for what it does not say. It does not describe monks, ascetics, or men who have withdrawn from the world. It describes *merchants*.

**"Neither Trade Nor Buying and Selling"**

*Rijālun lā tulhīhim tijāratun wa lā bayʿun ʿan dhikri'llāh.* The commentators observe that the verse names two things where one might have sufficed. *Tijārah* is trade or commerce in the general sense — the whole business of importing, exporting, wholesale dealing, long-distance caravans. *Bayʿ* is the specific transaction: the individual sale, the exchange at the counter. The pairing therefore covers both the *large scale* and the *small*: the great enterprise that consumes a man's ambition, and the immediate transaction that consumes his attention right now. Some commentators note the distinction as between the merchant's activity and the retailer's, or between the large concern and the daily deal. Both are named because both distract, and they distract differently. The great enterprise distracts by ambition — the project that occupies the mind at prayer and at night. The immediate sale distracts by urgency — the customer standing at the counter when the call to prayer sounds.

**Tulhīhim: The Verb of Being Diverted**

The verb is *alhā*, from the root *l-h-w*, which gives *lahw*: diversion, amusement, distraction, that which occupies a person and turns him away from something more important. It is one of the Qur'an's central diagnostic terms for the human condition. An entire sūrah is named from it:

> *"Rivalry in worldly increase distracts you (alhākumu'l-takāthur), until you visit the graves."* (Qur'an 102:1–2)

And the Book warns believers directly, in language very close to this verse:

> *"O you who believe! Let not your wealth or your children divert you (lā tulhikum) from the remembrance of God. And whosoever does so, it is they who are the losers."* (Qur'an 63:9)

The word does not mean "prevent." It means *divert*. Nothing about commerce forbids a man from praying. It simply occupies the space where prayer would have been, and does so pleasantly, so that the displacement is never noticed as a loss. This is the crucial insight of the verse, and it applies far beyond trade. The great danger to a religious life is almost never opposition; it is *absorption*. Very few believers abandon prayer after being argued out of it. Enormous numbers drift out of it because something else filled the hour.

**What Is Not Being Said: Islam Does Not Condemn Commerce**

It is essential to read this verse correctly, because a superficial reading turns it into a rebuke of business, which it is not. The Qur'an explicitly permits and honors trade:

> *"…whereas God has permitted trade and forbidden usury."* (Qur'an 2:275)

> *"There is no blame upon you in seeking bounty from your Lord."* (Qur'an 2:198 — revealed regarding trading during the pilgrimage season)

And the very sūrah that legislates the Friday congregation releases the worshippers back into the market the moment it ends:

> *"Then when the prayer is completed, disperse throughout the land and seek the bounty of God, and remember God much, that haply you may prosper."* (Qur'an 62:10)

The Prophet ﷺ was himself a merchant before prophethood; Abū Bakr was a cloth merchant; ʿUthmān was among the wealthiest traders of Arabia; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, on arriving in Madinah destitute, famously asked to be shown the market rather than given charity. And the Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"The truthful, trustworthy merchant is with the prophets, the truthful, and the martyrs."* (al-Tirmidhī 1209)

So the verse does not praise men who have no trade. It praises men who *have* trade and are not diverted by it. That is a far higher standard and a far more useful one, because it is achievable by ordinary people living ordinary lives. Ibn ʿAbbās and others are reported to have described these as men who buy and sell, but when the call to prayer comes, they leave the transaction where it stands. That is the picture: not the absence of business, but its subordination.

**"From the Remembrance of God, the Performance of Prayer, and the Giving of Alms"**

Three things are named, and the order is instructive. *Dhikr Allāh* — the remembrance of God. The inward, continuous, unstructured awareness that runs through a day. *Iqām al-ṣalāh* — the establishment of prayer. The formal, timed, structured obligation. The verb *aqāma* means to set up, to make stand upright, to maintain properly — not merely to perform, but to *establish*, with all its conditions and at its time. *Ītāʾ al-zakāh* — the giving of alms. The financial obligation, which touches precisely the wealth that trade produces. The progression moves from the heart, to the body's discipline in time, to the pocket. Each is harder than the last, and each tests the same subordination from a different angle. A man may feel devout and not pray; he may pray and not give. The verse names all three because a religion held in only one of them is incomplete. There is a particular sharpness in placing *zakāh* at the end of a verse about merchants. Wealth is what trade produces, and the alms are the point at which a man's relationship to his wealth is tested in a way no feeling can fake.

**"Fearing a Day When Eyes and Hearts Will Be Turned About"**

*Yakhāfūna yawman tataqallabu fīhi'l-qulūbu wa'l-abṣār.* The verse now names their motive. What keeps these men from being absorbed is not temperament, nor a lack of commercial talent, but *fear of a specific day*. The verb *taqallaba* is from *q-l-b* — the same root as *qalb*, the heart. Its meaning is to turn over, to be overturned, to flip about, to be in a state of agitated reversal. The heart is called *qalb* in Arabic precisely because of its restless turning. So the phrase describes hearts and eyes *convulsing* — turning from state to state in terror on the Day of Judgment. The commentators explain it variously: eyes darting in every direction in fear; hearts alternating between hope of salvation and dread of ruin; hearts rising to the throats, as the Qur'an describes elsewhere:

> *"…when hearts reach the throats, choking with anguish."* (Qur'an 40:18)

> *"Their gaze downcast, humiliation overwhelming them."* (Qur'an 70:44)

**Why This Fear Produces Freedom, Not Anxiety**

There is an apparent paradox in this verse that deserves attention, because it goes to the heart of how religion functions psychologically. The men described are *fearful*. Yet the picture is not one of anxiety. They are engaged in the world, doing business, and are described with evident approval and dignity. The resolution lies in what fear *of the right thing* does. A person who fears the Day of Judgment has, by that very fear, been released from a hundred smaller ones — the fear of losing a deal, of a competitor's advantage, of the loss of status, of the opinion of customers. The great fear consumes the small ones, and what remains is a man who can walk away from a transaction at the sound of the call to prayer without his hands shaking. This is why the Qur'an speaks of *khawf* not as a pathology but as a stabilizer. The person with nothing ultimate to fear is not calm; he is at the mercy of every proximate threat.

**The Modern Form of the Test**

The verse's specific application to commerce is directly transferable, and the transfer barely needs stating. The modern equivalents of *tijārah* and *bayʿ* are the career and the notification: the long-term ambition that occupies the mind, and the immediate demand that occupies the minute. Between them they can consume every waking hour of an intelligent, industrious, and entirely well-intentioned person. And the mechanism is the one the verb names. Nobody decides to stop praying. The meeting runs long. The deadline is tomorrow. The message needs answering. Prayer is deferred to a gap that never opens. The test the verse sets is a simple one, and it is testable in practice: *when the time of prayer arrives, what happens?* Not what one believes, not what one intends, but what actually happens to the hands and the schedule. The men praised here are identified by a single observable behaviour: the transaction stops. There is a practical wisdom in that criterion, and it is one of the reasons the five daily prayers are structured as they are. They are placed at intervals through the working day precisely so that this test recurs five times, at the exact moments when the world is most demanding. A weekly observance can be scheduled around. A prayer at midday and mid-afternoon cannot be.

**The Dignity of the Ordinary Believer**

A final observation, and it may be the most encouraging thing in the verse. The men in the niche of light are not a spiritual elite. They are not described as having renounced anything. They are traders — men with inventories, customers, debts, competitors, and profit margins, doing the same work their neighbours do. What separates them is not their occupation but their *ordering*. They have the same day as everyone else, filled with the same demands, and they have simply refused to let the demands take the top position. That is a standard an ordinary person can actually meet — which is precisely why the Qur'an set it, in a chapter about light, as the description of those who keep the lamp burning.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:38

> **that God may reward them for the best of that which they have done, and increase them from His Bounty. And God provides for whomsoever He will without reckoning.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Wage of Those Who Were Not Distracted**

This verse completes the portrait begun in verse 36 and developed in verse 37. The houses have been described, the people in them described, and now their recompense is stated. The grammar links it directly: they did what they did *so that* God may reward them. The remarkable feature of this verse is that its entire content is generosity, expressed in three escalating stages: reward for the best, increase from bounty, and provision without reckoning. Each stage exceeds the one before, and by the end the language of accounting has been abandoned altogether.

**Stage One: "Reward Them for the Best of What They Have Done"**

*Li-yajziyahumu'llāhu aḥsana mā ʿamilū.* The phrase can be read in two ways, and both are found among the commentators. *He rewards them for the best of their deeds.* On this reading, God takes the *finest* of a person's actions and rewards according to that standard, rather than averaging across a life that necessarily contains poor days, distracted prayers, and mediocre efforts. Consider what that means in practice. Every believer has a handful of moments in which he was genuinely at his best: a prayer in which he was truly present, an act of charity given without any thought of recognition, a night of sincere repentance, a kindness done at real cost. He also has thousands of hours of routine, half-attention, and failure. Human judgment of a person averages the whole. This reading says that God takes the peak as the measure. *He rewards them with something better than what they did.* On this reading, *aḥsana* describes the recompense rather than the deed: the reward exceeds the action. Both readings are consistent with the Qur'an's stated method of accounting:

> *"Whosoever brings a good deed shall have ten times the like thereof; and whosoever brings an evil deed shall be recompensed only with the like thereof, and they shall not be wronged."* (Qur'an 6:160)

The asymmetry is the point. Good is multiplied tenfold at minimum; evil is counted once. And the Prophet ﷺ transmitted a divine saying that extends the asymmetry further:

> *"God has written down good deeds and bad deeds. Whoever intends a good deed and does not do it, God writes it down as one complete good deed. If he intends it and does it, God writes it down as ten to seven hundred times or more. If he intends a bad deed and does not do it, God writes it down as one complete good deed. And if he intends it and does it, God writes it down as one bad deed."* (al-Bukhārī 6491; Muslim 131)

An accounting system in which an *intended and abandoned* sin is recorded as a good deed is not a system of strict justice. It is a system deliberately weighted toward mercy.

**Stage Two: "And Increase Them From His Bounty"**

*Wa yazīdahum min faḍlih.* Having rewarded the deeds, God adds *more* — and the word for the addition is *faḍl*, the surplus favor encountered four times in the slander passage. The distinction between the two stages is important. The first is *jazāʾ*, recompense — what corresponds to the action. The second is *faḍl* — what corresponds to nothing, given because the Giver is generous rather than because the recipient earned it. This distinction runs throughout the Qur'an's picture of the Hereafter. The believer enters Paradise by God's mercy, as the Prophet ﷺ insisted (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816), and then receives *within* it a station corresponding to his deeds, and then receives *beyond* that whatever God adds. The Qur'an names the final addition explicitly:

> *"For those who do good is the most beautiful reward, and more."* (Qur'an 10:26)

The commentators, on the authority of a report from the Prophet ﷺ transmitted by Muslim (181), explain the *"and more"* (*ziyādah*) in that verse as the vision of God's Face — the crowning gift that no deed could ever earn.

**Stage Three: "God Provides for Whomsoever He Will Without Reckoning"**

*Wa'llāhu yarzuqu man yashāʾu bi-ghayri ḥisāb.* The final clause breaks out of the framework entirely. *Bi-ghayri ḥisāb* — "without reckoning," "without measure," "without calculation." The phrase carries at least three senses, and the commentators note them. *Without limit* — the provision is not bounded by a quantity. *Without being counted against the recipient* — that is, not deducted from anything, not charged to an account, not a payment for services rendered. *Without the recipient being called to account for it* — given freely, with no reckoning demanded in return. The phrase appears several times in the Qur'an, always in contexts of overwhelming divine generosity:

> *"…and God provides for whomsoever He will without reckoning."* (Qur'an 2:212; 3:37; 40:40)

In Sūrah Āl ʿImrān it appears in the story of Mary, when Zachariah finds provision with her out of season and asks where it came from, and she replies:

> *"It is from God. Truly God provides for whomsoever He will without reckoning."* (Qur'an 3:37)

That episode is the phrase's best illustration: sustenance arriving from outside the visible causal chain, in a quantity and at a time nobody calculated.

**The Verse as an Answer to the Merchants**

The placement of this promise immediately after the description of the traders is precisely calculated, and it completes an argument that spans three verses. Verse 37 described men who *left transactions* for prayer, who *gave away* wealth in alms, who did not let commerce absorb them. In purely commercial terms, every one of those behaviours is a cost. Time spent in prayer is time not spent earning. Alms are capital removed from the business. The competitor who does not close his shop five times a day has an advantage. The merchant's implicit question is therefore: *what do I lose by this?* Verse 38 answers it in the merchant's own vocabulary and then destroys the vocabulary. Reward: yes, and calculated at your best rather than your average. Increase: yes, beyond what your deeds warrant. And then — *provision without reckoning*, which is to say: stop calculating, because the One you are dealing with does not operate on a ledger. The theological point is that a person who orders his life around God is not making a trade with unfavourable terms. He has stepped out of the market entirely and into a relationship with the Owner of the market. The Qur'an makes this explicit elsewhere, and in commercial language:

> *"O you who believe! Shall I show you a commerce that will deliver you from a painful punishment? Believe in God and His Messenger, and strive in the way of God with your wealth and your selves. That is better for you, if you but knew."* (Qur'an 61:10–11)

**On Rizq: What Provision Actually Is**

Since the verse ends with the word *yarzuqu*, provides, it is worth stating what *rizq* means in the Qur'an, because the term is regularly misunderstood. *Rizq* is not synonymous with money. It is whatever sustains — food, health, knowledge, children, a sound heart, a righteous spouse, time, safety, and the capacity to worship. The Qur'an uses the word for the sustenance of Paradise and for the rain that revives the earth. This matters for reading the promise correctly. The verse is not a guarantee that pious merchants become rich merchants; experience refutes that, and the Qur'an nowhere claims it. It is a guarantee that the One who assigns sustenance is not bound by the calculations that make a person anxious about giving alms or closing the shop for prayer. And the tradition is emphatic that provision is fixed and will arrive. The Prophet ﷺ said:

> *"If you were to rely upon God with the reliance He is due, He would provide for you as He provides for the birds: they go out hungry in the morning and return full in the evening."* (al-Tirmidhī 2344)

The image is precise: the birds are not idle — they *go out* — but they do not carry their provision with them or store it in barns. Effort and trust are both present.

**Living Under an Unlimited Account**

Two practical dispositions follow from this verse. *Generosity becomes rational.* The reason people hesitate to give is the arithmetic of a closed system: what I give is subtracted from what I have. This verse — and the name *al-Wāsiʿ* at verse 32 — denies the premise. The Prophet ﷺ stated the consequence directly: *"Charity does not decrease wealth"* (Muslim 2588). And in a divine saying:

> *"Spend, O son of Adam, and I shall spend on you."* (al-Bukhārī 5352; Muslim 993)

*Anxiety becomes unnecessary.* A person who genuinely believes that provision comes without reckoning from a Provider who cannot be exhausted has been relieved of the low-grade financial fear that occupies most of the waking life of most modern people. That relief is not laziness; the men in this passage are traders. It is the difference between working hard and working *frightened*.

**Closing the Section**

With this verse the positive portrait is complete. Verses 35 to 38 have moved from the light itself, to the houses where it is kept, to the people who keep it, to their reward. What follows in verses 39 and 40 is the counter-image: two parables of what the deeds of those who reject God amount to. The contrast is deliberate and total. Here: light, remembrance, prayer, alms, reward, increase, provision without reckoning. There: a mirage that turns out to be nothing, and darkness so complete that a man cannot see his own hand. The sūrah sets the two side by side and lets the reader choose.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:39

> **As for those who disbelieve, their deeds are like a mirage upon a desert plain which a thirsty man supposes is water, till when he comes upon it, he does not find it to be anything, but finds God there. He will then pay him his reckoning in full, and God is swift in reckoning.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counter-Parable**

The Light Verse gave the image of the believer's inner life: a lamp, sheltered, fed by pure oil, shining. This verse gives the counter-image, and the sūrah places the two within four verses of each other so that the contrast is unavoidable. Where the believer's light was *real and inward*, the disbeliever's works are *apparent and empty*. Where the lamp shone in a protected niche, the mirage shimmers on an open plain. Where the believer's oil was almost luminous before the flame touched it, the mirage produces the appearance of water where there is nothing at all.

**Sarāb: The Mirage**

The word is *sarāb*, and the Arabic root *s-r-b* carries the sense of flowing, running, slipping away. A *sarāb* is the shimmering appearance of water that forms over hot ground — a phenomenon every desert traveller knew intimately, and which the Qur'an's first audience did not need explained. The physics of it, known to us now, only deepens the image. A mirage is produced when a layer of superheated air near the ground bends light rays upward, so that the observer sees a reflected image of the sky at ground level and reads it as water. It is not a hallucination — the light is genuinely arriving at the eye. What is false is not the perception but the *interpretation*: the mind assigns to the image a substance it does not have. That is exactly the Qur'an's charge against the deeds of those who reject God. The deeds are real; the appearance is real; only the *substance* is missing.

**Bi-qīʿatin: The Open Plain**

The mirage forms *bi-qīʿatin* — on a flat, open expanse of low ground with no vegetation. The commentators note that the word denotes precisely the kind of level plain where water might plausibly collect, which is what makes the illusion convincing. The detail is not decorative. A mirage requires two things: heat and *flatness*. It cannot form in broken country. There is a suggestion in the image of an emptiness that has nothing in it to break the illusion — no landmark, no shade, no other feature to give the eye a corrective reference. A life without revelation is described as exactly that kind of terrain: nothing to check the appearance against.

**"Which a Thirsty Man Supposes Is Water"**

*Yaḥsabuhu'l-ẓamʾānu māʾan.* Two words carry the psychology of the parable. *Yaḥsabu* — "he supposes, reckons, calculates" — is the same verb used at verse 15 for the community that "supposed" the slander to be a light matter, and at verse 11 for those who "supposed" the affair to be an evil. The sūrah has been consistently interested in the gap between what people *suppose* and what is *actually the case*, and here the theme reaches its most vivid expression. *Al-ẓamʾān* — "the thirsty one." This is the detail that turns a natural phenomenon into a tragedy. The mirage is not merely a curiosity. It appears to a man who is *dying for water*. His need is genuine, his desperation is genuine, and the illusion is calibrated exactly to what he most wants. That is why he believes it: not because he is stupid, but because he is thirsty. And thirst is the Qur'an's chosen image for the deepest human need — the need for meaning, for permanence, for something that will finally satisfy. The parable is therefore not mocking the disbeliever. It is diagnosing him with considerable sympathy: here is a man with a real need, running toward what appears to answer it, and arriving at nothing.

**"Till When He Comes Upon It, He Does Not Find It To Be Anything"**

*Ḥattā idhā jāʾahu lam yajidhu shayʾan.* The mirage has one further property that the parable exploits perfectly: *it recedes*. A traveller can walk toward it for hours, and it maintains its distance, always appearing to be just ahead, until either the sun moves or the ground cools or he simply arrives at the place where it seemed to be — and finds dust. The moment of arrival is the parable's hinge. Everything up to that point was expenditure of effort in hope; at that point the account is settled, and the settlement is *nothing*. The Qur'an describes the deeds of the disbelievers in the Hereafter with a family of related images, each conveying the same discovery of emptiness at the moment of arrival:

> *"And We shall turn to whatever deeds they have done and make them as scattered dust."* (Qur'an 25:23)

> *"The parable of those who disbelieve in their Lord: their deeds are as ashes upon which the wind blows fiercely on a stormy day. They have no power over aught they have earned. That is the far-off error."* (Qur'an 14:18)

> *"Say: Shall we inform you of the greatest losers in respect of their deeds? Those whose efforts go astray in the life of this world, while they reckon that they are doing good."* (Qur'an 18:103–104)

That last passage names the same tragedy: not people who knew they were wasting their lives, but people who *reckoned they were doing well*.

**"But Finds God There"**

*Wa wajada'llāha ʿindahu fa-waffāhu ḥisābah.* This is the most arresting sentence in the verse, and it deserves to be read slowly. He ran toward water. He arrived. There was no water. *There was God.* The reversal is total. What he sought was an illusion; what he found was the ultimate Reality. And it is worth recalling that fourteen verses earlier this sūrah named God *al-Ḥaqq al-Mubīn* — the Manifest Truth, the Real (v. 25). The parable is the enacted form of that name: the pursuit of the unreal terminates in an encounter with the Real. There is a further precision in the verb *waffā* — "he paid him his account in full." It is the same verb used at verse 25 for the settlement of the slanderers' account: *yuwaffīhimu'llāhu dīnahumu'l-ḥaqq.* The vocabulary of complete payment recurs deliberately. The reckoning is exact, and it is discharged without shortfall. The commentators note the poignancy of the arrangement: the man does not arrive at *nothing*. Arriving at nothing would at least be a neutral disappointment. He arrives at *the reckoning*.

**Why Are Their Deeds Void?**

A serious question arises here, and the Qur'an raises it deliberately. Are the good deeds of a non-believer worthless? What of the honest, generous, self-sacrificing person who does not believe? Several observations are needed, and they should be made carefully. First, the Qur'an does not deny that such deeds are *good in themselves* or that they *benefit others*. Charity feeds the hungry regardless of the giver's creed. Second, the Qur'an consistently teaches that God does not wrong anyone, and the tradition records that worldly good is recompensed with worldly good. The Prophet ﷺ said of a man who had asked whether his pre-Islamic acts of kindness would benefit him:

> *"You accepted Islam upon the good you had already done."* (al-Bukhārī 1436; Muslim 123)

Third, the parable concerns the *ultimate* accounting, and the principle it states is one about the relationship between deeds and their end. A deed's eternal weight depends on the direction in which it was aimed. An action performed for reputation is paid in reputation; an action performed for profit is paid in profit; an action performed for God is paid by God. The Prophet ﷺ stated the principle as the foundation of the entire religion:

> *"Actions are only by intentions, and every man shall have only that which he intended. So whoever emigrated for God and His Messenger, his emigration was for God and His Messenger; and whoever emigrated to attain some worldly goal or to marry a woman, his emigration was for that for which he emigrated."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907)

This is not arbitrary. It is a statement about what a deed *is*. An arrow's destination is determined by where it was aimed, and no amount of force compensates for a wrong direction. Fourth — and this is the mercy in the doctrine — the verse concerns those who *reject* after the truth has become clear to them. The Qur'an is explicit that no one is held responsible for a message that never reached them: *"And We never punish until We have sent a messenger"* (17:15).

**The Modern Mirages**

The parable transfers to contemporary life with almost no adjustment, and it is worth naming the modern forms honestly. The most obvious is *accumulation*: the conviction that a sufficient quantity of money, property, or security will finally produce arrival. It is the purest mirage, because the target moves with the traveller — every acquired level reveals a higher one, and the Qur'an's own diagnosis is that this continues *"until you visit the graves"* (102:2). Then *status*: the promotion, the title, the recognition, each of which delivers a satisfaction that decays within weeks and reveals a further rank. Then *the audience*: the modern pursuit of visibility, which is perhaps the most exact mirage of all, since it consists literally in an image seen by people who are not there. And, most subtly, *the project of self-improvement pursued as an end in itself*: the endless optimization of a life whose purpose is never named. In each case the structure is identical: a genuine thirst, an appearance that answers it, a long expenditure of effort, and an arrival that produces nothing. The parable's power is that it does not deny the thirst. It says the thirst is real and the water is elsewhere.

**"And God Is Swift in Reckoning"**

*Wa'llāhu sarīʿu'l-ḥisāb.* The closing name is precisely placed. The commentators explain *swift in reckoning* in several ways: that the Day will come sooner than people imagine; that God needs no time to compute, since He does not calculate as creatures calculate; and that the accounting of the entire creation will be accomplished at once, without queues or delay. Al-Qurṭubī and others note that the reckoning of one does not distract Him from the reckoning of another — the whole of humanity is settled with simultaneously, each in complete detail. The rhetorical function here is to close the escape route the parable might otherwise leave open. A man might read the verse and think: *the arrival is far off; I have time.* The final clause removes the comfort. The reckoning is not slow, and the distance to it is not what it appears — any more than the water was.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:40

> **Or like the darkness of a fathomless sea, covered by waves with waves above them and clouds above them—darknesses, one above the other. When one puts out one's hand, one can hardly see it. He for whom God has not appointed any light has no light.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Parable: From Illusion to Blindness**

The previous verse described the *deeds* of those who reject God: a mirage, an appearance with nothing behind it. This verse describes their *state*: total darkness. The two parables are joined by *aw* — "or" — indicating that they are alternative images of one condition, each capturing a different aspect. And they are opposites in setting, which is part of their power. The mirage occurs in a blazing desert, in an excess of light and heat, on flat open ground. This parable occurs in the depths of the sea, in an absolute absence of light, under crushing layers. Between them they cover the whole range of human disorientation: the man who sees what is not there, and the man who cannot see at all.

**The Layered Structure of the Image**

*Aw ka-ẓulumātin fī baḥrin lujjiyyin yaghshāhu mawjun min fawqihi mawjun min fawqihi saḥāb — ẓulumātun baʿḍuhā fawqa baʿḍ.* The Arabic builds the image by stacking, and the repetition of *min fawqihi* — "above it… above it" — is the point of the construction. The reader is made to feel the accumulation: The *deep sea* itself (*baḥrin lujjiyy* — from *lujjah*, the deep, fathomless middle of the ocean, far from any shore). Above that, a *wave*. Above that, *another wave*. Above that, *clouds*. And the verse then names the sum: *ẓulumātun baʿḍuhā fawqa baʿḍ* — "darknesses, one above another." Note again the plural. As discussed at the Light Verse, the Qur'an uses *nūr* in the singular and *ẓulumāt* in the plural without exception. Light is one; darknesses are many and layered. Here the plural is not merely doctrinal but literally descriptive: the verse enumerates four distinct sources of darkness stacked upon one another.

**What the Image Knows About the Sea**

There is a striking feature of this description that deserves comment. The verse locates darkness in *the depths* of the sea, and describes the deep as a place where a man could not see his own hand. It also describes an *internal wave* — a wave with another wave above it, beneath the surface — which is not the ordinary experience of a person looking at the sea from a boat or a shore. Seventh-century Arabia was not a seafaring civilization in the manner of the Greeks or the Phoenicians, and no one could descend into the deep ocean to observe it. The description of the abyssal depths as a place of absolute, layered darkness, and of waves existing *beneath* other waves, is at minimum a remarkable image. Modern oceanography describes the progressive extinction of light with depth — the surface layer, then the twilight zone, then the aphotic depths where no sunlight penetrates at all — and describes internal waves that occur at the boundaries between water layers of different densities, beneath the visible surface waves. The point should not be overstated, and the classical commentators read the verse simply as a powerful description of terror and blindness at sea. But the image is unusually apt, and it rewards the reader who knows what the deep ocean is actually like.

**"When One Puts Out One's Hand, One Can Hardly See It"**

*Idhā akhraja yadahu lam yakad yarāhā.* This is the image's climax, and it is chosen with great psychological precision. The verse does not say he cannot see the horizon, or the shore, or the sky. It says he cannot see *his own hand* — the nearest object in the universe to him, the thing he knows best, the instrument by which he acts. That is what total spiritual darkness means in the Qur'anic account: not merely ignorance of God or of ultimate things, but *loss of self-knowledge*. A person in that state does not know his own motives, cannot assess his own conduct, and cannot see what his own hand is doing. Everything in this sūrah's first half illustrates the condition: people who repeated a slander and thought it was a light matter; people who entered houses and thought nothing of it; people who looked and did not consider what looking does. And note the precise wording: *lam yakad yarāhā* — "he could *hardly* see it," or on a stricter reading, "he was not even close to seeing it." The Arabic *lam yakad* is stronger than a simple negation. It denies not merely the seeing but the *approach* to seeing.

**"He For Whom God Has Not Appointed Any Light Has No Light"**

*Wa man lam yajʿali'llāhu lahu nūran fa-mā lahu min nūr.* The verse ends with a statement of stark theological finality, and its structure in Arabic is emphatic: the negation is doubled, so that the sense is "there is not, for him, *any* light whatsoever." This is the direct counterpart to the Light Verse's conclusion: *"God guides unto His Light whomsoever He will."* The two statements bracket the section, and together they state a single doctrine from its two sides. Light is given. Where it is not given, there is none — and no substitute exists.

**The Doctrine and Its Difficulty**

This is the point at which a reader may feel the theological pressure most acutely. If light is appointed by God, is the man in the dark not simply unfortunate? The Qur'an's own answer, given across many passages, has several parts, and they must be held together. *God does not withhold arbitrarily.* The Book repeatedly ties the deprivation of light to a prior refusal. Those described in this passage are, in the verse's own opening words, *alladhīna kafarū* — those who have *rejected*. The root *k-f-r* means to cover over, to conceal — a farmer covering seed with soil is a *kāfir* in the literal sense. The image is of a person who *covers* a truth that was available. The darkness described is thus, at least in part, self-imposed: they covered the light, and then they could not see. *The seeking of light is itself commanded and answered.* The Qur'an's standing promise is unambiguous: *"And those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them in Our ways"* (29:69). *And the deprivation follows a process.* The Book describes hearts becoming sealed, but always as a consequence rather than an arbitrary initial condition:

> *"Nay, but that which they used to earn has covered their hearts with rust."* (Qur'an 83:14)

The Prophet ﷺ described the same mechanism:

> *"When a servant commits a sin, a black spot is placed upon his heart. If he desists, seeks forgiveness, and repents, his heart is polished. But if he returns, it is increased until it overcomes his heart — and that is the rust which God mentioned."* (al-Tirmidhī 3334)

So the picture is not of a lamp arbitrarily withheld but of a window progressively occluded by the occupant. The layered darkness of the parable — wave upon wave upon cloud — is an image of exactly that accumulation.

**Why Darkness Compounds**

The layering is worth dwelling on, because it describes something observable. Spiritual darkness is not a single condition; it accumulates in strata, and each layer makes the next more likely and harder to remove. *The first layer*: a sin committed knowingly. The conscience registers it. *The second*: the justification constructed to make it tolerable. Now the intellect is enlisted against the conscience. *The third*: the habit formed once justification has succeeded. The act is no longer a decision. *The fourth*: the identity built around the habit, at which point recognizing the wrong would require dismantling the self. *And above all of them*: the company of others in the same condition, which functions like the clouds in the parable — cutting off even the diffuse light that might have reached through. At the end of that accumulation, the person genuinely cannot see his own hand. Not because he is lying, but because every faculty that might have shown him has been layered over. This is the state the Qur'an calls *ẓulumāt baʿḍuhā fawqa baʿḍ*, and its most frightening feature is that from the inside it does not feel like darkness. It feels like normality.

**Ẓulm and Ẓulumāt: A Root Worth Noticing**

The Arabic word for darkness, *ẓulmah*, shares its root with *ẓulm*, injustice or wrongdoing — the word used throughout the Qur'an for oppression and for wronging oneself. The lexicographers explain *ẓulm* as *putting a thing where it does not belong*. The connection to darkness is that in the dark, everything is misplaced: one cannot find the right position for anything, including oneself. This etymological link makes a moral point that the sūrah has been building throughout. Injustice and blindness are the same phenomenon viewed from different angles. The slanderer could not see; the man who entered without permission could not see; the one who looked where he should not could not see. And each of them, in the same act, put something where it did not belong.

**The Two Parables Together**

Standing back, verses 39 and 40 form a diptych, and their pairing is complete. *The mirage* addresses those who are *active*: people whose lives are full of striving, achievement, and deeds. Their problem is not inertia but direction. They arrive and find nothing. *The darkness* addresses those who are *lost*: people who cannot act meaningfully at all, because they cannot see. Their problem is not effort but orientation. Between them, they cover the two failure modes of a life without light: the one who runs toward the wrong thing, and the one who cannot move because he cannot see. And both are placed immediately after the portrait of the alternative: a lamp in a niche, in a house of remembrance, tended by men whom trade does not distract, rewarded beyond reckoning.

**The Practical Question**

The verse's closing sentence leaves the reader with a question rather than a rule, and it is the most personal question in the sūrah: *is there light here, or not?* The tradition supplies the tests. Does the heart feel the weight of a sin, or has it stopped registering? Is there any pull toward prayer, or only obligation? Does the Qur'an, when heard, do anything at all? And it supplies the response for anyone who finds the answers troubling — which is not despair but the request that this entire section has been pointing toward. Light is appointed, and the One who appoints it invites the asking. The Prophet's ﷺ own supplication, quoted at verse 35, is the appropriate reply to this verse: *"O God, place light in my heart, and light in my hearing, and light in my sight… and make for me a light."* A man in the dark cannot manufacture light. He can call out. That is precisely what the parable leaves him able to do.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:41

> **Hast thou not considered that God is glorified by whosoever is in the heavens and on the earth, and by the birds spreading their wings? Each indeed knows its prayer and its glorification, and God knows that which they do.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Parables to Evidence**

The sūrah now turns from images to observation. Having given the parable of light and the two counter-parables of darkness, it directs the reader outward, to the world itself: *a lam tara* — "have you not seen?" The phrase is a Qur'anic formula introducing evidence available to anyone. It occurs three times in this passage — here, at verse 43 concerning the clouds, and implicitly at verses 44 and 45 concerning the night and day and the creatures made from water. The section is a sustained argument from the visible world, and its placement is deliberate: after the statement that God appoints light to whom He wills, the reader is shown where the light shines.

**"Have You Not Seen": A Command to Look**

The verb *raʾā* means to see, but in this idiom it means to *consider*, to take note of, to reflect upon what is already in front of one. It is not asking whether the addressee has ever observed a bird. It is asking whether he has *understood* what he has been looking at his whole life. This is one of the Qur'an's most characteristic methods, and it distinguishes its argument for God from the abstract proofs of the philosophers. It does not begin with metaphysics. It begins with clouds, rain, birds, night, day, and animals — the ordinary furniture of existence — and asks the reader to attend to it properly. The Book calls these things *āyāt*, signs, the same word it uses for its own verses.

**"Whosoever Is in the Heavens and on the Earth"**

*Yusabbiḥu lahu man fi'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ.* The claim is universal: *everything* glorifies God. The Qur'an states this repeatedly and with emphasis:

> *"The seven heavens, and the earth, and whosoever is in them glorify Him. And there is no thing, save that it hymns His praise, though you do not understand their glorification. Truly He is Clement, Forgiving."* (Qur'an 17:44)

That verse is the fullest statement of the doctrine, and its final clause is crucial: *"though you do not understand their glorification."* Human beings are explicitly told that they are not equipped to perceive it. The commentators divided over the mode of this universal praise. Some understood it as *ḥāl al-lisān* — the "speech of state": every created thing, by its very existence, structure, and functioning, testifies to the wisdom and power of its Maker, as a building testifies to its architect without speaking. Others held it to be a real glorification, of a kind proper to each creature and inaccessible to us. Al-Qurṭubī records both positions, and the tradition preserves reports supporting the second — the food that glorified while being eaten in the presence of the Prophet ﷺ, the pebbles that were heard to glorify in his hand, the moaning of the palm-trunk he had leaned on when he left it for a pulpit (al-Bukhārī 3583). The verse before us seems to favor the stronger reading, since it says each creature *knows* its prayer and its glorification — and knowledge is not ordinarily predicated of a mere testimony of structure.

**"And the Birds Spreading Their Wings"**

*Wa'l-ṭayru ṣāffāt.* Of all creatures, the birds are singled out. Why? *Ṣāffāt* is a feminine plural active participle from *ṣ-f-f*, the root of *ṣaff*, a row or rank — the same root as the *ṣufūf*, the straight lines of worshippers in congregational prayer, and the name of Sūrah 61, *al-Ṣaff*. Applied to birds it describes wings *held out straight and extended*, as in gliding or soaring flight, as opposed to flapping. The choice is rich. A bird in gliding flight holds a posture of extension and stillness that resembles nothing so much as a worshipper standing with arms outstretched. The Arabic word connects it directly to the ranks of prayer. And the Qur'an elsewhere describes birds in flight as a sign requiring attention:

> *"Have they not considered the birds above them, spreading and folding their wings? None holds them aloft save the Compassionate. Truly He sees all things."* (Qur'an 67:19)

There is also the sheer strangeness of flight as an object of contemplation. A creature suspended in air, held up by nothing visible, moving with an accuracy no engineer can fully replicate — the migrating bird navigating thousands of miles to a specific field, the swift that remains airborne for months, the flock turning as a single body. The Qur'an points at this and asks what holds them up. And birds are associated with prophets in the Book: with David, who was given the mountains and the birds to glorify along with him (21:79; 34:10); with Solomon, who was taught the speech of birds (27:16). The tradition treats the birds as a community with their own order, as the Qur'an states:

> *"There is no creature that crawls upon the earth, nor bird that flies upon its wings, but that they are communities like yourselves."* (Qur'an 6:38)

**"Each Indeed Knows Its Prayer and Its Glorification"**

*Kullun qad ʿalima ṣalātahu wa tasbīḥah.* This is the verse's most remarkable claim, and its grammar has been read in two ways by the commentators. *Each creature knows its own prayer.* On this reading, every created thing has been taught a form of worship proper to its nature and performs it knowingly. The bird's worship is not the human's; the tree's is not the bird's. Each has been assigned a mode and each knows it. *God knows the prayer of each.* On the alternative reading, the implied subject of "knows" is God: each creature has a prayer, and *He* knows it. The final clause of the verse — *"and God knows that which they do"* — supports this reading by restating it. The first reading is the more striking and is widely adopted, and the two are not incompatible: each knows its own, and He knows all of them. The theological implication is significant. Worship is *native* to creation. Human beings are not introducing an activity into a neutral universe; they are joining something already underway. Everything else is already doing it, and doing it without deviation. This reframes the human situation entirely: the person who does not pray is not exercising a neutral liberty. He is the one creature out of step in a cosmos that is otherwise unanimous. The Qur'an makes the point explicitly:

> *"Hast thou not considered that unto God prostrates whosoever is in the heavens and whosoever is on the earth, and the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, the beasts, and many among mankind? But for many the punishment has become due."* (Qur'an 22:18)

Note the asymmetry in that verse: everything is listed without qualification, until it reaches mankind — and then it says *many* among mankind. Only the human being is a partial case.

**Why Human Worship Is Different**

This raises the natural question: if everything already glorifies God, what is the value of human worship? The answer the tradition gives is that the human being alone worships *by choice*. The bird's glorification is its nature; it cannot do otherwise. The human being was given the capacity to refuse — which is precisely what makes his compliance meaningful, and what makes him, in the Qur'an's account, a bearer of the trust that the heavens and the earth declined:

> *"Truly We offered the Trust unto the heavens and the earth and the mountains, but they refused to bear it, and were wary of it. Yet man bore it; truly he has proved himself an ignorant wrongdoer."* (Qur'an 33:72)

So the value of the human prayer lies in its being *given* rather than automatic. A creature that could have withheld its worship and did not has offered something the sun cannot offer.

**The Place of This Verse in the Sūrah**

Why does this appear here, after the parables of light and darkness? Because the previous verse ended in absolute blindness: *"he for whom God has not appointed any light has no light."* The natural question is *where would one find light?* — and the answer this verse begins to give is: *look around you*. The signs are not hidden. They are in every bird overhead. There is also a rebuke embedded in the arrangement. The sūrah has spent its first half correcting a community that would not discipline its tongue, its eyes, or its doorways. Now it points to the birds, which have never once deviated from what they were made for. The implicit comparison is one the Qur'an makes elsewhere with unflattering directness about those who reject guidance despite possessing intellect.

**"And God Knows That Which They Do"**

*Wa'llāhu ʿalīmun bimā yafʿalūn.* The closing name is *ʿalīm*, and its function here is twofold. It affirms that the glorification is *received*. Worship offered by creatures whose language no human understands is not lost; the One it is addressed to knows it exactly. And by extension, the same is true of the believer's own worship — the prayer offered in an empty room, the remembrance no one heard, the tear no one saw. And it carries the quiet warning that has run through the whole sūrah: He knows what they do. In a chapter that has spent forty verses on the discrepancy between what people suppose and what is actually the case, the recurring closing name is the correction.

**Living With This Verse**

The practical output of this verse is a habit of attention. The Qur'an is asking for something that costs nothing and that almost no one does: to look at an ordinary thing — a bird crossing a window, rain on a roof, the turn of the light in the evening — and to register it as a *sign* rather than as scenery. The faculty required is not intelligence but attention, and its enemy is not doubt but *ghaflah*, heedlessness. The Qur'an praises exactly this practice and connects it to the deepest form of prayer:

> *"Truly in the creation of the heavens and the earth, and in the alternation of night and day, are signs for those possessed of intellect — those who remember God standing, sitting, and lying upon their sides, and reflect upon the creation of the heavens and the earth: 'Our Lord, Thou hast not created this in vain. Glory be to Thee! Preserve us from the punishment of the Fire.'"* (Qur'an 3:190–191)

That is the response the sūrah is asking for. Not a conclusion argued to, but a noticing — and then joining what is already going on.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:42

> **And unto God belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth, and unto God is the journey's end.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Short Verse Between Two Signs**

This brief verse sits between the sign of the birds (v. 41) and the sign of the clouds (v. 43). It is not an interruption but a *conclusion drawn* from the first and a *premise supplied* for the second. Having said that all creation glorifies God, the verse states the reason: it is all His. And having stated that, it adds the destination: it all returns to Him. Two clauses, both beginning with *wa li'llāh* — "and to God belongs." The repetition of the divine name in place of a pronoun is a feature of Qur'anic emphasis: the Arabic could have said "and to Him," but says "and to God" twice, so that the ownership and the destination are each stamped with the name.

**Mulk: Sovereignty as Ownership**

*Wa li'llāhi mulku'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ.* The word *mulk* is from the root *m-l-k*, which yields both *malik* (king) and *mālik* (owner). Arabic does not sharply separate the two concepts, and the Qur'an exploits the overlap. Divine sovereignty is not merely political authority over a domain that exists independently; it is *proprietorship* over a domain He brought into being. The distinction matters. A human king rules territory he did not make and will not take with him. His authority depends on consent, force, or custom, and it ends. Divine *mulk* is of another kind entirely: the thing ruled is the thing made, and there is no moment at which it was not His. The Qur'an insists on this exclusivity:

> *"Say: O God, Master of sovereignty, Thou givest sovereignty to whomsoever Thou wilt, and Thou wrestest sovereignty from whomsoever Thou wilt. Thou exaltest whomsoever Thou wilt, and Thou abasest whomsoever Thou wilt. In Thy Hand is the good. Truly Thou art Powerful over all things."* (Qur'an 3:26)

Note the structure of that verse: human sovereignty exists, but it is *loaned*. Every throne is held on a lease from the Owner, terminable at will.

**Why This Statement Belongs in This Sūrah**

The placement is not incidental, and the connection to the chapter's first half repays attention. Sūrat al-Nūr has spent thirty-odd verses regulating things that human beings ordinarily consider *their own*: their bodies, their eyes, their homes, their reputations, their marriages, their wealth, their slaves. Every one of those regulations meets the same objection, spoken or unspoken: *this is mine, and I will decide.* This verse answers the objection at its root. There is no domain of absolute private ownership, because ownership belongs to God. The body is His, and so its use is regulated. The wealth is His — verse 33 said so explicitly, *"give them from the wealth of God which He has given you."* The household is His. The tongue is His. Islamic ethics rests on this, and it is worth stating as a principle: *the moral authority of the Lawgiver derives from the fact that He is the Owner*. This is not an arbitrary claim of power. A person who has been given something on trust does not have the standing to complain about conditions attached to the trust.

**Al-Maṣīr: The Journey's End**

*Wa ila'llāhi'l-maṣīr.* The second clause names the destination. *Maṣīr* is from the root *ṣ-y-r*, meaning to become, to come to be, to end up as. A *maṣīr* is where a thing *ends up* — its outcome, its terminus, the state it finally arrives at. The English "journey's end" captures it well. The word contains the sense of a process completing. The Qur'an uses *maṣīr* frequently and always with finality:

> *"…and unto God is the journey's end."* (Qur'an 3:28; 5:18; 24:42; 35:18; 42:15; 64:3)

> *"O our people, respond to the caller of God and believe in him; He will forgive you your sins and shield you from a painful punishment."* (Qur'an 46:31 — and the following verse warns that those who do not are in manifest error)

**The Two Clauses Together: Origin-Ownership and Destination**

The verse's structure yields a complete statement of the human situation in eight Arabic words. *Everything belongs to God* — that is the situation now.
*Everything returns to God* — that is the situation ahead. Between them there is no gap in which anything is autonomous. A person's life is bounded at both ends by the same fact. The Qur'an states the same double truth in the formula every Muslim utters at a death, or indeed at any calamity:

> *"…who, when affliction befalls them, say, 'Truly we are God's, and unto Him we return.'"* (Qur'an 2:156)

*Innā li'llāh* — the ownership. *Wa innā ilayhi rājiʿūn* — the return. It is precisely this verse in personal form, and the fact that Muslims say it at moments of loss shows how the doctrine actually functions: it is a consolation, because what was taken was never one's property in the first place, and because the separation is not final.

**The Psychological Effect of Believing This**

A doctrine of this kind is not merely a proposition to be assented to. If it is genuinely held, it produces a specific character, and it is worth describing what that character looks like. *It produces a particular relationship to possessions.* A person who understands himself as a trustee rather than an owner holds things more lightly. He is neither miserly, since it is not ultimately his, nor careless, since he will be asked about it. The Prophet ﷺ described exactly this accountability:

> *"The feet of a servant will not move on the Day of Resurrection until he is asked about four things: about his life and how he spent it, about his knowledge and what he did with it, about his wealth — how he earned it and how he spent it — and about his body and how he used it."* (al-Tirmidhī 2417)

*It produces courage before human power.* If sovereignty belongs to God and all other authority is loaned, then the person who threatens you holds a lease, not a title. This is the theological source of the extraordinary frankness of the early Muslims before kings, and of the tradition's insistence that *"the best struggle is a word of truth before a tyrannical ruler"* (Abū Dāwūd 4344). *It produces perspective on ambition.* Every project, career, and empire has the same *maṣīr*. This is not a counsel of despair — the sūrah has just praised working merchants — but a correction of proportion.

**The Sign Between the Signs**

One further point about placement. This verse stands between two arguments from nature: the birds above, and the clouds above. That is fitting, because both signs are *overhead*. The reader has been made to look up at the birds, and is about to be made to look up at the clouds. Between the two glances, the verse tells him who owns the sky he is looking at, and where he himself is going. There is a quiet integration here of the sūrah's two halves. The first half legislated the ground: houses, thresholds, marriages, tongues. The second half points at the sky.
