And this verse joins them: the One who owns the sky is the One who legislated the door, and the person walking between them is on a journey with a known terminus.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:43

> **Has thou not considered that God drives the clouds, then joins them together, then makes them as a heap, and then thou seest the rain come forth from amid them? And He sends down from the sky—from the mountains [of clouds] therein—hail, wherewith He smites whomsoever He will, and which He turns away from whomsoever He will. The flash of His lightning well-nigh takes away sight.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Invitation to Look**

*A lam tara* again — "have you not seen?" The formula that introduced the birds now introduces the weather, and the sūrah continues its argument from the ordinary and the visible. The choice of subject is significant. Rain is, in the Qur'an, the single most frequently invoked natural sign. It appears dozens of times, and always with the same argumentative function: it is *life arriving from above, on a schedule no one controls, from a source no one can manufacture*. For an agricultural society in an arid land, nothing was more consequential and nothing more clearly outside human power.

**The Sequence of Verbs: A Meteorological Description**

What distinguishes this verse from other Qur'anic mentions of rain is its *process description*. It does not simply say God sends rain. It traces a sequence of stages, each with its own verb: *Yuzjī saḥāban* — "He drives the clouds." The verb *azjā* means to drive gently, to urge forward slowly, as one drives a herd. The Qur'an uses the same verb of ships being driven across the sea (17:66). The image is of clouds being *moved* across the sky, not appearing where they stand. *Thumma yuʾallifu baynahu* — "then He joins them together." The verb *allafa* is from the root of *ulfah*, affection and familiarity — it means to bring into harmony, to unite things into a coherent whole. The same verb describes God uniting the hearts of the believers (8:63; 3:103). Scattered clouds are here described as being *composed* into one body. *Thumma yajʿaluhu rukāman* — "then He makes it a heap." *Rukām* means something piled up, accumulated vertically, stacked. The word describes exactly what the great rain-bearing clouds do: build upward into towering masses. *Fa-tara'l-wadqa yakhruju min khilālih* — "then you see the rain coming out from between them." *Khilāl* means the interstices, the gaps, the spaces in between — a precise word for rain emerging from within the body of the cloud rather than from its surface. The sequence — driving, merging, vertical accumulation, precipitation — corresponds recognizably to what modern meteorology describes in the formation of cumulonimbus clouds: horizontal transport by wind, coalescence of smaller clouds, vertical development, and finally precipitation from within the built-up mass. The classical commentators, without that vocabulary, read the passage as a description of the visible stages of a rainstorm, and rightly emphasized what the sequence is for: to make the observer see *agency* where he ordinarily sees only weather. Each verb has God as its subject.

**"From the Sky, From Mountains Therein, Hail"**

*Wa yunazzilu mina'l-samāʾi min jibālin fīhā min barad.* The phrase *min jibālin fīhā* — "from mountains in it" — has been read in two ways, and both are found in the commentaries. *Mountains of cloud.* The great storm clouds are themselves described as mountains, an image any observer of a towering thunderhead will recognize immediately. Hail comes down from these cloud-mountains. *Mountains in the sky.* Some early commentators read it more literally as referring to masses in the sky from which hail descends. The first reading is the more widely adopted and the more natural, and it produces a striking metaphor: the sky contains mountains. Anyone who has watched a summer storm build has seen exactly that — a vertical wall of cloud with the mass and presence of a mountain range, and modern measurement confirms that the largest cumulonimbus formations reach heights greater than Everest.

**Hail: The Sign With Two Faces**

*Fa-yuṣību bihi man yashāʾu wa yaṣrifuhu ʿan man yashāʾ* — "He strikes with it whomsoever He wills, and turns it away from whomsoever He wills." The mention of hail introduces something the mention of rain alone would not: *the same sky delivers benefit and damage*. Rain gives life to the fields; hail from the same cloud destroys the crop it falls on. And the verse is explicit that both the striking and the turning-away are willed. The commentators note the theological point. Nature is not a neutral machine producing outcomes indifferently; nor is it a benevolent force that only gives. It is governed, and the governance includes withholding and striking. This is a sober and honest view of the world, and it does not flinch from the fact that the same system which sustains us can destroy us. There is a mercy in the phrasing too: *"and turns it away from whomsoever He wills."* The hail that did *not* fall on your field is also an act of God, and it is one that no one notices. The verse asks the reader to count the disasters that did not happen as blessings, which is a discipline almost nobody practises.

**Lightning: "Well-Nigh Takes Away Sight"**

*Yakādu sanā barqihi yadhhabu bi'l-abṣār.* The verse ends with lightning, and the phrasing is exact. *Sanā* means the brilliant flash, the intense radiance. And *yakādu* — "it almost" — is the same construction used at the Light Verse ("its oil would *almost* shine") and at verse 40 ("he could *hardly* see his hand"). The sūrah uses this word three times, at three key moments, and here it means: the flash is so bright it comes near to destroying vision. The placement of lightning at the end of this passage, in a sūrah named for light, is not accidental. The chapter has now given three distinct lights. *The lamp in the niche* (v. 35): steady, sheltered, sustained, illuminating the space around it. This is the light of guidance. *The mirage* (v. 39): light that deceives, producing an appearance of what is not there. *The lightning* (v. 43): overwhelming, instantaneous, revealing everything for a fraction of a second and then leaving the observer more blinded than before. The contrast between the lamp and the lightning is the most instructive. Lightning is incomparably more powerful than a lamp, and incomparably less useful. It does not help you find your way; it dazzles you. The Qur'an makes this comparison explicitly in al-Baqarah, describing the hypocrites:

> *"The lightning well-nigh snatches away their sight. Whenever it gives them light, they walk therein; and when darkness comes over them, they halt."* (Qur'an 2:20)

That is a devastating portrait of a certain kind of religiosity: intermittent, dependent on dramatic moments of illumination, walking when the flash comes and stopping when it passes. The lesson the sūrah is teaching by its arrangement is that the believer is asked for the *lamp*, not the lightning. Steady, modest, protected, continuous light — the kind that is fed with oil and kept in a niche and tended morning and evening — rather than the spectacular episode that dazzles and fades. Anyone who has experienced a moment of intense religious feeling and then found himself unchanged a month later has experienced the difference.

**The Wider Qur'anic Treatment of Rain**

The signs of rain in the Qur'an typically carry a second meaning, and it is present here by implication: rain is the standing image of *resurrection*.

> *"And it is He who sends the winds as glad tidings before His Mercy, till, when they bear heavy clouds, We drive them to a dead land, and We send down water thereby, and bring forth thereby every kind of fruit. Thus shall We bring forth the dead, that haply you may remember."* (Qur'an 7:57)

The argument is direct: you have watched dead ground turn green every single year of your life. The revival of the dead is not a stranger proposition than the one you observe annually and find unremarkable.

**Why Natural Signs Persuade**

There is a methodological point worth drawing out, because it explains why the Qur'an argues this way. The signs the Book points to have three properties that make them ideal evidence. They are *universally available* — no education, wealth, or access is required to see rain. They are *repeated* — the argument is renewed every season. And they are *taken for granted*, which means the persuasion consists not in providing new information but in *removing familiarity*. That last point is the key to this whole passage. Nobody in Madinah was unaware of clouds. What the verse does is *slow the phenomenon down* — driving, merging, heaping, releasing — until the observer, who has watched rain a thousand times, actually sees it for the first time. That is what *a lam tara*, "have you not seen," is for. It is not a question about eyesight. It is an attempt to break a habit of not-noticing that begins in childhood and, for most people, is never broken again.

**Application: The Recovery of Wonder**

The practical instruction here is unusual among Qur'anic commands because it asks for a change of *attention* rather than of behaviour. Modern life makes the habit of not-noticing much stronger than it was in seventh-century Arabia. Weather is a notification on a screen; rain is an inconvenience between buildings; the sky is something one passes under. Very few people now watch a storm build. The verse asks for the reversal: to stand still long enough to watch clouds being driven, joined, and heaped; to register that the water falling on the roof was lifted from an ocean, carried hundreds of miles, and released precisely there; and to let that observation function as what the Qur'an says it is — a sign, pointing past itself to the One who drives it.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:44

> **God alternates the night and the day. Truly in that is a lesson for those possessed of sight.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sign That Never Stops**

After the birds and the clouds comes the most constant sign of all: the turning of night into day and day into night. *Yuqallibu'llāhu'l-layla wa'l-nahār.* It is the sign nobody can miss and nobody attends to. Rain does not come every day; birds may not cross one's window. The alternation of light and darkness has occurred without interruption for every day of every human life, and precisely because of that it is the least noticed thing in existence.

**Yuqallibu: The Verb of Turning Over**

The verb is *qallaba*, an intensive form from the root *q-l-b* — the same root as *qalb*, the heart, and the same verb used at verse 37 for the Day when *"hearts and eyes will be turned about."* Its use here is deliberate, and the echo across seven verses should be heard. *Yuqallibu* means to turn a thing over repeatedly, to flip it, to rotate it from one state to another. God turns over the night and day; on that Day He will turn over the hearts and eyes. The root also connects to one of the most frequently repeated supplications of the Prophet ﷺ. Anas reported that he would often say:

> *"O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."* And when asked about it, he said: *"There is no heart but that it is between two of the fingers of the Merciful; if He wills, He makes it firm, and if He wills, He turns it away."* (al-Tirmidhī 2140)

So the same verb governs three things in the Qur'anic vocabulary: the sky's alternation, the heart's fluctuation, and the terror of the Last Day. The connection is not accidental. A person who observes that the sky itself does not hold a single state is being told something about his own inner weather.

**The Meanings of the Alternation**

The commentators identify several distinct senses in which God "turns over" the night and the day, and each is a separate sign. *Succession.* One follows the other, without fail, in a fixed order that has never once been reversed. *Variation in length.* The nights lengthen and shorten through the year in a regular cycle, which is what makes seasons and agriculture possible. *Transformation.* The one is turned *into* the other — not merely replaced but transformed, through the gradations of dawn and dusk that belong fully to neither. *Variation in what they contain.* Some commentators note that the alternation includes the changing of conditions within them: heat and cold, ease and difficulty, the events that fill the hours. The Qur'an treats each of these as evidence:

> *"And a sign for them is the night: We strip the day from it, and behold, they are in darkness."* (Qur'an 36:37)

The verb there, *naslakhu*, means to *flay* or strip a hide from a carcass — a startlingly physical image for the removal of daylight.

> *"Say: Have you considered, if God were to make the night perpetual over you until the Day of Resurrection, what god other than God would bring you light? Will you not then hear? Say: Have you considered, if God were to make the day perpetual over you until the Day of Resurrection, what god other than God would bring you night wherein you might rest? Will you not then see?"* (Qur'an 28:71–72)

Those two verses make the argument by removal: imagine the alternation stopping. The thought experiment reveals immediately how total the dependence is, and how completely unearned.

**Why the Alternation Is a Mercy**

It is worth spelling out what the cycle actually provides, because familiarity hides it. *Night makes rest possible.* Sleep is not merely a convenience; it is a biological necessity without which the human organism fails within days. And the Qur'an names the provision explicitly:

> *"And He it is who made the night a garment for you, and sleep a repose, and made the day a resurrection."* (Qur'an 25:47)

The word for sleep there is *subāt*, from a root meaning cessation — and the day is called *nushūr*, the same word used for the resurrection of the dead. Every night is a small death and every morning a small rising. The Prophet ﷺ made this explicit in the words he taught for waking:

> *"Praise be to God, who gave us life after having caused us to die, and unto Him is the resurrection."* (al-Bukhārī 6312)

*Day makes work possible.* And the Qur'an pairs the two functions:

> *"And We made your sleep for rest, and We made the night as a covering, and We made the day for livelihood."* (Qur'an 78:9–11)

*The cycle makes time measurable.* Without it there would be no days, no months, no calendar, no appointments, no history, and no way to fix the times of prayer, fasting, or pilgrimage. *And the alternation prevents both extremes.* Perpetual day would exhaust; perpetual night would paralyze. The rhythm is the mercy.

**"A Lesson for Those Possessed of Sight"**

*Inna fī dhālika la-ʿibratan li-ūli'l-abṣār.* Two terms close the verse, and both are precise. *ʿIbrah* is from the root *ʿ-b-r*, meaning to *cross over* or *pass through* — the same root as *ʿubūr* (crossing), *ʿibārah* (an expression, which carries meaning across from speaker to hearer), and *taʿbīr* (the interpretation of dreams, which crosses from the image to its meaning). So an *ʿibrah* is not merely a "lesson" in the sense of a fact learned. It is a *crossing*: the movement of the mind from the visible thing to what lies beyond it. The night and day are the near bank; the recognition of the One who turns them is the far bank; and the *ʿibrah* is the passage between. This is exactly what the sūrah has been asking for since verse 41. Everyone sees the phenomena. What is required is the crossing. *Ūlū'l-abṣār* — "those possessed of sight." *Baṣar* is physical vision; *baṣīrah* is insight. The phrase carries both, and the pun is intentional. Those who have eyes are many; those who *see* are the ones who make the crossing. The Qur'an is explicit that the deficiency is not ocular:

> *"Truly it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is the hearts within the breasts that go blind."* (Qur'an 22:46)

**The Argument From Regularity**

There is a further philosophical point here that deserves to be drawn out, because it is sometimes inverted in modern discussion. It is often assumed that the *regularity* of nature is an argument against divine agency: since the sun rises by physical law, no further explanation is needed. The Qur'an's position is precisely the opposite. Regularity is the evidence. A universe in which things happened randomly would testify to no wisdom at all. It is the *reliability* of the alternation — its precision, its endurance, the fine dependence of biological life upon it — that constitutes the sign. The Qur'an states this in the language of the balance:

> *"The sun and the moon follow a reckoning, and the stars and the trees prostrate. And the sky He raised, and He set up the Balance, that you may not transgress the Balance."* (Qur'an 55:5–8)

The moral command in that last clause emerges directly from the cosmological observation. The universe runs on measure; therefore, do not violate measure.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Argument**

Why night and day here, in this chapter? Because the sūrah is named *al-Nūr*, and the alternation of light and darkness is the physical form of its central theme. The chapter has described the lamp in the niche and the darkness of the fathomless sea. Now it points to the fact that every human being lives, physically, in a daily oscillation between exactly those two states. And there is a spiritual application the commentators draw. Just as the outer world turns from light to dark and back, so does the inner life. Nobody sustains a single spiritual state. The believer has days of clarity and days of dullness, hours of presence in prayer and hours of absence. This verse is, read that way, a consolation. The turning is *God's* doing, and the night is not the end of the sequence. A person in a dark stretch of his religious life is not outside the system; he is in the part of the cycle that always precedes dawn. The Prophet ﷺ described the same alternation in the life of faith:

> *"Faith wears out in the heart of one of you as a garment wears out; so ask God to renew faith in your hearts."* (al-Ḥākim)

**A Practical Discipline**

There is a specific practice this verse supports, and it is one of the most reliable in the tradition: attaching remembrance to the two turning points. The morning and evening are named at verse 36 as the times of glorification in the houses of light, and the Qur'an repeatedly connects worship to the transitions of the day:

> *"So glorify God when you enter the evening and when you enter the morning. To Him is all praise in the heavens and the earth, and in the late afternoon and at midday."* (Qur'an 30:17–18)

The wisdom of anchoring remembrance to dawn and dusk is that these are the moments when the sign is actually *visible*. Anyone who stands still at either transition and watches it happen — the slow arrival of light, the slow withdrawal — is doing precisely what verse 44 asks: crossing over from what is seen to what it means.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:45

> **And God created every beast from water: among them are those that go upon their bellies, and among them are those that go upon two legs, and among them are those that go upon four. God creates whatsoever He will. Truly God is Powerful over all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Last of the Signs**

The sequence of natural signs concludes with the creatures themselves. The birds were above, the clouds were above, the night and day encompassed everything — and now the argument comes down to the ground, to the animals that move upon it, and to the substance from which they were all made.

**"Every Beast From Water"**

*Wa'llāhu khalaqa kulla dābbatin min māʾ.* *Dābbah* is from the root *d-b-b*, meaning to move slowly, to creep, to crawl. A *dābbah* is anything that moves upon the earth — the word is broader than "beast" and covers, in Qur'anic usage, every terrestrial living creature. The Book uses it inclusively:

> *"There is no creature (dābbah) upon the earth but that its provision rests upon God."* (Qur'an 11:6)

And the claim is that *every one of them* was created *min māʾ* — from water. The commentators read this in more than one way. *Water as the origin of all life.* This is the plain reading, and it aligns with the Qur'an's more general statement:

> *"…and We made every living thing from water. Will they not then believe?"* (Qur'an 21:30)

That verse occurs immediately after the description of the heavens and earth being separated, in one of the Book's most cosmologically striking passages. *Water as the seminal fluid.* A second reading, also well attested among the commentators, takes *māʾ* in the specific sense of the fluid of reproduction, from which each individual creature is generated. The Qur'an uses *māʾ* in that sense elsewhere:

> *"Was he not a drop of fluid emitted?"* (Qur'an 75:37)

> *"He is created from a fluid gushing forth."* (Qur'an 86:6)

Both readings are true and neither excludes the other. Life originates in water and each individual life originates in fluid. The observation itself is remarkable for its era. That *every* animal — the desert lizard, the camel, the bird, the fish, the human — should be constituted essentially of water is not an obvious inference from casual observation, particularly in an arid environment where the visible animals are conspicuously dry. Modern biology puts the water content of living organisms at roughly two-thirds to three-quarters of body mass, and the dependence of every biochemical process on an aqueous medium is total. The rhetorical force in an Arabian context is also worth noting. To a people for whom water was the scarcest and most precious substance, the statement that every living thing *is* water carries an immediate weight. The most valuable thing you know is what you are made of, and it comes from the sky, and you cannot make it.

**The Threefold Classification of Locomotion**

The verse then classifies creatures by how they move: *Fa-minhum man yamshī ʿalā baṭnihi* — those that go upon their bellies. Snakes, worms, and all limbless crawlers. *Wa minhum man yamshī ʿalā rijlayn* — those that go upon two legs. Human beings and birds. *Wa minhum man yamshī ʿalā arbaʿ* — those that go upon four. The great mass of mammals and reptiles. Several observations are in order. First, the classification is *by mode of locomotion*, which is an empirical and observational criterion rather than a theological one. It groups the human being with the birds and the ostrich, not in a separate category. This is characteristic of the Qur'an's treatment of humanity in the context of natural signs: as one creature among many, distinguished elsewhere by responsibility rather than by biology. Second, the commentators noted that the list does not claim to be exhaustive, and that creatures with more than four legs exist. Several classical exegetes addressed this, observing that the Arabic idiom names the principal categories, and that the following clause — *"God creates whatsoever He will"* — explicitly leaves the classification open. Al-Qurṭubī notes the point directly: the mention of these three does not negate others, and the concluding phrase covers what was not enumerated. That closing clause is therefore not filler. It is the verse's own acknowledgment that the list is illustrative, and it is a small but real demonstration of the Book's precision. Third — and this is the striking part — the verse uses *man* rather than *mā* for all three categories. In Arabic, *man* is the relative pronoun used for rational, personal beings ("whoever"), while *mā* is used for things and non-rational creatures ("whatever"). The verse says *minhum man yamshī*, using the personal pronoun for snakes and quadrupeds alike. The grammarians and commentators noticed this and explained it as *taghlīb* — the predominance of the higher category over the lower when they are grouped together, since human beings are among those who walk on two legs. But the effect on the reader is worth registering: the creatures are being spoken of with a grammar ordinarily reserved for persons. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the same move explicitly:

> *"There is no creature that crawls upon the earth, nor bird that flies upon its wings, but that they are communities like yourselves. We have neglected nothing in the Book. Then unto their Lord shall they be gathered."* (Qur'an 6:38)

**What the Diversity Argues**

Why does the verse enumerate modes of locomotion at all? What is the argument? It is an argument from *unity of substance combined with diversity of form*. One material — water — and from it, creatures of radically different design: the limbless, the bipedal, the quadrupedal, and everything the verse leaves unlisted. Each is exquisitely fitted to its environment and its manner of life. The reasoning is that identical raw material producing such divergent and functional outcomes points to design rather than to the properties of the material. Water does not, of itself, tend toward the production of an eagle's wing or a snake's musculature or a horse's leg. Something has shaped it. And the diversity is offered as a matter for wonder rather than merely for inference. The Qur'an repeatedly invites this attention:

> *"Do they not consider the camel, how it was created?"* (Qur'an 88:17)

> *"And in your creation, and in the creatures He has scattered abroad, are signs for a people who are certain."* (Qur'an 45:4)

**"God Creates Whatsoever He Will"**

*Yakhluqu'llāhu mā yashāʾ.* The clause performs two functions. It leaves the catalogue open, as noted. Whatever forms of life exist beyond the three named — the many-legged, the microscopic, the creatures of the deep sea, whatever else may be found — falls within it. And it asserts that creation is *by will*, not by necessity. The forms of life are not the automatic output of a process that could not have gone otherwise. They are chosen. This is the theological difference between a universe that is *made* and one that merely *is*.

**"Truly God Is Powerful Over All Things"**

*Inna'llāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr.* The closing name is *qadīr*, from the root *q-d-r*, which yields both *qudrah* (power, capability) and *qadar* (measure, determination, destiny). The overlap is characteristic of Arabic and theologically meaningful: divine power is not raw force but *measured* capability — power exercised according to determination. The name is placed here as the natural conclusion of the entire signs-passage that began at verse 41. Birds held aloft, clouds driven and heaped, hail directed and withheld, night and day turned over, every living thing composed of water and formed into a thousand shapes — and the summary: *He is capable of everything*. But the placement has a further function, and it looks forward rather than back. The next major theme of the sūrah, beginning at verse 47, concerns those who claim to believe and then withdraw when the ruling goes against them — and it culminates at verse 55 in the great promise that God will establish the believers as successors on the earth and exchange their fear for security. That promise requires exactly the attribute named here. A community reading verse 55 in the conditions of early Madinah — small, surrounded, insecure — would need grounds for believing that such a transformation was possible. Verses 41 to 45 supply the grounds: the One making the promise drives the clouds, turns over the night and day, and made every living thing from water.

**The Reasoning Behind the Whole Signs-Passage**

Standing back from verses 41 through 45, the structure of the argument is worth naming. The Qur'an does not attempt to *prove* God in the manner of a syllogism. It does something different and, arguably, more effective: it directs attention to phenomena the audience already accepts and asks them to notice what those phenomena imply. The method assumes that the human being's problem is not a lack of evidence but a *habit of inattention* — the condition the Book calls *ghaflah*. On that diagnosis, the correct treatment is not more argument but the interruption of habit, which is exactly what *a lam tara* — "have you not seen?" — is designed to accomplish. And the placement of this method in *this* sūrah completes the chapter's logic. The first half disciplined the eye against what it should not see. The second half now trains the same eye toward what it should. Lowering the gaze (v. 30) and lifting it to the birds (v. 41) are two halves of a single instruction about vision — which is precisely what one expects from a chapter called *The Light*.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:46

> **We have indeed sent down clarifying signs, and God guides whomsoever He will unto a straight path.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Closing Formula**

This verse repeats, almost exactly, the opening clause of verse 34: *"We have indeed sent down clarifying signs."* As noted there, such repetitions in the Qur'an mark the boundaries of sections. Verse 34 closed the sūrah's first movement — the legislation of verses 1 to 33. This verse closes the second — the parables and natural signs of verses 35 to 45. The parallel structure is a signal to the reader that a distinct block of the chapter has ended and a new one is about to begin at verse 47, which turns to the hypocrites and to the ethics of obedience.

**Two Kinds of Sign, One Word**

There is a satisfying precision in the fact that the *same* formula closes both sections, because the sections contain two different kinds of *āyāt*. The first section's signs were *revealed verses*: the ruling on adultery, the standard of four witnesses, the etiquette of doorways, the lowering of the gaze. The second section's signs were *created phenomena*: the birds in flight, the clouds driven and heaped, the alternation of night and day, the creatures made from water. Both are called *āyāt mubayyināt*, clarifying signs, and both are described as *sent down*. The identity of vocabulary asserts an identity of source and of function. The Qur'an and the cosmos are two books by one Author, and the sūrah has now presented both, in that order, and closed each with the same words. This has been a central insight of Islamic intellectual life. The tradition never treated the study of nature as an activity outside religion, precisely because the Book insists on calling natural phenomena by the same name it gives its own verses. The scholars of the classical period who measured the earth, mapped the stars, and studied anatomy and optics understood themselves to be reading *āyāt*.

**"And God Guides Whomsoever He Will Unto a Straight Path"**

*Wa'llāhu yahdī man yashāʾu ilā ṣirāṭin mustaqīm.* The second clause repeats, in different words, the statement made at the Light Verse: *"God guides unto His Light whomsoever He will"* (v. 35). Here the destination is named differently — not "His Light" but "a straight path." The pairing across the two verses is instructive. Light and path are the two great Qur'anic metaphors for guidance, and they answer two different needs. Light addresses the problem of *seeing*: without it, the traveller cannot perceive where he is. Path addresses the problem of *going*: even a person who can see needs a route. Guidance in the Qur'an is always both. It is not merely illumination that leaves a person enlightened and stationary, nor merely a rule-set followed blindly in the dark. It is a road with the lights on.

**Ṣirāṭ Mustaqīm: The Phrase Every Muslim Repeats Daily**

*Al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm* is the most frequently uttered phrase in Muslim life after the declaration of faith itself, because it stands at the centre of the Fātiḥah, recited in every unit of every prayer:

> *"Guide us upon the straight path, the path of those whom Thou hast blessed, not of those who incur wrath, nor of those who are astray."* (Qur'an 1:6–7)

The word *ṣirāṭ* denotes a wide, clear, main road — the lexicographers connect it to a root suggesting something that is *swallowed* or taken in wholesale, an image of a road that absorbs its travellers. It is distinguished from the narrower *sabīl* (way, plural *subul*), and the Qur'an exploits the distinction:

> *"And this is My path, straight; so follow it, and follow not the [other] ways, lest they separate you from His way."* (Qur'an 6:153)

Ibn Masʿūd's illustration of that verse is preserved in the tradition: the Prophet ﷺ drew a straight line and said *"this is the path of God,"* then drew lines branching from its sides and said *"these are the ways, upon each of which is a devil calling to it,"* and then recited the verse. *Mustaqīm* is from *q-w-m*, to stand upright, and in this form means straight, direct, upright, without deviation. A straight path has an obvious practical advantage: it is the shortest route between two points, and it does not require the traveller to guess at each turn.

**Why Guidance Is a Standing Request Rather Than a Possession**

A question is often raised about the Fātiḥah's petition: why does a Muslim, who already believes, ask five times a day to be guided to the straight path? Is he not on it already? The classical answer is threefold, and it illuminates this verse. *Guidance is continuous, not a one-time acquisition.* A person can be on the path at this moment and off it in an hour. The request is for the next step, not for a certificate. *Guidance has degrees.* Beyond knowing the path lies walking it; beyond walking lies walking it well; beyond that lies walking it with love. The petition asks for advancement, not merely for entry. *And guidance is a gift that can be withdrawn.* Hence the Prophet's ﷺ constant supplication, quoted at verse 44: *"O Turner of hearts, make my heart firm upon Your religion."*

**The Balance of the Two Clauses**

The verse's structure holds two truths in tension, and the tension is the point. *"We have indeed sent down clarifying signs"* — the responsibility is discharged. The evidence is public, plain, and available. Nobody can claim it was hidden. *"And God guides whomsoever He will"* — yet the reception of that evidence is not automatic. Clarity does not compel. Anyone who has ever presented an unanswerable argument to someone who remained unmoved has encountered this. Evidence does not convert; it only *removes excuses*. What converts is something else, and this verse names its source. The Qur'an is unsparing about the limits of even prophetic persuasion:

> *"Truly thou dost not guide whomsoever thou lovest, but God guides whomsoever He will. And He knows best those who are rightly guided."* (Qur'an 28:56)

That verse was revealed, according to the commentators, concerning the Prophet's ﷺ own uncle Abū Ṭālib, who protected him for a lifetime and did not accept the message. If the Messenger of God could not guide the person he most wanted to guide, the doctrine is settled.

**The Comfort in the Doctrine**

This teaching, properly understood, is one of the most freeing in the religion, and it has direct practical application. Every believer has someone he desperately wishes would come to faith, or return to prayer, or leave a destructive path — a child, a parent, a sibling, a friend. The temptation is to take responsibility for the outcome, and the result is either exhausting pressure applied to the other person or corrosive guilt absorbed by oneself. This verse divides the labour precisely. *Clarification* is the human task: to present the signs, to live them visibly, to speak clearly and kindly. *Guidance* is God's, and it is not delegated. The Prophet's ﷺ own mandate is stated in these terms repeatedly, and this sūrah will state it again in eight verses:

> *"…and naught is incumbent upon the Messenger save the clear proclamation."* (Qur'an 24:54)

The word there is *al-balāgh al-mubīn* — clear delivery, from the same root as this verse's *mubayyināt*. Deliver clearly; the rest is not yours.

**The Transition Ahead**

This verse is a hinge, and it prepares the next section with some subtlety. Verses 47 onward will describe people who *heard* the clear signs and turned away — those who say "we believe and we obey" and then withdraw when summoned to judgment. The problem the next section addresses is precisely the one this verse anticipates: what happens when clarity is delivered and not received. By stating both halves here — the signs *are* clear, and guidance *is* God's to give — the sūrah forecloses two errors before they arise. It prevents anyone from excusing the hypocrites on grounds of insufficient evidence, and it prevents the believers from congratulating themselves on their own perception. The community reading verse 51 — *"the only words of the believers… will be to say, we hear and we obey"* — is being told in advance that its own hearing and obeying were gifts.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:47

> **And they say, "We believe in God and in the Messenger, and We obey." Then a group of them turn away thereafter, and believers they are not.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gap Between the Declaration and the Deed**

The sūrah now turns to a new subject, and it is one the Madinan revelation returns to repeatedly: the people whose profession of faith is contradicted by their behaviour under pressure. *Wa yaqūlūna āmannā bi'llāhi wa bi'l-rasūli wa aṭaʿnā, thumma yatawallā farīqun minhum min baʿdi dhālika, wa mā ulāʾika bi'l-muʾminīn.* The verse has a simple and devastating structure: a statement, a delay, and a withdrawal. They say the right words; time passes; a portion of them turns away. And the verdict follows: *these are not believers.*

**The Immediate Context: The Occasion of Revelation**

The commentators record a specific case behind these verses. A dispute arose between a hypocrite and a Jewish man of Madinah over land or property. The Jewish man proposed that they take the case to Muhammad ﷺ for judgment; the hypocrite refused and insisted on going to a Jewish authority or to a pre-Islamic arbiter instead — the reports differ in detail but agree in substance. The reason, in the accounts, was that the hypocrite knew the Prophet ﷺ would judge justly and that justice would go against him, whereas another forum might be susceptible to influence. The irony is exact and is exactly what the verses go on to expose: the man who *claimed* to believe in the Messenger would not accept his ruling, while the man who did *not* claim to believe in him was willing to be judged by him. Whether or not one attaches the passage to this particular incident, the pattern it describes is general, and the sūrah presents it as such.

**"We Believe… And We Obey"**

The declaration they make is, in itself, perfect. It contains both halves of what Islam requires: *belief* in God and His Messenger, and *obedience*. Nothing in the words is deficient. Note the tense shift in the Arabic, which is easily lost in translation. *Āmannā* — "we have believed" — and *aṭaʿnā* — "we have obeyed." Both are in the perfect tense, describing completed acts. They are not saying "we will obey"; they are asserting that obedience is an accomplished fact about them. This is the characteristic speech of the person whose religion is *self-description* rather than practice. He does not say "I intend to"; he says "I am." And the claim is made in the plural, as part of a group, which is where such claims are safest and least tested.

**Thumma: The Word That Exposes Them**

The pivot of the verse is *thumma* — "then," "thereafter." Arabic has several conjunctions for sequence, and *thumma* specifically indicates a sequence with *an interval*: not immediately after, but after some time has passed. The choice is precise and psychologically acute. The hypocrite does not contradict himself in the same breath; that would be too obvious, and he would not deceive even himself. He declares his faith sincerely enough in the moment of declaring it. The contradiction emerges *later*, when the declaration meets a situation with a cost. This is why hypocrisy in the Qur'anic sense is not usually a conscious lie. Very few people knowingly profess a faith they inwardly reject. What the Book describes is subtler: a person who believes his own declaration and discovers, when the moment arrives, that it does not govern him.

**"A Group of Them" — The Careful Partitive**

*Farīqun minhum* — "a party among them," "a portion of them." As at verse 11, where the slanderers were called *ʿuṣbatun minkum*, the Qur'an is careful not to condemn wholesale. Not everyone who says "we believe and we obey" turns away. Some mean it. The verse identifies a subset and leaves the rest unaccused. This precision is a consistent feature of Qur'anic rebuke and a model for how communities should speak about their own failings: name the behaviour, quantify honestly, and do not slander the whole for the conduct of a part.

**Yatawallā: Turning the Back**

The verb *tawallā* is from *w-l-y*, a root with a striking double life in the Qur'an. In one form it produces *walī* — a friend, ally, protector, guardian — and *tawallā* can mean "to take as a patron" or "to take charge of," as at verse 11 where the chief slanderer *"undertook"* the greater part of the lie. In the form used here it means to *turn away*, to present one's back, to withdraw. The same root yields both drawing near and turning away, depending on construction — because both involve *turning*, and everything depends on the direction of the turn. The physical image is worth holding: the person does not argue, refute, or announce a departure. He simply turns his back and walks. It is the gesture of someone who has decided that the conversation does not apply to him.

**"And Believers They Are Not"**

*Wa mā ulāʾika bi'l-muʾminīn.* The verdict is delivered with maximum grammatical emphasis. Arabic negation could be expressed simply; here the construction uses *mā* with the demonstrative *ulāʾika* and the preposition *bi-* before the predicate, all of which intensify the denial. The sense is: *those ones, they are certainly not among the believers.* This is a serious statement and requires careful handling, because Islamic theology has always been cautious about declaring individuals to be outside faith. The verse should be read as the Qur'an's own commentary on the *specific behaviour* it has just described: the person who says "we obey" and then refuses the judgment of God and His Messenger when it goes against his interest has, in that act, falsified his own claim. The declaration and the conduct cannot both be true. What follows for the reader is a criterion of self-examination rather than a licence to classify others. And the criterion is stated in the next several verses with unusual clarity: verse 48 gives the test, verse 49 gives the diagnostic, verse 50 gives the possible motives, and verse 51 gives the correct alternative.

**The Definition of Hypocrisy in the Sunnah**

The Prophet ﷺ described the marks of this condition in terms that match the verse exactly:

> *"The signs of the hypocrite are three: when he speaks he lies, when he promises he breaks his promise, and when he is entrusted he betrays the trust."* (al-Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59)

And in another version, with a fourth:

> *"…and when he disputes, he behaves immorally."* (al-Bukhārī 34; Muslim 58)

Every one of these is a gap between a stated position and an actual performance. The liar's words do not match reality; the promise-breaker's future does not match his commitment; the betrayer's conduct does not match the trust placed in him; the disputant's behaviour does not match his professed principles. That last one — behaving badly in a dispute — is precisely the situation of this passage: a legal conflict in which the professed believer will not accept the ruling.

**Why This Passage Follows the Signs**

The placement is deliberate and worth noticing. Verses 41 to 46 established that the signs are clear — in the sky, in the birds, in the alternation of day and night, and in the revealed verses. The section closed by acknowledging that clarity alone does not produce guidance. Verses 47 onward now show what that looks like in practice. Here are people who have received the clear signs, who acknowledge them verbally, and who nevertheless turn away when the signs make a demand. They are the living demonstration of the previous verse's point.

**The Universal Application**

It would be a serious misreading to treat this verse as being about a class of people called "the hypocrites" who existed in Madinah and no longer exist. The Qur'an's descriptions of hypocrisy are diagnostic categories, and the early Muslims applied them to themselves. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb is reported to have asked Ḥudhayfah — the Companion to whom the Prophet ﷺ had confided the names of the hypocrites — whether he was among them. And al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī is famously reported to have said that none feels safe from hypocrisy but a hypocrite, and none fears it but a believer. That is the correct posture toward this verse. It is not a description of other people. It is a question addressed to the reader: *when the ruling goes against you, what do you do?* The everyday forms are not obscure. The Muslim who accepts every religious teaching except the one touching his income. The person who defers to scholarship until scholarship contradicts a family custom he loves. The one who says "Islam is my life" and then, in a dispute over inheritance, over a divorce settlement, over a business partnership, finds that the Islamic ruling is inconvenient and discovers a reason to look elsewhere. The verse's diagnosis of all of these is the same, and it is stated without softening: *these are not the believers.* The next four verses will explain exactly why.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:48

> **And when they are called to God and His Messenger, that He may judge between them, behold, a group of them turn away.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Test Applied**

The previous verse stated the pattern in general terms: they say they believe and obey, and then a group turns away. This verse names the *specific situation* in which the turning happens. *Wa idhā duʿū ila'llāhi wa rasūlihi li-yaḥkuma baynahum idhā farīqun minhum muʿriḍūn.* The test is a summons to judgment. And the Qur'an has here identified, with considerable precision, the single most reliable diagnostic of whether a person's submission is real.

**Why Judgment Is the Test**

Consider why this particular situation exposes what nothing else does. In most of religious life, obedience and self-interest are not in direct conflict. Prayer costs time but confers peace. Charity costs money but confers dignity and reward. Fasting is difficult but admired. A person can perform all of these while never once being required to accept an outcome he does not want. Litigation is different. In a dispute, a ruling means that one party *loses something concrete* — money, property, a claim, a right he believed was his. There is no way to comply and also win. And crucially, the loss is not to God in the abstract but to *the other party*, a person one may dislike, who may be less deserving, and who will walk away with what one wanted. This is why the Qur'an selects it. Submission that has never been tested against a material loss is not yet known to be submission. As the Book states elsewhere:

> *"Do people suppose that they will be left to say 'We believe' and will not be tried?"* (Qur'an 29:2)

**"Called to God and His Messenger"**

The phrase *duʿū ila'llāhi wa rasūlih* — "called to God and His Messenger" — carries an important theological point about what a summons to the Prophet's ﷺ court actually was. The Prophet ﷺ judged by revelation and by the wisdom given to him. To be called to his judgment was to be called to God's judgment; the verse names both together and then uses a singular verb, *li-yaḥkuma*, "that *he* may judge," which the grammarians note unifies the two into a single authority. The Qur'an elsewhere establishes the same principle with unusual force:

> *"But no, by thy Lord, they will not believe until they make thee the judge in their disputes, and then find no resistance in their souls to what thou hast decreed, and submit with full submission."* (Qur'an 4:65)

That verse sets three conditions, each stricter than the last: they must *bring* the case to the Messenger; they must find *no inward resistance* to his ruling; and they must submit *fully*. Outward compliance with an inwardly rejected verdict does not satisfy it. And the Book makes the same point in the language of choice:

> *"It is not for a believing man or a believing woman, when God and His Messenger have decreed a matter, to have any choice in their affair."* (Qur'an 33:36)

**Muʿriḍūn: The Vocabulary of Turning Aside**

Verse 47 used *yatawallā* — turning the back. This verse uses a different word: *muʿriḍūn*, from the root *ʿ-r-ḍ*, which relates to the *side* or *breadth* of a thing. To be *muʿriḍ* is to turn one's *side* to something — to angle away, to avert, to present a shoulder rather than a face. The distinction is subtle and real. *Tawallī* is departure; *iʿrāḍ* is *avoidance while remaining present*. The person who is *muʿriḍ* has not left the room. He is still there, still nominally part of the community, but he has turned himself sideways to the thing being said. He does not respond, does not engage, does not accept — he simply declines to face it. Anyone who has watched a person receive advice they have no intention of taking has seen *iʿrāḍ*. The body is present and the attention has been withdrawn.

**"Behold" — The Suddenness of the Reveal**

The Arabic uses *idhā* twice in one sentence — *wa idhā duʿū… idhā farīqun minhum muʿriḍūn* — and the second is what the grammarians call *idhā al-fujāʾiyyah*, the *idhā* of surprise. It is the equivalent of "and lo!" or "and there they are —". The construction stages the moment dramatically. They were called to judgment, and *suddenly* — there is a group of them, turned aside. The suddenness is the point: nothing about these people had revealed itself until the summons came. They had been indistinguishable from everyone else. The call to judgment developed the image.

**The Nature of the Islamic Court**

It is worth pausing on what is being refused here, because the modern reader may not appreciate what a summons to the Prophet's ﷺ judgment involved. It was not the imposition of an alien authority. It was the offer of a forum in which the powerful and the weak stood on identical footing — which was precisely the problem for those with worldly leverage. In a tribal system, disputes were settled by negotiation weighted by the strength of one's clan. A man with a large tribe behind him could expect a favourable settlement regardless of the merits. The Prophet's ﷺ court destroyed that advantage. His most famous statement on the matter was made when the Quraysh sought intercession for a noblewoman who had stolen, and he said:

> *"Those before you were destroyed only because when a noble among them stole, they let him go; and when a weak one among them stole, they carried out the penalty upon him. By God, if Fāṭimah the daughter of Muhammad were to steal, I would cut off her hand."* (al-Bukhārī 3475; Muslim 1688)

To turn away from such a court is therefore not the rejection of an authority but the rejection of *equality before the law* — the refusal of a man who prefers a forum where his leverage counts.

**Why the Guilty Avoid Judgment**

The behaviour described has a straightforward explanation, and the next verse will make it explicit: they turn away when they expect to lose, and come running when they expect to win. But it is worth naming the psychology, because it is universal and not confined to seventh-century Madinah. *Avoidance of an adverse ruling.* The most obvious motive, and the one the passage addresses. *The preference for a forum one can influence.* People who cannot win on the merits look for a venue where other factors operate — connections, sympathy, pressure, delay. *The unwillingness to let the matter be settled at all.* Some disputants do not want resolution; they want the grievance. A judgment would end it, and the grievance is more useful to them than the outcome.

**The Contemporary Forms**

The direct application concerns Muslims and Islamic legal rulings, and it should be stated plainly. A believer who takes a matter to a religious authority, receives an answer, and then goes shopping for a scholar who will say what he wants has performed the exact behaviour this verse condemns. So has the one who accepts the ruling verbally and delays compliance indefinitely. The practice has acquired a modern name — the search for a convenient verdict — and a modern infrastructure, since it is now possible to search until one finds an opinion, however isolated, that permits what one wanted. The classical scholars warned about this severely, holding that a person who deliberately seeks out concessions in every question, assembling a personal religion from the easiest position on each, has effectively followed no religion at all but his own preference. Some of them said explicitly that whoever pursues the concessions of the scholars has gathered up all evil. But the verse's reach extends beyond formal rulings. It applies to any principle a person claims to hold. The test is always the same: what happens when the principle rules *against you*? The one who believes in fairness until fairness costs him. The one who supports accountability until it is his conduct being examined. The one who insists on evidence until the evidence supports his opponent. In every case the profession was sincere and the withdrawal is sudden, and the Qur'an's *idhā al-fujāʾiyyah* — "and lo, there they are, turned aside" — describes the moment exactly.

**The Standard the Believer Is Held To**

The passage does not leave the reader with only a condemnation. Three verses later, at verse 51, it will supply the alternative: *"The only words of the believers when they are called unto God and His Messenger, that he may judge between them, will be to say: We hear and we obey."* The two verses are constructed as a matched pair, describing the same summons and the two possible responses. And the difference between them is not a matter of belief in the abstract. It is a matter of what a person does in the ten seconds after a ruling goes against him.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:49

> **But if the right is theirs, they come unto Him submissively.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sentence That Removes All Doubt**

This short verse is the passage's masterstroke. In eight Arabic words it demolishes any possible defence the people described in verse 48 might have offered. *Wa in yakun lahumu'l-ḥaqqu yaʾtū ilayhi mudhʿinīn.* Had they simply refused all judgment, they might have claimed a principled objection — doubts about the forum, about the procedure, about the authority. This verse forecloses the excuse by observing what they do in the *other* case: when the ruling will go in their favour, they arrive without hesitation, and eagerly. Their objection, therefore, was never to the court. It was to losing.

**The Argument From Inconsistency**

This is one of the most powerful forms of moral argument, and it is worth naming because the Qur'an uses it repeatedly: *inconsistency reveals motive*. A person's stated reasons for a position can rarely be tested directly. But if the same person applies the principle when it favours him and abandons it when it does not, the stated principle is exposed as a rationalization. The real operating rule was never the principle; it was self-interest, and the principle was invoked only when the two happened to coincide. The method is universally applicable and uncomfortably sharp. It is the test to apply to oneself when one suspects one's own reasoning: *would I accept this argument if it produced the opposite conclusion?*

**Al-Ḥaqq: The Word That Carries the Irony**

The verse says: *if the ḥaqq is theirs*. As discussed at verse 25, *ḥaqq* means simultaneously truth, reality, and *right* or just claim. Here it carries the legal sense: if the entitlement in the dispute belongs to them. But the word's other meanings hover over the sentence, and the irony is deliberate. These people come running when *the right* is theirs — and thereby demonstrate that they have no commitment to *the truth* at all. They will embrace *al-ḥaqq* as a possession and reject it as a standard.

**Mudhʿinīn: The Most Damning Word in the Verse**

The final word is *mudhʿinīn*, from the root *dh-ʿ-n*. The lexicographers explain it as denoting *willing, prompt, submissive compliance* — and it is used particularly of a riding animal that is docile and easily led, that comes when called and goes where it is directed without resistance. The sarcasm is unmistakable. These are the same people who, moments earlier, turned their sides away from the summons. Now they arrive *like well-trained animals*: eager, obedient, hastening, offering no resistance whatsoever. The contrast the Arabic sets up between *muʿriḍūn* (turned aside) and *mudhʿinīn* (docile and hurrying) within two verses is one of the sharpest pieces of characterization in the sūrah. The same person, in the same week, before the same judge — transformed entirely by nothing but the expected direction of the verdict. And there is a further sting. The word describes a submission that is real but *worthless*, because it is not submission to authority at all; it is submission to advantage. A camel that comes to the trough is not obedient; it is hungry. The verse gives them the vocabulary of obedience precisely in order to show that what looks like obedience is nothing of the kind.

**The Universality of the Behaviour**

The pattern this verse describes is so common that it is nearly invisible, and it operates far outside the sphere of religion. Every institution has members who invoke its rules when the rules help them and denounce the same rules as bureaucratic obstruction when they do not. Every family has members who appeal to a principle of fairness in one dispute and to a principle of need in the next, depending on which produces the desired result. Every legal system produces litigants who praise the court's independence upon winning and allege bias upon losing. Sports supply the clearest everyday illustration, because the pattern is so undisguised: the identical decision by an official is a correct call when it favours one's team and an outrage when it does not. The behaviour is comic there because nothing is at stake. This verse describes the same behaviour where everything is.

**The Test of Genuine Submission**

The verse implies a criterion, and it is worth stating explicitly because it is one of the most useful self-diagnostics in the Qur'an. *Your acceptance of an authority is only demonstrated by your acceptance of its rulings against you.* Compliance when the ruling favours you demonstrates nothing whatsoever, because there is nothing to distinguish it from simple self-interest. It is only the adverse ruling that reveals whether the submission was real. This applies to the believer's relationship with divine law in the most direct way. A Muslim who follows every ruling that suits his temperament, his culture, his profession, and his appetites, and finds difficulty precisely and only where the ruling costs him — that person should not conclude from his general compliance that he has submitted. He may simply have found a religion that happens to agree with him. The Prophet ﷺ named the required condition:

> *"None of you truly believes until his desire follows what I have brought."* (al-Nawawī's *Forty Hadith*, no. 41)

The verb is the important one: *follows*. Desire is not required to be absent — that would be impossible for a human being. It is required to be *behind* rather than in front.

**The Believer's Alternative**

The Qur'an provides the model in the same passage. Two verses ahead:

> *"The only words of the believers when they are called unto God and His Messenger, that he may judge between them, will be to say: We hear and we obey. And it is they who shall prosper."* (Qur'an 24:51)

The believers' response is stated *without reference to the outcome*. Nothing in verse 51 makes the response conditional on which way the judgment goes. That absence is the whole difference between the two parties described in this passage.

**A Note on Self-Examination**

The temptation in reading verses 47 to 50 is to identify the people being described — historically, or in one's own community. The passage resists that use. The Qur'an gives no names. It describes a behaviour, and the behaviour is available to anyone. And the specific quality it exposes — the invisible operation of self-interest beneath a sincere-feeling principle — is by its nature something a person cannot easily detect in himself, which is exactly why the verse supplies an external test rather than asking for introspection. The test is behavioural and it can be run at any time: *find a case where the ruling went against you, and examine what you did.* Not what you said, and not what you felt, but what you did. The next verse will press the examination further, asking what could possibly explain the behaviour — disease of the heart, doubt, or fear of injustice — and will dismiss all three.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:50

> **Is there a disease in their hearts? Or do they doubt, or fear that God and His Messenger will deal unjustly with them? Nay, but it is they who are the wrongdoers.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Cross-Examination**

Having exposed the behaviour, the Qur'an now interrogates it. This verse is structured as a series of questions — three possible diagnoses offered in succession — followed by a verdict that dismisses the search for an excuse. *A fī qulūbihim maraḍun, ami'rtābū, am yakhāfūna an yaḥīfa'llāhu ʿalayhim wa rasūluh? Bal ulāʾika humu'l-ẓālimūn.* The rhetorical method is one of *elimination*. Behaviour of this kind must have an explanation. Here are the three available ones. And the closing *bal* — "nay, rather" — sets them aside and names the truth.

**The First Possibility: Disease of the Heart**

*A fī qulūbihim maraḍ.* *Maraḍ* is illness. The Qur'an's diagnosis of hypocrisy as a *disease of the heart* is one of its most frequent and most penetrating metaphors:

> *"In their hearts is a disease, so God has increased them in disease; and theirs is a painful punishment for having lied."* (Qur'an 2:10)

The metaphor is worth taking seriously, because it says something precise about the condition. *A disease is a malfunction, not a design.* The heart was made to recognize and love truth; when it does not, something has gone wrong with it, as with an eye that cannot see or a limb that will not move. *A disease often goes unnoticed by the patient.* Many serious illnesses produce no symptoms until they are advanced. A person can carry a fatal condition while feeling well — and the hypocrite, as the Qur'an describes him, believes himself sound: *"And when it is said to them, 'Do not work corruption upon the earth,' they say, 'We are only working righteousness'"* (2:11). *A disease progresses if untreated.* Note the terrible phrasing of 2:10: *"so God increased them in disease."* Untreated conditions worsen, and the worsening is itself a consequence of the neglect. *And a disease is treatable.* This is the hopeful side of the metaphor, and the Qur'an presses it: revelation is repeatedly described as *shifāʾ*, healing.

> *"O mankind! There has come to you an admonition from your Lord, and a healing for what lies within the breasts, and a guidance and a mercy for the believers."* (Qur'an 10:57)

The Prophet ﷺ described the heart's condition as decisive for the whole person:

> *"Truly in the body there is a morsel of flesh which, if it is sound, the whole body is sound, and if it is corrupt, the whole body is corrupt. Truly it is the heart."* (al-Bukhārī 52; Muslim 1599)

**The Second Possibility: Doubt**

*Ami'rtābū.* The verb is from *r-y-b*, giving *rayb* — unsettling doubt, disquiet, suspicion. It is the word used in the Qur'an's second verse: *"This is the Book in which there is no doubt (lā rayba fīh)"* (2:2). *Rayb* is not the honest uncertainty of someone still investigating. The lexicographers distinguish it as a doubt accompanied by *unease and suspicion* — the sense that something is not right. The Qur'an offers this as a possible explanation: perhaps they turn away because they are not, in fact, convinced. Perhaps their profession of faith was never grounded. And this diagnosis, unlike the first, would at least be *intelligible*. A person who genuinely doubts the authority of the Messenger has a coherent reason not to submit to his court. It would be a wrong position, but it would not be a contradictory one. What makes the passage devastating is that verse 49 has already ruled this out. People who genuinely doubted the legitimacy of the court would not come *running* to it when they expected to win. Their eager arrival in the favourable case is proof that they accept the court's authority perfectly well.

**The Third Possibility: Fear of Injustice**

*Am yakhāfūna an yaḥīfa'llāhu ʿalayhim wa rasūluh.* The verb *ḥāfa* means to deviate from justice, to be partial, to give a crooked or unfair judgment. It denotes precisely the fear that a court is biased. This third possibility is the most interesting of the three, because it is the excuse that people in this situation most commonly offer — then and now. *I am not refusing judgment; I am refusing an unfair judge.* The Qur'an does not even argue against it. It simply places it in the list and moves past it, and the reason is that the proposition refutes itself the moment it is stated. To fear that *God* will judge unjustly is to have misunderstood the meaning of the word God. Injustice arises from ignorance (not knowing the facts), from need (wanting something from one party), or from bias (preferring one party). The Judge in question is subject to none of these. The Qur'an states the impossibility directly:

> *"Truly God does not wrong so much as a mote's weight; and if it be a good deed, He multiplies it and grants a great reward from His Presence."* (Qur'an 4:40)

And in the divine saying transmitted by the Prophet ﷺ:

> *"O My servants, I have forbidden injustice for Myself and have made it forbidden among you, so do not wrong one another."* (Muslim 2577)

That report is remarkable: God declares injustice *forbidden to Himself*, and derives from that the prohibition upon His creatures. The believer's confidence in divine justice is not a hope but a stated guarantee.

**"Nay, But It Is They Who Are the Wrongdoers"**

*Bal ulāʾika humu'l-ẓālimūn.* The particle *bal* is a strong corrective: "no — rather." It dismisses everything preceding it and substitutes the truth. And the reversal is perfect. They feared being wronged. In fact they *are* the wrongdoers. The accusation they levelled at the court is returned to them, and the emphatic construction — *ulāʾika humu*, "those, they specifically" — is the same restrictive form used at verse 4 for the iniquitous and at verse 13 for the liars. *Ẓālim* is from *ẓ-l-m*, the root discussed at verse 40, whose core sense is *putting a thing where it does not belong* — and whose other product is *ẓulumāt*, darkness. The connection is exact. Their refusal of judgment was an act of misplacement: they placed their own interest where God's ruling belonged.

**What the Verse Teaches About Excuse-Making**

There is a general lesson in the *structure* of this verse that extends far beyond its immediate subject. The Qur'an lists the excuses before rejecting them. It does not pretend they were never offered. It states them fairly, in the strongest form the offender might use, and then dismisses them. This is a model of honest argument, and it is also a model of self-examination. When a person finds himself resisting a clear obligation, the useful exercise is precisely this one: to name the reasons honestly and then test them. *Is it that I do not believe this is true?* Then say so, and face the question directly rather than dressing avoidance as principle. *Is it that I think the ruling is unjust?* Then examine the claim, and ask whether the injustice consists in anything other than the fact that it costs me. *Or is it — as it usually is — that I know it is true and just, and I do not want to pay?* The verse's answer is that the third is almost always the real one, and that the first two are the costumes it wears.

**The Diseased Heart and the Reader**

A final observation on the first diagnosis, because it is the one that should concern a believer most. The Qur'an's category of *maraḍ al-qalb* is not reserved for a class of enemies. The tradition treats spiritual illness as a condition every heart is liable to, and the scholars of the inward life catalogued its symptoms in detail: the finding of worship burdensome, the absence of any pain at committing sin, the inability to be moved by the Qur'an, the preference for the company of the heedless, the fear of loss in giving charity. Each of these is a diminished response — the heart failing to register what it was made to register, exactly as a diseased organ fails to perform its function. And the treatment prescribed is the one this entire sūrah has been building: the remembrance of God morning and evening (v. 36), the discipline of the eye and the tongue (vv. 12–31), the refusal of Satan's incremental steps (v. 21), and the recognition that no one is purified except by God's bounty (v. 21). Which is why the correct response to this verse is not to identify the hypocrites but to run the three questions on oneself the next time a religious obligation feels unreasonable.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:51

> **The only words of the believers when they are called unto God and His Messenger, that he may judge between them, will be to say, "We hear and we obey." And it is they who shall prosper.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Portrait of the Alternative**

Four verses have described the person who says he believes and turns away when judgment is called. This verse describes the believer, and it does so by rewriting verse 48 word for word with a different ending. Compare them directly. Verse 48: *"And when they are called to God and His Messenger, that he may judge between them, behold, a group of them turn away."* Verse 51: *"The only words of the believers when they are called unto God and His Messenger, that he may judge between them, will be to say: We hear and we obey."* The situation is identical — the same summons, the same judge, the same phrasing. Only the response differs. The Qur'an has isolated a single variable and shown the two possible values it can take.

**"The Only Words… Will Be to Say"**

*Innamā kāna qawla'l-muʾminīna… an yaqūlū.* The particle *innamā* is the Arabic device of restriction: it means "only," and it excludes everything else. The verse is not saying that believers *usually* respond this way, or that this is the ideal response. It is saying that this is the *only* thing that comes out of their mouths in this situation. There is no second option listed. No conditional clause about the merits, no allowance for a case where the ruling is unexpected, no exception for a matter of great importance. The response is invariant.

**"We Hear and We Obey"**

*Samiʿnā wa aṭaʿnā.* Two words, and they constitute one of the great formulas of the Qur'an. *Samiʿnā* — "we have heard." In Arabic, as in Hebrew and in ordinary human usage, "hearing" carries a sense beyond the physical registration of sound. To say "I hear you" is to say "I have received and accepted what you said." The Qur'an uses *samʿ* throughout for *responsive* hearing, which is why it can say of the disbelievers that they do not hear, though their ears function perfectly:

> *"…and among them are those who listen to thee, but We have placed coverings over their hearts, lest they understand it."* (Qur'an 6:25)

*Aṭaʿnā* — "we have obeyed." And note the tense: the perfect, indicating a completed act. Not "we will obey" but "we have obeyed." This tense is the whole meaning of the formula. The obedience is declared *in the same breath as the hearing*, before the content of the ruling is known. There is no interval in which the believer evaluates the judgment and then decides. Hearing and obeying are treated as a single event.

**The Contrast With a Rejected Formula**

The Qur'an gives this phrase enormous weight, and part of its force comes from a contrast it draws elsewhere. Of the Children of Israel it says:

> *"And when We took your covenant and raised the Mount above you: 'Take hold of what We have given you with strength, and hear.' They said: 'We hear and we disobey.'"* (Qur'an 2:93)

*Samiʿnā wa ʿaṣaynā* — the exact formula with one letter changed. The Qur'an sets the two side by side across its pages, and the community that says the second is presented as the negative image of the community that says the first. And the affirmative formula is placed at the very end of Sūrat al-Baqarah as the believers' response to revelation:

> *"The Messenger believes in what was sent down to him from his Lord, as do the believers. Each believes in God, His angels, His books, and His messengers. 'We make no distinction between any of His messengers.' And they say, 'We hear and we obey. Thy forgiveness, our Lord! And unto Thee is the journey's end.'"* (Qur'an 2:285)

The commentators record that when the preceding verse was revealed, the Companions found it heavy, and the Prophet ﷺ said to them: *"Do you wish to say as the two communities before you said — 'we hear and we disobey'? Rather, say: we hear and we obey."* And they said it, and the relief came in the following verse. The report is preserved in Muslim (125).

**Is This Blind Obedience?**

The objection presents itself immediately to a modern reader, and it deserves a serious answer rather than a defensive one. Is the Qur'an demanding that a person surrender his judgment entirely, accepting rulings without evaluation? Is this not the definition of the uncritical mind? Several points must be made, and together they answer the objection. *First, the submission is to an identified authority, not to authority in general.* The verse is not commanding obedience to whoever happens to hold power. It concerns *God and His Messenger* — an authority whose credentials the person has already examined and accepted. The Qur'an spends enormous energy arguing for those credentials: the entire signs-passage of verses 41 to 46, immediately preceding this section, is such an argument. Submission comes *after* the evidence, not instead of it. *Second, the Qur'an explicitly condemns unexamined imitation.* It ridicules those who follow their fathers without reason:

> *"And when it is said unto them, 'Follow what God has sent down,' they say, 'Nay, we follow that which we found our fathers doing.' What! Even if their fathers understood naught and were not rightly guided?"* (Qur'an 2:170)

A Book that mocks inherited conformity is not demanding conformity. *Third, the alternative is not neutrality but the rule of desire.* This is the decisive point. A person who reserves the right to accept only the rulings he finds agreeable has not preserved his independence; he has simply made his own preferences the final authority. The Qur'an names this directly:

> *"Hast thou seen the one who takes his caprice as his god?"* (Qur'an 25:43)

There is no position outside submission. One submits to revelation, or to one's own appetite, or to the opinion of the age. The question is never *whether* to submit but *to what*. *Fourth, the submission concerns rulings, not understanding.* Islamic intellectual history is not a record of silence. It produced centuries of the most vigorous legal debate, in which scholars disagreed publicly, argued from evidence, and revised positions. What the verse forbids is not *inquiry* but *evasion* — the refusal to comply with a ruling one accepts as valid because one does not like the outcome.

**The Logic of Trusting a Superior Judgment**

There is an argument from reason here that the Qur'an assumes rather than states, and it is worth making explicit. Every rational person defers, constantly, to judgments he cannot independently verify. A patient accepts a diagnosis he cannot evaluate. A passenger boards an aircraft designed by engineers he has never met. A traveller follows directions from someone who knows the route. In each case the deference is rational precisely because the other party has knowledge one lacks, and no interest in one's harm. It would be irrational to insist on personally verifying the metallurgy of the aircraft before boarding. The believer's position is this argument applied at its limit. If the source of the ruling is *ʿalīm* — knowing all the facts, including those invisible to the person affected — and *ḥakīm* — placing everything in its right proportion — and *raḥīm* — having no interest in the servant's harm, then deference is not the abandonment of reason but its conclusion. This is why the sūrah has been so careful to close its rulings with the divine names. Verse 18: *Knowing, Wise*. Verse 10: *Relenting, Wise*. Verse 20: *Kind, Merciful*. Each is a premise in the argument that this verse's response is rational.

**"And It Is They Who Shall Prosper"**

*Wa ulāʾika humu'l-mufliḥūn.* The verse ends with the Qur'an's characteristic verdict-formula, the same one that closes the description of the reverent at the opening of al-Baqarah (2:5). *Falāḥ*, as discussed there, is a word from agriculture. Its root *f-l-ḥ* is the root of the *fallāḥ*, the ploughman — the word describes the opening of the earth and the thriving that follows from well-worked ground. Its use here completes an argument that has run through the whole sūrah. Verse 31 ended with the same word: *"repent unto God all together, O believers, that haply you may prosper."* Both times, the word for a harvest is attached to an act of submission. The image is exact and it answers the objection this verse most often provokes. Submission looks, from outside, like the surrender of one's autonomy. The Qur'an describes it as *cultivation*: the bounding of a field so that it can bear. A field with no boundaries is not free; it is trampled. And no crop has ever grown from soil that refused to be worked. And the daily call to prayer, five times over, makes the same claim in the same word: *ḥayya ʿala'l-falāḥ* — come to flourishing.

**The Practical Measure**

The verse offers a test that a believer can apply to himself immediately, and it requires no introspection at all. Recall the last time a clear religious ruling ran against something you wanted. What was the first sentence out of your mouth? If it was a question about the evidence, that may be legitimate inquiry. If it was a search for a different opinion, or a delay, or an explanation of why the general rule does not apply in your particular case — then the verse has described you, and it has described you in verses 47 to 50 rather than in verse 51. The believers' response is two words long, and it is spoken before the outcome is known.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:52

> **Whosoever obeys God and His Messenger, and who fears God and reverences Him, it is they who shall triumph.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Conditions and a Verdict**

The previous verse described the believers' response to a summons; this verse generalizes it into a formula for the successful life.
