Four qualities are named, and a single outcome is promised. *Wa man yuṭiʿi'llāha wa rasūlahu wa yakhsha'llāha wa yattaqhi fa-ulāʾika humu'l-fāʾizūn.* The verse repays close reading because the four terms are not synonyms accumulated for emphasis. Each names something distinct, and together they describe a complete religious personality — outward and inward, present and future.

**One: Obedience to God**

*Yuṭiʿi'llāh.* The root *ṭ-w-ʿ* carries the sense of willing compliance; *ṭāʿah* is contrasted in Arabic with *ikrāh*, coercion. Obedience in this vocabulary is not the submission of the conquered but the compliance of the willing. This is the *outward* dimension: the performance of what has been commanded and the avoidance of what has been forbidden. It is observable and measurable.

**Two: Obedience to the Messenger**

*Wa rasūlah.* The pairing of the two obediences is constant in the Qur'an, and it makes a doctrinal point of the first importance: the Messenger's instruction carries binding authority.

> *"Whosoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed God."* (Qur'an 4:80)

> *"Say: If you love God, follow me, and God will love you and forgive you your sins."* (Qur'an 3:31)

> *"And whatsoever the Messenger gives you, take it; and whatsoever he forbids you, forgo."* (Qur'an 59:7)

This is why the Sunnah is a source of law and not merely biography. A Qur'an that repeatedly commands obedience to the Messenger cannot be followed by anyone who ignores what the Messenger said. There is a further point in the grammar that the commentators noticed. Where the Qur'an speaks of the two obediences, it typically joins them; where it speaks of fear and reverence, it names God alone — as in this very verse: *"and who fears **God** and reverences **Him**."* Obedience is owed to God and, derivatively, to His Messenger; but *khashyah* and *taqwā* terminate in God alone.

**Three: Khashyah — Fear Grounded in Knowledge**

*Wa yakhsha'llāh.* The root *kh-sh-y* denotes fear, but the scholars distinguish it carefully from the general word *khawf*. *Khawf* is fear as such — the reaction to a perceived threat, which any creature can experience. *Khashyah* is fear that arises from *knowledge of the greatness of the one feared*. It is closer to awe than to fright, and the Qur'an makes the connection between knowledge and this kind of fear explicit:

> *"Only those of His servants who have knowledge fear God (yakhsha'llāha min ʿibādihi'l-ʿulamāʾ)."* (Qur'an 35:28)

The relationship is worth grasping. A child fears the dark because he does not know what is in it; a scientist fears a live current because he does. The first fear diminishes with knowledge; the second increases with it. *Khashyah* is of the second kind, and this is why the Qur'an attributes it specifically to those who know. This is also the *inward* counterpart to the outward obedience just named. A person can obey without *khashyah* — from habit, from social pressure, from fear of people. The verse requires both the act and the awareness behind it.

**Four: Taqwā — The Wariness That Protects**

*Wa yattaqhi.* The root *w-q-y*, as discussed at verse 34, means to shield or guard against harm. *Taqwā* is the protective wariness of a person who knows there is real danger and adjusts his conduct continuously in response. The classical image bears repeating because it captures the quality exactly: walking a path thick with thorns, gathering one's garment close, placing each step with attention. Not panic, not paralysis, not obliviousness — *care*. Or in the modern idiom used earlier in this commentary: driving carefully on a road one knows to be dangerous. The careful driver is not frightened. He is *awake to the actual conditions*, and his awareness translates into a continuous stream of small adjustments that prevent harm before it arises.

**How Khashyah and Taqwā Differ**

Since both concern fear, the distinction between them is worth stating. The commentators offer several formulations. One is temporal: *khashyah* concerns the past — awe and fear regarding sins already committed — while *taqwā* concerns the future — the guarding of oneself against what is to come. Another is directional: *khashyah* is the *inward state* of awe, while *taqwā* is the *outward conduct* that state produces. Both formulations point to the same completeness. The verse describes a person who acts rightly (obedience), who is inwardly aware of before whom he acts (*khashyah*), and whose awareness governs his conduct going forward (*taqwā*). Nothing is left out: the deed, the heart behind it, and the vigilance that sustains it.

**"It Is They Who Shall Triumph"**

*Fa-ulāʾika humu'l-fāʾizūn.* The verse ends with a different word from the one that ended verse 51. There the successful were *al-mufliḥūn*, the flourishing — the agricultural image. Here they are *al-fāʾizūn*, from the root *f-w-z*. *Fawz* means winning, attainment, triumph — the achievement of a prize and the escape from danger. Its Qur'anic usage is overwhelmingly eschatological: it names the outcome at the end, not prosperity along the way.

> *"Whosoever is spared the Fire and made to enter the Garden has triumphed (fāza)."* (Qur'an 3:185)

That verse defines the term. *Fawz* is not a good life; it is *deliverance from the Fire and entry into the Garden*. Everything else the Qur'an calls by other names. And the Book raises the term to its highest form in the phrase *al-fawz al-ʿaẓīm*, the great triumph, which it attaches to the pleasure of God:

> *"God has promised the believing men and the believing women Gardens with rivers running below, abiding therein, and goodly dwellings in the Gardens of Eden. And Contentment from God is greater. That is the great triumph."* (Qur'an 9:72)

Note the structure of that verse: gardens, rivers, eternal dwellings — and then *"and contentment from God is greater."* The triumph is not finally the reward but the pleasure of the Rewarder.

**The Two Words for Success Together**

The sūrah has now used both of the Qur'an's great success-terms within two verses, and the pairing is instructive. *Falāḥ* (v. 51) is agricultural, gradual, and this-worldly as well as next-worldly: the thriving of a field that has been properly worked. *Fawz* (v. 52) is competitive and final: the winning of a contest, the escape from a danger, the arrival at a prize. Together they describe the two aspects of a religious life. It is a *cultivation* — slow, patient, seasonal, producing growth over decades. And it is a *race* — with a finish line, a prize, and a real possibility of loss. Neither image alone is sufficient. Cultivation without a finish line becomes aimless self-improvement; a race without cultivation becomes anxiety without growth. The Qur'an supplies both in adjacent verses.

**Why This Verse Follows the Hypocrites**

The placement completes the passage's argument. Verses 47 to 50 described people who calculated. They came when the ruling favoured them and withdrew when it did not, and their whole conduct was governed by a running estimate of advantage. Verses 51 and 52 describe the alternative — and then, crucially, reveal that the alternative *wins*. The believer who said "we hear and we obey" without asking about the outcome is declared *muflīḥ* and *fāʾiz*: the one who flourishes and the one who triumphs. The irony is complete. The calculators lost by calculating; the ones who did not calculate won. And the reason is that the calculators were working with an incomplete ledger — they counted the property in the dispute and left out the Fire and the Garden.

**Living the Four Conditions**

A closing practical note. The four terms of this verse can be read as a sequence of increasing interiority, and each supports the next. *Begin with obedience*, even where the heart is not yet engaged. The tradition is clear that action can precede feeling and produce it; the person who prays without presence is nonetheless praying, and presence is often given to those who persist. *Seek knowledge*, because *khashyah* is described in 35:28 as the property of those who know. Awe is not manufactured by effort; it arrives when one understands what one is dealing with. *And then practise taqwā*, which is simply the continuous application of that awareness to the next step, the next word, the next glance — which is, in the end, exactly what this entire sūrah has been teaching from its first verse.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:53

> **And they swear by God with their most solemn oaths that, wert thou to command them, they would surely go forth. Say, "Swear not! [But give] honorable obedience! Surely God is Aware of whatsoever you do."**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Loud Promises and Quiet Absences**

The passage returns to the people described in verses 47 to 50, and catches them in a new posture. Having been exposed as those who turn away from judgment, they now compensate with something they can produce in abundance and at no cost: *oaths*. *Wa aqsamū bi'llāhi jahda aymānihim laʾin amartahum la-yakhrujunn.* They swear — and not casually. The phrase *jahda aymānihim* means "the utmost of their oaths," the strongest and most emphatic forms of swearing available in the language. The word *jahd* denotes maximum exertion; they are straining at the swearing. And the content is a promise of total commitment: *"if you command us, we will surely go forth."*

**"Go Forth": What Is Being Promised**

*La-yakhrujunn* — "they will certainly go out." The commentators understand this as referring to military expeditions: going out on campaign, leaving home and property to march with the Prophet ﷺ. Some extend it more generally to any command he might issue. The promise is therefore of the most demanding kind available. They are not offering to pray more or to give a little charity. They are offering their lives on demand. And the offer is *unconditional* and *hypothetical*. Note the construction: *"if you were to command us."* No command has been issued. They are volunteering for an obligation that does not yet exist — which is the cheapest possible commitment, since it costs nothing today and may never be called in.

**The Psychology of the Grand Uncalled-For Promise**

There is a precise piece of human self-knowledge here that the verse exposes with great economy. People who are failing at a small, present, concrete obligation very often respond by making a large, future, hypothetical one. The mechanism is not usually conscious deceit. It is a way of restoring one's own self-image: *I may have avoided the court, but look how committed I am in principle — I would give my life if asked.* The grand promise functions as a substitute for the small compliance, and it works, psychologically, because it is *emotionally* more impressive. Swearing to march to war feels more religious than accepting an adverse judgment about a piece of land. But only one of the two is actually being requested. The Qur'an addresses this pattern elsewhere with the same directness:

> *"O you who believe! Why do you say what you do not do? It is greatly odious in the sight of God that you say what you do not do."* (Qur'an 61:2–3)

And it records the same pattern historically, of people who promised extravagantly in advance:

> *"And they had previously made a covenant with God that they would not turn their backs. And covenants with God will be asked about."* (Qur'an 33:15)

**"Say: Swear Not!"**

*Qul lā tuqsimū.* The response commanded of the Prophet ﷺ is a two-word interruption, and it is startling in its bluntness. He is not told to thank them for their commitment, nor to test it, nor to explain why he doubts them. He is told to cut the oath off mid-flow: *don't swear.* The commentators note what this implies. An oath is normally an honorable act; the Qur'an itself swears by created things, and legitimate oaths have a recognized place in Islamic law. To be told *"do not swear"* is to be told that in this instance the swearing is itself the problem — that it is functioning as a substitute for the thing it promises rather than as a guarantee of it. There is also an implicit statement about the value of their word. An oath adds weight to a statement only where the speaker's honesty is presumed. Where it is not, additional swearing adds nothing, and the more elaborate it becomes the more suspicious it appears. Excessive protestation is, universally, a signal of its own.

**"Honorable Obedience"**

*Ṭāʿatun maʿrūfah.* The Arabic is elliptical — two words with an implied verb — and the commentators supply it in two ways: either "what is required is honorable obedience," or "let there be honorable obedience." The key term is *maʿrūf*, from the root *ʿ-r-f*, to know or recognize. As noted at verse 21, *maʿrūf* means simultaneously "the known" and "the good" — that which any sound person recognizes as right. It is the standard Qur'anic word for decent, recognized, customary good conduct, as in the community's duty to enjoin *al-maʿrūf*. So *ṭāʿatun maʿrūfah* means obedience of the *recognized* kind: ordinary, known, actual compliance in the matters that are actually before you. Not extraordinary, not hypothetical, not sworn — simply *done*. The commentators gloss it variously as sincere obedience, or obedience in what is right, or — most tellingly — obedience *without the need for an oath*. The contrast the verse constructs is therefore between two things: *Sworn commitment to a dramatic future act.* Loud, emotional, impressive, and free. *Quiet compliance in an ordinary present one.* Silent, unremarkable, unimpressive, and costly. The Qur'an asks for the second and dismisses the first.

**The Ordinary Is the Test**

This is a principle of considerable practical importance, and it deserves to be stated apart from the verse's immediate context. Religious commitment is not measured by what a person would do in an imagined crisis. Almost everyone believes they would be heroic if the occasion arose, and the belief is untestable and therefore comfortable. It is measured by what a person does with the small, present, unglamorous obligations that are actually in front of him — the prayer at its time, the debt repaid, the ruling accepted, the temper controlled, the promise kept to a child. The Prophet ﷺ located the value of religious practice precisely there:

> *"The deeds most beloved to God are the most constant ones, even if they are few."* (al-Bukhārī 6464; Muslim 783)

The criterion is *constancy*, not intensity. A small act sustained is preferred to a large one performed once — which is the exact inverse of what the people in this verse were offering.

**"Surely God Is Aware of Whatsoever You Do"**

*Inna'llāha khabīrun bimā taʿmalūn.* The closing name is *khabīr*, the Aware — the same name that closed verse 30's command to lower the gaze. As noted there, *khabīr* denotes knowledge of the *inner reality* of a thing, not merely its surface. Its placement here is exact. The sentence they wanted heard was the oath; the reality God knows is the record of what they actually do. Note also the final word: *bimā taʿmalūn* — "what you *do*," not what you say. The whole verse has been about the gap between the two, and the closing clause names which side of the gap is being recorded. There is a further precision in the choice of *khabīr* over, say, *samīʿ*, the Hearing. God certainly heard their oaths. But hearing an oath tells one nothing about whether it will be honored. What is required to evaluate a promise is knowledge of the *inner state of the promiser* — whether the resolve behind the words exists. That is precisely what *khabīr* names.

**The Verse Applied**

Three practical consequences. *Distrust the impulse to promise when you have just failed.* The pattern this verse exposes is nearly automatic in human beings: a lapse in a small duty produces an urge toward a grand declaration. The declaration feels like recovery and is actually avoidance. The correct response to a missed obligation is to perform the next one, not to swear about a future one. *Beware of the religion that lives in the hypothetical.* A great deal of religious enthusiasm consists in strong opinions about situations one will never face and strong commitments to actions one will never be asked to take. It is emotionally satisfying and produces nothing. *Ṭāʿatun maʿrūfah* is the corrective: what is actually being asked of you, today. *Let compliance be quiet.* The verse's instruction — do not swear, simply obey — carries an implication about the manner of obedience. There is something in the human being that wants credit for its submission, and announcement is how the credit is claimed. The two-word rebuke *lā tuqsimū* falls on exactly that instinct.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:54

> **Say, "Obey God and obey the Messenger." But if they turn away, he is only accountable for that wherewith he has been burdened, and you are accountable for that wherewith you have been burdened. But if you obey him, you will be rightly guided, and naught is incumbent upon the Messenger save the clear proclamation.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Division of Responsibility**

This verse settles a question that runs underneath the whole passage on the hypocrites: *who is answerable for what?* The community had been described as containing people who profess faith and withdraw from judgment, who swear grand oaths and avoid ordinary compliance. The Prophet ﷺ was in the position of every teacher, leader, parent, and caller to truth who has ever faced the same situation: how much of their failure is his? The verse answers with a clean division. He is responsible for the delivery. They are responsible for the response.

**"Obey God and Obey the Messenger"**

*Qul aṭīʿu'llāha wa aṭīʿu'l-rasūl.* Note that the verb is *repeated*: obey God, *and obey* the Messenger. The Qur'an sometimes joins the two under a single verb (as at verse 52: *"whosoever obeys God and His Messenger"*), and sometimes repeats it as here. The commentators drew a point from the repetition. Where the verb is repeated, the Messenger's obedience is given independent standing — meaning that his instructions are to be obeyed *as such*, including in matters where no specific Qur'anic text exists. This is the scriptural basis for the authority of the Sunnah as an independent source of law rather than merely a commentary on the Book. The Prophet ﷺ warned against the position that would reject it:

> *"Let me not find one of you reclining on his couch, and when a matter I have commanded or forbidden comes to him, he says: 'I do not know — whatever we find in the Book of God, we follow.'"* (Abū Dāwūd 4605; al-Tirmidhī 2663)

**"He Is Accountable for What He Was Burdened With, and You for What You Were Burdened With"**

*Fa-innamā ʿalayhi mā ḥummila wa ʿalaykum mā ḥummiltum.* The verb is *ḥummila*, the passive of *ḥammala*, to load — the same root as *ḥiml*, a load or burden, and *ḥāmil*, a bearer. Both parties have been *loaded* with something, and each is answerable for his own load. The image is that of two porters, each carrying his own weight. Neither can carry the other's, and neither is asked to. *The Messenger's load* is delivery: proclamation, clarification, warning, and example. It is named explicitly at the end of the verse. *The community's load* is response: hearing, accepting, and acting. The Qur'an states this principle of individual accountability throughout:

> *"And no bearer of burdens shall bear the burden of another."* (Qur'an 6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38)

And it applied it to the Prophet ﷺ himself with striking directness:

> *"Thou art not a warder over them."* (Qur'an 88:22)

> *"So remind, for thou art but a reminder; thou art not a taskmaster over them."* (Qur'an 88:21–22)

> *"Truly thou dost not guide whomsoever thou lovest, but God guides whomsoever He will."* (Qur'an 28:56)

**The Consolation in the Division**

This teaching is, in the first instance, a mercy directed at the Prophet ﷺ, and the Qur'an addressed his distress at the refusal of his people more than once:

> *"Perhaps thou wouldst destroy thyself with grief, [sorrowing] after them, if they believe not in this discourse."* (Qur'an 18:6)

> *"Perhaps thou wouldst torment thyself that they are not believers."* (Qur'an 26:3)

The verb in both is severe: *bākhiʿ*, one who destroys or exhausts himself. The Prophet ﷺ was grieving himself over people who would not respond, and God repeatedly relieved him of the weight by telling him it was not his to carry. That relief extends to every believer in an analogous position, and the analogous positions are numerous: the parent whose child has left the religion, the teacher whose students do not learn, the caller whose invitation is refused, the friend whose advice is ignored. The division of loads applies exactly. *Deliver well* — that is the load, and it is a real one, with real standards: clarity, patience, kindness, good example, persistence. *Do not take responsibility for the reception* — that load belongs to someone else, and attempting to carry it produces exhaustion, resentment, and often the very pressure that hardens the other person against the message.

**"But If You Obey Him, You Will Be Rightly Guided"**

*Wa in tuṭīʿūhu tahtadū.* A conditional of the simplest possible form: obey, and you are guided. There is no third element, no intermediary, no complexity. The verb *tahtadū* is from *h-d-y*, the root of *hudā*, guidance, and of the Fātiḥah's *ihdinā*, "guide us." It is in the reflexive form: you will *find yourselves guided*, you will come to be upon guidance. There is a theological point in the *order* of the conditional that is easy to miss and worth stating. One might expect guidance to precede obedience: first God guides, then the guided person obeys. The verse states the reverse: *obey, and you will be guided*. Obedience is the cause and guidance the consequence. The tradition explains this as a description of how guidance actually deepens. Initial guidance is a gift — the sūrah has said so twice (vv. 35, 46). But its *increase* is earned by acting on what has already been received. The Qur'an makes the principle explicit:

> *"And as for those who are rightly guided, He increases them in guidance and gives them their reverence."* (Qur'an 47:17)

> *"And those who strive for Us, We shall surely guide them in Our ways."* (Qur'an 29:69)

This has a direct practical consequence, and it is one of the most useful teachings in the religion. A person who feels distant, unsure, or spiritually dry frequently responds by waiting — for clarity, for motivation, for a feeling to return. The verse prescribes the opposite: *act on what you already know*. Guidance follows obedience rather than preceding it, and the person waiting for certainty before acting will wait indefinitely.

**"Naught Is Incumbent Upon the Messenger Save the Clear Proclamation"**

*Wa mā ʿala'l-rasūli illa'l-balāghu'l-mubīn.* The verse ends by naming the Messenger's load precisely: *al-balāgh al-mubīn*. *Balāgh* is from *b-l-gh*, to reach or arrive. It means the *conveying* of something so that it *arrives* at its destination. The word implies more than transmission; it implies successful delivery. A message spoken into the air is not *balāgh*; a message that reaches the hearer is. *Mubīn* is from *b-y-n*, the root that has run through this entire sūrah — the *āyāt mubayyināt* of verses 1, 34, and 46; the *ifk mubīn* of verse 12; the *al-Ḥaqq al-Mubīn* of verse 25. It means clear, plain, distinguishing. So the Messenger's burden is: *to make it arrive, and to make it plain*. That is a demanding standard, and it should not be read as a low bar. It requires that the message be delivered in a language the audience understands, in a manner they can receive, with sufficient explanation to remove ambiguity, and with an example that demonstrates it. The Qur'an insists on the linguistic dimension:

> *"And We sent no messenger save with the language of his people, that he might make clear unto them."* (Qur'an 14:4)

**The Standard for Anyone Who Speaks for the Religion**

The phrase *al-balāgh al-mubīn* has an obvious application to anyone in the position of conveying religious teaching today, and it cuts in two directions. It *limits* the responsibility: no one is answerable for whether the listener accepts. The measure of a teacher, a preacher, or a parent is not the outcome. But it *raises* the standard for the delivery itself. If the whole of one's obligation is clear conveyance, then clarity is not optional. A message delivered in a register the audience cannot follow, in a tone that repels, in a language they do not speak, or with the substance obscured, has not satisfied *balāgh mubīn* — and the deficiency is on the deliverer's side of the ledger. This is worth stating plainly because the verse is sometimes used as a shield: *my job is only to convey.* True — but the conveyance must *arrive*, and it must be *clear*. Those two adjectives contain a great deal of work.

**The Verse in the Passage**

Standing back, verses 47 to 54 have moved through a complete sequence. The claim of faith (v. 47). The test of judgment (v. 48). The inconsistency exposed (v. 49). The excuses eliminated (v. 50). The believers' alternative (v. 51). The reward promised (v. 52). The substitute of grand oaths dismissed (v. 53). And now the division of responsibility, which closes the matter. Nothing remains to be argued. The message has been made clear, the two responses have been described, the outcomes have been named, and the accountability has been assigned. What follows at verse 55 is not further argument but a promise — one of the greatest in the Qur'an — addressed to those who took the first road.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:55

> **God has promised those among you who believe and perform righteous deeds that He will surely make them vicegerents upon the earth, as He caused those before them to be vicegerents, and that He will establish for them their religion, which He has approved for them, and that He will surely change them from a state of fear to [one of] security. They will worship Me, not ascribing any partners unto Me. And whosoever disbelieves thereafter, it is they who are iniquitous.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Great Promise**

This is among the most consequential verses in the Qur'an for Muslim political and historical consciousness. It is a divine promise of *worldly establishment* — succession, security, and the firm rooting of the religion — given to a specific description of people, under a specific condition, with a specific purpose. It should be read slowly, because every clause has been the subject of centuries of hope, argument, and occasionally of serious misuse.

**The Context in Which It Was Revealed**

The commentators preserve the setting, and it is essential to feeling the verse's force. The Muslims in Madinah at the time were in a condition of continuous insecurity. Reports describe them as sleeping in their weapons, keeping watch by night, unable to lay down arms even at home, surrounded by hostile tribes and by a Makkah still committed to their destruction. Ubayy ibn Kaʿb is reported to have described the state as one in which they could not put down their arms morning or evening, and to have asked the Prophet ﷺ whether a time would come when they could sit in safety. The verse was revealed in that atmosphere. To such a community — a few thousand people in an oasis town, militarily precarious, economically strained — God promised sovereignty over the earth, the establishment of their religion, and the exchange of fear for security. Within a generation the promise had been fulfilled to a degree that would have been unimaginable to the people who first heard it: the Arabian peninsula unified, and within two decades the two great empires of the age — Byzantium's eastern provinces and Sasanian Persia entire — overtaken by a community that had once been unable to sleep without weapons.

**"Those Among You Who Believe and Perform Righteous Deeds"**

*Alladhīna āmanū minkum wa ʿamilu'l-ṣāliḥāt.* The promise is *conditional*, and the condition is stated first, before anything else. This must be emphasized, because the verse has often been read as a guarantee to Muslims as a demographic category rather than to Muslims of a particular description. Two conditions are named, and the Qur'an pairs them constantly throughout: *faith* and *righteous action*. Belief without works and works without belief are both excluded by the conjunction. The Qur'an states the general law of which this promise is an instance:

> *"…Truly God alters not what is in a people until they alter what is in themselves."* (Qur'an 13:11)

That verse is the key to reading this one honestly. The condition of a community's outward circumstances tracks the condition of its inward state. A promise conditional on faith and righteous action does not apply to a community that has retained the label and abandoned the substance — and any reading that treats it as an unconditional entitlement contradicts the verse's own first clause.

**Three Promised Things**

**One: Istikhlāf — Succession on the Earth**

*La-yastakhlifannahum fi'l-arḍ.* The verb is from *kh-l-f*, the root of *khalīfah* — successor, deputy, viceroy. Its basic sense is *coming after* and *taking the place of*. The Qur'an uses the term at the very beginning of human history:

> *"And when thy Lord said unto the angels, 'I am placing a vicegerent (khalīfah) upon the earth…'"* (Qur'an 2:30)

And it uses it of previous communities:

> *"Then We appointed you as vicegerents upon the earth after them, that We might observe how you would behave."* (Qur'an 10:14)

That second verse supplies the crucial gloss, and the present verse echoes it with *"as He caused those before them to be vicegerents."* Succession is a *test*, not a trophy. The purpose clause in 10:14 is explicit: *"that We might observe how you would behave."* This reframes the promise entirely. To be given authority on the earth is to be handed an examination, and the Qur'an is full of communities that were given exactly this and failed it. The verse's own comparison — *"as He caused those before them"* — is therefore double-edged: they too were established, and their establishment ended when they ceased to meet its conditions.

**Two: Tamkīn — The Establishment of the Religion**

*Wa la-yumakkinanna lahum dīnahumu'lladhi'rtaḍā lahum.* The verb *makkana* is from *m-k-n*, the root of *makān*, place. To be *mukkan* is to be given a *place*, to be firmly situated, to be rooted rather than provisional. The image is architectural or botanical: something that had no secure position is set on a foundation. For a religion, *tamkīn* means the ability to be practised openly, transmitted to children, institutionalized in law and learning, and sustained across generations — as opposed to being practised in secret, under threat, or in the margins. And note the qualifier: *dīnahumu'lladhi'rtaḍā lahum* — "their religion, *which He has approved for them*." The verb *irtaḍā* is from *r-ḍ-w*, contentment and approval, and the same root produced the declaration of the final revelation:

> *"This day I have perfected for you your religion, and completed My Blessing upon you, and have approved for you Islam as a religion (raḍītu lakumu'l-islāma dīnan)."* (Qur'an 5:3)

The promise is thus not merely that the community will be secure, but that *this particular religion, as God approved it*, will be given a place. Which implies a further condition, quietly stated: the thing established must be the religion He approved, not a version of it altered to suit the establishment.

**Three: The Exchange of Fear for Security**

*Wa la-yubaddilannahum min baʿdi khawfihim amnan.* The verb *baddala* means to *exchange*, to substitute one thing for another. It is not that fear is reduced; it is *replaced*. This is the most humanly immediate of the three promises, and the most easily underestimated by those who have never lived without security. Security is the precondition of nearly every human good. A community in fear cannot build, cannot educate, cannot trade, cannot plan, cannot raise children with stability, and cannot worship with attention. The Qur'an names *amn* as a foundational blessing repeatedly, and the Prophet ﷺ made it one of three sufficiencies:

> *"Whoever among you wakes up secure in his household, healthy in his body, having his food for the day — it is as though the world in its entirety has been given to him."* (al-Tirmidhī 2346)

And the Book presents Abraham praying for it before anything else:

> *"And when Abraham said: 'My Lord, make this a secure land, and provide its people with fruits…'"* (Qur2:126)

Security first, then provision.

**The Purpose Clause: Why the Promise Is Given**

*Yaʿbudūnanī lā yushrikūna bī shayʾan* — "They will worship Me, not ascribing any partners unto Me." This clause is the interpretive centre of the entire verse, and it is the one most frequently omitted when the verse is quoted. The promise of succession, establishment, and security is not given for its own sake. It is given *so that* God may be worshipped without associating anything with Him. The political outcome is the *means*; the pure worship is the *end*. This inverts the reading that treats the verse as a charter for power. Power here is instrumental. A community that obtains authority and does not use it for the unassociated worship of God has not fulfilled the verse; it has merely acquired the means and abandoned the purpose. And the Qur'an states the same purpose as the reason for human existence:

> *"And I did not create jinn and mankind save to worship Me."* (Qur'an 51:56)

There is a further precision in the phrasing. *Lā yushrikūna bī shayʾan* — "not associating with Me anything." *Shirk*, association, is not only the worship of idols. It includes the elevation of anything to a position of ultimate authority alongside God — wealth, power, nation, appetite, or one's own opinion. A community that gains political dominance and then serves its dominance rather than its Lord has fallen into precisely the *shirk* the clause excludes.

**"And Whosoever Disbelieves Thereafter"**

*Wa man kafara baʿda dhālika fa-ulāʾika humu'l-fāsiqūn.* The verse ends with a warning, and its placement is deliberate: it comes *after* the promise, addressing what happens once the promise has been fulfilled. *Baʿda dhālika* — "after that." After the succession, after the establishment, after the exchange of fear for security. The danger the verse anticipates is not defeat but *success*: the ingratitude that follows the granting of what was prayed for. This is a pattern the Qur'an documents repeatedly. Communities are steadfast in adversity and corrupted by ease. The Prophet ﷺ named the danger directly:

> *"By God, it is not poverty that I fear for you. Rather, I fear that the world will be spread out before you as it was spread out before those before you, and that you will compete for it as they competed for it, and that it will destroy you as it destroyed them."* (al-Bukhārī 3158; Muslim 2961)

The verse and the hadith make the same point: the test of hardship is real, but the test of establishment is harder, and more people fail it. And the label assigned is *fāsiqūn* — the iniquitous, from the root discussed at verse 4: those who have burst out of the shell of obedience. The same word applied to the slanderer at the sūrah's opening now applies to the ungrateful inheritor of divine favour.

**How the Verse Should and Should Not Be Used**

Because this verse touches politics, it requires an honest statement of its limits. *It is not a guarantee to a demographic.* The condition is faith and righteous action, stated in the first clause. Any use of the verse that skips the condition has misquoted it. *It is not a licence for the pursuit of power as an end.* The purpose clause makes worship the objective and authority the means. A movement that reverses the two has inverted the verse. *It is not a prediction that can be forced.* The promise is God's to fulfil, in His timing, upon His conditions. The Qur'an is consistent that the believer's task is the meeting of conditions, not the engineering of outcomes. *And it is not a promise of ease.* The generation to whom it was first given fulfilled it through decades of extreme difficulty, and many of those who heard the verse did not live to see it realized. What it *is*, read properly, is a statement of causation: that the inward state of a community determines its outward condition; that faith joined to righteous action produces, by God's promise, stability and security; and that the purpose of any such stability is the unassociated worship of God.

**The Personal Reading**

There is also an application at the scale of an individual life, and the commentators on the spiritual dimension of the Qur'an have drawn it. Each of the three promises has a personal analogue. *Istikhlāf*: the person of faith is given a real place and function in the world rather than drifting. *Tamkīn*: his religion becomes rooted in him — established as habit and character rather than sporadic enthusiasm. *Amn*: the exchange of fear for security describes an inward condition as much as an outward one, and the Qur'an says of it:

> *"Behold, in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* (Qur'an 13:28)

And the condition is identical at both scales: belief joined to righteous action, aimed at the worship of God without partners. What is promised to a community is promised on the same terms to a soul.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:56

> **And perform the prayer, and give the alms, and obey the Messenger, that haply you may receive mercy.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Practical Programme After the Great Promise**

Verse 55 promised succession on the earth, the establishment of religion, and the exchange of fear for security. A reader who has just absorbed a promise of that magnitude naturally asks: *what am I to do?* This verse answers, and the answer is startling in its modesty. Not a political programme, not a military strategy, not an institutional design. Three things: pray, give, and obey. The placement is the teaching. The Qur'an follows its greatest worldly promise with its most ordinary religious instructions, and thereby says something about the relationship between the two: *the conditions of the promise are met in the daily practice of religion, not in the pursuit of the promise itself.*

**Three Commands, Three Domains**

The three commands are not arbitrary. They cover the whole of a person's obligations in a way that the Qur'an repeats constantly. *Aqīmu'l-ṣalāh* — establish the prayer. This is the *vertical* dimension: the direct relationship between the servant and God, structured in time. *Wa ātu'l-zakāh* — give the alms. This is the *horizontal* dimension: the relationship with other people, structured in wealth. *Wa aṭīʿu'l-rasūl* — obey the Messenger. This is the *normative* dimension: the acceptance of an external standard rather than self-direction in either of the first two. Prayer and alms are paired dozens of times in the Qur'an — so consistently that the pairing amounts to a definition of what religious life consists of. And the addition of obedience to the Messenger here connects the verse back to the passage that preceded it, in which the failure to obey was the entire subject.

**"Establish" the Prayer, Not Merely "Perform" It**

The verb is *aqīmū*, from *q-w-m*, to stand. The causative form means to *set something upright*, to make it stand, to maintain it in its proper condition. The Qur'an never says "do the prayer" (*ifʿalū*); it consistently says *establish* it. The commentators drew a substantial teaching from this. To establish prayer means more than to complete its physical motions. It means to perform it at its appointed time, with its conditions fulfilled, with attention (*khushūʿ*), in congregation where possible, and — crucially — *consistently*, so that it stands as a permanent structure in a life rather than an occasional event. The distinction is between a building erected and a building maintained. A prayer performed is an act; a prayer *established* is an institution. And the Qur'an ties the quality of the prayer to its effect:

> *"Truly the prayer prevents indecency and wrong."* (Qur'an 29:45)

That is a testable claim, and it functions as a diagnostic. A prayer that leaves the one praying unchanged in his conduct has not been established in the sense the verb intends.

**"Give" the Alms**

*Zakāh*, as noted at verse 21, is from the root *z-k-w*, meaning both purity and growth. The obligatory alms are so called because the act of giving simultaneously *purifies* the remaining wealth and causes it to *grow* — the same paradox as the pruning of a tree. The Qur'an states the purifying function explicitly:

> *"Take alms from their wealth, to cleanse them and purify them thereby."* (Qur'an 9:103)

And the Prophet ﷺ stated the growth:

> *"Charity does not decrease wealth."* (Muslim 2588)

Placing the alms immediately after the prayer, as the Qur'an does everywhere, makes an argument about the nature of worship: that a religion consisting only of the vertical relationship is incomplete. The Book is emphatic that ritual observance divorced from social obligation is worthless, and it says so with considerable severity:

> *"Hast thou seen the one who denies religion? That is the one who repels the orphan and urges not the feeding of the indigent. So woe unto those who pray, who are heedless of their prayers, who make display, and who refuse small kindnesses."* (Qur'an 107:1–7)

That sūrah describes people who *pray* and are nonetheless described as deniers of religion, and the disqualifying evidence is entirely social: the orphan repelled, the poor unfed, the small kindness withheld.

**"And Obey the Messenger"**

The third command connects this verse to everything from verse 47 onward. That whole passage concerned people who would not obey, and this verse gives the positive instruction in a single phrase. Its placement after prayer and alms also serves a corrective function. Prayer and charity are activities a person can shape according to his own preference — praying when convenient, giving to causes he finds appealing, in amounts he considers reasonable. The addition of *obey the Messenger* subjects both to an external standard: pray *as he prayed*, and give *as he instructed*. The Prophet ﷺ made the first of these explicit:

> *"Pray as you have seen me pray."* (al-Bukhārī 631)

**"That Haply You May Receive Mercy"**

*Laʿallakum turḥamūn.* The purpose clause names the outcome, and the choice of word deserves attention. The verse does not say "that you may be rewarded," or "that you may be successful," or "that you may enter Paradise." It says *that you may receive mercy* — the passive of *raḥima*, from the root of the womb, discussed at verse 5. Why mercy rather than reward? Because reward implies desert, and the tradition is unanimous that no one enters Paradise by desert. The Prophet ﷺ stated it in the report already quoted at verse 21:

> *"None of you will enter Paradise by his deeds."* They said: "Not even you, O Messenger of God?" He said: *"Not even I, unless God covers me with His mercy."* (al-Bukhārī 6463; Muslim 2816)

So the three commands are not presented as a transaction in which obedience purchases an outcome. They are presented as the *positioning* of oneself in the path of a mercy that is given rather than earned. This is a subtle but important point about how Islamic practice should be understood. The prayers, the alms, and the obedience do not create an entitlement. They place a person where mercy falls — much as opening a window does not create the sunlight but determines whether it enters the room. And the particle *laʿalla*, "perhaps," preserves exactly this. As noted at verse 1, it expresses the expected outcome from the human side while leaving the granting where it belongs. The believer acts in hope, not in the confidence of a creditor.

**Why These Three Sustain a Community**

There is a sociological observation embedded in this verse that is worth drawing out, particularly given its placement after the promise of establishment. Consider what the three commands actually do to a population that practises them. *Prayer* imposes a shared schedule on the entire community, five times a day, and requires physical assembly. It produces, as a by-product, the most reliable mechanism of social cohesion ever devised: neighbours standing shoulder to shoulder, without rank, several times daily, indefinitely. *Alms* impose a continuous transfer from those with surplus to those without, at a fixed rate, as a religious obligation rather than a discretionary kindness. It prevents the accumulation of the resentments that destroy societies from within. *Obedience to the Messenger* provides a common normative standard, which means disputes have a shared reference point and are resolvable — the exact thing the hypocrites of verses 47 to 50 were refusing. A community that does these three things is stable, cohesive, and internally just. A community that abandons them is none of these, whatever its wealth or military strength. So the verse's placement after the great promise is not a change of subject. It is the *mechanism* of the promise. Verse 55 stated that God would establish those who believe and act righteously; verse 56 specifies what righteous action consists of at its foundation.

**The Application**

The practical lesson of this verse's position is one that applies to every ambitious believer and every ambitious Muslim community. The temptation, when confronted with a great promise or a great task, is to look for a great response — a strategy, a movement, a project of appropriate scale. The Qur'an's answer to the promise of succession on the earth is: *pray, give, and obey.* Small, daily, repeatable, unglamorous, and — as verse 53 has just warned — the exact opposite of the grand oath sworn about a hypothetical future. Whatever transformation is coming will be built out of these, or not at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:57

> **Do not suppose that the disbelievers thwart [aught] on the earth. The Fire will be their refuge—what an evil journey's end!**

**Expanded Commentary**

**Reassurance to an Outmatched Community**

This verse addresses a specific anxiety, and it is worth naming it plainly because it recurs in every generation. The believers had just been promised succession, establishment, and security (v. 55). Looking around, they saw the opposite: powerful opponents, hostile tribes, empires beyond the horizon, and hypocrites within their own ranks. The natural question is whether the opposition might actually *prevent* the promise from being fulfilled. The verse forecloses it: *lā taḥsabanna'lladhīna kafarū muʿjizīna fi'l-arḍ.*

**Taḥsabanna: The Verb of Miscalculation, Again**

The verb is *ḥasiba* — to suppose, reckon, calculate — and its appearance here completes a pattern running through the sūrah. Verse 11: *"Do not suppose it to be an evil for you."*
Verse 12: the believers should have thought well.
Verse 15: *"supposing it to be slight, though it is great in the Eyes of God."*
Verse 39: *"a mirage which a thirsty man supposes is water."*
And now: *"Do not suppose that the disbelievers thwart anything on the earth."* In every instance the Qur'an is correcting a *calculation* — a reckoning that human beings perform automatically and get wrong. The sūrah is, among other things, a sustained critique of the human capacity to assess a situation correctly, and it repeatedly substitutes God's assessment for the reader's. Here the miscalculation is about *power*. What appears overwhelming is not.

**Muʿjizīn: Those Who Render Powerless**

The word is *muʿjizīn*, from the root *ʿ-j-z*, meaning inability, incapacity, weakness. The form used means "those who render another incapable" — to *thwart*, to *frustrate*, to *escape from* someone's grasp so that he cannot reach you, to *defeat* an attempt. The same root produces *iʿjāz*, the term used for the Qur'an's inimitability: the quality of rendering all challengers *incapable* of matching it. So the verse denies two things simultaneously, and the commentators note both senses. *They cannot thwart God's plan.* Their opposition does not obstruct what God has determined. *They cannot escape God's reach.* There is nowhere on earth they can go where He does not have them. The Qur'an states the second sense repeatedly:

> *"And know that you cannot thwart God, and that God disgraces the disbelievers."* (Qur'an 9:2)

> *"Do not suppose that God is heedless of what the wrongdoers do. He only grants them respite until a day when eyes will stare, transfixed."* (Qur'an 14:42)

**"On the Earth"**

The qualifier *fi'l-arḍ* — "on the earth" — is not filler. It defines the entire scope of what the opposition possesses. Their power, whatever it amounts to, is *terrestrial*: bounded by geography, by lifespan, by the reach of armies and the durability of institutions. The verse concedes the earthly domain to them rhetorically and then, in the next breath, names the destination that lies outside it. And the sūrah has already established who owns even that domain: *"And unto God belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth, and unto God is the journey's end"* (v. 42). The vocabulary of that verse returns in this one — *al-maṣīr*, the journey's end — so that the reader who has been paying attention hears the connection.

**The Danger This Verse Addresses**

It is worth being precise about the psychological condition being treated, because it is not simple fear. The verse does not say "do not be afraid." It says "do not *suppose*" — it targets a *belief*, not an emotion. And the belief in question is that opposing power is *ultimate*: that what one's opponents decide will finally determine the outcome. This belief is corrosive in a specific way. A person who holds it does not necessarily abandon his principles; more often he simply concludes that principled action is futile. He continues to believe and stops acting, on the grounds that acting would change nothing. Despair produces inaction far more efficiently than fear does. The Qur'an addresses this by relocating the question of ultimate outcomes. Whatever the balance of visible forces, the final settlement is not administered by the visible forces. The Prophet ﷺ gave the same reassurance to Khabbāb ibn al-Aratt, who came to him while he was resting in the shade of the Kaʿbah during the worst of the Makkan persecution and asked him to pray for relief. He sat up, angry, and said:

> *"Among those before you, a man would be seized and a pit dug for him, and he would be placed in it; then a saw would be brought and placed upon his head and he would be sawn in two, and combs of iron would be raked through the flesh below his bones — and that would not turn him from his religion. By God, this affair will be completed, until a rider travels from Ṣanʿāʾ to Ḥaḍramawt fearing none but God, and the wolf for his sheep. But you are people who are hasty."* (al-Bukhārī 3612)

The final sentence is the point. The outcome was certain; the impatience was the problem.

**"The Fire Will Be Their Refuge"**

*Wa maʾwāhumu'l-nār.* The word *maʾwā* is from *ʾ-w-y*, to take shelter, to seek refuge, to come home to. A *maʾwā* is a place of resort — where one goes for safety and rest. The irony in the choice of word is savage and deliberate. The Fire is called their *refuge*. What they fled to for protection turns out to be the destination. The Qur'an uses this word for both destinations, which sharpens the contrast:

> *"But as for one who feared the station of his Lord and restrained the soul from caprice, truly the Garden shall be the refuge (al-maʾwā)."* (Qur'an 79:40–41)

And *Jannat al-Maʾwā*, "the Garden of Refuge," is named at 53:15. So the vocabulary is identical and the destinations are opposite. Everyone is heading toward a *maʾwā*; the verse concerns which one.

**"What an Evil Journey's End"**

*Wa la-biʾsa'l-maṣīr.* The exclamation uses *biʾsa*, the Arabic verb of dispraise — the counterpart to *niʿma*, the verb of praise. It is a formula of condemnation, roughly "how evil a…!" And *maṣīr*, as discussed at verse 42, means the place one *ends up*, the outcome of a process. The word's return here creates a deliberate echo: verse 42 said *"unto God is the journey's end"*; this verse names what that end is for those who spent their lives thwarting.

**The Balance the Verse Strikes**

There is a danger in a verse of this kind, and it should be named. Read carelessly, it could produce complacency: *our opponents cannot thwart anything, so nothing is required of us.* That reading is excluded by the verse immediately before it, which commanded prayer, alms, and obedience, and by verse 55, which made the entire promise conditional on faith and righteous action. The correct reading is the one that produces *steadiness* rather than either panic or passivity. The believer works, and works hard, without the anxiety that comes from believing that the outcome rests on his shoulders or on his opponents' permission. This is the disposition the Qur'an calls *tawakkul*, reliance — which the tradition is careful to distinguish from abandonment of effort. When a man asked whether he should tie his camel or trust in God, the Prophet ﷺ replied:

> *"Tie it and trust in God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2517)

Both halves are commanded. The tying is the human load, named in verse 54; the outcome belongs to the One who cannot be thwarted.

**Contemporary Application**

The verse speaks directly to a condition common among Muslims today and among any minority or principled group facing overwhelming odds: the sense that the forces arrayed against one are simply too large, too well resourced, and too entrenched for anything to matter. The verse's response is not to dispute the assessment of relative strength. It grants it, and then denies its finality. Power on the earth is real; it is also *only* on the earth, and it is held by beings who are themselves proceeding, at the ordinary rate of one day per day, toward a *maṣīr* they did not choose and cannot avoid. What follows from this is neither triumphalism nor despair, but the freedom to act rightly without needing to win — which is, in the end, the only condition under which people reliably act rightly at all.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:58

> **O you who believe! Let those whom your right hands possess and those who have not come of age ask your leave three times: before the dawn prayer, when you doff your garments at noon, and after the night prayer—three times of privacy for you. Beyond these, there is no blame upon you or upon them should you go about [visiting] one another. Thus does God make clear unto you the signs, and God is Knowing, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Return to the Threshold**

The sūrah now performs a striking manoeuvre. Having ascended through the Light Verse, the parables, the cosmic signs, the exposure of the hypocrites, and the great promise of establishment on the earth, it returns — at verse 58 — to the subject of knocking on doors. The return is not anticlimactic; it is the chapter's argument restated. A community promised sovereignty over the earth is instructed, in the same breath, in the etiquette of a child entering his parents' room at midday. The sūrah refuses to let the grand and the domestic separate. Whatever establishment God grants will be built by people who observe the small rules, or it will not last. Some commentators, as noted in the introduction, held this particular verse to be Makkan, though the sūrah as a whole is Madinan.

**Who Must Ask, and When**

The rule of verses 27 to 29 concerned entering *other people's houses*. This verse concerns entry within *one's own household* — and it addresses two categories of person who would ordinarily move freely through it: *Those whom your right hands possess* — household servants and those in bonded service, who by the nature of their work pass in and out of every room. *Those who have not come of age* — the children of the household, who have not yet reached puberty. The Arabic is *alladhīna lam yablughu'l-ḥuluma minkum*, "those among you who have not reached the age of dreams," using a delicate idiom for the onset of sexual maturity. These two groups are exempt from the general rule of permission-seeking, precisely because they belong to the household. This verse therefore does not impose the general rule on them; it imposes a *limited* version, restricted to three specific times.

**The Three Times**

*Min qabli ṣalāti'l-fajr* — before the dawn prayer. The hour when people are asleep or rising, unclothed or partially so, before the day's dressing has occurred. *Wa ḥīna taḍaʿūna thiyābakum mina'l-ẓahīrah* — when you lay aside your garments at midday. In the heat of the Arabian noon, outer clothing was removed and people rested — the *qaylūlah*, the midday nap, which the Prophetic tradition encouraged. *Wa min baʿdi ṣalāti'l-ʿishāʾ* — after the night prayer. When the household has retired. The verse then names what these three have in common: *thalāthu ʿawrātin lakum* — "three times of *ʿawrah* for you."

**ʿAwrah: The Word and Its Meaning**

The term *ʿawrah* is central to Islamic modesty law and deserves explanation, particularly since this verse uses it in an unusual way. Its root, *ʿ-w-r*, carries the sense of a *defect, gap, or vulnerability* — the same root gives *aʿwar*, one-eyed, and is used of a breach in a wall or an undefended point in a fortification. The Qur'an uses it in exactly that military sense elsewhere:

> *"…saying, 'Truly our houses are exposed (ʿawrah),' though they were not exposed; they desired naught but flight."* (Qur'an 33:13)

So *ʿawrah* is fundamentally *the point at which something is unprotected*. Applied to the body, it names what must be covered; applied here to *times*, it names the hours at which a household is most exposed. The usage in this verse is remarkable: the times themselves are called *ʿawrāt*. Privacy is treated as a property of *moments*, not only of places or of bodies. There are hours in which a home is undressed.

**Why These Three Times**

The logic is simple and observable. Every household has a rhythm of composure and relaxation. During the working day, people are dressed, alert, and prepared to be seen. At the three named times — before dawn, at midday rest, and after the night prayer — that preparation is set down. Clothing is loosened. Spouses are together. Bodies are uncovered for sleep. Nobody is expecting to be observed. The verse identifies these three windows and requires permission at them, precisely because they are when an unannounced entry would encounter something no one intended to display.

**"Beyond These, There Is No Blame"**

*Laysa ʿalaykum wa lā ʿalayhim junāḥun baʿdahunna, ṭawwāfūna ʿalaykum baʿḍukum ʿalā baʿḍ.* Outside the three times, the requirement is lifted, and the reason given is beautiful: *ṭawwāfūna ʿalaykum* — "they go around among you, some of you with others." The word *ṭawwāf* is from *ṭ-w-f*, the root of *ṭawāf*, the circumambulation of the Kaʿbah. It means to move around and about continuously. The image is of household members *circulating* — passing through rooms, coming and going, moving around one another all day long. The verse is saying: this is a household, not a set of sealed chambers. People live here together. The children come and go; the servants pass through; life circulates. To require formal permission at every threshold at every hour would make ordinary domestic life impossible. There is a subtle dignity in the phrasing *baʿḍukum ʿalā baʿḍ* — "some of you upon others" — which is *mutual*. The circulation goes in both directions. The parents also enter the children's spaces; the household is a shared organism.

**The Balance the Verse Strikes**

What emerges is a model of domestic privacy that is neither of the two extremes societies typically adopt. *It is not the total transparency* in which family members have no private moments at all, doors are never closed, and children grow up with no experience of a boundary. That arrangement, common in crowded circumstances, produces adults with no sense of the sanctity of another person's space. *Nor is it the total separation* in which every family member locks a door and the household becomes a set of strangers sharing an address — the arrangement toward which affluent modern housing tends, with predictable effects on family cohesion. The Qur'an legislates *bounded* openness: free circulation for most of the day, protected privacy at three specific times. It is a design of considerable practical wisdom, and any parent who has tried to manage a household will recognize the problem it solves.

**Why Children Are Trained in This**

The inclusion of children is the verse's most interesting feature, and it is worth asking why God legislates the knocking habits of pre-pubescent children in a chapter that also promises the succession of nations. Several reasons emerge. *It protects the child.* The primary function is to prevent children from encountering what they are not ready to encounter — specifically, the marital intimacy of their parents. The three named times are precisely the times when such an encounter is most likely. Modern developmental psychology has said a great deal about the effects of such exposure on children; the Qur'an legislated against it directly. *It forms the child.* Habits acquired before puberty become second nature. A child who has knocked before entering since the age of five will not need to be taught the rule at fifteen — which is exactly what the next verse addresses, when it requires full permission-seeking once maturity arrives. *It teaches the concept of another person's space.* This is perhaps the deepest function. The knock is a small physical ritual that carries an idea: *there is a boundary here, and it belongs to someone else, and I acknowledge it.* A child who internalizes that idea in the home carries it into every relationship he will ever have. *And it protects the parents' marriage.* The verse quietly guarantees a couple three protected windows a day. In a household with several children, that is not a small provision.

**"Thus Does God Make Clear Unto You the Signs"**

*Kadhālika yubayyinu'llāhu lakumu'l-āyāt.* The formula returns — the fourth appearance of the root *b-y-n* in a closing position, after verses 18, 34, and 46, and it will appear twice more at verses 59 and 61. Its presence here makes a point about the *dignity of detail*. These small domestic instructions are called *āyāt*, signs — the same word used for the birds in flight, the driven clouds, and the alternation of night and day. The Qur'an does not classify its teachings into the profound and the trivial. The verse about knocking at midday is an *āyah*, as the Light Verse is an *āyah*.

**"And God Is Knowing, Wise"**

*Wa'llāhu ʿalīmun ḥakīm.* The same pair that closed verse 18. As discussed there, the two names together constitute the credentials of a legislator: complete knowledge of the facts, and perfect proportion in the ruling. Their relevance here is specific. *ʿAlīm* — He knows what happens in houses at those three hours, which no legislator working from outside could know. *Ḥakīm* — He has fixed the rule at exactly three times, neither burdening the household with constant formality nor leaving it exposed. The choice of *three* is itself the wisdom the name asserts. A lesser lawgiver would have gone to one extreme or the other. The Qur'an identifies precisely the windows that require protection and leaves the rest of the day free.

**Application**

The transfer to contemporary households is direct and requires almost no adjustment, though the specific hours will vary with the culture. The principle is: *identify the times when the household is unguarded, and protect them by requiring a knock.* In most modern homes these will include early morning, bedtime, and whatever period functions as private rest. The two implementations that follow are equally direct. *Teach children to knock*, from an age well before it seems necessary, because the habit must be formed before it is needed. And *knock on children's doors yourself* — the verse's phrase *baʿḍukum ʿalā baʿḍ*, "some of you upon others," makes the circulation mutual, and a parent who demands a knock and does not give one has taught the child that privacy is a function of power rather than of respect.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:59

> **And when the children among you come of age, let them ask leave as those before them asked leave. Thus does God make clear unto you His signs, and God is Knowing, Wise.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Graduation**

The previous verse gave children a partial rule: permission at three times, free circulation otherwise. This verse states what happens when they grow up. *Wa idhā balagha'l-aṭfālu minkumu'l-ḥuluma fa'l-yastaʾdhinū ka-ma'staʾdhana'lladhīna min qablihim.* At maturity, the concession ends. The young adult now falls under the *general* rule of verse 27: permission at all times, not merely at three.

**Balagha'l-Ḥulum: Reaching the Age of Dreams**

The phrase for coming of age is *balagha'l-ḥulum* — literally "reached the *ḥulum*." The word *ḥulum* means a dream, and the idiom refers to the nocturnal emission that marks the onset of sexual maturity in males, with corresponding signs recognized for females. The delicacy of the expression is characteristic of the Qur'an's treatment of such matters. It names a biological threshold without crudeness, using a word that a child would not immediately decode and an adult would understand at once. In Islamic law, *bulūgh* — the attainment of this threshold — is the moment at which a person becomes fully accountable: obligated to pray, to fast, to observe the rulings of modesty, and answerable for his deeds. It is one of the two conditions of legal responsibility, the other being soundness of mind. The Prophet ﷺ described the lifting of the pen:

> *"The pen is lifted from three: from the sleeper until he awakes, from the child until he reaches maturity, and from the insane until he regains his reason."* (Abū Dāwūd 4403; al-Tirmidhī 1423)

**Why the Rule Changes at This Point**

The rationale is stated implicitly by the change itself, and it connects directly to the Prophetic explanation given at verse 27: *"Permission is sought only because of the gaze."* Before maturity, the child's gaze carries no charge; verse 31 described children as those *"innocent of the private areas of women."* At maturity, that innocence ends — not through any fault, but as a matter of biology. The same young person who could pass freely through the house yesterday now has a different relationship to what he might see. The law responds to the change in the person rather than to any change in his behaviour. This is characteristic of Islamic modesty legislation: it tracks *capacity* rather than *conduct*, and it does not wait for a problem to arise before addressing it.

**"As Those Before Them Asked Leave"**

*Ka-ma'staʾdhana'lladhīna min qablihim.* Two readings are offered by the commentators for who "those before them" are. *The adults of the household* — the older siblings, the parents, the mature members who have been observing the full rule all along. On this reading, the young adult is simply joining the existing practice of the grown-ups around him. *The generations before* — those of earlier communities and earlier times who observed the same etiquette. The first reading is the more natural, and it carries an important implication for how the rule is transmitted: *by example*. The verse does not say "let them learn the rule"; it says let them ask permission *as those before them asked*. The young person adopts a practice he has watched. This tells us something about how such norms actually take hold. A household in which the adults knock will produce young adults who knock, without instruction. A household in which the adults do not will produce nothing, whatever is taught verbally. The transmission mechanism named by the verse is imitation, not exhortation.

**The Wisdom of a Staged Rule**

Reading verses 58 and 59 together reveals a graduated system, and its design is worth appreciating. *Stage one*: young children, free circulation with three protected windows. The rule is light, learnable, and tied to specific times a child can understand. *Stage two*: at maturity, the full rule. The transition is therefore not abrupt in practice, even though it is abrupt in law. By the time a young person reaches *bulūgh*, he has already been knocking at three times a day for years. The habit exists; only its frequency changes. This is a general principle of Islamic pedagogy, and it appears in other domains. Children are instructed in prayer well before it becomes obligatory upon them:

> *"Command your children to pray when they are seven, and strike them for it when they are ten, and separate them in their beds."* (Abū Dāwūd 495)

Note the final clause of that hadith, which belongs to the same subject as this verse: the separation of children's sleeping arrangements at ten — before maturity, in preparation for it. The principle in both cases is that a duty which arrives suddenly at maturity, with no prior formation, will not be adopted. Obligations are preceded by training.

**A Note on the Difficulty of the Transition**

There is something worth saying about the human reality of this rule, because it addresses one of the more delicate moments in family life. The arrival of maturity changes a relationship that has existed unchanged since birth. A child who has been physically close to his parents and siblings, who has moved freely through the house, must now begin to observe a formality that did not previously exist. And the parents must accept it in return — the son they dressed and carried is now a man whose room they knock on. This is not merely a legal adjustment; it is emotionally significant on both sides, and families often handle it badly, either by refusing to acknowledge the change or by imposing it so abruptly that it feels like a rejection. The verse's method softens both errors. By having the practice already partially established, the transition becomes a *deepening* of something familiar rather than the sudden imposition of distance. And by framing it as joining the practice of "those before them," it presents the change as an *elevation* — the young person is being admitted to the adult standard, not excluded from the family.

**"Thus Does God Make Clear Unto You His Signs"**

*Kadhālika yubayyinu'llāhu lakum āyātih.* The formula from verse 58 repeats with one small difference: verse 58 said *al-āyāt*, "the signs," while this verse says *āyātih*, "*His* signs." The variation adds the possessive, attaching the rulings explicitly to their Author. And once again, the repetition of this closing formula within two consecutive verses about domestic knocking makes the sūrah's point about the dignity of detail. Twice in two verses, instructions about when a teenager should knock on a door are called *the signs of God*.

**"And God Is Knowing, Wise"**

*Wa'llāhu ʿalīmun ḥakīm.* The same closing pair as verse 58, and its relevance here is precise. *ʿAlīm* — the Knowing. He knows when a child has ceased to be a child. Human beings frequently do not: parents notoriously fail to register the maturity of their own children, continuing to treat an adult as a boy for years past the point. The verse's threshold is objective and known to the One who set it. *Ḥakīm* — the Wise. He knows how to stage a rule so that it can actually be adopted, and He has placed the change at exactly the point where the reason for the rule comes into existence.

**Practical Reflection**

Three applications for a household. *Mark the transition consciously.* The verse legislates a change at maturity, and a change that is never acknowledged does not occur. Families that handle this well tend to mark it explicitly — a conversation, a recognition, a granting of adult standing along with adult obligations. *Give what you require.* The rule runs in both directions, as verse 58's *"some of you upon others"* established. A young adult expected to knock is a young adult entitled to be knocked upon. *And understand what the rule is protecting.* It is not distance for its own sake. It is the preservation of a household in which every member — parent, child, and sibling — has a space in which they are not observed, and in which the intimacy proper to a marriage remains proper to a marriage. That is what the knock is for, and it is worth explaining to a young person rather than merely imposing.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:60

> **As for elderly women who no longer anticipate marriage, there is no blame upon them to doff their garments without displaying any ornament. And it is better for them to be modest, and God is Hearing, Knowing.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Concession, and Then a Recommendation**

This verse grants a relaxation of the dress requirements of verse 31 to a specific group, and then — remarkably — immediately advises against taking it. The structure is worth noting before the details: *there is no blame… and it is better for them not to.* The Qur'an permits something and in the same breath commends its avoidance. This combination appears elsewhere in the Book and reflects a distinction Islamic law takes seriously: between what is *permitted* and what is *best*.

**Al-Qawāʿid: The Women Who Have Sat Down**

The women described are *al-qawāʿid mina'l-nisāʾ*. The word *qawāʿid* is the plural of *qāʿid*, from the root *q-ʿ-d*, to sit — the opposite of *qāma*, to stand. The commentators explain the idiom: these are women who have "sat down" from menstruation and childbearing, and therefore from the ordinary expectations of marriage. The metaphor of sitting captures the withdrawal from an active stage of life with more grace than any clinical term. The same root produces *qawāʿid* in the sense of foundations — the bases on which a building *sits*, as in Abraham raising *"the foundations of the House"* (2:127). The word thus carries, alongside the sense of withdrawal, a quiet dignity: these women are among the foundations of the community.

**"Who No Longer Anticipate Marriage"**

*Allātī lā yarjūna nikāḥan.* The qualifying clause is essential and does the real defining work. The verb *rajā* means to hope for, to expect, to look forward to. Two elements are combined: advanced age *and* the absence of any expectation of marriage. The commentators note that both must be present. Age alone does not qualify a woman; there are older women who are still in the field of marriage, and the concession does not apply to them. The criterion is therefore functional rather than merely chronological, and it follows directly from the purpose of the modesty legislation. As explained at verse 31, the rules of dress regulate a specific social space — the domain of potential attraction between marriageable adults. A woman who is entirely outside that domain, by age and by universal expectation, falls outside the rule's purpose, and the rule accordingly relaxes. This is a fine example of how Islamic law operates when its underlying reasons are properly understood. The ruling tracks its rationale. Where the rationale is absent, the ruling adjusts.

**"To Doff Their Garments"**

*An yaḍaʿna thiyābahunna.* The permission is to *lay aside* garments — and the commentators are careful to specify which. The reference is to the *outer* garments: the overcloak (*jilbāb*) and the head-covering worn over ordinary clothing when in the presence of unrelated men. It is not a permission to uncover generally. Al-Qurṭubī and others note that what is permitted is essentially the removal of the outermost layer, so that such a woman may appear before others as she would within her own household — with her hair, neck, arms, and the like uncovered — without incurring blame.

**"Without Displaying Any Ornament"**

*Ghayra mutabarrijātin bi-zīnah.* The concession comes with a condition, and it is precisely worded. *Tabarruj* is from the root *b-r-j*, which gives *burj*, a tower — something raised up and conspicuous, visible from a distance. The verbal form means to *display oneself conspicuously*, to make a show of one's adornment. The Qur'an uses the term elsewhere in a pointed comparison:

> *"And abide in your homes, and flaunt not your charms as they flaunted them (tabarruj) in the earlier Age of Ignorance."* (Qur'an 33:33)

So the condition attached to the concession is: *you may lay aside the outer garment, provided you are not doing so in order to display.* This is the same principle that governed the anklet at the end of verse 31. There, a woman fully covered defeated the purpose of covering by stamping her feet. Here, a woman lawfully uncovered would defeat the purpose of the concession by making a display of it. In both cases the Sharīʿah is shown to legislate *purposes*, not merely forms, and formal compliance combined with substantive violation is recognized and prohibited.

**"And It Is Better for Them to Be Modest"**

*Wa an yastaʿfifna khayrun lahunn.* Having granted the relaxation, the verse immediately commends the opposite. The verb is *yastaʿfifna*, from *ʿ-f-f* — the same root as *ʿiffah*, chastity and self-restraint, and the same verb form used at verse 33 for those who cannot marry: *"let them strive to be chaste."* Its use here means: let them maintain the higher standard voluntarily, though they are not required to. Why would the Qur'an grant a permission and then advise against its use? Several reasons emerge, and they illuminate a general feature of Islamic ethics. *Because permission and excellence are different categories.* Islamic law distinguishes the obligatory, the recommended, the permitted, the disliked, and the forbidden. A great deal of the moral life takes place in the space between the permitted and the excellent, and a believer who lives at the edge of what is merely allowed has forfeited most of the territory. *Because habits are not easily resumed.* A woman who has covered for fifty years and then relaxes has begun to undo a practice built over a lifetime. The verse's advice preserves the continuity of a discipline. *Because of the example set.* Elderly women in any community are watched by the young. A grandmother who maintains her covering when she is not required to has taught something no lecture could convey. *And because modesty is not merely a rule but a quality of character.* This is the deepest reason. *Ḥayāʾ* — modesty, dignified reserve — is described in the Sunnah as intrinsic to faith rather than as a legal imposition:

> *"Faith has seventy-odd branches, the highest of which is the statement 'there is no god but God,' and the lowest of which is removing something harmful from the road. And modesty is a branch of faith."* (al-Bukhārī 9; Muslim 35)

> *"Modesty brings nothing but good."* (al-Bukhārī 6117; Muslim 37)

> *"Every religion has a character, and the character of Islam is modesty."* (Ibn Mājah 4181)

If modesty is a *character* rather than a rule, then it is not something one is released from when a rule ceases to apply. It is something one continues to be.

**The Dignity Extended to Older Women**

There is something in this verse that deserves recognition on its own account. The Qur'an legislates specifically for elderly women.
