It notices their situation, addresses their particular circumstances, eases a requirement in recognition of the burden it might place on them, and does so in language of considerable delicacy — describing them as those who have "sat down" rather than by any diminishing term. This attentiveness is not incidental. Aging women are, in a great many societies, the most socially invisible category of person: no longer objects of attention, often no longer economically productive, frequently unconsidered in law and custom alike. The Qur'an sees them. And in the same passage in which it promises the succession of nations, it pauses to address the comfort of an old woman in her clothing. The Prophet ﷺ's own conduct reinforced this. He maintained relationships with elderly women of Madinah, visited them, and the tradition records his asking after a woman who used to sweep the mosque and, upon learning she had died and been buried without his knowledge, going to her grave and praying over it (al-Bukhārī 458; Muslim 956). The community's least conspicuous member was not overlooked.

**"And God Is Hearing, Knowing"**

*Wa'llāhu samīʿun ʿalīm.* The closing pair is precisely chosen for a verse about a voluntary standard. *Samīʿ* — the Hearing. He hears what is said about such matters, and He hears the intention voiced in a person's own heart. *ʿAlīm* — the Knowing. And this is the operative name here. The verse has just distinguished between a woman who lays aside her garment out of legitimate ease and one who does so *mutabarrijah*, in display. No observer can reliably tell the two apart. The distinction lies entirely in intention, and only the *ʿAlīm* has access to it. The names therefore close the verse the way the sūrah has closed nearly every ruling that cannot be externally enforced: by naming the One before whom the intention is transparent. Verse 29 did the same after granting the exemption for uninhabited houses — *"God knows what you disclose and what you conceal."* A permission is precisely the kind of ruling that invites self-deception, and the Qur'an consistently guards its permissions with a divine name.

## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:61

> **There is no fault against the blind, nor fault against the lame, nor fault against the sick, nor upon yourselves in that you eat from your houses, or your fathers' houses, or your mothers' houses, or your brothers' houses, or your sisters' houses, or your paternal uncles' houses, or your paternal aunts' houses, or your maternal uncles' houses, or your maternal aunts' houses, or those whose keys you possess, or [those of] your friends. There is no blame upon you whether you eat together or separately. So when you enter houses, greet each other with a salutation from God, blessed and good. Thus does God make clear unto you the signs, that haply you may understand.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Longest Verse of Hospitality in the Qur'an**

This remarkable verse dissolves a whole cluster of social anxieties at once. It addresses people with disabilities, the etiquette of eating in relatives' homes, the awkwardness of eating alone or in company, and the greeting upon entry — and it does all of it in the register of *relief*. The formula *laysa ʿalā… ḥaraj* — "there is no fault/blame/constraint upon…" — governs the entire first half. The word *ḥaraj* is worth pausing on: from a root meaning narrowness, constriction, difficulty. A *ḥaraj* is a tight place, a bind. The Qur'an uses the term for the general principle of the religion:

> *"…and He has not placed upon you any hardship (ḥaraj) in religion."* (Qur'an 22:78)

So the verse's repeated refrain is: *this is not a tight place for you.* Relax.

**The First Concession: The Blind, the Lame, and the Sick**

*Laysa ʿala'l-aʿmā ḥarajun wa lā ʿala'l-aʿraji ḥarajun wa lā ʿala'l-marīḍi ḥaraj.* Three categories are named, and the commentators record several occasions of revelation, which together illuminate what was troubling people. One set of reports concerns *the military expeditions*: those with disabilities felt shame at remaining behind while others went out, and the verse relieved them of blame. The same three categories appear together for exactly this reason elsewhere:

> *"There is no blame upon the blind, nor blame upon the lame, nor blame upon the sick…"* (Qur'an 48:17)

Another set concerns *eating together*. Some reports say that people were reluctant to eat with a blind person, who could not see what he was taking; with a lame person, who took up more room; or with a sick person, whose condition or appetite differed. Others say the reverse — that those with disabilities themselves felt embarrassed to eat in company, fearing that they took more than their share or ate in a manner that would be found unpleasant. Both readings converge on the same social reality: disability was producing exclusion from the shared table, whether by the avoidance of others or by the withdrawal of the person himself. The verse removes it. And the way it removes it is instructive: not by commanding people to be kind to the disabled, but by *declaring that there is no fault in the situation at all*. It does not manage the discomfort; it denies its basis.

**Why This Matters**

The shared meal is, in nearly every human society, the primary act of social inclusion. To eat with someone is to be in community with them; to be excluded from the table is to be excluded from the group. Placing this ruling at the head of a verse about eating in one another's homes is therefore a statement about who belongs. The community's meals are not to be organized around the comfort of the unimpaired. The Qur'an legislated the inclusion of people with disabilities at the table fourteen centuries before the modern vocabulary of accessibility existed. And the Prophetic practice reinforced it. He ﷺ appointed the blind Companion Ibn Umm Maktūm to lead the people of Madinah in his absence, and the Qur'an's own gentle rebuke to the Prophet ﷺ over the blind man who came seeking knowledge — *"He frowned and turned away, because the blind man came to him"* (80:1–2) — is one of the most striking passages in the Book, precisely because it corrects the Messenger of God himself in the matter of how a disabled person was treated.

**The Second Concession: Eating in Relatives' Houses**

The verse then lists an extraordinary catalogue of homes in which one may eat without permission or embarrassment: one's own, one's father's, mother's, brothers', sisters', paternal uncles', paternal aunts', maternal uncles', maternal aunts', those whose keys one holds, and those of one's friends. Eleven categories, enumerated individually. The Qur'an rarely lists at such length; when it does, the enumeration is doing work. Here it is closing every possible gap in a person's mental map of "where I may eat comfortably." *The occasion*, as the commentators record, was the scrupulosity created by earlier revelation. Two Qur'anic teachings had made people anxious. The first was the prohibition on consuming others' property unjustly:

> *"O you who believe! Consume not each other's wealth falsely, but rather trade by mutual consent."* (Qur'an 4:29)

The second was the etiquette of verse 27 of this very sūrah: do not enter houses without permission. The result was that pious Muslims began refusing to eat in the homes of their closest relatives, fearing they were consuming property without proper authorization. Some reports describe people leaving their houses in the care of relatives while travelling and the relatives refusing to eat from the food there. Others describe families declining to eat at their own children's or siblings' homes. The verse dissolves the anxiety by declaring the ordinary hospitality of kinship *presumptively permitted*. Among close family, one does not need explicit permission for a meal; the permission is understood.

**"Those Whose Keys You Possess"**

*Aw mā malaktum mafātiḥah* — "or that of which you possess the keys." The commentators explain this as covering those entrusted with the management of another's property: a caretaker, an agent, a servant left in charge of a house. Such a person may eat from what he is managing, within reason, since the trust implies it. The Prophetic practice extended similar permission to workers in general:

> *"When the servant of one of you brings his food, if he does not seat him with him, let him at least give him a morsel or two."* (al-Bukhārī 2557)

**"Or Your Friends"**

*Aw ṣadīqikum.* This is the striking one. The list has proceeded through the degrees of kinship in careful order — parents, siblings, uncles and aunts on both sides — and then adds: *or your friends*. The word *ṣadīq* is from *ṣ-d-q*, the root of *ṣidq*, truthfulness. A friend, in Arabic, is etymologically *one who is true* — and the classical lexicographers make the connection explicit: the *ṣadīq* is one whose affection is genuine. The commentators noted with some wonder that friendship is here placed alongside blood relationship. Some early authorities observed that a friend may in some respects be closer than kin, since kinship is given and friendship is chosen. The inclusion says something about the Qur'anic vision of community. The circle of trust is not limited to lineage. A friend's house is, for this purpose, one's own.

**"Whether You Eat Together or Separately"**

*Laysa ʿalaykum junāḥun an taʾkulū jamīʿan aw ashtātan.* Another anxiety dissolved. Some reports indicate that certain Arab tribes considered it shameful to eat alone and would wait, sometimes at cost to themselves, until a companion arrived. Others held the reverse. The verse settles it: both are permitted, and neither carries blame. There is a general principle here worth naming. Cultural conventions about eating — who may eat with whom, in what order, in what company — are among the most emotionally loaded and least examined norms in any society. The Qur'an addresses several of them in a single clause and declares them matters of indifference. It is a small liberation, and a real one. A great deal of human misery is manufactured by rules that nobody imposed and everybody obeys. Though it should be noted that the Sunnah *recommends* eating together, without making it obligatory:

> *"Eat together and do not eat separately, for the blessing is in the company."* (Ibn Mājah 3287)

The relationship between the two texts is the same as at verse 60: the Qur'an removes the *blame*, and the Sunnah indicates the *better*.

**"So When You Enter Houses, Greet Each Other"**

*Fa-idhā dakhaltum buyūtan fa-sallimū ʿalā anfusikum taḥiyyatan min ʿindi'llāhi mubārakatan ṭayyibah.* The verse now returns to where the sūrah's etiquette section began, at verse 27: the greeting upon entry. And note the phrasing: *sallimū ʿalā anfusikum* — "greet *yourselves*." This is the same construction used at verse 12, where the believers were told to think well *"of their own selves"* about a fellow believer. The identification is the same: the community is one body, and to greet another member is to greet oneself. The commentators also note a second application: that a person entering an *empty* house should greet himself, saying peace upon us and upon the righteous servants of God — a practice reported from several of the early authorities.

**"A Salutation From God, Blessed and Good"**

Three descriptions are given of the greeting, and each adds something. *Min ʿindi'llāh* — "from God." The greeting was not invented by the community; it was legislated. The Prophet ﷺ traced it to the very first human being:

> *"God created Adam in his form, sixty cubits tall. When He created him, He said: 'Go and greet that group of angels who are sitting, and listen to how they greet you, for it will be your greeting and the greeting of your descendants.' So he said: 'Peace be upon you.' They said: 'Peace be upon you and the mercy of God' — and they added 'and the mercy of God.'"* (al-Bukhārī 6227; Muslim 2841)

*Mubārakah* — "blessed." From *barakah*, the increase and endurance that God places in a thing. A greeting of peace is a supplication, and it carries the blessing of every answered prayer. *Ṭayyibah* — "good, wholesome, pleasant." The word met at verse 26, from the vocabulary of clean water and sound food. The greeting is wholesome: it does no harm, costs nothing, and improves whatever it touches. The Prophet ﷺ described its social function in the report quoted at verse 27:

> *"You shall not enter Paradise until you believe, and you shall not believe until you love one another. Shall I not tell you of something which, if you do it, you will love one another? Spread the greeting of peace among yourselves."* (Muslim 54)

And he specified its reward:

> A man came and said, "Peace be upon you." The Prophet ﷺ returned the greeting and said, *"Ten."* Another came and said, "Peace be upon you and the mercy of God." He said, *"Twenty."* Another came and said, "Peace be upon you and the mercy of God and His blessings." He said, *"Thirty."* (Abū Dāwūd 5195)

**"That Haply You May Understand"**

*Laʿallakum taʿqilūn.* The closing formula appears for the last time in the sūrah, and the final word has changed. Verse 1 ended *"that you may remember"* (*tadhakkarūn*); verse 27 the same; and now the sūrah's last legislative verse ends *"that you may understand"* (*taʿqilūn*). The verb is from *ʿaql*, intellect — a word whose root sense, in Arabic, is the *hobble* used to tie a camel's leg to prevent it wandering. The intellect is named for its function of *restraint*: it is what binds a person to what is right and prevents him drifting. The shift from *remember* to *understand* across the sūrah is fitting. The chapter opened by asking for recollection of what the reader already knew; it closes by asking for comprehension of what it has taught.

**The Verse's Underlying Vision**

Stand back from the details, and a picture emerges of the society the sūrah has been constructing. Its houses have doors that must be knocked upon (v. 27), and inside those doors, three protected hours (v. 58). Its members lower their gazes (vv. 30–31) and guard their tongues (vv. 11–26). And *this* verse shows what such a society looks like from the inside: an extended network of homes in which a person may walk in and eat without asking, in which the blind and the lame and the sick are at the table, in which friends are counted with kin, and in which every entry begins with a prayer for the other person's peace. The privacy legislated earlier was never for the sake of isolation. It was the boundary that makes genuine intimacy possible — and here, at the end, is the intimacy it was protecting.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:62

> **Only they are believers who believe in God and His Messenger and who, when they are with him in a collective affair, go not forth until asking his leave. Truly those who ask thy leave, it is they who believe in God and His Messenger. So when they ask thy leave in some matter of theirs, give leave unto whomsoever thou wilt, and seek forgiveness for them from God. Truly God is Forgiving, Merciful.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Etiquette of Leaving**

The sūrah has legislated the etiquette of *entering* — houses, rooms, the protected hours. It now legislates the etiquette of *leaving*, and it does so in a specific setting: the assembly of the Prophet ﷺ when the community is gathered for a matter of common concern. *Innama'l-muʾminūna'lladhīna āmanū bi'llāhi wa rasūlihi wa idhā kānū maʿahu ʿalā amrin jāmiʿin lam yadhhabū ḥattā yastaʾdhinūh.*

**"Only They Are Believers"**

The verse opens with *innamā*, the particle of restriction — the same construction used at verse 51 for the believers' response to judgment. It means "only," and it excludes everything outside the description. This is a strong opening, and it should be read in connection with the passage from verses 47 to 54. There the hypocrites were identified by their behaviour when *called* to a matter. Here the believers are identified by their behaviour when *departing* from one. Both are tests of whether the profession of faith governs conduct in a small, observable, easily-evaded moment.

**Amr Jāmiʿ: A Collective Affair**

*Amrin jāmiʿin* — literally "a gathering matter," an affair that assembles people. The commentators explain this as any matter of communal importance requiring the community's presence: consultation on a course of action, preparation for an expedition, the Friday sermon, a public address, the digging of the trench before the battle of the Confederates. The reports frequently connect the verse to the trench, where men working alongside the Prophet ﷺ would slip away without notice. What defines an *amr jāmiʿ* is that it *requires the assembly itself* to function. It is not a lecture one may attend or not; it is a collective undertaking in which each person's presence is part of the substance.

**The Rule: Do Not Leave Without Asking**

*Lam yadhhabū ḥattā yastaʾdhinūh.* The instruction is simple: when the community is gathered for a serious common purpose, no one departs without seeking permission. The reason is practical before it is spiritual. A gathering from which people quietly vanish disintegrates. Each departure signals to those remaining that the matter is not important, that attendance is optional, and that others have found somewhere better to be. The unannounced exit is contagious in a way the announced one is not. There is also a matter of information. A leader conducting an assembly needs to know who is present. Someone slipping away may be leaving a task undone, may be needed for a decision, or may be avoiding an assignment. And there is the matter of respect. To leave a person's presence while they are speaking, without acknowledgement, is a statement — and it is heard as one, whatever was intended.

**The Standard Applied to the Prophet's ﷺ Assembly**

It should be noted that the verse concerns, in the first instance, the assembly of the Messenger of God ﷺ, which gives the rule a particular weight. The commentators connect it to the following verse's warning about those who *"steal away under shelter."* But the wording generalizes naturally. The Qur'an is establishing a principle about the *conduct of members of a community toward its legitimate common undertakings*, and the reason it gives — that this is what belief looks like — is not confined to a single assembly.

**"Truly Those Who Ask Thy Leave, It Is They Who Believe"**

*Inna'lladhīna yastaʾdhinūnaka ulāʾika'lladhīna yuʾminūna bi'llāhi wa rasūlih.* The statement is repeated in reverse form, and the repetition is emphatic: those who ask permission *are* the ones who believe. Why should so small a courtesy be treated as a marker of faith? Because faith, in the Qur'anic understanding, is not primarily a set of propositions held in the mind. It is a *disposition toward authority and community* that manifests in ordinary conduct. A person who genuinely believes that this gathering is convened by God's Messenger for God's purposes does not slip out of it — not because a rule forbids it, but because his understanding of the situation makes slipping out unthinkable. The small act is diagnostic precisely because it is small. Nobody performs it for show; there is no credit in asking permission to leave. It is therefore a reliable signal of what a person actually thinks about what he is leaving. The Prophet ﷺ made the same connection between faith and ordinary courtesy in the hadith quoted at verse 17:

> *"Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him speak good or remain silent. Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him honour his neighbour. Whoever believes in God and the Last Day, let him honour his guest."* (al-Bukhārī 6018; Muslim 47)

Neighbourliness and hospitality are treated as expressions of belief in the Last Day. The logic of this verse is identical.

**"Give Leave Unto Whomsoever Thou Wilt"**

*Fa'dhan li-man shiʾta minhum.* The rule is not one-sided. Having required the community to ask, the verse grants the leader *discretion* over the answer. Two things follow. *Permission may be refused.* The requirement to ask would be empty if the answer were automatically yes. The leader may judge that a person's presence is needed. *And permission may be granted.* The verse is not a mechanism for trapping people in assemblies. Genuine needs exist, and the leader is authorized to release those with them. The Arabic *li-man shiʾta* — "to whomever you wish" — vests the judgment in the one presiding, which is the only workable arrangement. He alone knows what the gathering requires.

**"And Seek Forgiveness for Them From God"**

*Wa'staghfir lahumu'llāh.* This is the verse's most unexpected turn, and it deserves careful attention. The Prophet ﷺ is instructed, having granted permission, to *ask God's forgiveness for the person he has just excused.* Why? The person did nothing wrong. He had a legitimate need, he asked properly, and permission was given. The commentators offer an explanation of considerable subtlety. Even a lawful departure from a collective religious undertaking involves a shortfall — not a sin, but a *falling short of the best*. The person who remained gave more than the person who left, even if the leaver was entirely within his rights. The instruction to seek forgiveness acknowledges the gap between the permitted and the excellent. This is the same distinction encountered at verse 60, where a permission was granted and the higher course simultaneously commended. Here it appears in a different form: the permission is granted *and* forgiveness is sought, as if to say — you are excused, and there is still something to be covered. There is also a note of extraordinary tenderness in the arrangement. The leader does not merely release the person; he *prays for him*. The exchange is not administrative but pastoral. Whoever has been in a position of authority and has had occasion to excuse someone will recognize how rare this is, and how transforming.

**"Truly God Is Forgiving, Merciful"**

*Inna'llāha ghafūrun raḥīm.* The closing pair — met at verses 5, 22, and 33 — is precisely fitted to the instruction just given. *Ghafūr*, as noted, is from the root meaning to *cover*. The shortfall of the one who left is covered rather than counted. And *raḥīm* adds the positive: not merely erased, but met with mercy. The names also complete the verse's structure. It began by defining the believers strictly — *innamā*, only those — and ends by naming God's forgiveness. Strictness of standard, gentleness of treatment: the sūrah has held these two together from its opening verses, where a hundred lashes were legislated and the chapter's refrain was the withheld punishment.

**Contemporary Application**

The verse's principle transfers to any serious collective undertaking, and its modern violations are so routine as to be invisible. The meeting from which people drift out without a word. The class left mid-lecture. The congregational gathering exited during the address. The group project abandoned quietly. The conversation left by a person who simply stops replying. In each case the departing person has usually calculated correctly that no one will object. And in each case the cumulative effect is the erosion of the undertaking itself, because a gathering that people leave freely becomes a gathering nobody takes seriously. The verse's remedy costs almost nothing: *ask*. A sentence, or in a modern setting a message, acknowledging the departure and requesting release. It preserves the seriousness of the matter, respects the people remaining, and — as the Qur'an says with startling directness — is what belief looks like. There is a corresponding instruction for those in authority, and it is the harder one. When someone asks to be released, grant it graciously where you can, and pray for them. The verse gives the leader discretion and then immediately gives him a supplication to make on behalf of the person he has excused. Authority exercised in that manner is very difficult to resent.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:63

> **Do not deem the Messenger's calling among you to be like your calling to one another. Indeed, God knows those among you who steal away under shelter. So let those who contradict his command be wary, lest a trial befall them or a painful punishment befall them.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Weight of the Messenger's Voice**

The sūrah's penultimate verse addresses the *status* of the Prophet's ﷺ address, and it does so with a warning of unusual severity. *Lā tajʿalū duʿāʾa'l-rasūli baynakum ka-duʿāʾi baʿḍikum baʿḍan.*

**Two Readings of "The Messenger's Calling"**

The phrase *duʿāʾa'l-rasūl* is grammatically ambiguous in a productive way, and the commentators recognized both possibilities. *The Messenger calling you.* On this reading — the one most consonant with the preceding verse and with the warning that follows — the meaning is: do not treat his summons as you treat one another's. When a friend calls you, you may come or not, answer or not, excuse yourself casually. When the Messenger of God calls, the summons is of a different order. The Qur'an states this elsewhere in the strongest terms:

> *"O you who believe! Respond to God and the Messenger when he calls you unto that which gives you life."* (Qur'an 8:24)

*Your calling to the Messenger.* On the alternative reading, the meaning is: do not address him as you address one another. Do not call out "Muhammad!" across a courtyard as you would call a companion, but address him with the honorifics due to a prophet — "O Messenger of God," "O Prophet of God." This reading is supported by the parallel legislation in Sūrat al-Ḥujurāt:

> *"O you who believe! Raise not your voices above the voice of the Prophet, and be not loud in speech with him as you are loud with one another, lest your deeds come to naught while you are unaware."* (Qur'an 49:2)

> *"Truly those who call out to thee from behind the private chambers, most of them do not understand."* (Qur'an 49:4)

Both readings are sound, and both make the same underlying point: *the relationship with the Messenger is not a peer relationship*, and treating it as one is a category error with consequences.

**Why This Matters**

The reason is not that the Prophet ﷺ required deference for his own sake. The Sunnah is full of his refusal of ceremony: he sat where there was room, mended his own sandals, and was described by visitors as indistinguishable from his Companions when they entered the mosque. When a man trembled in his presence, he said gently: *"Be at ease; I am only the son of a woman of the Quraysh who used to eat dried meat."* The reason is that the summons carries the authority of its source. To treat the Messenger's call as one voice among many is, functionally, to treat revelation as one opinion among many — and the whole passage from verse 47 onward has been about people who did exactly that.

**"Those Who Steal Away Under Shelter"**

*Qad yaʿlamu'llāhu'lladhīna yatasallalūna minkum liwādhan.* This is one of the most vivid phrases in the sūrah, and its vocabulary is exact. *Yatasallalūna* is from the root *s-l-l*, which means to draw something out gently and imperceptibly — the same root as *salla*, to draw a sword slowly from its sheath so as to make no sound. The reflexive form used here means to *slip oneself away*, quietly, without being noticed. *Liwādhan* is from *l-w-dh*, to take shelter behind something, to shield oneself. The commentators picture it precisely: a man positioning himself behind another person, or behind a wall or a mount, using the cover to make his exit invisible. Together the two words draw a complete picture of the behaviour: a person edging backwards through a crowd, keeping someone between himself and the speaker, slipping out unseen. The precision of the description is itself part of the warning. The Qur'an is not condemning departure in general; it has just legislated the lawful way to depart (v. 62). It is describing, with almost cinematic exactness, the *manner* of the person who leaves without asking — and it is describing it in a way that makes clear the behaviour was observed.

**"Indeed, God Knows"**

*Qad yaʿlamu'llāh.* And here is the sting. The entire technique of *liwādh* — the shelter, the concealment, the careful positioning — is designed to escape *one* pair of eyes. It succeeds in that. The verse notes that it was never the relevant pair. This closes a theme that has run through the whole sūrah. Every ruling that cannot be externally enforced has been sealed with a divine name of knowledge: *khabīr* after the lowering of the gaze (v. 30); *"God knows what you disclose and what you conceal"* after the exemption for empty houses (v. 29); *ʿalīm* after the concession to elderly women (v. 60). The chapter's legal architecture rests, at every point where human observation fails, on the awareness of the One who does not fail.

**"Let Those Who Contradict His Command Be Wary"**

*Fa'l-yaḥdhari'lladhīna yukhālifūna ʿan amrihi.* The verb *yaḥdhar* means to be cautious, wary, on guard — the same disposition described by *taqwā* at verse 34, the careful step on a thorny path. The phrase *yukhālifūna ʿan amrih* — "those who go contrary to his command" — uses a construction the grammarians found notable. *Khālafa* ordinarily takes a direct object; the addition of *ʿan* suggests turning *away from* as well as contradicting, so that the sense is: those who deviate from and set themselves against his command. And *amr* means both *command* and *affair*. The commentators note both: to contravene his instruction, and to depart from his way.

**Two Consequences Named**

*An tuṣībahum fitnatun aw yuṣībahum ʿadhābun alīm.* Two possible outcomes are held over those who deviate, and they are of different kinds. *Fitnah.* The word is from a root meaning to *test gold by fire* — the smelting process that separates the pure metal from the dross. In Qur'anic usage *fitnah* covers trial, temptation, discord, civil strife, and affliction. What all these share is the *revealing* function of heat: a *fitnah* exposes what a person or a community is actually made of. The commentators explain the *fitnah* threatened here in several ways: a trial in this world, discord within the community, the hardening of the heart, or the deprivation of guidance. That last is perhaps the most severe: that a person who repeatedly sets himself against the Prophet's ﷺ command may find his heart turned, so that he no longer wishes to comply. The Qur'an warns of this mechanism:

> *"And know that God comes between a man and his own heart."* (Qur'an 8:24)

> *"So when they deviated, God caused their hearts to deviate."* (Qur'an 61:5)

That second verse describes precisely the danger: deviation, persisted in, produces the inability to return. *ʿAdhāb alīm.* A painful punishment — the ordinary Qur'anic term for the consequence in the Hereafter. The pairing covers both worlds, as the sūrah has done throughout (vv. 14, 19, 23).

**The Severity and Its Proportion**

A reader may find the severity striking. The immediate context is people leaving an assembly without permission. The consequence named is trial in this world and painful punishment in the next. Two observations resolve the apparent disproportion. *First, the verse is not addressing the act but the attitude.* Slipping away is the symptom; the disease is the treatment of the Messenger's summons as optional. The Qur'an is consistent that small acts are weighed by what they reveal — as at verse 15, where a repeated rumor was declared *great in the Eyes of God* while its speakers thought it slight. *Second, the warning is conditional and preventive.* The verb is *yaḥdhar* — *let them be wary*. It is an instruction to take care, issued before anything has happened, precisely so that it does not. The Qur'an's threats are overwhelmingly of this character: they are given in order to be unnecessary.

**The General Principle**

There is a principle here that extends well beyond the historical setting, and it has been drawn out by scholars across the centuries: *deviation from the Prophetic way carries consequences even where no obvious harm is visible*. This is a claim about causation that the modern mind finds difficult, because it asserts a connection between religious conduct and worldly outcomes that cannot be traced step by step. But it is the consistent teaching of the Book, stated most directly in:

> *"Truly God alters not what is in a people until they alter what is in themselves."* (Qur'an 13:11)

And it corresponds to something observable at the scale of communities, even if the mechanism is not visible in the individual case. Communities that abandon their standards do not usually collapse dramatically; they experience a slow *fitnah* — the loss of cohesion, the multiplication of internal disputes, the erosion of trust — that is difficult to attribute to any single cause and is nonetheless real.

**Application**

The verse asks two things of a contemporary reader. *Treat the Prophetic instruction as categorically different from human opinion.* Not because scholarship is forbidden — the tradition is built on it — but because the moment a person begins weighing an authenticated Prophetic command against his own preference as though the two were commensurable, he has made the error this verse names. *And notice the instinct to slip away.* The behaviour described — using cover, avoiding notice, leaving without acknowledgement — is not confined to seventh-century assemblies. It is the standard human method for evading an obligation without paying the social cost of refusing it: the unanswered message, the quiet withdrawal from a commitment, the avoidance of the person who will ask. The verse's response is not a rule but a fact: *God knows those among you who slip away.* The technique works on everyone except the One it needed to work on.

---
## Sūrah al-Nūr 24:64

> **Behold! Unto God belongs whatsoever is in the heavens and on the earth. God certainly knows [the state] you are in. On the day they will be returned unto Him, He will inform them of that which they did. And God is Knower of all things.**

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Closes as It Must**

The final verse of Sūrat al-Nūr gathers the chapter's themes into four statements: divine ownership, divine knowledge of the present, the return, and the disclosure of what was done. Nothing new is introduced. Everything the sūrah has taught is placed, at the end, on the foundation that supports it.

**"Behold!"**

*A lā.* The verse opens with the Arabic particle of alerting — a call to attention, roughly "lo!" or "take note!" It is used in the Qur'an to introduce statements of particular consequence:

> *"Behold, in the remembrance of God do hearts find rest."* (Qur'an 13:28)

> *"Behold, truly the friends of God — no fear shall come upon them, nor shall they grieve."* (Qur'an 10:62)

The particle signals that what follows is the point.

**"Unto God Belongs Whatsoever Is in the Heavens and on the Earth"**

*Inna li'llāhi mā fi'l-samāwāti wa'l-arḍ.* This repeats, with a small variation, the statement of verse 42: *"And unto God belongs sovereignty over the heavens and the earth, and unto God is the journey's end."* The repetition at the sūrah's close is deliberate. The doctrine of divine ownership, introduced in the middle of the chapter among the natural signs, is now placed as the final word — because it is the premise upon which every ruling in the chapter rests. The argument, stated once more: the sūrah has legislated the use of bodies, eyes, tongues, homes, wealth, and marriages. Every such legislation faces the objection *this is mine*. The answer, delivered at the beginning of the last verse of the chapter, is that nothing is. And there is a comfort in it as well as an authority. A person whose reputation was destroyed, whose case was never heard, whose right was never restored — that person's grievance is held by the Owner of the heavens and the earth. Nothing has fallen out of the system.

**"God Certainly Knows the State You Are In"**

*Qad yaʿlamu mā antum ʿalayh.* The Arabic is compact: "He knows *what you are upon*." The idiom denotes a person's condition, state, situation, or the course he is on. The phrase reaches in several directions at once, and each of them completes a thread from earlier in the sūrah. *He knows your circumstances.* The pressures, the constraints, the difficulty of what has been asked. The person struggling to lower his gaze in a world engineered against it; the young man who cannot afford to marry; the woman whose reputation was attacked; the household without security. The One legislating knows the conditions in which the legislation must be kept. *He knows your intentions.* Whether the outward compliance corresponds to something inward — the question left open at verse 29 ("what you disclose and what you conceal"), verse 30 (*khabīr*), and verse 60. *And He knows your state of belief.* The sūrah has spent eight verses distinguishing those who say "we hear and we obey" from those who slip away under cover. The distinction is not always visible to the community. It is never invisible to Him. The particle *qad* before the verb adds emphasis and certainty: *He most certainly knows.*

**"On the Day They Will Be Returned Unto Him"**

*Wa yawma yurjaʿūna ilayh.* Note the shift in person. The verse has been addressing the community directly — *"what **you** are upon"* — and now switches to the third person: *"the day **they** will be returned."* The grammarians call this device *iltifāt*, the turning of address, and the Qur'an uses it deliberately for effect. Here the shift creates distance at exactly the moment the Day is mentioned: the reader, who has been personally addressed throughout, suddenly hears himself described from outside, as one of a crowd being returned. The verb *yurjaʿūna* is passive: they *will be returned*. Nobody arrives at that Day by choice or by travel. They are brought.

**"He Will Inform Them of That Which They Did"**

*Fa-yunabbiʾuhum bimā ʿamilū.* The verb *nabbaʾa* is from the root *n-b-ʾ*, which gives *nabaʾ*, a piece of news of great consequence, and *nabī*, a prophet — one who brings such news. To *nabbaʾa* someone is to inform them of something momentous. And the content of the news is: *what they did*. There is something almost startling in this. The great disclosure of the Last Day is not information about God, or about the universe, or about the unseen. It is information about *oneself*. The news that will be broken to every human being is the record of his own life. The Qur'an describes the moment repeatedly:

> *"And the Book will be set down, and thou wilt see the guilty fearful of what is in it. They will say: 'Woe unto us! What a book this is! It leaves out nothing, small or great, but that it has counted it.' And they find present all that they did, and thy Lord wrongs no one."* (Qur'an 18:49)

> *"On that Day man will be informed of what he sent forth and left behind. Nay, but man is a clear proof against himself, though he offer his excuses."* (Qur'an 75:13–15)

That last phrase — *"man is a clear proof against himself"* — connects directly to verse 24 of this sūrah, where the tongues, hands, and feet bear witness. The evidence at that trial is the defendant.

**Why Information Rather Than Sentence?**

It is worth noticing that the verb is *inform* rather than *judge* or *punish*. The commentators draw a point from this. The sentence follows, but the first act is disclosure — and for many, the disclosure will itself be the shock. People genuinely do not know what they have done. The sūrah has said so directly: *"you supposed it slight, though it is great in the Eyes of God"* (v. 15). The Day is therefore described, first of all, as a correction of the record: a complete and accurate account, delivered to a person who has spent a lifetime with an inaccurate one.

**"And God Is Knower of All Things"**

*Wa'llāhu bi-kulli shayʾin ʿalīm.* The sūrah's last words. And they are the same words that closed the Light Verse: *"and God is Knower of all things"* (v. 35). The repetition is a structural signature. The chapter's central verse and its final verse end identically, so that the reader who reaches the last word of the sūrah is returned to its heart. The connection is more than formal. The Light Verse described God as the source of all illumination — the One by whom things are seen. This verse describes Him as the One who knows all things. Light and knowledge are, in the Qur'anic vocabulary, the same reality viewed from two sides: to illuminate is to make known, and to know is to have light upon a thing. So the sūrah ends by naming, once more, what it named at its centre. The chapter called *The Light* closes with the Knower of all things — and they are the same.

**The Sūrah in Retrospect**

It is worth gathering, at the end, what this chapter has done. It opened by announcing itself as revealed, ordained, measured, and clear (v. 1). It legislated the gravest sexual offence and, in the same breath, made the accusation of it nearly impossible to prove and severely punishable to make (vv. 2–9). It narrated the destruction of a woman's reputation by her own community and spent sixteen verses dissecting how a decent society does such a thing to itself (vv. 10–26). It then built the walls that would prevent a recurrence: the door that must be knocked upon, the eye that must be lowered, the dress that must not display, the marriage that must be facilitated, the powerless who must not be exploited (vv. 27–34). And then, having done all that, it said: *God is the Light of the heavens and the earth* (v. 35). It described the houses where that light is kept and the men who keep it (vv. 36–38), the mirage and the layered darkness that are the alternative (vv. 39–40), and the signs written across the sky and the earth for anyone willing to look (vv. 41–46). It exposed those who profess and withdraw (vv. 47–54), promised establishment to those who believe and act (v. 55), and prescribed the daily practice by which that promise is met (v. 56). Then — astonishingly, after a promise of sovereignty over the earth — it returned to the door, the knock, the child at midday, the old woman's garment, the blind man at the table, the greeting on entering, and the courtesy of asking before one leaves (vv. 58–63). That return is the sūrah's final teaching, and it is the reason the chapter is shaped as it is. There is no separation, in this vision, between the cosmic and the domestic. The Light of the heavens and the earth is encountered in a house whose door is respected, by a person whose eyes and tongue are governed, among people who greet one another with peace. And the last thing the chapter says, before it ends, is that the One who arranged all of it knows all of it — including the state you are in as you finish reading.

---
